<meta>This page serves as the introductory disclaimer and usage guide for a digitized volume, establishing the legal and functional context for the digital reproduction that follows.</meta>

This is a digital copy of a book that was preserved for generations on library shelves before it was carefully scanned by Google as part of a project to make the world’s books discoverable online.

It has survived long enough for the copyright to expire and the book to enter the public domain. A <term>public domain</term> <note>Works no longer protected by intellectual property laws</note> book is one that was never subject to copyright or whose legal copyright term has expired. Whether a book is in the public domain may vary country to country. Public domain books are our gateways to the past, representing a wealth of history, culture and knowledge that’s often difficult to discover.

Marks, notations and other marginalia present in the original volume will appear in this file — a reminder of this book’s long journey from the publisher to a library and finally to you.

## Usage guidelines

Google is proud to partner with libraries to digitize public domain materials and make them widely accessible. Public domain books belong to the public and we are merely their custodians. Nevertheless, this work is expensive, so in order to keep providing this resource, we have taken steps to prevent abuse by commercial parties, including placing technical restrictions on automated querying.

We also ask that you:

+ **Make non-commercial use of the files** We designed Google Book Search for use by individuals, and we request that you use these files for personal, non-commercial purposes.
+ **Refrain from automated querying** Do not send automated queries of any sort to Google’s system: If you are conducting research on <term>machine translation</term> <note>The use of software to translate text between languages</note>, <term>optical character recognition</term> <note>OCR; the electronic conversion of images of typed or handwritten text into machine-encoded text</note> or other areas where access to a large amount of text is helpful, please contact us. We encourage the use of public domain materials for these purposes and may be able to help.
+ **Maintain attribution** The Google “watermark” you see on each file is essential for informing people about this project and helping them find additional materials through Google Book Search. Please do not remove it.
+ **Keep it legal** Whatever your use, remember that you are responsible for ensuring that what you are doing is legal. Do not assume that just because we believe a book is in the public domain for users in the United States, that the work is also in the public domain for users in other countries. Whether a book is still in copyright varies from country to country, and we can’t offer guidance on whether any specific use of any specific book is allowed. Please do not assume that a book’s appearance in Google Book Search means it can be used in any manner anywhere in the world. Copyright infringement liability can be quite severe.

## About Google Book Search

Google’s mission is to organize the world’s information and to make it universally accessible and useful. Google Book Search helps readers discover the world’s books while helping authors and publishers reach new audiences. # To the Reader <note>original: "L. S." (Lectori Salutem), a traditional Latin greeting meaning "Greetings to the Reader."</note>

This new edition of the **Ethics**, which we have decided to offer separately to the students and followers of Spinoza who have requested it from all sides, reproduces as faithfully as possible the text of the 1895 edition of the *Collected Works*.

For since an unkind fate <note>This refers to the death of the scholar Jan Pieter Nicolaas Land in 1897.</note> prevented the illustrious Land, who had curated that edition, from also dedicating his efforts to the publication of this specific book—and from revising, reworking, or correcting the work himself where necessary—we felt it was a matter of professional integrity not to rashly or independently change what that most learned man, who was a most serious and cautious authority on these matters, had established with such prudent judgment.

We have only sought to remove typographical errors occurring here and there; we have also included several corrections proposed by the learned H. Gorter, which Land himself had acknowledged and approved in the preface to Volume III of the earlier edition.

Finally, in preparing certain passages, we had at our disposal manuscript notes which Land had written in his own copy of the **first edition** <term>original: "Ed. principis" (editio princeps); the very first printed version of a text, in this case, the 1677 posthumous edition of Spinoza's works.</term> when he first edited Spinoza's works.

Thus, we trust that through these efforts we have both provided for the convenience of our readers and honored the memory of a man who served the study of Spinoza so well.

->The Hague,<- <note>original: "Hagae Comitis," the Latin name for the city of The Hague in the Netherlands.</note>
->MARTINUS NIJHOFF.<-
->1905.<-

<summary>This is the publisher's preface for the 1905 edition of Spinoza's Ethics, explaining that the text is based on the authoritative 1895 version by J.P.N. Land, with minor corrections and notes from Land's personal first-edition copy.</summary>

# ETHICS

## DEMONSTRATED IN GEOMETRICAL ORDER
<note>Spinoza famously applied the "geometrical method" (similar to Euclid's geometry) to philosophy, building his arguments through strict definitions, axioms, and propositions to ensure logical certainty.</note>

### AND

### DIVIDED INTO FIVE PARTS,

->*which treat of*<-

I. Concerning **GOD**.
II. Concerning the **NATURE** and origin of the **MIND**.
III. Concerning the **ORIGIN** and **NATURE** of the **EMOTIONS** <term>Affects: Translated from the Latin "affectus," these refer to the various states of mind and body—such as joy, sadness, or desire—that increase or decrease a person’s power to act.</term>.
IV. Concerning **HUMAN SERVITUDE**, or the **POWER OF THE EMOTIONS** <note>original: "servitute humana"; Spinoza uses "servitude" to describe the state of being controlled by external forces and irrational passions rather than by reason.</note>.
V. Concerning the **POWER OF THE INTELLECT**, or **HUMAN FREEDOM**.

<summary>This is the title page of Baruch Spinoza's seminal work, outlining a five-part philosophical journey from the nature of God to the attainment of human freedom through reason.</summary>
<keywords>Spinoza, Ethics, Geometrical Method, God, Mind, Emotions, Human Servitude, Freedom, Intellect</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page marks the beginning of Baruch Spinoza’s "Ethics," one of the most significant works of Western philosophy. The translation ensures that the technical vocabulary of 17th-century metaphysics is accessible to a modern reader while maintaining the logical rigor of the original Latin text.</meta>

# ETHICS

## PART ONE,

## CONCERNING GOD.

### DEFINITIONS.

I. By <term>cause of itself <note>original: "causa sui"</note></term> I understand that whose essence involves existence; or, that whose nature cannot be conceived except as existing.

II. A thing is called **finite in its own kind** when it can be limited by another thing of the same nature. For example, a body is called finite because we can always conceive of another body that is larger. Likewise, a thought is limited by another thought. But a body is not limited by a thought, nor a thought by a body.

III. By <term>substance <note>Spinoza uses "substance" to describe the most fundamental reality that does not depend on anything else to exist.</note></term> I understand that which is in itself and is conceived through itself; that is, that whose concept does not require the concept of another thing from which it must be formed.

IV. By **attribute** I understand that which the intellect perceives of substance as constituting its essence.

V. By **mode** I understand the <term>affections <note>In this context, "affections" refers to the specific qualities, states, or variations of a substance.</note></term> of substance, or that which is in something else through which it is also conceived.

VI. By **God** I understand a being absolutely infinite, that is, a substance consisting of infinite attributes, each of which expresses an eternal and infinite essence.

### EXPLANATION.

I say *absolutely infinite*, but not *infinite in its own kind*; for whatever is only infinite in its own kind, we can deny infinite attributes of it; but whatever is absolutely infinite contains within its essence everything that expresses essence and involves no negation.

VII. That thing shall be called **free** which exists solely by the necessity of its own nature and is determined to action by itself alone. But it is called **necessary**, or rather **compelled**, if it is determined by another thing to exist and operate in a fixed and determined manner.

VIII. By **eternity** I understand existence itself, insofar as it is conceived to follow necessarily from the definition alone of the eternal thing.

### EXPLANATION.

For such existence is conceived as an eternal truth, just like the essence of the thing, and therefore it cannot be explained by duration or time, even if the duration is conceived to be without beginning or end.

<summary>This page introduces the foundational definitions of Spinoza’s philosophical system, establishing his unique concepts of God, Substance, and Freedom. It sets the stage for his argument that God is an absolutely infinite and necessary substance.</summary>

<keywords>Spinoza, Ethics, metaphysics, substance, God, cause of itself, attributes, modes, infinity, eternity, free will vs necessity</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues from the definitions of Part One of Spinoza’s "Ethics." The text completes the final definition before moving into the Axioms—the self-evident truths upon which Spinoza builds his logical proof of the nature of God and the universe.</meta>

# ETHICS PART I.

and therefore cannot be explained through duration or time, even if duration is conceived to lack a beginning and an end.

## A X I O M S.

I. Everything that exists is either in itself or in something else. <note>This is the starting point of Spinoza's logic: a thing is either independent (substance) or dependent on something else (an organic property or "mode").</note>
II. That which cannot be conceived through another thing must be conceived through itself.
III. From a given, determinate cause, an effect necessarily follows; and conversely, if no determinate cause is given, it is impossible for an effect to follow.
IV. Knowledge of an effect depends on, and involves, knowledge of its cause.
V. Things that have nothing in common with each other also cannot be understood through each other; or, the concept of one does not involve the concept of the other.
VI. A true idea must agree with its <term>object <note>original: "ideato." This refers to the actual thing that an idea represents.</note></term>.
VII. Whatever can be conceived as not existing, its essence does not involve existence.

## P R O P O S I T I O N I.

*Substance is prior in nature to its <term>affections <note>original: "affectionibus." These are the "modes" or specific states of a substance defined earlier.</note></term>.*

### D E M O N S T R A T I O N

This is evident from *Definitions 3 and 5.*

## P R O P O S I T I O N II.

*Two substances having different attributes have nothing in common with one another.*

### D E M O N S T R A T I O N

This is also evident from *Definition 3.* For each must be in itself and be conceived through itself; or, the concept of one does not involve the concept of the other.

## P R O P O S I T I O N III.

*If things have nothing in common with each other, one of them cannot be the cause of the other.*

### D E M O N S T R A T I O N

If they have nothing in common with each other, then (*by Axiom 5*) they cannot be understood through each other, and therefore (*by Axiom 4*) one cannot be the cause of the other. <note>original: "Q. E. D." (Quod erat demonstrandum) — Which was to be demonstrated.</note>

## P R O P O S I T I O N IV.

*Two or more distinct things are distinguished from one another either by the diversity of the attributes of the substances, or by the diversity of the affections of those substances.*

### D E M O N S T R A T I O N

Everything that exists is either in itself or in something else (*by Axiom 1*); that is (*by Definitions 3 and 5*), nothing exists outside the intellect except substances and their <note>1)</note> affections. Therefore, nothing exists outside the intellect by which several things can be distinguished from one another, except substances, or—what is the same thing (*by Definition <note>2)</note> 4.*)—their attributes and their affections. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

---

<margin>
1) Dutch Version. — First Edition: *its* [referring to the singular 'substance'].
2) Editors Schmidt, Sigwart, Camerer. — First Edition: *Axiom [4].*
</margin>

<summary>This page establishes the fundamental Axioms of Spinoza’s system and the first four Propositions, proving that substances with different attributes are entirely distinct and cannot cause one another.</summary>
<keywords>axioms, cause and effect, substance, attributes, affections, logic, rationalism, Spinoza</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical sequence of Spinoza’s "Ethics," Part I. Having established the basic definitions of substance and attribute and the first four propositions, Spinoza now moves to prove that substance is unique, self-caused, and infinite. This builds directly on Proposition IV, which stated that things are distinguished either by their attributes or their specific states (affections).</meta>

<header>CONCERNING GOD.</header>

# PROPOSITION V.

*In the nature of things, there cannot be two or more substances of the same nature or <term>attribute <note>original: "attributi." To Spinoza, an attribute is what the intellect perceives as the very essence of a substance.</note></term>.*

## DEMONSTRATION.

If several distinct substances were granted, they would have to be distinguished from one another either by a diversity of attributes or by a diversity of <term>affections <note>original: "affectionum." These are the "modes" or specific modifications of a substance (see Definition 5).</note></term> (*by the preceding Proposition*). If they are distinguished only by a diversity of attributes, it will therefore be granted that there is only one substance of the same attribute. But if they are distinguished by a diversity of affections—since substance is prior in nature to its affections (*by Proposition 1*)—then if we set aside the affections and consider the substance in itself, that is (*by Definition 3 and Axiom 1 <note>The original text notes a variation here: the first edition cites Axiom 6, while the editor Saisset suggests Definition 4.</note>*) truly considered, it cannot be conceived as being distinguished from another. That is (*by the preceding Proposition*), there cannot be several substances, but only one. <note>original: "Q. E. D." (Quod Erat Demonstrandum), meaning "Which was to be demonstrated."</note>

# PROPOSITION VI.

*One substance cannot be produced by another substance.*

## DEMONSTRATION.

In the nature of things, there cannot be two substances of the same attribute (*by the preceding Proposition*); that is (*by Proposition 2*), they have nothing in common with one another. And so (*by Proposition 3*), one cannot be the cause of the other, or one cannot be produced by the other. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

## COROLLARIUM.

From this it follows that a substance cannot be produced by anything else. For in the nature of things, nothing exists except substances and their affections, as is clear from *Axiom 1 and Definitions 3 and 5.* And yet, it cannot be produced by a substance (*by the preceding Proposition*). Therefore, substance absolutely cannot be produced by anything else. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

## Alternatively.

This is demonstrated even more easily by the <term>absurdity of the contradiction <note>original: "ex absurdo contradictorio." A logical proof where one assumes the opposite of the goal to show it leads to an impossible result.</note></term>. For if a substance could be produced by something else, the knowledge of it would have to depend on the knowledge of its cause (*by Axiom 4*); and so (*by Definition 3*) it would not be a substance.

# PROPOSITION VII.

*It belongs to the nature of substance to exist.*

## DEMONSTRATION.

Substance cannot be produced by anything else (*by the Corollary to the preceding Proposition*); it will therefore be the <term>cause of itself <note>original: "causa sui." This does not mean the substance "created" itself in time, but that its nature is such that it cannot be thought of as non-existent.</note></term>, that is (*by Definition 1*), its essence necessarily involves existence, or it belongs to its nature to exist. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

# PROPOSITION VIII.

*Every substance is necessarily infinite.*

## DEMONSTRATION.

A substance of one attribute exists only as a unique entity (*by Proposition 5*), and it belongs to its nature to exist (*by Proposition 7*). It will therefore be of its...

---

1) First Edition: *by Definitions 3 and 6.* Saisset: *Definitions 3 and 4.*

<note>Vocabulary: substance, attribute, affection (mode), cause of itself, to exist, infinite, demonstration, corollary, Axiom, Definition.</note>

<summary>Spinoza demonstrates that no two substances can share an attribute, leading to the conclusion that substance is unique, cannot be created by anything else, and must necessarily exist by its own nature.</summary>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical progression of Spinoza’s "Ethics," Part I. The preceding page established that substances cannot be produced by other substances. Here, Spinoza completes the proof for Proposition 7 (that existence belongs to the nature of substance) and Proposition 8 (that every substance is necessarily infinite), before moving into a lengthy "Scholium" or commentary on how these concepts are often misunderstood.</meta>

<header>ETHICS, PART I.</header>

nature, to exist as either finite or infinite <gloss>1) Perhaps: either finite or infinite.</gloss> But it is not finite. For (*by Definition 2*) it would have to be limited by another of the same nature, which also would necessarily have to exist (*by Proposition 7*); and so there would be two substances of the same attribute, which is absurd (*by Proposition 5*). Therefore, it exists as infinite. <note>original: "Q. E. D." (Quod erat demonstrandum), meaning "Which was to be demonstrated." This concludes the proof for Proposition 8.</note>

## SCHOLIUM I.

Since being finite is, in reality, a partial negation, and being infinite is the absolute affirmation of the existence of some nature, it follows from Proposition 7 alone that every substance must be infinite.

## SCHOLIUM II.

I do not doubt that it is difficult for all those who judge things confusedly, and who are not accustomed to knowing things through their primary causes, to grasp the demonstration of Proposition 7; this is surely because they do not distinguish between the <term>modifications <note>original: "modificationes," also referred to as "modes." These are the particular forms, states, or "accidents" that a substance takes.</note></term> of substances and the substances themselves, nor do they know how things are produced. Hence it happens that they attribute the beginning, which natural things are seen to have, to substances; for those who are ignorant of the true causes of things confuse everything. Without any mental resistance, they imagine both trees and men speaking, and they imagine men being formed from stones as well as from seed, and any forms whatever being changed into any other forms. So also, those who confuse the divine nature with the human easily attribute human <term>affects <note>original: "affectus," referring to emotions, passions, or psychological states.</note></term> to God, especially as long as they are also ignorant of how affects are produced in the mind.

If, however, men would attend to the nature of substance, they would not doubt the truth of Proposition 7 at all; indeed, this Proposition would be an axiom to everyone and would be numbered among common notions. For by substance they would understand that which is in itself and is conceived through itself—that is, that whose knowledge does not need the knowledge of another thing. But by modifications, they would understand that which is in another, and whose concept is formed from the concept of the thing in which they are. Therefore, we can have true ideas of non-existing modifications; for although they do not exist actually outside the intellect, their essence is nevertheless so comprehended in another that they can be conceived through it. But the truth of substances outside the intellect is not found anywhere except in themselves, because they are conceived through themselves. If anyone, therefore, were to say that he had a clear and distinct—that is, true—idea of a substance, and nevertheless doubted whether such a substance existed, it would truly be the same—by Hercules!—as if he said that he had a true idea and nevertheless doubted whether it were false (as is manifest to anyone who pays sufficient attention). Or, if anyone asserts that a substance is created, he simultaneously asserts that a false idea has become true, than which surely nothing more absurd can be conceived. Therefore, it must necessarily be confessed that the existence of a substance, just like its essence, is an eternal truth.

And from this, we can conclude in another way that there is only one substance of the same nature, which I have thought worthwhile to show here. To do this in an orderly way, it must be noted first (I) that the true definition of each thing involves and expresses nothing except the nature of the thing defined. From which follows this second point (II), namely, that no definition involves any certain number of individuals—

<summary>Spinoza demonstrates that substance is necessarily infinite and its existence is an eternal truth, while critiquing the common tendency to confuse substances with their temporary states or modes.</summary>
<keywords>substance, infinity, modes, modifications, essence, existence, Spinoza, Scholium, divine nature</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Scholium II" (a detailed commentary) following Proposition 8 in Spinoza's "Ethics," Part I. The text continues an argument about why definitions of a thing's nature do not dictate the number of individuals that exist, leading to the conclusion that there can only be one substance of any given nature.</meta>

<header>ON GOD.</header>

[...tance, they easily imagine that substances can also have a beginning. For a true definition does not] involve or express a number of individuals, since it expresses nothing other than the nature of the thing defined. For example, the definition of a triangle expresses nothing other than the simple nature of a triangle, but not a certain number of triangles. III. It must be noted that for every existing thing, there must necessarily be a certain cause for why it exists. IV. Finally, it must be noted that this cause, for which some thing exists, must either be contained within the nature and definition of the existing thing itself <insert>(namely, because it belongs to its nature to exist)</insert>, or it must be given outside of that thing. 

From these points, it follows that if a certain number of individuals exists in nature, there must necessarily be a cause why those individuals—and why neither more nor fewer—exist. If, for example, 20 men exist in the nature of things <insert>(whom, for the sake of greater clarity, I suppose exist simultaneously, and that no others existed before them)</insert>, it will not be enough <note>meaning: it is not a sufficient logical explanation</note> to show the cause of human nature in general in order to account for why 20 men exist; instead, it will be necessary to show the cause why 20 exist, and neither more nor fewer, since <note>referring back to Note III</note> for each individual there must necessarily be a cause why it exists. But this cause <note>referring back to Notes II and III</note> cannot be contained in human nature itself, since the true definition of a man does not involve the number twenty. Therefore <note>referring to Note IV</note>, the cause why these twenty men exist, and consequently why each one exists, must necessarily be given outside each one of them. For this reason, it must be concluded absolutely that everything of such a nature that multiple individuals can exist must necessarily have an external cause in order for them to exist. 

Now, since it belongs to the nature of <term>substance <note>original: "substantiæ." In Spinoza's philosophy, substance is that which exists in itself and is conceived through itself; it is the fundamental reality.</note></term> to exist (as already shown in this Scholium), its definition must involve necessary existence, and consequently its existence must be concluded from its definition alone. However, from its definition <note>as shown from Notes II and III</note> the existence of multiple substances cannot follow; it therefore follows necessarily from that definition that only a single substance of the same nature exists, as was proposed.

## PROPOSITION IX.

*The more reality or being each thing has, the more attributes belong to it.*

### DEMONSTRATION

This is evident from <term>Definition 4 <note>Definition 4 states: "By attribute I understand what the intellect perceives of a substance, as constituting its essence."</note></term>.

## PROPOSITION X.

*Each attribute of a single substance must be conceived through itself.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

For an <term>attribute <note>original: "attributum." These are the essential characteristics of substance that the mind perceives, such as "thought" or "extension" (physical space).</note></term> is that which the intellect perceives of a substance as constituting its essence <note>per Definition 4</note>; and therefore <note>per Definition 3</note> it must be conceived through itself. <note>original: "Q. E. D." (Quod erat demonstrandum), meaning "Which was to be demonstrated."</note>

### SCHOLIUM.

From these points it is apparent that, although two attributes may be conceived as <term>really distinct <note>original: "realiter distincta." This is a technical term in 17th-century philosophy meaning they can be understood as separate things without one depending on the concept of the other.</note></term>—that is, one without the help of the other—we cannot nonetheless conclude from this that they constitute two beings, or two diverse substances. For it is in the nature of substance that each of its attributes...

be conceived through itself; since all the attributes it has were always in it simultaneously, and one could not be produced by another; but each one expresses the reality or being of substance. It is therefore far from being absurd to attribute several attributes to one substance; indeed, nothing in nature is clearer than that every being must be conceived under some attribute, and the more reality or being it has, the more attributes it has which express both necessity (or eternity) and infinity. Consequently, nothing is clearer than that an absolutely infinite being must necessarily be defined (as we stated in <note>original: "Defin. 6."</note> *Definition 6*) as a being that consists of infinite attributes, each of which expresses a certain eternal and infinite essence. If anyone now asks by what sign, then, we can distinguish a diversity of substances, let them read the following Propositions, which show that in the nature of things only one substance exists, and that it is absolutely infinite; for this reason, it would be futile to seek such a sign.

## PROPOSITION XI.

*God, or a substance consisting of infinite attributes, each of which expresses an eternal and infinite essence, necessarily exists.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

If you deny this, conceive, if it is possible, that God does not exist. Therefore (<note>original: "per Axiom. 7."</note> *by Axiom 7*) his essence does not involve existence. But this (*by Proposition 7*) is absurd: Therefore God necessarily exists. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### *Alternative Proof.*

For every thing, a cause or reason must be assigned, both for why it exists and for why it does not exist. For example, if a triangle exists, a reason or cause must be given for why it exists; if, however, it does not exist, a reason or cause must also be given which prevents it from existing, or which takes its existence away. This reason or cause must either be contained in the nature of the thing or outside of it. For example, the nature of a square circle indicates the reason why it does not exist; namely, because it involves a contradiction. Why a substance exists, however, follows also from its nature alone, <gloss>1)</gloss> <note>The Latin text notes a correction from "which" to "because"</note> because it involves existence (*see Proposition 7*). But the reason why a circle or triangle <gloss>2)</gloss> <note>The Latin text notes a correction in the grammatical ending of "triangle"</note> exists, or why it does not exist, does not follow from their nature, but from the order of the whole of corporeal nature; for from that order it must follow either that the triangle already necessarily exists, or that it is impossible for it to exist now. And these things are self-evident. From which it follows that a thing necessarily exists if no reason or cause is given which prevents it from existing. If, therefore, no reason or cause can be given which prevents God from existing, or which takes his existence away, it must be concluded entirely that he necessarily exists. But if such a reason or cause were given, it would have to be given either in the nature of God itself or outside of it—that is, in another substance of another nature. For if it were of the same nature, by that very fact it would be granted that God exists. But a substance that

<meta>This page continues the demonstration of Proposition 11 from the previous page, providing the final logic for the necessary existence of God and introducing an alternative proof based on the concept of power. It then begins a Scholium (explanatory note) addressing common prejudices regarding the difficulty of existence.</meta>

-># ON GOD.<-

...of another nature, it would have nothing in common with God (according to <note>original: "per 2. Prop."</note> *Proposition 2*), and therefore could neither establish nor take away his existence. Since, therefore, a reason or cause that would take away the divine existence cannot exist outside the divine nature, it would necessarily have to exist—if indeed he does not exist—within his very nature itself, which would therefore involve a contradiction. And to affirm this of a Being absolutely infinite and supremely perfect is absurd; therefore, neither in God nor outside God is any cause or reason given which would take away his existence, and consequently God necessarily exists. <note>original: "Q. E. D." - Quod erat demonstrandum: "Which was to be demonstrated."</note>

### *Another Way.*

The inability to exist is a lack of power, and conversely, the ability to exist is a power (as is self-evident). If, then, the only things that already necessarily exist are finite beings, then finite beings are more powerful than the absolutely infinite Being. This (as is self-evident) is absurd; therefore, either nothing exists, or an absolutely infinite Being also necessarily exists. But we ourselves exist, either in ourselves or in something else that necessarily exists (see <note>original: "vid. Axiom. 1. et Prop. 7."</note> *Axiom 1 and Proposition 7*). Therefore, an absolutely infinite Being—that is (<note>original: "per Defin. 6."</note> *by Definition 6*) God—necessarily exists. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### SCHOLIUM.
<note>A Scholium is an informal discussion where the author explains the implications of the formal proofs just provided.</note>

In this last demonstration, I wanted to show God's existence *a posteriori* <note>original: "a posteriori" — an argument based on experience or observed effects (like our own existence) rather than starting from a definition.</note> so that the proof might be more easily understood; but not because God’s existence does not also follow *a priori* <note>original: "a priori" — an argument based on the inherent nature or definition of a thing, prior to experience.</note> from this same foundation. For, since the ability to exist is a power, it follows that the more reality belongs to the nature of a thing, the more strength it has from itself to exist; and so an absolutely infinite Being, or God, has from himself an absolutely infinite power of existing, and for that reason exists absolutely. 

Many, however, will perhaps not easily see the clarity of this demonstration, because they are accustomed to contemplate only those things which are produced by external causes. Of these things, they see that those which are produced quickly—that is, which easily come into existence—also easily perish. Conversely, they judge those things to be more difficult to produce—that is, not so easy to bring into existence—if they conceive that more properties belong to them. Truly, to free them from these prejudices, I do not need to show here in what way the saying *what is quickly made, quickly perishes* is true, nor whether, in respect to the whole of nature, all things are equally easy or not. 

But it is enough to note only this: I am not speaking here of things that are produced by external causes, but only of <term>substances</term> <note>In Spinoza’s system, a substance is something that exists independently and does not need anything else to create or define it.</note> which (by <note>original: "per Prop. 6."</note> *Proposition 6*) can be produced by no external cause. For things produced by external causes—whether they consist of many parts or few—whatever perfection or reality they have is all due to the power of the external cause; therefore, their existence arises from the perfection of the external cause alone, and not from their own. On the contrary, whatever perfection a substance has is due to no external cause; therefore, its existence must follow from its nature alone, which is nothing other than its essence. Perfection, then, does not take away the existence of a thing, but on the contrary, establishes it; whereas imperfection takes it away. Consequently, we can be certain of the existence of nothing more than the existence of an absolutely infinite or perfect Being, that is, God. For since his essence excludes all imperfection and involves absolute perfection, by that very fact it [removes] every cause for doubting his...

Spinoza also clarifies that unlike physical objects, a substance's existence depends solely on its own essence, not external causes.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the demonstration of Proposition 11 regarding the necessary existence of God and transitions into a series of proofs about the indivisibility of substance, leading to the core argument of Spinoza's monism: that only one substance (God) exists.</meta>

# ETHICS, PART I.

...removes existence, and gives the highest certainty regarding it, which I believe will be clear to anyone paying moderate attention.

## PROPOSITION 12.

*No attribute of a substance can be truly conceived from which it would follow that the substance can be divided.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

For the parts into which a substance so conceived would be divided will either retain the nature of substance, or they will not. If the first case holds <note>That is, if the parts remain substances themselves.</note>, then (according to *Proposition 8*) each part must be infinite, and (according to *Proposition 6*) the <term>cause of itself</term> <note>original: "causa sui"; the idea that something's essence involves existence.</note>, and (according to *Proposition 5*) must consist of a different attribute; thus, many substances could be formed from one, which (according to *Proposition 6*) is absurd. Add to this that the parts (according to *Proposition 2*) would have nothing in common with their whole, and the whole (according to *Definition 4 and Proposition 10*) could both exist and be conceived without its parts, which no one will be able to doubt is absurd. If, however, the second case is assumed—namely, that the parts will not retain the nature of substance—then, since the whole substance would be divided into equal parts, it would lose the nature of substance and would cease to be, which (according to *Proposition 7*) is absurd.

## PROPOSITION 13.

*Substance which is absolutely infinite is indivisible.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

For if it were divisible, the parts into which it would be divided will either retain the nature of an absolutely infinite substance, or they will not. If the first holds, there will then be several substances of the same nature, which (according to *Proposition 5*) is absurd. If the second is assumed, then (as above) an absolutely infinite substance could cease to be, which (according to *Proposition 11*) is also absurd.

### COROLLARY.

From these points it follows that no substance, and consequently no corporeal substance <note>Spinoza is arguing against the common view that "matter" or "body" can be split into smaller, independent pieces of reality.</note>, insofar as it is substance, is divisible.

### SCHOLIUM.

That substance is indivisible is understood more simply from this alone: that the nature of substance cannot be conceived except as infinite, and that by a "part" of substance nothing else can be understood than a finite substance, which (according to *Proposition 8*) involves a manifest contradiction.

## PROPOSITION 14.

*No substance can be granted or conceived besides God.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

Since God is a Being absolutely infinite, of whom no attribute which expresses the essence of substance can be denied (according to *Definition 6*), and he necessarily exists (according to *Proposition 11*); if any substance besides God were granted, it would have to be explained through some attribute of God, and thus two substances of the same attribute would exist, which (according to *Proposition 5*) is absurd; and therefore no substance outside of God can be granted, and consequently cannot even be conceived. For if it could be conceived, it would necessarily have to...

<meta>This page continues the logical progression of Spinoza’s "Ethics," concluding the proof that God is the only substance and beginning the famous argument that everything that exists does so "within" God (Panentheism). It follows the discussion of the indivisibility of infinite substance from page 8.</meta>

# ON GOD.

...conceived as existing; but this (by the first part of this Demonstration) is absurd. Therefore, outside of God no substance can be given or conceived. <note>original: "Q. E. D."; abbreviation for "Quod Erat Demonstrandum," meaning "Which was to be demonstrated."</note>

### COROLLARARY I.

From this it follows most clearly, first: that God is unique, that is (by Definition 6), that in the nature of things only one substance is given, and that it is absolutely infinite, as we have already hinted in the Scholium to Proposition 10.

### COROLLARY II.

It follows, second: that <term>extended thing</term> <note>original: "rem extensam"; the physical world or matter.</note> and <term>thinking thing</term> <note>original: "rem cogitantem"; the world of thought or mind.</note> are either attributes of God, or (by Axiom 1) <term>affections</term> <note>original: "affectiones"; the specific states or "modes" that an attribute can take, such as a specific idea or a specific physical object.</note> of God’s attributes.

## PROPOSITION XV.

*Whatever is, is in God, and nothing can be or be conceived without God.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

Besides God, no substance can be given or conceived (by Proposition 14), that is (by Definition 3), a thing which is in itself and is conceived through itself. But <term>modes</term> <note>original: "modi"; the specific expressions or versions of substance, like a wave is a "mode" of the ocean.</note> (by Definition 5) can neither be nor be conceived without substance; wherefore these can be only in the divine nature, and can be conceived through it alone. But besides substances and modes, nothing exists (by Axiom 1). Therefore, nothing can be or be conceived without God. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### SCHOLIUM.

There are those who imagine God as a human being, composed of body and mind, and subject to passions; but how far these wander from the true knowledge of God is sufficiently clear from what has already been demonstrated. But I leave these people aside: for all who have in any way contemplated the divine nature deny that God is corporeal. This they also prove very well by the fact that by "body" we understand any quantity—long, broad, and deep—bounded by some certain figure; nothing more absurd than this could be said of God, namely, of an absolutely infinite being. <margin>a</margin> <note>This margin marker likely refers to a specific cross-reference or source Spinoza intended to cite regarding the definition of body.</note> Yet, meanwhile, by the other arguments with which they attempt to demonstrate this same thing, they clearly show that they entirely remove corporeal or extended substance itself from the divine nature, and they maintain that it was created by God. But from what divine power it could have been created, they are utterly ignorant; which clearly shows that they do not understand what they themselves are saying. I, at least, have demonstrated clearly enough, in my judgment (see Corollary to Proposition 6 and Scholium 2 of Proposition 8), that no substance can be produced or created by another. Furthermore, in Proposition 14, we have shown that besides God, no substance can be given or conceived; and from this we concluded that extended substance is one of God’s infinite attributes. However, for a fuller explanation, I will refute the arguments of my opponents, all of which come down to this. *First*, that corporeal substance, insofar as it is substance, consists, so they think, of parts; and therefore they deny that it can be infinite, and consequently they deny that it can belong to God. And they explain this with many examples, of which I will bring forward one or two. If corporeal substance, they say, is infinite, let it be conceived as divided into two parts; each part will be either finite or infinite. If the former, then the infinite is composed of two finite parts, which is absurd. If the latter, then there exists one infinite twice as large as another infinite, which...

<meta>This page continues Spinoza’s defense of the idea that "extension" (matter/space) is an attribute of God. He is currently addressing and refuting the traditional philosophical arguments which claimed that matter is "unworthy" of God because it is divisible and finite.</meta>

# ETHICS PART I.

is also absurd. Furthermore, if infinite quantity were measured by parts equal to feet, it would have to consist of infinite such parts; as it would also if it were measured by parts equal to inches; and therefore one infinite number would be twelve times greater than another infinite number. **Finally**, if from one point 
<image-desc>A geometric diagram showing an angle with vertex A and two rays extending to the right, labeled B (top) and C (bottom). A small "1)" is written near the vertex A.</image-desc>
of a certain infinite quantity, two lines, such as AB and AC, are conceived to extend to infinity from a certain and determined distance at the beginning, it is certain that the distance between B and C increases continuously, and finally from being determined, it will become indeterminable. Since, therefore, these absurdities follow, as they think, from the fact that an infinite quantity is supposed, they conclude from this that corporeal substance must be finite and, consequently, does not pertain to God’s essence.

The second argument is also sought from the supreme perfection of God. For God, they say, since He is a most perfect being, cannot <term>suffer</term> <note>original: "pati"; in 17th-century philosophy, to "suffer" or "be passive" means to be changed or acted upon by something else. Since God is the ultimate cause, critics argued He cannot be acted upon by anything.</note>; yet corporeal substance, because it is <term>divisible</term>, can suffer; it follows, therefore, that it does not pertain to God’s essence.

These are the arguments I find among writers by which they attempt to show that corporeal substance is unworthy of the divine nature and cannot pertain to it. But truly, if anyone attends to this correctly, they will find that I have already responded to these things; since these arguments are based only on the fact that they suppose corporeal substance to be composed of parts, which I have already shown (*Proposition 12, with the Corollary to Proposition 13*) to be absurd. 

Next, if anyone wishes to weigh the matter correctly, they will see that all those absurdities (*if indeed they are all absurdities, which I am not now disputing*), from which they wish to conclude that extended substance is finite, do not follow at all from the fact that an infinite quantity is supposed, but from the fact that they suppose an infinite quantity to be <term>measurable</term> and to be composed of finite parts. Therefore, from the absurdities that follow from that, they can conclude nothing else than that infinite quantity is not measurable and cannot be composed of finite parts. And this is the very thing we have already demonstrated above (*Proposition 12, etc.*). Therefore, the weapon they aim at us, they actually hurl back at themselves. 

If, therefore, they still wish to conclude from this absurdity of theirs that extended substance must be finite, they are doing nothing else, by Hercules <note>original: "hercle"; an interjection emphasizing the absurdity of the opponents' logic.</note>, than if someone, from the fact that they imagined a circle to have the properties of a square, concluded that a circle has no center from which all lines drawn to the circumference are equal. For corporeal substance, which cannot be conceived except as infinite, unique, and indivisible (*see Propositions 8, 5, and 12*), they—in order to conclude that it is finite—conceive as being composed of finite parts, and as being manifold and divisible. So too others, after they imagine that a line is composed of points, know how to find many arguments to show that a line cannot be divided infinitely. And indeed, it is no less absurd...

--- Page 26 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Scholium" to Proposition 15 in Part I of the *Ethics*. Spinoza is finishing his refutation of the idea that "extension" (physical matter and space) is unworthy of God because it is supposedly divisible. He is arguing that while we *imagine* things to be divisible, our *reason* understands that substance itself is unified and indivisible.</meta>

# ON GOD.

it is to posit that corporeal substance is composed of bodies or parts, as it is to posit that a body is composed of surfaces, surfaces of lines, and finally lines of points. And this everyone who knows that clear reason is infallible must confess, especially those who deny that a <term>vacuum</term> <note>original: "vacuum"; a space entirely empty of matter. Spinoza, following Cartesian physics, argued that space is a "plenum"—completely full—meaning "empty space" is a logical impossibility.</note> exists. For if corporeal substance could be so divided that its parts were really distinct, why then could one part not be annihilated while the rest remained connected to each other as before? And why must all parts be so fitted together that no vacuum is given? Truly, of things which are <term>really distinct</term> <note>In 17th-century philosophy, things are "really distinct" if they can exist independently of one another. Spinoza argues that the parts of matter are only conceptually distinct, not truly independent.</note> from each other, one can exist and remain in its state without the other. Since, therefore, there is no vacuum in Nature (about which I will speak elsewhere), but all parts must so concur that no vacuum is given, it follows from this also that these parts cannot be "really" distinguished—that is, corporeal substance, insofar as it is substance, cannot be divided.

If anyone should now ask why we are so prone by nature to dividing quantity? I answer him that quantity is conceived by us in two ways: namely, abstractly or superficially, as we **imagine** it; or as substance, which is done by the **intellect** alone. If, therefore, we attend to quantity as it is in the imagination—which is done often and more easily by us—it will be found to be finite, divisible, and composed of parts; but if we attend to it as it is in the intellect, and conceive it insofar as it is substance—which is done with great difficulty—then, as we have already sufficiently demonstrated, it will be found to be infinite, unique, and indivisible.

This will be manifest enough to all who have known how to distinguish between the imagination and the intellect: especially if this is also noted, that matter is everywhere the same, nor are parts distinguished in it except insofar as we conceive matter to be affected in different ways, whence its parts are distinguished only <term>modally</term> <note>original: "modaliter"; meaning they are different versions or "modes" of the same thing, like waves in the ocean, rather than separate things.</note>, but not really. For example, we conceive of water, insofar as it is water, being divided and its parts being separated from each other; but not insofar as it is corporeal substance; for insofar as it is that, it is neither separated nor divided. Furthermore, water, insofar as it is water, is generated and corrupted; but insofar as it is substance, it is neither generated nor corrupted.

And by these things, I think I have also responded to the second argument; since it is also founded on this: that matter, insofar as it is substance, is divisible and composed of parts. And even if this were not so, I do not know why it would be unworthy of the divine nature: since (*by Proposition 14*) no substance can be given outside of God by which it would <term>suffer</term> <note>original: "pateretur" / "pati"; to be acted upon or changed by an external force. Spinoza argues that because God is everything, nothing exists "outside" Him to change Him.</note>. All things, I say, are in God, and all things which happen, happen only through the laws of the infinite nature of God, and follow from the necessity of His essence (as I will soon show); wherefore it can by no means be said that God suffers from another, or that extended substance is unworthy of the divine nature, even if it is supposed to be divisible, provided it is granted to be eternal and infinite. But enough of these things for the present.

## PROPOSITION XVI.

***From the necessity of the divine nature, infinite things in infinite ways (that is, all things which can fall under an infinite intellect) must follow.***

### DEMONSTRATION.

This Proposition ought to be manifest to everyone, if only...

<meta>This page concludes the proof of Proposition 16 regarding the infinite nature of God’s production and introduces Proposition 17, which defines God as a free cause acting only by the laws of his own nature.</meta>

# PART I OF THE ETHICS.

...let him observe that, from the given definition of any thing whatever, the intellect concludes several properties, which in truth necessarily follow from the definition (that is, from the very essence of the thing). The more reality the definition of the thing expresses—that is, the more reality the essence of the defined thing involves—the more properties the intellect concludes. Since, however, the divine nature has absolutely infinite attributes <note>original: "per Defin. 6." (according to Definition 6); Spinoza previously defined God as a substance consisting of infinite attributes, each expressing eternal and infinite essence.</note>, each of which also expresses an infinite essence in its own kind, it follows necessarily that infinite things in infinite ways (that is, everything which can fall under an infinite intellect) must follow from this necessity. <note>original: "Q. E. D." (Quod erat demonstrandum); a traditional Latin marking for the end of a proof, meaning "which was to be demonstrated."</note>

->COROLLARY I.<-

From this it follows that God is the <term>efficient cause</term> <note>The "efficient cause" is the primary source of change or creation. In this context, it means God is the actual producer of all things that the mind can conceive.</note> of all things which can fall under an infinite intellect.

->COROLLARY II.<-

It follows, secondly, that God is a cause <term>through himself</term> <note>original: "per se"; meaning God acts by his own power and nature, rather than being triggered by something else ("per accidens").</note>, and not by accident.

->COROLLARY III.<-

It follows, thirdly, that God is absolutely the first cause.

### PROPOSITION XVII.

*God acts solely from the laws of his own nature, and is compelled by no one.*

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

We have just shown in *Proposition 16* that from the sole necessity of the divine nature, or (what is the same thing) from the sole laws of that same nature, infinite things follow absolutely; and in *Proposition 15* we demonstrated that nothing can exist or be conceived without God, but that all things are in God. Therefore, nothing can exist outside of him by which he might be determined or compelled to act. Thus, God acts solely from the laws of his own nature and is compelled by no one. *Q. E. D.*

->COROLLARY I.<-

From this it follows, first, that there is no cause, whether external or internal, which moves God to act, except the perfection of his own nature.

->COROLLARY II.<-

It follows, secondly, that God alone is a <term>free cause</term>. For God alone exists solely from the necessity of his own nature (*by Proposition 11 and Corollary 1 of Proposition 14*), and acts solely from the necessity of his own nature (*by the preceding Proposition*). Therefore (*by Definition 7*), he alone is a free cause. *Q. E. D.*

->SCHOLIUM.<-

<note>A "Scholium" is an explanatory note or informal commentary used to clarify the preceding technical proofs.</note>

Others think that God is a free cause because he can, as they suppose, bring it about that those things which we said follow from his nature—that is, those things which are in his power—do not happen, or are not produced by him. But this is the same as if they said that God could bring it about that it does not follow from the nature of a triangle that its three angles are equal to two right angles; or that an effect does not follow from its cause, which is absurd. Furthermore, I will show later on, without the help of this Proposition, that neither intellect nor will pertain to the nature of God. I know indeed that there are many who think they can demonstrate that supreme intellect and free will pertain to God's nature; for they say they know of nothing more perfect...

<summary>Spinoza concludes that God is the efficient and first cause of all things, acting entirely by his own necessity rather than by external compulsion. He argues that God's freedom consists in being self-determined, not in having the "free will" to change the laws of logic or nature.</summary>
<keywords>efficient cause, first cause, free cause, divine nature, necessity, free will vs necessity, Spinoza's Ethics</keywords>

--- Page 28 ---
<meta>This page continues the scholium (explanatory note) to Proposition 17, where Spinoza argues against the traditional view of God’s will and intellect. He is refuting the idea that God chose to create only a small selection of what he was capable of creating.</meta>

# OF GOD.

...they say they recognize that they can attribute to God what is the highest perfection in us. Furthermore, even though they conceive of God as actually understanding in the highest degree, they do not believe he can cause everything he understands to exist; for they think that in this way they would destroy God's power. If, they say, he had created everything in his intellect, he could have created nothing more thereafter, which they believe contradicts God's omnipotence; therefore they prefer to establish God as indifferent to everything, creating nothing except what he decreed to create by a certain absolute will. 

But I think I have shown clearly enough <note>original: "vid. Prop. 16." (see Proposition 16)</note> that from the supreme power of God, or his infinite nature, infinite things in infinite ways—that is, all things—have necessarily flowed, or always follow with the same necessity; in the same way as it follows from the nature of a triangle from eternity to eternity that its three angles equal two right angles. Therefore, God's omnipotence was in action from eternity, and will remain in the same actuality for eternity. And in this way, God's omnipotence is established as far more perfect, in my judgment. 

Indeed, his opponents (if I may speak openly) seem to deny God's omnipotence. For they are forced to admit that God understands infinite creatable things, which he nevertheless can never create. For otherwise, if he were to create everything he understands, he would, according to them, exhaust his omnipotence and render himself imperfect. To establish God as perfect, they are reduced to having to state at the same time that he cannot do everything to which his power extends; I cannot see how anything more absurd or more contrary to God's omnipotence could be imagined. 

Furthermore, to say something here about intellect and will, which we commonly attribute to God: if intellect and will belong to the eternal essence of God, something else must surely be understood by each of these attributes than what people usually do. For the intellect and will that would constitute God's essence would have to differ <note>original: "toto cœlo"; literally "by the whole sky," an idiom meaning "as much as possible" or "entirely."</note> from our intellect and will, nor could they agree in anything except the name; no differently than how the "Dog," the heavenly constellation, and the "dog," the barking animal, agree with each other. <note>Spinoza is using a classic logic example: the word "dog" refers to two completely different things (the star Sirius and the pet), sharing only a name but no physical or essential properties.</note> 

Which I will demonstrate thus: If intellect belongs to the divine nature, it cannot be, like our intellect, posterior—as most prefer—or simultaneous by nature with the things understood, since God is prior to all things in <term>causality</term> <note>original: "per Coroll. 1. Prop. 16." (through Corollary 1 of Proposition 16). Spinoza means God is the source of everything, so his "thought" cannot be a reaction to things that already exist.</note>; but on the contrary, the truth and <term>formal essence</term> <note>The "formal essence" is the actual, objective reality of a thing as it exists in itself, as opposed to how it is perceived.</note> of things is such because it exists <term>objectively</term> <note>In 17th-century philosophy, "objectively" meant "as an object of thought in the mind." Spinoza argues that things are the way they are because that is how God thinks them.</note> as such in God's intellect. 

Therefore, God's intellect, insofar as it is conceived to constitute God's essence, is truly the cause of things, both of their <term>essence</term> and their <term>existence</term>; which seems to have been noticed by those who asserted that God's intellect, will, and power are one and the same. Since, therefore, God's intellect is the sole cause of things—namely (as we have shown) both of their essence and their existence—it must necessarily differ from them both in its essence and its existence. For an effect <note>original: "causatum"</note> differs from its cause precisely in what it receives from the cause. 

For example, one man is the cause of the existence, but not the essence, of another man; for the essence <note>original: "hæc æterna veritas" (this eternal truth)</note> is an eternal truth; and therefore they can entirely agree according to essence; but they must differ in existing. And for that reason, if the existence of one perishes, the other's does not; but if the essence of one could be destroyed and become false,

<summary>Spinoza argues that God's omnipotence is not a choice of will, but a logical necessity: everything God "thinks" must exist. He further explains that "divine intellect" is fundamentally different from human intellect, acting as the cause of things rather than a reflection of them.</summary>
<keywords>omnipotence, divine intellect, essence vs existence, causality, Spinoza, necessity, formal essence, objective existence</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the argument from Proposition 17 regarding the difference between divine and human intellect, then moves into the fundamental nature of God's causality and eternity. It maintains the mathematical "geometric" style of Spinoza's Ethics, providing proofs for each claim.</meta>

# ETHICS PART I.

...the essence of the other would also be destroyed. Therefore, a thing that is the cause of both the essence and the existence of some effect must differ from that effect, both in regard to essence and in regard to existence. But God's intellect is the cause of both the essence and the existence of our intellect: therefore God's intellect, insofar as it is conceived to constitute the divine essence, differs from our intellect both in regard to essence and in regard to existence, and cannot agree with it in any respect except in name, as we intended to show. <note>Spinoza famously illustrates this elsewhere by saying the "intellect" of God and the "intellect" of man are as different as the "Dog" which is a constellation in the sky and the "dog" which is a barking animal.</note> The same procedure applies to the will, as anyone can easily see.

### PROPOSITION XVIII.

*God is the immanent cause of all things, but not the transitive cause.*

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

Everything that is, is in God and must be conceived through God (*by Prop. 15*); and so (*by Coroll. 1, Prop. 16 of this part*) God is the cause of the things which are in him; this is the first point. Next, outside of God no substance can be given (*by Prop. 14*), that is (*by Def. 3*), a thing which is in itself outside of God; this was the second point. Therefore, God is the immanent <term>immanent cause: a cause that remains within itself while producing effects, rather than a "transitive" cause which acts upon something outside itself</term> cause of all things, not the transitive cause. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### PROPOSITION XIX.

*God, or all of God’s attributes, are eternal.*

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

For God (*by Def. 6*) is a substance which (*by Prop. 11*) necessarily exists—that is (*by Prop. 7*), it belongs to his nature to exist, or (what is the same thing) from his definition it follows that he exists, and so (*by Def. 8*) he is eternal. Next, by God's attributes we must understand that which (*by Def. 4*) expresses the essence of divine substance, that is, that which pertains to substance: this very thing, I say, the attributes themselves must involve. But eternity belongs to the nature of substance (*as I have already demonstrated from Prop. 7*); therefore each one of the attributes must involve eternity, and so they are all eternal. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

->SCHOLIUM.<-

This Proposition is also most clearly evident from the way in which (*Prop. 11*) I demonstrated God's existence; from that demonstration, I say, it is established that God's existence, like his essence, is an eternal truth. Furthermore, (*Prop. 19 of Descartes's Principles*) <note>original: "Principiorum Cartesii"; referring to Spinoza's own earlier work, *The Principles of Cartesian Philosophy* (1663), where he translated Descartes's ideas into a geometric format.</note> I have demonstrated God's eternity in another way as well, and there is no need to repeat it here.

### PROPOSITION XX.

*God’s existence and his essence are one and the same.*

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

God (*by the preceding Prop.*) and all his attributes are eternal—that is (*by Def. 8*), each of his attributes expresses existence. Therefore, those same attributes of God which (*by Def. 4*) explain God's eternal essence, simultaneously explain his eternal existence, that

<meta>Catchword: that</meta>

<summary>Spinoza argues that God is an immanent cause, meaning all things exist within God, and establishes that God's essence is identical to his existence.</summary>

--- Page 30 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical progression of Spinoza’s "Ethics," specifically concluding the proof that in God, existence and essence are identical. It then moves into the implications of this identity: God’s immutability and the infinite nature of things that follow directly from God’s attributes.</meta>

<page-num>15</page-num>
<header>CONCERNING GOD.</header>

is, that very thing which constitutes the essence of God, at the same time constitutes His existence; and so, this [existence] and His essence are one and the same. <note>original: "Q. E. D." — abbreviation for 'Quod Erat Demonstrandum', meaning 'Which was to be demonstrated.'</note>

## COROLLARY I.

From this it follows, first, that God's existence, just like His essence, is an eternal truth.

## COROLLARY II.

It follows, second, that God, or all of God's <term>attributes: the fundamental characteristics that the mind perceives as constituting the essence of substance (e.g., Thought and Extension)</term>, are immutable. For if they were changed in respect to existence, they would also have to be changed (*by the preceding Proposition*) in respect to essence; that is (*as is self-evident*), truths would become false, which is absurd.

# PROPOSITION XXI.

*All things which follow from the absolute nature of any attribute of God must always and as infinite exist, or they are eternal and infinite through the same attribute.*

## DEMONSTRATION.

Conceive, if it is possible (if indeed you deny it), that something in some attribute of God follows from its absolute nature which is finite, and has a determined existence or duration—for example, the idea of God in <term>thought: for Spinoza, 'Thought' is an infinite attribute of God, while individual human thoughts are 'modes' or expressions of that attribute</term>. But thought, since it is assumed to be an attribute of God, is necessarily (*by Prop. 11*) infinite by its nature. However, insofar as it contains the idea of God, it is assumed to be finite. But (*by Definition 2*) a finite thing cannot be conceived unless it is determined by thought itself. But it cannot be determined by thought itself insofar as it constitutes the idea of God; for to that extent it is assumed to be finite. Therefore, it must be [determined] by thought insofar as it does *not* constitute the idea of God; which nevertheless (*by Prop. 11*) must necessarily exist.

There is, therefore, thought not constituting the idea of God, and therefore from its nature, insofar as it is absolute thought, the idea of God does not necessarily follow. (For it is conceived as both constituting and not constituting the idea of God.) Which is contrary to the hypothesis. Therefore, if the idea of God in thought, or anything (it is all the same, whatever is taken, since the demonstration is universal) in any attribute of God follows from the necessity of the absolute nature of that attribute, it must necessarily be infinite. This was the first point.

Next, that which follows in this way from the necessity of the nature of any attribute cannot have a determined duration. For, if you deny it, let it be assumed that a thing which follows from the necessity of the nature of some attribute is given in some attribute of God—for example, the idea of God in thought—and let it be assumed to have once not existed, or that it will not exist in the future. Since, however, thought is assumed to be an attribute of God, it must exist both necessarily and immutably (*by Prop. 11 and Coroll. 2, Prop. 20*). Therefore, beyond the limits of the duration of the idea of God (for it is assumed to have once not existed, or that it will not exist), thought must exist without the idea of God. But this is contrary to the hypothesis; for it is assumed that from given thought, the idea of God necessarily follows. Therefore, the idea of God in thought, or anything that follows necessarily from the absolute nature of any attribute of God, cannot have a determined duration; but is eternal through the same attribute. This was the second point. Note, this same thing must be affirmed of any thing which follows necessarily from the absolute nature of God in any attribute of God.

<summary>This page establishes that God's existence and essence are identical and eternal, making God and His attributes immutable. It further proves that any direct consequence of God's absolute nature (like the "idea of God") must itself be infinite and eternal.</summary>
<keywords>essence, existence, immutability, attributes, thought, idea of God, infinity, eternity, Spinoza</keywords>

### PROPOSITION XXII.

*Whatever follows from any attribute of God, insofar as it is modified by a modification that exists both necessarily and as infinite through the same [attribute], must also exist both necessarily and as infinite.*

->D E M O N S T R A T I O N.<-

The demonstration of this Proposition proceeds in the same manner as the demonstration of the preceding one. <note>The "preceding one" refers to Proposition 21, which established that things following from the absolute nature of an attribute are eternal and infinite.</note>

### PROPOSITION XXIII.

*Every <term>mode: a specific "state" or "affection" of a substance; for Spinoza, all individual things (like a specific thought or a physical object) are modes of God</term> which exists both necessarily and as infinite, must have necessarily followed either from the absolute nature of some attribute of God, or from some attribute modified by a modification which exists both necessarily and as infinite.*

->D E M O N S T R A T I O N.<-

For a mode is in something else, through which it must be conceived (*by Definition 5*); that is (*by Proposition 15*), it is in God alone, and can be conceived through God alone. If, therefore, a mode is conceived to exist necessarily and to be infinite, both of these must necessarily be concluded or perceived through some attribute of God, insofar as that same [attribute] is conceived to express infinity and necessity of existence, or (*which is the same thing by Definition 8*) eternity; that is (*by Definition 6 and Proposition 19*), insofar as it is considered absolutely. Therefore, a mode which exists both necessarily and as infinite must have followed from the absolute nature of some attribute of God; and this either immediately (*concerning which see Proposition 21*), or through the mediation of some modification which follows from its absolute nature, that is (*by the preceding Proposition*), which exists both necessarily and as infinite. <note>original: "Q. E. D." — Which was to be demonstrated.</note>

### PROPOSITION XXIV.

*The essence of things produced by God does not involve existence.*

->D E M O N S T R A T I O N.<-

This is evident from Definition 1. For that whose nature (considered in itself, of course) involves existence is the "cause of itself" <note>original: "causa sui"</note>, and exists from the necessity of its nature alone.

->C O R O L L A R Y.<-

From this it follows that God is the cause not only for things to begin to exist, but also for them to persevere in existing; or (to use a <term>Scholastic term: language used by medieval philosophers and theologians</term>), God is the **cause of being** for things <note>original: "causa essendi rerum"</note>. For, whether things exist or do not exist, whenever we attend to their essence, we find that it involves neither existence nor duration; and therefore their essence can be the cause neither of their existence nor of their duration; but only God [can], to whose nature alone existence belongs (*by Corollary 1 of Proposition 14*).

### PROPOSITION XXV.

*God is the <term>efficient cause: the primary agent or source that brings something into being</term> not only of the existence of things, but also of their essence.*

->D E M O N S T R A T I O N.<-

If you deny this, then God is not the cause of the essence of things; and therefore (*by Axiom 4*) the essence of things can be conceived without God: but this (*by...*)

<header>ON GOD.</header>

*Proposition 15) is absurd. Therefore, God is the cause of the essences of things as well.*
*Which was to be demonstrated.*

->S C H O L I U M<-
<note>A "scholium" is an explanatory note or commentary used in formal proofs to clarify the preceding logic.</note>

This Proposition follows more clearly from Proposition 16. For it follows from that, that from a given divine nature, both the essence and the existence of things must necessarily be concluded; and, to put it in a word, in the same sense that God is called the <term>cause of himself (original: "causa sui")</term>, he must also be called the cause of all things, which will become even clearer from the following Corollary.

->C O R O L L A R Y<-
<note>A "corollary" is a logical consequence that follows directly from a proven proposition.</note>

Particular things are nothing but <term>affections: the specific states, properties, or "feelings" of a substance</term> of God's attributes, or <term>modes: the specific expressions or "versions" of God's attributes, like a single thought or a physical object</term>, by which the attributes of God are expressed in a certain and determined way. The demonstration is clear from Proposition 15 and Definition 5.

## PROPOSITION XXVI.

*A thing which has been determined to act in a certain way was necessarily determined by God; and that which has not been determined by God cannot determine itself to act.*

->D E M O N S T R A T I O N<-

That by which things are said to be determined to act is necessarily something positive (*as is self-evident*); and so, God is the <term>efficient cause: the actual agent or force that brings something into being</term> of both its essence and its existence from the necessity of his nature (*by Propositions 25 and 16*); which was the first point. From this, the second point proposed also follows most clearly. For if a thing that is not determined by God could determine itself, the first part of this proposition would be false; which is absurd, as we have shown.

## PROPOSITION XXVII.

*A thing which has been determined by God to act in a certain way cannot render itself undetermined.*

->D E M O N S T R A T I O N<-

This Proposition is evident from the third Axiom. <note>Axiom 3 states that from a given determined cause, an effect necessarily follows; conversely, if there is no determined cause, no effect can follow.</note>

## PROPOSITION XXVIII.

*Every singular thing, or any thing which is finite and has a determined existence, cannot exist, nor be determined to act, unless it is determined to exist and act by another cause, which is also finite and has a determined existence: and again, this cause also cannot exist, nor be determined to act, unless it is determined to exist and act by another, which is also finite and has a determined existence, and so on to infinity.*

->D E M O N S T R A T I O N<-

Whatever is determined to exist and act is so determined by God (*by Proposition 26 and the Corollary to Proposition 24*). But that which is finite and has a determined existence could not have been produced by the absolute nature of any attribute of God: for whatever follows from the absolute nature of any attribute of God is infinite and eternal (*by...*

<summary>Spinoza establishes that God is the cause of both the existence and the essence of all things, defining individual objects as "modes" or expressions of God's attributes. He argues that every finite thing is part of an infinite chain of causes, where each thing is determined to act by another finite cause, ultimately tracing back to God.</summary>
