<meta>This page continues Spinoza’s geometric proof regarding the chain of causality. It concludes the demonstration of Proposition 28 (erroneously labeled 21 in this specific transcription, though continuing the logic of how finite things are determined) and moves into the foundational argument for universal determinism in Proposition 29.</meta>

<header>ETHICS PART I.</header>

*Proposition 21.* <note>The text here concludes the proof that every finite thing must be produced by another finite thing in an infinite chain.</note>). Therefore, it must have followed from God, or from some attribute of his, insofar as it is considered as affected in some way; for besides substance and modes, nothing exists (*by Axiom 1 and Definitions 3 and 5*); and modes (*by Corollary to Proposition 25*) are nothing but <term>affections: the specific states or properties</term> of God's attributes. But it also could not have followed from God, or from some attribute of his, insofar as it is affected by a modification that is eternal and infinite (*by Proposition 22*). Therefore, it must have followed from, or been determined to exist and operate by, God or some attribute of his, insofar as it is modified by a modification which is finite and has a determined existence. Which was the first point. Then this cause in turn, or this mode (*by the same reasoning by which we have just now demonstrated the first part of this*), must also have been determined by another, which is also finite and has a determined existence, and again this last (*by the same reasoning*) by another, and so on (*by the same reasoning*) forever to infinity. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

->S C H O L I U M<-
<note>A scholium is an explanatory note or commentary used in formal proofs to clarify the preceding logic.</note>

Since certain things had to be produced by God immediately—namely, those which follow necessarily from his absolute nature—through the mediation of these primary things <note>Spinoza refers here to "infinite modes," which are the most fundamental laws or structures of the universe that stand between God's absolute nature and individual objects.</note> 1), which nevertheless can neither be nor be conceived without God; from this it follows:

I. That God is the <term>absolutely proximate cause: the direct and immediate reason for an effect, without any intermediate steps</term> of things produced immediately by him; but not "in his own kind," as the saying goes. For the effects of God can neither be nor be conceived without their cause (*by Proposition 15 and Corollary to Proposition 24*).

II. That God cannot properly be called the *remote cause* of singular things, except perhaps for this reason: namely, so that we may distinguish them from those things which he produced immediately, or rather, which follow from his absolute nature. For by a <term>remote cause: a cause that is separated from its effect by a chain of other events</term>, we understand one that is in no way joined with its effect. But all things that are, are in God, and so depend on God that without him they can neither be nor be conceived.

### PROPOSITION XXIX.

*In the nature of things, there is nothing <term>contingent: something that could either be or not be, or could be different than it is</term>; but all things are determined by the necessity of the divine nature to exist and operate in a certain way.*

->D E M O N S T R A T I O N<-

Whatever is, is in God (*by Proposition 15*): but God cannot be called a contingent thing: For (*by Proposition 11*) he exists necessarily, not contingently. Next, the <term>modes: the specific expressions or versions of God's substance, such as individual thoughts or physical objects</term> of the divine nature have also followed from it necessarily, not contingently (*by Proposition 16*), and this either insofar as the divine nature is considered absolutely (*by Proposition 21*), or insofar as it is considered determined to act in a certain way (*by Proposition 27*). Furthermore, God is the cause of these modes not only insofar as they simply exist (*by Corollary to Proposition 24*), but also (*by Proposition 26*) insofar as they are considered determined to operate in some way. But if they were not determined by God (*by the same Proposition*), it would be impossible, not contingent—

<meta>This page continues the conclusion of Proposition 29, which establishes that everything in the universe is determined by necessity. # CONCERNING GOD.

it is for them to determine themselves; and on the contrary (*by Proposition 27*), if they have been determined by God, it is impossible, and not indeed <term>contingent: something that happens by chance or could be otherwise</term>, for them to render themselves undetermined. Therefore, all things are determined by the necessity of the divine nature, not only to exist, but also to exist and operate in a certain way, and there is no such thing as the contingent. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

->SCHOLIUM.<-
<note>A scholium is an informal explanation or commentary added to a formal proof to help the reader understand the broader context.</note>

Before I proceed further, I wish to explain here—or rather to remind you—what we should understand by "Nature Naturing" <note>original: "Naturam Naturantem"</note> and "Nature Natured" <note>original: "Naturam Naturatam"</note>. For from what has gone before, I think it is already established that by **Nature Naturing** we must understand that which is in itself and is conceived through itself, or such attributes of substance as express an eternal and infinite essence—that is (*by Corollary 1 of Proposition 14 and Corollary 2 of Proposition 17*), God, insofar as he is considered as a free cause. By **Nature Natured**, however, I understand everything that follows from the necessity of God's nature, or from any one of God's attributes—that is, all the <term>modes: the specific forms or expressions</term> of God's attributes, insofar as they are considered as things which are in God, and which can neither be nor be conceived without God.

## PROPOSITION XXX.

*An intellect, whether actually finite or actually infinite, must comprehend the attributes of God and the affections of God, and nothing else.*

->DEMONSTRATIO.<-

A true idea must agree with its object (*by Axiom 6*); that is (*as is self-evident*), that which is contained objectively in the intellect must necessarily exist in Nature. But in Nature (*by Corollary 1 of Proposition 14*) only one substance exists, namely God; nor do any other <term>affections: the specific states or properties of a substance</term> exist (*by Proposition 15*) than those which are in God, and which (*by the same Proposition*) can neither be nor be conceived without God. Therefore, an intellect, whether actually finite or actually infinite, must comprehend the attributes of God and the affections of God, and nothing else. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

## PROPOSITION XXXI.

*The actual intellect, whether it be finite or infinite, as well as will, desire, love, etc., must be referred to Nature Natured, and not indeed to Nature Naturing.*

->DEMONSTRATIO.<-

For by "intellect" (*as is self-evident*) we do not understand absolute thought, but only a certain mode of thinking, which mode differs from others, namely desire, love, etc., and therefore (*by Definition 5*) must be conceived through absolute thought; namely (*by Proposition 15 and Definition 6*), it must be so conceived through some attribute of God which expresses the eternal and infinite essence of thought, that without it, it can neither be nor be conceived. And for that reason (*by the Scholium to Proposition 29*), it must be referred to **Nature Natured**, and not to **Nature Naturing**, just like the remaining modes of thinking. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

->SCHOLIUM.<-

The reason why I speak here of the "actual intellect" <note>original: "intellectu actu"</note> is not because I concede that...

<summary>Spinoza argues that everything is strictly determined by God's nature and introduces the distinction between God as a creative force (Nature Naturing) and the universe as the produced result (Nature Natured). He concludes that the human intellect and emotions are parts of this produced "Nature Natured."</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the discussion on the intellect (Proposition 31) and moves into the nature of the will and the absolute necessity of the universe (Propositions 32 and 33). It continues the geometric proof-style argument regarding God's attributes and their effects.</meta>

# PART I. CONCERNING GOD.

that any intellect exists in <term>potentiality: a capacity to do something that is not yet being done</term>; but, because I desire to avoid all confusion, I have chosen to speak only of that which we perceive as clearly as possible—namely, of intellection itself, for nothing is perceived by us more clearly than this. Indeed, we can understand nothing that does not lead to a more perfect knowledge of our own understanding.

### PROPOSITION XXXII.

*The will cannot be called a free cause, but only a necessary one.*

->**DEMONSTRATION.**<-

The will is only a certain <term>mode: a specific expression or state</term> of thinking, just like the intellect; and therefore (*by Proposition 28*) any single act of will cannot exist, nor be determined to act, unless it is determined by another cause, and this again by another, and so on to infinity. Even if the will were supposed to be infinite, it must still be determined to exist and act by God, not insofar as He is an absolutely infinite substance, but insofar as He possesses an attribute that expresses the infinite and eternal essence of thought (*by Proposition 23*). In whatever way it is conceived, whether as finite or infinite, the will requires a cause by which it is determined to exist and act; and therefore (*by Definition 7*), it cannot be called a free cause, but only a necessary or compelled one. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

->**COROLLARY I.**<-

From this it follows, first, that God does not act from freedom of will. <note>Spinoza argues here against the traditional religious view that God "chooses" to create the world out of various options; for him, God's actions follow necessarily from His nature.</note>

->**COROLLARY II.**<-

It follows, second, that the will and the intellect relate to the nature of God in the same way as motion and rest, and absolutely as all natural things do, which (*by Proposition 29*) must be determined by God to exist and act in a certain way. For the will, like everything else, requires a cause by which it is determined to exist and act in a specific manner. And, although an infinite number of things follow from a given will or intellect, God cannot for that reason be said to act from freedom of will any more than He can be said to act from the "freedom of motion and rest" (for infinite things also follow from these). Therefore, the will does not belong to the nature of God any more than other natural things do, but stands in the same relation to Him as motion and rest, and all other things which we have shown follow from the necessity of the divine nature and are determined by it to exist and act in a certain way.

### PROPOSITION XXXIII.

*Things could have been produced by God in no other way, and in no other order, than they have been produced.*

->**DEMONSTRATION.**<-

For all things have followed necessarily from the given nature of God (*by Proposition 16*), and have been determined to exist and act in a certain way by the necessity of God’s nature (*by Proposition 29*). If, therefore, things could have been of a different nature, or could have been determined to act in another way, so that the order of nature were different, then the nature of God could also have been different than it now is; and consequently (*by Proposition 11*), that other nature would also have to exist, and—

<meta>Digitized by Google</meta>

<summary>Spinoza argues that the will is not free but is a "mode" of thought governed by necessity, and concludes that the universe could not have been created in any other way than it currently exists.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the proof of Proposition 33, which argues that the universe is a necessary result of God's nature and could not have been created differently. It then moves into two extensive commentaries (Scholia) defining the nature of necessity and contingency, and critiquing the traditional religious concept of God's "absolute will."</meta>

# CONCERNING GOD.

...consequently, two or more Gods could exist, which (*by Corollary 1 of Proposition 14*) is absurd. Therefore, things [could have been produced] in no other way and in no other order, etc. *Which was to be demonstrated.* <note>original: "Q. E. D." (Quod Erat Demonstrandum)</note>

### SCHOLIUM I.

<note>original: "Scholium" — a scholarly note or commentary used to explain a preceding argument in more detail.</note>

Since I have shown here, clearer than the noonday sun, that there is absolutely nothing in the nature of things that can be called **contingent** <term>contingent: something that might happen or might not, often implying it occurs by chance</term>, I now wish to explain briefly what we should understand by the "Contingent"; but first, what we mean by the "Necessary" and the "Impossible."

A thing is called **necessary** either by reason of its <term>essence: the fundamental nature or definition of a thing</term> or by reason of its cause. For the existence of any thing follows necessarily either from its own essence and definition, or from a given <term>efficient cause: the primary source or agent that brings a thing into being</term>.

Next, for these same reasons, a thing is called **impossible**; namely, because either its essence or definition involves a contradiction, or because no external cause is provided that is determined to produce such a thing.

But a thing is called **contingent** for no other reason than a defect in our knowledge. For if we do not know that a thing's essence involves a contradiction, or if we know well that it involves no contradiction and yet we can affirm nothing for certain about its existence because the order of causes is hidden from us—such a thing can never appear to us as either necessary or impossible. Therefore, we call it either "contingent" or "possible."

### SCHOLIUM II.

From the preceding, it follows clearly that things were produced by God with the highest perfection; since they followed necessarily from a given nature that is most perfect. Nor does this charge God with any imperfection; for His own perfection has compelled us to affirm this. Indeed, from its opposite, it would follow clearly (as I have just shown) that God is not supremely perfect; namely, because if things had been produced in another way, another nature would have to be attributed to God, different from that which we were compelled to attribute to Him from the consideration of a **Most Perfect Being**.

Truly, I do not doubt that many will reject this opinion as absurd, and will not wish to apply their minds to weighing it; for no other reason than that they are accustomed to attribute to God another kind of liberty, far different from that which was handed down by us (*in Definition 7* <gloss>The editor Sigwart notes that the first edition reads "Definition 6"</gloss>); namely, **absolute will**.

But I also do not doubt, if they were willing to meditate on the matter and rightly weigh the series of our demonstrations, that they would at last plainly reject such liberty as they now attribute to God, not only as trifling, but as a great obstacle to science. There is no need for me to repeat here what was said in the Scholium to Proposition 17. Nevertheless, for their sake, I will still show that even if it were granted that "will" belongs to God's essence, it nevertheless follows from His perfection that things could have been created by God in no other way and in no other order. This will be easy to show if we first consider what they themselves grant: namely, that it depends on God's decree and will alone that each thing is what it is. For otherwise, God would not be the cause of all things. Next, [we must consider] that all God's decrees were sanctioned by God Himself from eternity. For otherwise, imper- <note>The text ends mid-word: "imper-" likely begins "imperfection."</note>

---
1) Sigwart. — First Edition: *Definition 6.*

<summary>Spinoza argues that the world is a necessary expression of God's perfect nature, and that 'contingency' is merely a term for things we do not yet understand. He begins a critique of the idea that God has an 'absolute will' or choice in creation, arguing instead that everything follows eternally from God's decrees.</summary>
<keywords>Necessity, Contingency, Divine Perfection, Absolute Will, Efficient Cause, Spinoza's Ethics, Determinism</keywords>

--- Page 37 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Scholium II" (the second commentary) to Proposition 33. On the previous page, Spinoza argued that things could not have been produced by God in any other way or order. Here, he defends that claim against critics who believe God has the freedom to change His mind or that God chooses based on an external standard of "Goodness."</meta>

# ETHICS PART I.

...[he would] be accused of imperfection and inconstancy. But since in the eternal there is no **when, before, or after** <note>original: "quandò, antè, nec post"</note>: from this it follows, namely from God's perfection alone, that God can never decree anything else, nor could he ever have done so; or rather, God did not exist before his decrees, nor can he exist without them.

But they say that, even if it were supposed that God had made a different nature of things, or that from eternity he had decreed something else concerning Nature and its order, no imperfection in God would follow from it. Truly, if they say this, they will simultaneously concede that God can change his decrees. For if God had decreed something else concerning Nature and its order than what he did decree—that is, if he had willed and conceived something else concerning Nature—he would necessarily have had a different <term>intellect: the faculty of understanding or thought</term> than he now has, and a different <term>will: the faculty of choice or decree</term> than he now has. 

And if it is permissible to attribute a different intellect and a different will to God without any change to his essence and his perfection, what reason is there why he cannot now change his decrees concerning created things, and nevertheless remain equally perfect? For his intellect and will concerning created things and their order is the same in respect to his essence and perfection, however it is conceived. 

Furthermore, all the **Philosophers** I have seen concede that in God there is no <term>intellect in potentiality: the capacity to think of something not yet realized</term>, but only <term>intellect in act: thought that is fully realized and identical with its object</term>; since, however, both his intellect and his will are not distinguished from his essence, as all also concede, it follows from this as well that if God had had a different actual intellect and a different will, his essence would also necessarily be different; and consequently (as I concluded from the beginning), if things had been produced by God differently than they now are, God's intellect and his will—that is (as is conceded), his essence—would have to be different, which is absurd.

Since therefore things could have been produced by God in no other way and in no other order, and since the truth of this follows from God's supreme perfection, no sound reason can indeed persuade us to believe that God did not wish to create all things that are in his intellect with that same perfection with which he understands them. 

But they will say that in things there is no perfection nor imperfection; but that which is in them, because of which they are called perfect or imperfect, and good or bad, depends only on God's will; and thus if God had willed, he could have brought it about that what is now perfection would be the greatest imperfection, and vice-versa. 

But what else would this be but to openly affirm that God, who necessarily understands that which he wills, can by his will bring it about that he understands things in a way other than how he understands them; which (as I have just shown) is a great absurdity. Therefore I can turn the argument back against them in this way. All things depend on God's power. For things to be able to be different, God's will would also necessarily have to be different; but God's will cannot be different (as we have most evidently shown from God's perfection). Therefore things cannot be different either. 

I admit that this opinion—which subjects everything to a certain indifferent will of God and establishes that everything depends on his **good pleasure** <note>original: "beneplacitum"</note>—strays less from the truth than the opinion of those who establish that God acts in all things under the "reason of the Good." For these [latter] people seem to place something outside of God that does not depend on God, to which God, like a model, attends in his work, or at which he aims as a certain goal. Which indeed is nothing else...

<summary>Spinoza argues that because God's intellect and will are identical to His essence, the universe must be exactly as it is; to suggest God could have chosen otherwise is to suggest God's very essence could be different, which is a logical absurdity.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the argument against the idea that God acts by a "will" separate from his nature. Spinoza transitions from formal geometric proofs about God's power to a famous Appendix where he addresses common religious and philosophical misconceptions (prejudices), specifically the idea that God acts with a purpose or "goal" in mind.</meta>

# OF GOD.

...[it is] than to subject God to fate. Nothing more absurd than this can be stated about God, whom we have shown to be the first and only free cause, as much of the essence <term>essence: the core nature that makes a thing what it is</term> of all things as of their existence. Therefore, there is no reason for me to spend time refuting this absurdity.

### PROPOSITION XXXIV.

*God's power is his very essence.*

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

For from the sole necessity of God's essence, it follows that God is the <term>cause of himself: a thing whose nature cannot be conceived except as existing</term> <note>original: "causa sui"</note> (by Proposition 11), and (by Proposition 16 and its Corollary) the cause of all things. Therefore, the power of God, by which he and all things exist and act, is his very essence. <note>Q. E. D. stands for "Quod Erat Demonstrandum," Latin for "which was to be demonstrated."</note>

### PROPOSITION XXXV.

*Whatever we conceive to be in God's power, necessarily exists.*

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

For whatever is in God's power must (by the preceding Proposition) be contained in his essence in such a way that it follows from it necessarily, and therefore it necessarily exists. Q. E. D.

### PROPOSITION XXXVI.

*Nothing exists from whose nature some effect does not follow.*

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

Whatever exists expresses the nature or essence of God in a certain and determinate way (by the Corollary to Proposition 25); that is (by Proposition 34), whatever exists expresses in a certain and determinate way the power of God, which is the cause of all things; and therefore (by Proposition 16), some effect must follow from it. Q. E. D.

### APPENDIX.

In the foregoing, I have explained the nature of God and his properties: that he exists necessarily; that he is unique; that he exists and acts from the sole necessity of his nature; that he is the free cause of all things, and in what way; that all things are in God and so depend on him that without him they can neither exist nor be conceived; and finally, that all things were predetermined by God—not indeed from freedom of will or absolute good pleasure, but from the absolute nature of God, or his infinite power. 

Furthermore, wherever the occasion was given, I have taken care to remove the prejudices <note>Spinoza uses "prejudices" to mean "preconceived notions" or "unexamined beliefs" that cloud human judgment.</note> that could prevent my demonstrations from being understood. But since many prejudices still remain, which could—and indeed, most certainly would—hinder people from being able to embrace the connection of things in the way I have explained it, I thought it worthwhile to bring these same prejudices here to the examination of reason. 

All the prejudices I intend to point out here depend on this one: namely, that people commonly suppose that all natural things, like themselves, act for an end <note>The "end" here refers to "purpose" or "goal."</note>. Indeed, they hold it as certain that God himself directs all things toward some certain end. For they say that God made all things for the sake of man, and man...

<meta>This page is part of the Appendix to Part I of the Ethics. In the preceding section, Spinoza began his critique of teleology—the belief that God or Nature acts with a specific purpose or "end goal" in mind. He is now outlining his plan to dismantle these common prejudices.</meta>

# ETHICS PART I.

...so that [men] might worship him. This, therefore, is the one thing I shall consider first, by seeking *firstly* the cause why most people persist in this prejudice, and why all are so naturally inclined to embrace it. *Secondly*, I shall show its falsehood, and *finally*, how from this have arisen prejudices *concerning good and evil, merit and sin, praise and blame, order and confusion, beauty and deformity,* and others of this kind. To derive these from the nature of the human mind, however, is not the task for this place. It will suffice here if I take as a foundation that which ought to be universally acknowledged; namely, that all men are born ignorant of the causes of things, and that all men have an appetite for seeking what is useful to them, of which they are conscious. For from these it follows, *firstly*, that men believe themselves to be free, since they are conscious of their own volitions and their appetite, and do not think, not even in a dream <note>original: "ne per somnium." A footnote in the source notes that an anonymous edition from Tübingen suggests "ne per somnium quidem" (not even in a dream), citing classical references to Livy.</note>, of the causes by which they are disposed to wanting and willing, because they are ignorant of them. It follows *secondly*, that men do all things for the sake of an end; namely, for the sake of the utility they desire. Hence it happens that they always seek to know only the <term>final causes: the purpose or "goal" for which something is done, rather than the physical cause that produced it</term> of things that have been done, and once they have heard them, they rest easy; for they have no cause for further doubt. But if they cannot hear these causes from another, nothing remains for them but to turn toward themselves, and to reflect on the ends by which they themselves are usually determined to similar actions, and thus they necessarily judge the character of another by their own character. Furthermore, since they find within and without themselves quite a few means that contribute not a little to attaining their own utility—as, *for example*, eyes for seeing, teeth for chewing, plants and animals for food, the sun for giving light, the sea for nourishing fish, *etc.*—it has come about that they consider all natural things as means for their own use. And because they know that those means were found by them, but not prepared by them, they had reason to believe that there was someone else who prepared those means for their use. For after they considered things as means, they could not believe that those things had made themselves; but from the means which they are accustomed to preparing for themselves, they had to conclude that there exists some Ruler or Rulers of Nature, endowed with human freedom, who have taken care of everything for them, and made all things for their use. And since they had never heard anything about the character of these Rulers, they had to judge it from their own; and thus they established that the Gods direct all things for the use of men, so that they might bind men to themselves and be held by them in the highest honor. Hence it has happened that each individual has devised, according to his own character, different ways of worshipping God, so that God might love them above the rest, and direct the whole of Nature for the use of their blind desire and insatiable greed. And 
<margin>deep |</margin> <note>The Latin text "atlas" (a typo) is corrected in the margin to "altas" (deep).</note> 
thus this prejudice has turned into superstition, and has driven <insert>deep</insert> roots into their minds; which was the reason why everyone strove with the greatest effort to understand and explain the final causes of all things. But while they sought to show that Nature does nothing in vain (that is, nothing that is not for the use of men), they seem to have shown nothing else than that Nature and the Gods are as mad as men. Consider, I pray, where...

<meta>This page continues the Appendix to Part I of Spinoza's Ethics. In the previous page, Spinoza explored how humans naturally assume everything in nature—and God—acts for a purpose (a "final cause"), much as humans do. He now addresses how people explain away the "imperfections" of nature, such as disasters, within this worldview.</meta>

# OF GOD.

...the matter finally turned out! Among so many advantages of Nature, they had to find not a few disadvantages—namely storms, earthquakes, diseases, and so on. They established that these happen because the Gods were angry due to injuries done to them by men, or due to sins committed in their worship. And although experience daily protested and showed with infinite examples that advantages and disadvantages happen to the pious and impious alike without distinction, they did not for that reason cease from their deep-seated prejudice. For it was easier for them to place this among other unknown things whose use they did not know—and thus retain their present and innate state of ignorance—than to destroy that whole structure and devise a new one. Hence they established as certain that the judgments of the Gods far surpass human understanding. This surely would have been the only reason why truth would have remained hidden from the human race forever, if <term>Mathematics (Mathesis): the study of numbers and shapes</term> <note>original: "Mathesis"</note>, which is not concerned with ends but only with the essences and properties of figures, had not shown men another standard of truth; and besides Mathematics, other causes can also be assigned (which it is unnecessary to enumerate here) by which it was possible for men to notice these common prejudices and be led to the true knowledge of things.

With these points, I have sufficiently explained that which I promised in the first place. But now, so that I may show that Nature has no goal set before it, and that all <term>final causes: the belief that things in nature exist for a specific purpose or "end"</term> are nothing but human fictions, there is no need for many words. For I believe this is already clear enough, both from the foundations and causes from which I showed this prejudice drew its origin, and from *Proposition 16 and the Corollaries* <note>The editor Bruder notes that the First Edition reads "Corollary" in the singular.</note> of *Proposition 32*, and furthermore from all those points by which I showed that all things of nature proceed by a certain eternal necessity and the highest perfection. I will yet add this: namely, that this doctrine of the "end" [purpose] completely overturns Nature. For it considers as an effect that which is truly a cause, and vice-versa. Then, it makes that which is prior in nature into something posterior. And finally, it makes that which is supreme and most perfect into the most imperfect. For (omitting the first two points, because they are self-evident), as is clear from *Propositions 21, 22, and 23*, that effect is most perfect which is produced immediately by God, and the more a thing requires intermediate causes to be produced, the more imperfect it is. But if the things produced immediately by God were made for the sake of God attaining his goal, then necessarily the final things, for whose sake the prior ones were made, would be the most excellent of all. Furthermore, this doctrine takes away God's perfection: For if God acts for the sake of an end, he necessarily desires something that he lacks. And although theologians and metaphysicians distinguish between an "end of need" <note>original: "finem indigentiæ"</note> and an "end of assimilation" <note>original: "finem assimilationis"; the idea that God acts to make things like Himself rather than out of a personal lack</note>, they nevertheless admit that God did all things for his own sake, and not for the sake of the things to be created; because before creation they can assign nothing besides God for the sake of which God would act. Thus they are necessarily forced to admit that God lacked those things for which he willed to prepare the means, and that he desired them, as is clear in itself. Nor should I here pass over the fact that the followers of this doctrine, who show off their ingenuity in assigning purposes to things...

<summary>Spinoza argues that humans invent the idea of divine "judgment" to explain natural disasters, and that Mathematics provides a way out of this ignorance. He further claims that believing God acts for a purpose (teleology) implies that God is imperfect and "lacks" something.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the Appendix to Part I of Spinoza's Ethics. In the previous section, Spinoza argued that humans erroneously believe Nature acts with a purpose (teleology). He now moves to explain how people use the "will of God" to explain things they don't understand and how human-centric labels like "Good" and "Order" are subjective rather than objective truths.</meta>

# ETHICS PART I.

...wanted to prove this doctrine of theirs, they introduced a new way of arguing—namely, by reducing the matter not to the impossible, but to ignorance; which shows that there was no other means of arguing for this doctrine. For if, for example <note>original: "ex. gr." (exempli gratia)</note>, a stone fell from some roof onto someone's head and killed them, they will demonstrate in this way that the stone fell in order to kill the man. For if it did not fall for that end, God willing it, how could so many circumstances (for many often coincide at once) have coincided by chance? You will perhaps answer that it happened because the wind blew and the man was walking that way. But they will insist: why did the wind blow at that time? why was the man walking that way at that same time? If you answer again that the wind arose then because the sea had begun to be tossed the previous day, while the weather was still calm; and that the man had been invited by a friend; they will insist again—for there is no end to asking—but why was the sea tossed? why was the man invited for that time? And so they will not cease asking for the causes of causes until you flee to the will of God, that is, the <term>asylum of ignorance</term> <note>original: "asylum ignorantiæ"; a famous phrase where Spinoza mocks the habit of stopping scientific inquiry by simply attributing events to God's mysterious will</note>.

So also, when they see the structure of the human body, they are struck with amazement, and because they are ignorant of the causes of such great art, they conclude that it is constructed not by mechanical art, but by divine or supernatural art, and formed in such a way that one part does not harm another. And hence it happens that whoever seeks the true causes of miracles, and strives to understand natural things like a scholar, rather than to marvel at them like a fool, is everywhere regarded and proclaimed as a heretic and impious person by those whom the common people adore as the interpreters of Nature and the Gods. For they know that, once ignorance is removed, that amazement—which is the only means they have for arguing and defending their authority—is taken away. But I leave these things and proceed to what I decided to treat in the *third* place here.

After men persuaded themselves that everything that happens happens for their sake, they had to judge that the most important thing in each object was what was most useful to them, and to value all those things most highly by which they were most favorably affected. Hence they had to form these notions by which they might explain the natures of things, namely *Good, Evil, Order, Confu-* <column-break/> *sion, Hot, Cold, Beauty*, and *Deformity*; and because they consider themselves free, these notions arose: *Praise* and *Vituperium* <note>blame or censure</note>, *Sin* and *Merit*; but these I will explain below, after I have treated human nature, but the former I will explain briefly here. Namely, they called everything that leads to health and the worship of God *Good*, but what is contrary to these, *Evil*. And because those who do not understand the nature of things affirm nothing about things, but only imagine things and take imagination for intellect, they therefore firmly believe that *Order* is in things, being ignorant of things and of their own nature. For when things are so arranged that, when they are represented to us through the senses, we can easily imagine them, and consequently easily remember them, we say they are well ordered; but if the contrary, we say they are badly ordered or confused. And since those things are pleasing to us above all others which we can easily imagine, men therefore prefer order to confusion, as if order were anything in Nature besides a relation to our imagination; and they say that God created all things in order, and in this way they unknowingly...

<summary>Spinoza critiques the "asylum of ignorance," where people attribute unexplained events to God's will, and explains that concepts like "Good," "Evil," and "Order" are subjective human projections rather than inherent properties of Nature.</summary>
<keywords>asylum of ignorance, teleology, superstition, Good and Evil, Order vs Confusion, imagination vs intellect, mechanical causes, divine art</keywords>

--- Page 42 ---
<meta>This page continues Spinoza’s critique of anthropocentrism in the Appendix to Part I. Having argued that humans mistakenly believe Nature acts with purpose, he now explains that human sensory categories—like beauty, order, and even "good and evil"—are not objective qualities of the universe, but merely subjective reactions based on how our bodies are affected by external things.</meta>

# ON GOD.

...knowingly attribute imagination to God; unless perhaps they wish to claim that God, providing for human imagination, disposed all things in such a way that they could be most easily imagined; nor will it perhaps stop them that infinite things are found which far surpass our imagination, and many which, because of its weakness, confound it. But enough of this. Then, the other notions besides the modes of imagining, by which the imagination is variously affected, are nothing; yet they are considered by the ignorant as the principal attributes of things; because, as we have already said, they believe all things were made for their sake; and they call the nature of a thing "good" or "evil," "healthy" or "rotten" and "corrupt," according as they are affected by it. For example, if the motion which the nerves receive from objects represented through the eyes is conducive to health, the objects by which it is caused are called *beautiful*, but those which excite a contrary motion are called *deformed*. Those things which then move the sense through the nostrils, they call *fragrant* or *foul-smelling*; through the tongue, *sweet* or *bitter*, *savory* or *insipid*, etc. Those through touch, *hard* or *soft*, *rough* or *smooth*, etc. And finally, those which move the ears are said to give off *noise*, *sound*, or *harmony*, the last of which has so maddened men that they believed God also is delighted by harmony. Nor are there lacking Philosophers who have persuaded themselves that the celestial motions compose a harmony <note>Spinoza refers here to the ancient "Music of the Spheres," a Pythagorean and Platonic idea that the movement of planets produces a divine musical scale.</note>. All of which sufficiently show that everyone has judged things according to the disposition of their brain, or rather, has accepted the affections of the imagination as things themselves. Therefore it is no wonder (as we may also note in passing) that so many controversies have arisen among men as we experience, from which <term>Skepticism</term> <note>original: "Scepticismus"; the philosophical position that certain knowledge is impossible, which Spinoza views as a result of relying on subjective imagination rather than objective reason.</note> at last arose. For although human bodies agree in many things, they yet differ in very many, and therefore what seems good to one seems evil to another; what seems ordered to one seems confused to another; what is pleasing to one is unpleasant to another; and so with the rest, which I here omit, both because it is not the place to treat of them specifically, and because everyone has experienced this enough. For everyone has on their lips:

> As many heads, so many opinions <note>original: "Quot capita, tot sensus"</note>; everyone is satisfied with his own sense; the differences of brains are no less than those of palates:

which sayings show clearly enough that men judge things according to the disposition of their brain, and rather imagine things than understand them. For if they had understood things, those things <note>1: The scholar Schmidt reads "all those would [convince] them." Saisset and Auerbach read "those would [convince] them all."</note>, as <term>Mathematics</term> <note>original: "Mathesi"; for Spinoza, the mathematical method is the gold standard of objective truth because it does not care about human purposes or feelings.</note> testifies, would, if not attract them, at least convince them.

We see, therefore, that all the reasons by which the common people are accustomed to explain Nature are only modes of imagining, and do not indicate the nature of any thing, but only the constitution of the imagination; and because they have names as if they were entities existing outside the imagination, I call them entities not of reason, but of imagination; and so all the arguments directed against us based on similar notions can be easily repelled. For many are accustomed to argue thus: If all things followed from the necessity of God’s most perfect nature, why then are there so many imperfections in Nature <note>2: The corrections in the First Edition add the word "arisen" (ortæ) here.</note>? Namely, the corruption of things even to a stench, the deformity of things that moves one to nausea, confusion, evil,

<summary>Spinoza argues that sensory labels like "beauty," "harmony," and "good" are subjective human imaginations rather than objective properties of Nature. He concludes that universal agreement is only possible through rational, mathematical understanding rather than through the variable "dispositions of the brain."</summary>
<keywords>subjectivity, imagination vs. reason, skepticism, harmony of the spheres, mathematical certainty, human prejudice, ethics</keywords>

--- Page 43 ---
<meta>This page concludes the Appendix to Part I, "On God." Spinoza has been dismantling the "prejudices" that lead humans to believe Nature acts with a purpose or follows human-centered categories of beauty, order, and morality. He now addresses the final objections regarding the existence of "imperfection" and "sin."</meta>

# ETHICS PART I.

...sin <note>original: "peccatum"</note>, and so forth. But, as I have just said, these views are easily refuted. For the perfection <note>In Spinoza’s philosophy, "perfection" is not a moral category but a measure of reality. A thing is "perfect" to the extent that it exists and exerts its own natural power, regardless of whether humans find it useful or pleasant.</note> of things is to be judged solely by their own nature and power; things are not more or less perfect simply because they delight or offend human senses, or because they benefit human nature or are contrary to it. 

To those who ask, "Why did God not create all human beings so that they would be governed by the guidance of reason alone?" I reply only this: because he did not lack the material to create all things, from the highest to the lowest grade of perfection; or, to speak more accurately, because the laws of Nature itself were so vast that they were sufficient to produce everything that can be conceived by an infinite intellect, as I demonstrated in Proposition 16 <note>Proposition 16 is a cornerstone of Spinoza's thought, stating that from the necessity of the divine nature, everything that can be conceived by an infinite mind must necessarily exist. This removes any element of "choice" or "favoritism" from God's creation.</note>. 

These are the prejudices I set out to address here. If any others of this sort still remain, they can be corrected by anyone through a moderate amount of reflection.

### *End of the First Part.*

---

<summary>Spinoza concludes his argument on the nature of God by asserting that perfection is an inherent measure of reality rather than human utility, and that the diversity of the world (including "imperfect" things) is a necessary result of Nature's infinite power.</summary>
<keywords>perfection, nature, power, reason, infinite intellect, Spinoza, prejudices, necessity</keywords>

<meta>This page marks the beginning of Part II. Having established the nature of God (or Substance) in Part I, Spinoza now turns his focus toward the human mind, defining the basic components of his psychology and epistemology, such as bodies, ideas, and duration.</meta>

## ETHICS
### PART TWO,
### ON THE NATURE AND ORIGIN OF THE MIND.

I now proceed to explain those things which must necessarily have followed from the essence of God, or the eternal and infinite Being. Not indeed all of them; for we demonstrated in Proposition 16, Part 1, that an infinity of things in infinite ways must follow from it. Rather, I will explain only those things that can lead us, as if by the hand, to the knowledge of the human Mind and its highest happiness <note>original: "beatitudinis"</note>.

->DEFINITIONS.<-

I. By **body** I understand a mode <term>mode: A specific manifestation or "version" of God's attributes. An individual body is a mode of Extension, just as a thought is a mode of Thinking.</term> which expresses the essence of God, insofar as He is considered as an extended thing <note>original: "res extensa"</note>, in a certain and determined way; *see the Corollary to Proposition 25, Part 1.*

II. I say that there belongs to the **essence** of any thing that which, if given, the thing is necessarily posited, and which, if taken away, the thing is necessarily removed; or that without which the thing, and conversely, which without the thing, can neither be nor be conceived.

III. By **idea** I understand a concept <note>original: "conceptum"</note> of the Mind which the Mind forms because it is a thinking thing.

->EXPLANATION.<-

I say *concept* rather than *perception*, because the name perception seems to indicate that the Mind is acted upon by the object; but concept seems to express an action of the Mind.

IV. By an **adequate idea** I understand an idea which, insofar as it is considered in itself without relation to an object, has all the properties or intrinsic denominations of a true idea.

->EXPLANATION.<-

I say intrinsic so that I may exclude that which is extrinsic, namely the agreement of the idea with its object <note>original: "ideato"; the thing represented by the idea</note>.

V. **Duration** is an indefinite continuation of existing.

->EXPLANATION.<-

I say indefinite, because [duration] cannot be determined by the very nature of the existing thing... 

<summary>Spinoza transitions from discussing the nature of God to defining the fundamental building blocks of the human mind, including his specific definitions for body, essence, and ideas.</summary>
<keywords>Part II, human mind, body, concept vs perception, adequate idea, duration, essence</keywords>

# ETHICS PART II.

...by no means can [duration] be determined, nor even by an efficient cause, which namely posits the existence of a thing necessarily, but does not take it away.

VI. By **reality** and **perfection** I understand the same thing.

VII. By **singular things** <term>singular things: Individual objects or events, like a specific tree or a specific thought, rather than general categories.</term> I understand things that are finite and have a determined existence. But if many individuals work together in one action so that they are all simultaneously the cause of one effect, I consider them all as one singular thing.

## A X I O M S.

I. The **essence of man** <term>essence: The core nature of a thing; what makes it what it is.</term> does not involve necessary existence; that is, from the order of **Nature**, it is just as possible that this or that man exists, as it is that he does not exist.

II. **Man** thinks.

III. **Modes of thinking** <term>modes: Specific "versions" or expressions of an attribute.</term>, such as **love**, **desire**, or whatever is labeled by the name **affects of the mind** <note>original: "affectus animi"</note>, do not occur unless there is an idea of the thing loved, desired, etc., in the same **Individual**. But an idea can exist even if no other mode of thinking exists.

IV. We feel a certain **body** to be affected in many ways.

V. We neither feel nor perceive any **singular things** besides bodies and modes of thinking.
*See the Postulates after Proposition 13.*

### P R O P O S I T I O N I.
* **Thought** is an attribute of **God**, or **God** is a thinking thing.*

### D E M O N S T R A T I O N.
Singular thoughts, or this and that thought, are modes which express the nature of God in a certain and determined way (by the *<term>Corollary to Proposition 25, Part 1</term>*). There belongs, therefore, to **God** (by *<term>Definition 5, Part 1</term>*) an attribute whose concept all singular thoughts involve, and through which they are also conceived. Therefore, **Thought** is one of the infinite attributes of **God**, which expresses the eternal and infinite essence of **God** (see *<term>Definition 6, Part 1</term>*), or **God** is a thinking thing. Which was to be demonstrated. <note>original: "Q. E. D."</note>

### S C H O L I U M.
This Proposition is also evident from the fact that we can conceive of an infinite thinking being. For the more things a thinking being can think, the more reality or perfection we conceive it to contain; therefore, a being that can think infinite things in infinite ways is necessarily infinite in its power of thinking. Since, therefore, by attending to thought alone, we conceive an infinite Being, **Thought** is necessarily (by *<term>Definitions 4 and 6, Part 1</term>*) one of the infinite attributes of **God**, as we intended to show.

### P R O P O S I T I O N II.
* **Extension** <term>extension: The attribute of having physical space or "reach"; the essence of matter.</term> is an attribute of **God**, or **God** is an extended thing.*

### D E M O N S T R A T I O N.
The proof of this proceeds in the same way as the demonstration of the preceding Proposition.

<summary>Spinoza concludes his definitions for Part II and presents axioms regarding human existence and perception, then demonstrates that both Thought and Extension are fundamental attributes of God.</summary>
<keywords>reality, perfection, singular things, axioms, human essence, thought, affects, body, extension, attributes of God</keywords>

--- Page 46 ---
<meta>This page continues the text of Part II of the Ethics, moving from the opening axioms into the core propositions regarding the nature of God's intellect and the relationship between ideas and their causes.</meta>

# ON THE MIND.

### P R O P O S I T I O N III.
*In **God** there is necessarily an idea, both of His **essence** and of all things which necessarily follow from His essence.*

## D E M O N S T R A T I O N.
For **God** (by Proposition 1 of this part) can think infinite things in infinite ways, or (what is the same thing, by Prop. 16, Part 1) He can form an idea of His own essence and of all things which necessarily follow from it. But everything that is in the power of **God** necessarily exists (by Prop. 35, Part 1); therefore, such an idea necessarily exists, and (by Prop. 15, Part 1) it exists only in **God**. <note>original: "Q. E. D." — an abbreviation for "Quod Erat Demonstrandum," meaning "Which was to be demonstrated."</note>

## S C H O L I U M.
The common people <note>original: "vulgus"</note> understand by the **power of God** His free will and His right over all things that exist, which are therefore commonly considered as **contingent** <term>contingent: Something that might or might not happen; not strictly necessary.</term>. For they say that **God** has the power of destroying all things and reducing them to nothing. Furthermore, they very often compare the power of **God** with the power of **Kings**. But we have refuted this in the 1st and 2nd Corollaries of Proposition 32, Part 1; and in Proposition 16, Part 1, we have shown that **God** acts by the same necessity by which He understands Himself. That is, just as it follows from the necessity of the divine nature (as everyone unanimously affirms) that **God** understands Himself, by that same necessity it also follows that **God** performs infinite acts in infinite ways. Next, in Proposition 34, Part 1, we showed that the **power of God** is nothing other than the **active essence** <note>original: "actuosam essentiam"</note> of **God**; and so it is as impossible for us to conceive that **God** does not act as it is to conceive that **God** does not exist. Further, if I were inclined to pursue these matters further, I could show here that the power which the common people assign to **God** is not only a human power (which shows that **God** is conceived by the common people as a man, or like a man), but that it even involves **impotence**. But I do not wish to speak so often on the same subject. I only ask the reader, again and again, to weigh over and over what was said on this matter in the First Part, from Proposition 16 to the end. For no one will be able to rightly perceive the things I intend unless they take great care not to confuse the **power of God** with the human power or right of **Kings**.

### P R O P O S I T I O N IV.
*The **idea of God**, from which infinite things follow in infinite ways, can only be unique.*

## D E M O N S T R A T I O N.
An infinite intellect comprehends nothing except the **attributes** of **God** and His **affections** <term>affections: Specific states or modifications of a substance; the way a thing is "affected."</term> (by Prop. 30, Part 1). But **God** is unique (by Corol. 1, Prop. 14, Part 1). Therefore, the **idea of God**, from which infinite things follow in infinite ways, can only be unique. <note>original: "Q. E. D."</note>

### P R O P O S I T I O N V.
*The **formal being** <term>formal being: The reality of a thing as it exists in itself—in this case, the reality of an idea as a "thought-thing."</term> of ideas acknowledges **God** as its cause only insofar as He is considered as a **thinking thing**, and not insofar as He is explained by any other **attribute**. That is, the ideas of both the attributes of **God** and of **singular things** do not acknowledge the objects they represent—the things perceived—as their **efficient cause**, but **God** Himself, insofar as He is a **thinking thing**.*

<summary>Spinoza argues that God necessarily possesses a unique idea of His own essence and power, asserting that divine power is identical to divine essence rather than a "free will" similar to that of a human king.</summary>
<keywords>God's power, active essence, necessity, idea of God, thinking thing, formal being, efficient cause, critique of anthropomorphism</keywords>

--- Page 47 ---
<meta>This page continues Part II of Spinoza's Ethics, providing the demonstration for the concluding thought of the previous page and introducing the fundamental principles that link the physical world with the world of ideas.</meta>

# ETHICS PART II.

### DEMONSTRATION.

This is evident from Proposition 3 of this part. For there we concluded that God could form the idea of His essence and of everything that necessarily follows from it, solely from the fact that God is a thinking thing, and not from the fact that He is the object of His own idea. Therefore, the **formal being** <term>formal being: The actual existence of a thing in its own right—in this case, the existence of an idea as a mental state.</term> of ideas acknowledges God as its cause insofar as He is a thinking thing. But it is demonstrated in another way as follows: The formal being of ideas is a mode of thinking (*as is self-evident*); that is (*by the Corollary to Proposition 25, Part 1*), a mode which expresses God’s nature in a certain way insofar as He is a thinking thing, and therefore (*by Proposition 10, Part 1*) it involves the concept of no other attribute of God. Consequently, (*by Axiom 4, Part 1*) it is the effect of no other attribute except Thought. And so, the formal being of ideas has God as its cause only insofar as He is considered as a thinking thing, etc. <note>original: "Q. E. D." — Which was to be demonstrated.</note>

## PROPOSITION VI.

*The modes of any attribute have God as their cause only insofar as He is considered under that attribute of which they are modes, and not insofar as He is considered under any other attribute.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

For each attribute is conceived through itself without any other (*by Proposition 10, Part 1*). Therefore, the modes of each attribute involve the concept of their own attribute, but not that of another; and so (*by Axiom 4, Part 1*), they have God as their cause only insofar as He is considered under that attribute of which they are modes, and not insofar as He is considered under any other. <note>original: "Q. E. D."</note>

### COROLLARY.

From this it follows that the **formal being** of things which are not modes of thinking does not follow from the divine nature because God first knew them; rather, the objects of ideas <note>original: "res ideatæ"</note> follow from and are concluded from their own attributes with the same necessity and in the same way as we have shown ideas to follow from the attribute of Thought.

## PROPOSITION VII.

*The order and connection of ideas is the same as the order and connection of things.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

This is evident from **Axiom 4, Part 1**. For the idea of anything caused depends on the knowledge of the cause of which it is an effect.

### COROLLARY.

From this it follows that God’s power of thinking is equal to His actual power of acting. That is, whatever follows **formally** from God’s infinite nature, all of it follows **objectively** <term>objectively: To Spinoza, this refers to how a thing exists as the subject of a thought or representation in the mind.</term> in God from the idea of God in the same order and with the same connection.

### SCHOLIUM.

Here, before we proceed further, we must recall to mind what we showed above; namely, that whatever can be perceived by an infinite intellect as constituting the essence of substance, all of it belongs to one substance only, and consequently that

<summary>This page establishes that the world of ideas and the world of physical things are parallel systems caused by God, famously concluding that the "order and connection of ideas is the same as the order and connection of things."</summary>
<keywords>formal being, objective being, causality, attribute of thought, parallel order, divine power, modes, substance</keywords>

--- Page 48 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the relationship between God's attributes, specifically focusing on the identity of the mental and physical realms.</meta>

-># ON THE MIND<-
->3<-

<term>Thinking substance</term> <note>original: "substantia cogitans"</note> and <term>extended substance</term> <note>original: "substantia extensa"</note> are one and the same substance, which is understood now under this <term>attribute</term>, now under that. So also a mode of extension and the idea of that mode are one and the same thing, but expressed in two ways. This is what some of the <note>original: "Hebræorum"; Spinoza likely refers here to medieval Jewish philosophers like Maimonides</note> Hebrews seem to have seen, as if through a cloud, when they maintain that God, God's intellect, and the things understood by Him are one and the same. For example, a circle existing in Nature and the idea of an existing circle, which is also in God, are one and the same thing, explained through different attributes. Therefore, whether we conceive Nature under the attribute of Extension, or under the attribute of Thought, or under any other attribute whatever, we shall find one and the same order, or one and the same connection of causes—that is, that the same things follow one another. I had no other reason for saying that God is the cause of the idea of a circle, for example, only insofar as He is a thinking thing, and of the circle itself only insofar as He is an extended thing, than because the <term>formal being</term> <note>original: "esse formale"; referring to the actual existence of a thing in reality</note> of the idea of a circle can only be perceived through another mode of thinking as its proximate cause, and that again through another, and so on to infinity. Thus, as long as things are considered as modes of thinking, we must explain the order of the whole of Nature, or the connection of causes, through the attribute of Thought alone <sup>1)</sup>; and insofar as they are considered as modes of Extension, the order of the whole of Nature must be explained through the attribute of Extension alone; and I understand the same regarding other attributes. Therefore, of things as they are in themselves, God is truly the cause insofar as He consists of infinite attributes; and I cannot explain these things more clearly for the time being.

## PROPOSITION VIII.

*The ideas of singular things, or modes, that do not exist must be comprehended in the infinite idea of God in the same way that the <term>formal essences</term> of singular things, or modes, are contained in the attributes of God.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

This Proposition is evident from the preceding one, but it is understood more clearly from the preceding Scholium.

### COROLLARY.

From this it follows that, as long as singular things do not exist except insofar as they are contained in God’s attributes, their <term>objective being</term> <note>original: "esse objectivum"; the reality of a thing as it exists as an object of thought or an idea</note>, or ideas, do not exist except insofar as the infinite idea of God exists; and when singular things are said to exist, not only insofar as they are contained in God’s attributes, but insofar as they are also said to have duration, their ideas will also involve the existence through which they are said to endure.

### SCHOLIUM.

If anyone desires an example for a fuller explanation of this matter, I can certainly give none that adequately explains the thing I am speaking of here, since it is unique; nevertheless, I will try to illustrate the matter as best I can. Specifically, a circle is of such a nature that of all the straight lines...

<summary>This page outlines Spinoza’s theory of "parallelism," arguing that mind and matter are different ways of viewing the same reality. It explains that the ideas of non-existent things are preserved within the infinite intellect of God as "essences."</summary>

<meta>This page continues the Scholium to Proposition 8, using a geometric analogy to explain how ideas of non-existing things are contained in the infinite idea of God, and then transitions into Proposition 9 regarding the chain of causes for individual ideas.</meta>

# ETHICS PART II.

...of the same [lines] intersecting each other, the rectangles formed by the segments are equal to one another; therefore, an infinite number of equal rectangles are contained within a circle. Yet none of them can be said to exist except insofar as the circle exists, nor can the idea of any of these rectangles be said to exist except insofar as it is contained within the idea of the circle. Now, let us imagine that only two of these infinite [rectangles], namely **E** and **D**, exist. Truly, their ideas also now exist not only insofar as they are contained solely in the idea of the circle, but also insofar as they involve the existence of those rectangles; thus it happens that they are distinguished from the remaining ideas of the other rectangles.

<image-desc>A diagram of a circle with two intersecting chords. One chord is vertical and the other horizontal. The vertical chord has the letter 'D' to its left. The horizontal chord has the letter 'E' below it near the intersection point.</image-desc>

### PROPOSITION IX.

*The idea of an <term>individual thing</term> <note>original: "rei singularis"</note>, existing in <term>actuality</term> <note>original: "actu"; meaning currently existing in time and space rather than just potentially</note>, has God as its cause, not insofar as He is infinite, but insofar as He is considered to be affected by another idea of an individual thing existing in actuality, of which God is also the cause, insofar as He is affected by a third, and so on to infinity.*

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

The idea of an individual thing, existing in actuality, is an individual mode of thinking, and distinct from the rest (*by the Corollary and Scholium of Proposition 8 of this part*); and so (*by Proposition 6 of this part*) it has God as its cause, insofar as He is only a thinking thing. But He is not the cause (*by Proposition 28, Part 1*) insofar as He is an absolutely thinking thing, but insofar as He is considered as affected by another mode of thinking; and God is also the cause of this <note>1) The Dutch version and the First Edition include: "and of this also, insofar as."</note>, insofar as He is affected by another, and so on to infinity. But the order and connection of ideas (*by Proposition 7 of this part*) is the same as the order and connection of causes; therefore, the cause of one individual idea is another idea—or, God, insofar as He is considered as affected by another idea—and of this [idea] also [God is the cause], insofar as He is affected by another, and so on to infinity. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

->COROLLARY.<-

Whatever happens in the individual object of any idea, knowledge of it is given in God, insofar only as He has the idea of that same object.

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

Whatever happens in the object of any idea, the idea of it is given in God (*by Proposition 3 of this part*), not insofar as He is infinite, but insofar as He is considered to be affected by another idea of an individual thing (*by the preceding Proposition*); but (*by Proposition 7 of this part*) the order and connection of ideas is the same as the order and connection of things; therefore, knowledge of what happens in some individual object will be in God, insofar only as He has the idea of that same object. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### PROPOSITION X.

*To the essence of man it does not belong to be the being of substance, or: substance does not constitute the <term>form</term> <note>original: "formam"; in this context, the essential nature or defining characteristic</note> of man.*

---

1) <note>original: "Vers. Belg. — Ed. Pr.: et hujus etiam, quatenus."</note> Dutch Version — First Edition: and of this also, insofar as.

<summary>This page explains that individual ideas are not caused by God's infinite nature directly, but by a finite chain of preceding ideas, using a circle analogy to distinguish between potential and actual existence.</summary>
<keywords>individual thing, actuality, cause, infinite chain, God, thinking thing, essence of man, substance, Spinoza</keywords>

--- Page 50 ---
<meta>This page continues Part II of the Ethics, moving from Proposition 9 (concerning the causal chain of individual ideas) to Proposition 10, which distinguishes the essence of humanity from the nature of substance.</meta>

# ON THE MIND.

### DEMONSTRATION.

**For the being of <term>substance</term> <note>original: "substantiæ"; in Spinoza's system, substance is that which exists in itself and is conceived through itself, i.e., God</note> involves necessary existence (*by Proposition 7, Part 1*). Therefore, if the being of substance pertained to the essence of a human, then once the substance was given, the human would necessarily be given (*by Definition 2 of this part*), and consequently the human would necessarily exist, which (*by Axiom 1 of this part*) is absurd. Therefore, etc. *Which was to be demonstrated.* **

### SCHOLIUM.

This Proposition is also demonstrated from Proposition 5, Part 1—namely, that two substances of the same nature are not given. Since many humans can exist, it follows that what constitutes the form of a human is not the being of substance. Furthermore, this Proposition is evident from the other properties of substance: namely, that substance is by its nature infinite, immutable, indivisible, etc., as anyone can easily see.

### COROLLARY.

From this it follows that the essence of a human is constituted by certain modifications of God’s attributes. For the being of substance (*by the preceding Proposition*) does not belong to the essence of a human. It is, therefore, (*by Proposition 15, Part 1*) something that is in God, and which without God can neither be nor be conceived; that is (*by the Corollary to Proposition 25, Part 1*), it is an affection or <term>mode</term> <note>original: "modus"; a specific "way" or "state" of being, as opposed to the underlying substance</note> that expresses God’s nature in a certain and determined way.

### SCHOLIUM.

Everyone surely must grant that nothing can be or be conceived without God. For it is universally acknowledged that God is the sole cause of all things—both of their essence and of their existence. That is, God is not only the cause of things in their <term>coming to be</term> <note>original: "secundum fieri"; referring to the process of being created or generated</note>, as they say, but also in their <term>being</term> <note>original: "secundum esse"; referring to their ongoing existence</note>. Yet, meanwhile, many people say that whatever a thing can neither be nor be conceived without belongs to the essence of that thing. Consequently, they believe either that the nature of God belongs to the essence of created things, or that created things can be or be conceived without God—or, what is more likely, they are not consistent with themselves. 

The cause of this, I believe, was that they did not follow the proper order of philosophizing. For the divine nature—which they should have contemplated before all else, since it is prior both in knowledge and in nature—they believed to be last in the order of knowledge; and they believed that those things called "objects of the senses" were prior to everything. Hence it happened that while they were contemplating natural things, they thought of nothing less than the divine nature; and when they later turned their minds to contemplating the divine nature, they could think of nothing less than their initial fictions upon which they had built their knowledge of natural things, since those fictions could not help at all in understanding the divine nature. It is no wonder, then, if they contradicted themselves everywhere. 

But I pass over this. My intent here was only to give the reason why I did not say that whatever a thing cannot be or be conceived without belongs to its essence. My reason is that <term>individual things</term> <note>original: "res singulares"</note> cannot be or be conceived without God, and yet God does not belong to their essence. Rather, I have said that what necessarily constitutes the essence of a thing is that which, when given, the thing is established, and when taken away, the thing is removed; or that without which the thing (and conversely, that which without the thing) can neither be nor be conceived.

<summary>Spinoza argues that human beings are not independent substances but are "modes" or expressions of God's attributes. He critiques earlier philosophers for prioritizing sensory objects over the study of the divine nature, which he argues is the necessary foundation for all logical consistency.</summary>

<keywords>essence of man, substance vs mode, divine nature, order of philosophy, cause of being, individual things, Spinoza's definitions</keywords>

--- Page 51 ---
<meta>This page continues Part II of Spinoza’s Ethics, "On the Nature and Origin of the Mind." Having established that the essence of a human consists of "modes" (specific expressions) of God’s attributes, Spinoza now defines exactly what the human mind is: the idea of the human body.</meta>

# PART II OF THE ETHICS.

## PROPOSITION XI.

*The first thing that constitutes the actual being of the human Mind is nothing other than the idea of a particular, actually existing thing.*

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

The essence of a human (*by the Corollary to the preceding Proposition*) is constituted by certain modes of God’s attributes; namely (*by Axiom 2 of this part*), by modes of thinking. Of all these modes (*by Axiom 3 of this part*), an idea is naturally prior; and once an idea is given, the other modes (to which the idea is naturally prior) must exist in the same individual (*by the same Axiom* $^1$)). Therefore, an idea is the first thing that constitutes the being of the human Mind. But it cannot be the idea of a non-existing thing. For then (*by the Corollary to Proposition 8 of this part*) the idea itself could not be said to exist; it will therefore be the idea of a thing actually existing. Yet, it is not the idea of an infinite thing. For an infinite thing (*by Propositions 21 and 22* $^2$) *of Part 1*) must always necessarily exist; but this (*by Axiom 1 of this part*) is absurd <note>Axiom 1 states that the essence of man does not involve necessary existence; we are finite beings who can or cannot exist.</note>. Therefore, the first thing that constitutes the actual being of the human Mind is the idea of a particular thing actually existing. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

->COROLLARIUM.<-

From this it follows that the human Mind is a part of the infinite <term>intellect of God</term> <note>original: "intellectus Dei"; Spinoza argues that our thinking is not separate from God, but a localized expression of the divine capacity to think</note>. Consequently, when we say that the human Mind perceives this or that, we are saying nothing other than that God—not insofar as He is infinite, but insofar as He is explained through the nature of the human Mind, or insofar as He constitutes the essence of the human Mind—has this or that idea. And when we say that God has this or that idea, not only insofar as He constitutes the nature of the human Mind, but insofar as He also has the idea of another thing along with the human Mind, then we say that the human Mind perceives the thing only partially, or <term>inadequately</term> <note>original: "inadæquate"; an inadequate idea is like seeing a fragment of a picture without the context of the whole</note>.

->SCHOLIUM.<-

At this point, readers will undoubtedly find themselves stuck and will imagine many difficulties that may cause them to pause. For this reason, I ask them to walk with me at a slow pace and to withhold judgment on these matters until they have read through everything.

## PROPOSITION XII.

*Whatever happens in the object of the idea constituting the human Mind must be perceived by the human Mind; or, an idea of that thing will necessarily be given in the Mind. That is to say: if the object of the idea constituting the human Mind is a body, nothing can happen in that body that is not perceived by the Mind.*

->DEMONSTRATIO.<-

For whatever happens in the object of any idea, the knowledge of that thing is necessarily given in God (*by the Corollary to Proposition 9 of this part*), insofar as He is considered to be affected by the idea of that same object; that is (*by Proposition 11 of this part*), insofar as He constitutes the mind of some thing. Therefore, whatever happens in the object of the idea constituting the human Mind, the knowledge of it is necessarily given

1) First Edition: *by Axiom 4 of this part.*
2) First Edition: *21 and 23.*

<summary>This page defines the human mind as the idea of a specific, existing thing (the body) and asserts that the mind is a localized part of God's infinite intellect. It also establishes that anything occurring in the body must be reflected as an idea within the mind.</summary>
