# ON THE MIND.

knowledge in God, insofar as it constitutes the nature of the human Mind, that is (per the *Corollary of Proposition 11 of this part*), the knowledge of that thing will necessarily be in the Mind, or the Mind perceives it. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

->SCHOLIUM<-

This Proposition is also evident and more clearly understood from the *Scholium to Proposition 7 of this part, which see.* <note>Proposition 7 states that the order and connection of ideas is the same as the order and connection of things.</note>

## PROPOSITION XIII.

***The object of the idea constituting the human Mind is the Body, or a certain mode of Extension actually existing, and nothing else.*** <note>By "Extension," Spinoza refers to the physical world or matter. He is arguing that the mind and body are two sides of the same coin: the mind is the mental representation of the body's physical existence.</note>

->DEMONSTRATION<-

For if the Body were not the object of the human Mind, the ideas of the <term>affections</term> <note>original: "affectionum"; the ways in which the body is acted upon or changed by external things</note> of the Body would not be in God (*per the Corollary of Proposition 9 of this part*) insofar as He constitutes our Mind, but insofar as He constitutes the mind of another thing; that is (*per the Corollary of Proposition 11 of this part*), the ideas of the body’s affections would not be in our Mind. But (*per Axiom 4 of this part*) we do have ideas of the affections of the Body. Therefore, the object of the idea constituting the human Mind is the Body, and that (*per Proposition 11 of this part*) is actually existing. Furthermore, if there were also something else besides the Body that was the object of the Mind, then since nothing exists (*per Proposition 36, Part 1*) from which some effect does not follow, there would (*per Proposition 11 of this part*) necessarily have to be an idea of its effect in our Mind; but (*per Axiom 5 of this part*) no such idea is given. Therefore, the object of our Mind is the existing Body, and nothing else. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

->COROLLARY<-

From this it follows that a human being consists of a Mind and a Body, and that the human Body, just as we feel it, exists.

->SCHOLIUM<-

From these points, we not only understand that the human mind is united to the Body, but also what is to be understood by the union of Mind and Body. However, no one will be able to understand this union adequately, or distinctly, unless they first know the nature of our Body adequately. For the things we have shown so far are quite general and pertain no more to humans than to other Individuals, all of which, although in different degrees, are nevertheless <term>animate</term> <note>original: "animata"; Spinoza suggests a form of panpsychism here—that everything in nature has a mental aspect or "idea" corresponding to its physical form, not just humans.</note>. For of whatever thing, there is necessarily an idea in God, of which God is the cause, in the same way as there are ideas of the human Body; and thus, whatever we have said of the idea of the human Body must necessarily be said of the idea of any thing. Yet, we also cannot deny that ideas differ among themselves, just as their objects do, and that one is more excellent than another and contains more reality, just as the object of one is more excellent than the object of another and contains more reality. Therefore, to determine what distinguishes the human Mind from others, and in what it excels them, it is necessary for us, as we have said, to know the nature of its object—that is, the human Body. I can neither explain that nature here, nor is it necessary for the things I wish to demonstrate. I will say this in general: the more a Body is more capable than others of doing or suffering many things at once, the more its Mind is more capable than others of perceiving many things at once; and the more the actions of one body...

<summary>Spinoza demonstrates that the human mind is specifically the "idea" of the human body and famously suggests that all things in nature possess a degree of "animation" or mental presence.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the demonstration of Proposition 13 in Part II, explaining why the mind's clarity depends on its relationship with the body. Spinoza then transitions into a series of Axioms and Lemmas to define a "physical interlude," laying the groundwork for how bodies—and thus the human body—function.</meta>

# ETHICS PART II.

depend on it alone, and the less other bodies concur with it in acting, the more its mind is suited for understanding distinctly. From these things we can recognize the superiority of one mind over others; next, we can also see the reason why we have only a very confused knowledge of our Body, and several other things which I will deduce from these in what follows. For which reason I have thought it worthwhile to explain and demonstrate these very things more accurately, for which it is necessary to first set forth a few things regarding the nature of bodies.

### A X I O M I.
All bodies are either moving or at rest.

### A X I O M II.
Each and every body moves now more slowly, now more quickly.

### L E M M A I.
*Bodies are distinguished from one another by reason of motion and rest, speed and slowness, and not by reason of <term>substance</term>.* <note>Spinoza holds that there is only one Substance (God or Nature). Therefore, physical objects are not different "stuff," but different patterns of motion and rest within that one substance.</note>

->D E M O N S T R A T I O N.<-

I suppose the first part of this is self-evident. But that bodies are not distinguished by reason of substance is clear both from *Proposition 5 as well as 8 of Part I*. But even more clearly from those things said in the *Scholium to Proposition 15 of Part I*.

### L E M M A II.
*All bodies agree in certain things.*

->D E M O N S T R A T I O N.<-

For in these things all bodies agree: that they involve the concept of one and the same <term>attribute</term> <note>original: "attributi"; here meaning "Extension," the fundamental quality of being physical</note> (*per Definition 1 of this part*). Next, that they can move now more slowly, now more quickly, and simply <note>original: "absolute"</note> move or be at rest.

### L E M M A III.
*A body in motion or at rest must have been determined to motion or rest by another body, which was also determined to motion or rest by another, and that again by another, and so on to infinity.*

->D E M O N S T R A T I O N.<-

Bodies (*per Definition 1 of this part*) are <term>singular things</term> <note>original: "res singulares"; specific, individual objects</note> which (*per Lemma 1*) are distinguished from one another by reason of motion and rest; and so (*per Proposition 28 of Part I*) each one must necessarily have been determined to motion or rest by another singular thing, namely (*per Proposition 6 of this part*) by another body, which (*per Axiom 1*) is also either moving or at rest. But this also (*by the same reasoning*) could not have moved or rested unless it had been determined to motion or rest by another, and this again (*by the same reasoning*) by another, and so on to infinity. <note>original: Q. E. D. (Quod Erat Demonstrandum)</note> *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### C O R O L L A R Y.
From this it follows that a body in motion continues to move until it is determined to rest by another body; and a body at rest also continues to rest until it is determined to move by another. This is also self-evident...

<summary>Spinoza begins a brief scientific explanation of physics, asserting that all bodies are modes of one substance and are governed by the laws of motion and rest. He establishes that every physical change is part of an infinite chain of cause and effect.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the physical "interlude" started on page 38. Spinoza is establishing the basic laws of physics (specifically mechanics) which he believes govern all physical matter. This is necessary because he intends to show that the human mind is the "idea" of a specific physical body, so he must first define what a body is and how it functions.</meta>

<page-num>39</page-num>
# ON THE MIND.

It is clear. For when I suppose, for example, that body A is at rest, and I do not attend to other bodies in motion, I can say nothing about body A except that it is at rest. If it should happen later that body A moves, that certainly could not have happened because it was at rest; for from that rest nothing else could follow than that body A should continue to be at rest. If, on the contrary, A is supposed to be in motion, as long as we attend only to A, we can affirm nothing about it except that it moves. If it should later happen that A rests, that also could not have happened from the motion it had; for from motion nothing else could follow than that A should move. It happens, therefore, by a thing that was not in A, namely from an external cause, by which it was determined to come to rest.

# AXIOM I.

All the ways in which a body is affected by another body follow from the nature of the body affected and at the same time from the nature of the affecting body; so that one and the same body is moved in different ways according to the diversity of the nature of the moving bodies, and conversely, different bodies are moved in different ways by one and the same body.

# AXIOM II.

When a moving body strikes another body at rest which it cannot move, it is reflected so that it continues to move, and the angle of the line of the motion of reflection with the plane of the body at rest which it struck will be equal to the angle which the line of the motion of incidence makes with the same plane.

<image-desc>A line diagram representing the law of reflection on a plane. A thin parallelogram depicts a horizontal surface. A continuous line forms a V-shape, where the vertex touches the center of the parallelogram, illustrating a path of incidence and a path of reflection at equal angles to the surface.</image-desc>

And so much for the <term>simplest bodies</term> <note>Spinoza defines "simplest bodies" as the most basic units of matter, which differ from one another only by how fast they move or whether they are still.</note>, which are distinguished from one another only by motion and rest, speed and slowness: now let us ascend to composite bodies.

# DEFINITION.

> *When several bodies of the same or different size are constrained by others so that they press against one another, or if they move at the same or different speeds such that they communicate their motions to each other in a certain fixed ratio, we shall say that those bodies are united with each other and that all together they compose one body, or an <term>Individual</term> <note>In Spinoza’s system, an "Individual" is any complex system—from a molecule to a human to the entire universe—that maintains a specific proportion of motion and rest among its parts.</note>, which is distinguished from others by this union of bodies.*

# AXIOM III.

In proportion as the parts of an Individual, or composite body, press against one another over larger or smaller surfaces, they can be forced to change their position with more or less difficulty; and consequently, it can be brought about more or less easily that the Individual itself takes on another figure. And hence I shall call those bodies <term>hard</term> whose parts press against each other over large surfaces; <term>soft</term> whose parts press against each other over small surfaces; and finally <term>fluid</term> whose parts move among themselves.

# LEMMA IV.

*If of a body, or Individual, which is composed of several bodies,*

<meta>This page continues Spinoza’s "physical digression" from page 39. Having defined what makes an object a "body," he now moves into how complex objects (which he calls "Individuals") maintain their identity even when their internal parts change, move, or are replaced. This is a crucial step in his argument to explain how the human body—and by extension, the human mind—remains the same thing over time.</meta>

<page-num>40</page-num>
<header>ETHICS PART II.</header>

*if certain bodies are separated from an <term>Individual</term><note>In Spinoza’s physics, an "Individual" is any composite thing that functions as a single unit, from a tiny molecule to a human being to the entire universe.</note>, and at the same time an equal number of other bodies of the same nature take their place, the Individual will retain its nature as before, without any change of its form.*

->DEMONSTRATION<-

For bodies (by Lemma 1) are not distinguished in respect to their <term>substance</term><note>Substance refers to the fundamental "stuff" of the universe. Spinoza believes all bodies are made of the same underlying substance (Extension), so they only differ in how they move or are shaped.</note>; however, that which constitutes the form of an Individual consists in the union of bodies (by the preceding Definition); and this union (by hypothesis) is retained even if a continuous change of the bodies occurs. Therefore, the Individual will retain its nature as before, both in respect to its substance and its mode. <note>original: "Q. E. D." (Quod Erat Demonstrandum), meaning "Which was to be demonstrated."</note>

## LEMMA V.

*If the parts composing an Individual become larger or smaller, but in such proportion that they all keep the same ratio of motion and rest toward one another as before, the Individual will likewise retain its nature as before, without any change of form.*

->DEMONSTRATION<-

The demonstration of this is the same as that of the preceding Lemma.

## LEMMA VI.

*If certain bodies composing an Individual are forced to change the direction of the motion they have from one way to another, but in such a way that they can continue their motions and communicate them to one another in the same ratio as before, the Individual will likewise retain its nature, without any change of form.*

->DEMONSTRATION<-

This is self-evident. For the Individual is supposed to retain everything which we said in its definition constitutes its form.

## LEMMA VII.

*Furthermore, the Individual so composed retains its nature, whether it moves as a whole or remains at rest, and whether it moves in this or that direction, provided that each part retains its motion and communicates it to the others as before.*

->DEMONSTRATION<-

This is evident from the definition of an Individual, which see before Lemma 4.

## SCHOLIUM.

By these propositions, we see how a composite Individual can be affected in many ways, while its nature is nevertheless preserved. Thus far we have conceived an Individual which is composed of nothing but bodies distinguished from one another only by motion and rest, speed and slowness—that is, an Individual composed of the simplest bodies. But if we now conceive of another Individual, composed of several Individuals of different natures, we shall find that it can be affected in many other ways, while its nature is nevertheless preserved. For since each of its parts is composed of several bodies, each part (by the preceding Lemma) can move now more slowly, now more quickly, and

This establishes a physical basis for why a living thing (like a human body) remains the same individual throughout its life.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes Spinoza’s brief detour into physics—explaining how the universe can be seen as one giant, interconnected machine—and begins to apply these rules to the human body and mind. It establishes the "Postulates," or basic assumptions, about how our bodies are built and how they interact with the world.</meta>

<header>ON THE MIND.</header>

consequently to communicate their motions more quickly or more slowly to the rest. If we furthermore conceive of a third kind of <term>Individuals</term><note>An "Individual" here means any complex system that acts as a single unit.</note>, composed of these second ones, we shall find that it can be affected in many other ways without any change of its form. And if we proceed thus to infinity, we will easily conceive that the whole of Nature is one single Individual, whose parts—that is, all bodies—vary in infinite ways without any change to the whole Individual. If it had been my purpose to treat the body specifically, I should have had to explain and demonstrate these things more at length. But I have already stated that I intend something else, and I bring these matters up for no other reason than that I can easily deduce from them those things which I have decided to demonstrate.

->P O S T U L A T E S<-

I. The human body is composed of very many <insert>of diverse nature</insert> individuals, each of which is highly composite.
II. Of the individuals of which the human body is composed, some are fluid, some are soft, and finally, some are hard.
III. The individuals composing the human body, and consequently the human body itself, are affected in many ways by external bodies.
IV. The human body requires, for its preservation, many other bodies, by which it is, as it were, continually regenerated.
V. When a fluid part of the human body is determined by an external body so that it frequently strikes against another soft part, it changes the surface of that part and impresses upon it certain traces of the external body pushing it.
VI. The human body can move external bodies in many ways, and arrange them in many ways.

->P R O P O S I T I O N XIV<-

*The human mind is capable of perceiving many things, and it is more capable in proportion as its Body can be disposed in more ways.*

->D E M O N S T R A T I O N<-

For the human body (by Postulates 3 and 6) is affected in many ways by external bodies and is disposed to affect external bodies in many ways. But the human mind must perceive everything that happens in the human body (by Proposition 12 of this part); therefore, the human mind is capable of perceiving very many things, and is more capable, etc. <note>original: "Q. E. D." (Quod Erat Demonstrandum), meaning "Which was to be demonstrated."</note>

->P R O P O S I T I O N XV<-

*The <term>formal being</term><note>The "formal being" of the mind refers to its actual existence as a thing in the realm of thought, as opposed to what it represents.</note> that constitutes the human mind is not simple, but composed of very many ideas.*

->D E M O N S T R A T I O N<-

The idea that constitutes the formal being of the human mind is the idea of the Body (by Proposition 13 of this part), which (by Postulate 1) is composed of many highly composite individuals. But there is necessarily an idea in God of every individual composing the body (by the Corollary to Proposition 8 of this part); therefore (by Proposition 7 of this part), the idea of the human body is composed of these many ideas of its component parts. <note>original: "Q. E. D."</note>

->P R O P O S I T I O N XVI<-

*The idea of every mode in which the human body is affected by external bodies*

<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion of the "Postulates" of the human body. Spinoza is now transitioning from the physical mechanics of the body to how those mechanics create "ideas" or perceptions in the mind. He is specifically explaining why our perception of the world is always filtered through our own physical state.</meta>

<header>ETHICS PART II.</header>

*...is affected, must involve the nature of the human Body, and at the same time the nature of the external body.* <note>This is the conclusion of the statement for Proposition 16, which began on the previous page.</note>

### DEMONSTRATION.
For all the ways in which any body is affected follow from the nature of the affected body, and at the same time from the nature of the affecting body (by *Axiom 1 after the Corollary of Lemma 3*); therefore, the idea of each of these (by *Axiom 4, Part 1*) will necessarily involve the nature of both bodies; and so the idea of every way in which the human Body is affected by an external body involves the nature of the human Body and the nature of the external body. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### COROLLARY I.
From this it follows, first, that the human Mind perceives the nature of very many bodies along with the nature of its own body.

### COROLLARY II.
It follows, second, that the ideas we have of external bodies indicate the constitution of our own body more than the nature of the external bodies; which I have explained with many examples in the *Appendix to Part One*. <note>Spinoza is arguing that "redness" or "warmth" tells us more about how our eyes and skin work than about the actual physics of the object we are looking at or touching.</note>

### PROPOSITION XVII.
*If the human Body is affected in a way that involves the nature of some external Body, the human Mind will regard that same external body as actually existing, or as present to itself, until the Body is affected by an effect that excludes the existence or presence of the same body.*

### DEMONSTRATION.
This is clear. For as long as the human Body is so affected, the human Mind (by *Prop. 12 of this part*) will contemplate this <term>affection</term><note>original: "affectionem." In Spinoza's philosophy, an "affection" is a specific state or change produced in the body by an external force.</note> of the body; that is (by the *preceding Prop.*), it will have an idea of a state actually existing, which involves the nature of the external body; that is, an idea that does not exclude the existence or presence of the nature of the external body, but posits it. And so the Mind (by *Corollary 1 of the preceding Prop.*) will regard the external body as actually existing, or as present, until it is affected etc. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### COROLLARY.
The Mind will be able to regard external bodies by which the human Body has once been affected as if they were present, even though they no longer exist or are not present.

### DEMONSTRATION.
When external bodies determine the <term>fluid parts</term><note>Spinoza likely refers here to "animal spirits" or blood—substances early scientists believed moved through the nerves to transmit sensations.</note> of the human Body so that they frequently strike against the softer parts, they change the surfaces of those parts (by *Postulate 5*); from this it happens (see *Axiom 2 after the Corollary of Lemma 3*) that they are reflected from there in a different way than they used to be, and also that later, when they encounter those same new surfaces by their own spontaneous motion, they are reflected in the same way as when they were pushed toward those surfaces by external bodies; and consequently, while they continue to move as if reflected in that way, they affect the human Body in the same manner, of which the Mind (by *Prop. 12 of this part*) will again think; that is (by *Prop. 17 of this part*), the Mind will again regard the external body as present; and this as often as the fluid parts of the human Body encounter those same surfaces by their own spontaneous motion. Therefore, although...

<summary>Spinoza explains that human perception is a mix of our own physical makeup and the influence of external objects. He also provides a mechanical explanation for memory and imagination: how the mind can "see" things that are no longer there because of physical changes in the body's internal surfaces.</summary>

<header>ON THE MIND.</header>

...even if the external bodies, by which the human Body was once affected, do not exist, the Mind will nevertheless regard them as present just as often as this action of the body is repeated. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

# SCHOLIUM <note>A "scholium" is a term used in early modern philosophy and mathematics for an explanatory note or commentary on a preceding proof.</note>

We see, therefore, how it can happen that we regard things that do not exist as if they were present, as often occurs. It may be that this happens for other reasons; but it is enough for me here to have shown one cause through which I can explain <gloss>1) The first edition reads: "might explain"</gloss> the matter just as if I had shown it through its true cause. Yet I do not believe I wander far from the truth, since all those postulates I have assumed contain hardly anything that is not established by experience, which we are not permitted to doubt after we have shown that the human Body, as we feel it, exists (*see the Corollary after Proposition 13 of this part*).

Furthermore (*from the preceding Corollary and Corollary 2 of Proposition 16 of this part*), we clearly understand the difference between the idea of, for example, Peter, which constitutes the essence of Peter’s own Mind, and the idea of Peter which is in another man, say, in Paul. For the former directly explains the essence of Peter’s own Body, and does not involve existence except as long as Peter exists; the latter, however, indicates the constitution of Paul’s Body more than the nature of Peter, and therefore, while that constitution of Paul’s Body lasts, Paul’s Mind will regard Peter as present to itself, even if Peter does not exist. 

Further, to keep to common language, we will call the affections <term>affections: the way the body is modified or "touched" by external objects</term> of the human Body—whose ideas represent external bodies as present to us—**images** of things, even if they do not reproduce the actual shapes of the things. And when the Mind regards bodies in this way, we shall say it **imagines**. 

And here, to begin to indicate what "error" is, I would like you to note that the imaginations of the Mind, considered in themselves, contain no error; or rather, the Mind does not err because it imagines, but only insofar as it is considered to lack an idea that would exclude the existence of those things it imagines to be present to itself. For if the Mind, while imagining non-existent things as present to it, knew at the same time that those things did not really exist, it would surely attribute this power of imagining to a virtue of its nature, not a defect; especially if this faculty of imagining depended on its nature alone—that is (*by Definition 7, Part 1*), if this faculty of the Mind to imagine were free. <note>Spinoza is arguing that "hallucination" or imagination is only an "error" because we lack the secondary information that tells us the object isn't really there. If we had that information, the imagination would simply be a creative power.</note>

# PROPOSITION XVIII.

*If the human Body has been affected by two or more bodies at the same time, when the Mind later imagines one of them, it will immediately remember the others as well.*

# DEMONSTRATION.

The Mind (*by the preceding Corollary*) imagines a certain body for this reason: because the human Body is affected and disposed by the traces of an external body in the same way as it was affected when certain parts of it were pushed by that external body; but (*by hypothesis*) the Body was then so disposed that the Mind imagined two bodies at once,

<meta>This page continues from the demonstration on page 43, concluding the proof that the Mind associates ideas based on how the Body was previously affected by external things. <page-num>44</page-num>
<header>ETHICS PART II.</header>

...therefore it will now imagine both at once, and when the Mind imagines either one, it will immediately remember the other. *Which was to be demonstrated.* <note>original: "Q. E. D." (Quod erat demonstrandum), a standard closing for a logical proof.</note>

### SCHOLIUM.
From this we clearly understand what *Memory* is. For it is nothing other than a certain linking of ideas involving the nature of things which are outside the human Body, which happens in the Mind according to the order and linking of the affections <note>In Spinoza’s philosophy, an "affection" (affectio) is a state of the body or a change produced in it by an external force.</note> of the human Body. I say, first, that it is a linking of those ideas only which involve the nature of things that are outside the human Body, but not of the ideas which explain the nature of those same things. For they are indeed (by Proposition 16 *of this part*) ideas of the affections of the human Body, which involve both its own nature and that of external bodies. 

I say, secondly, that this linking happens according to the order and linking of the affections of the human Body, in order to distinguish it from the linking of ideas which happens according to the order of the intellect, by which the Mind perceives things through their primary causes and which is the same in all humans. And from this we further clearly understand why the Mind, from the thought of one thing, immediately falls into the thought of another thing which has no similarity to the first. 

For example, from the thought of the word *pomum* <note>Latin for "apple."</note>, a Roman will immediately fall into the thought of the fruit, which has no similarity to that articulated sound, nor anything in common with it, except that the Body of that same man was often affected by these two things; that is, the man himself often heard the word *pomum* while he saw the fruit. And so, each person will fall from one thought into another, according to how the habit <note>original: "consuetudo"</note> of each person has ordered the images of things in the body. For a soldier, for example, having seen the tracks of a horse in the sand, will immediately fall from the thought of the horse into the thought of a rider, and from there into the thought of war, etc. But a peasant will fall from the thought of a horse into the thought of a plow, a field, etc. And so each person, according as they have been accustomed to join and link the images of things in this or that way, will fall from one thought into this or that other thought.

### PROPOSITION 19.
*The human Mind does not know the human Body itself, nor does it know it to exist, except through the ideas of the affections by which the Body is affected.*

### DEMONSTRATION.
For the human Mind is the very idea or knowledge of the human Body (by Proposition 13 *of this part*), which (by Proposition 9 *of this part*) is indeed in God, insofar as He is considered as affected by another idea of a singular thing; or, because (by Postulate 4) the human Body needs many bodies by which it is continually, as it were, regenerated; and the order and connection of ideas is the same (by Proposition 7 *of this part*) as the order and connection of causes; this idea will be in God insofar as He is considered as affected by the ideas of many singular things. God, therefore, has the idea of the human Body, or knows the human Body, insofar as He is affected by many other ideas, and not insofar as He constitutes the nature of the human Mind; that is (by the Corollary to Proposition 11 *of this part*), the human Mind does not know the human Body. But the ideas of the affections of the Body are in God, insofar as they [constitute] the nature of the human Mind...

<summary>Spinoza defines memory as the association of ideas based on physical habits and external experiences, rather than pure logic. He then argues that the Mind only knows the Body through the sensations or "affections" it receives, rather than through direct, essential knowledge.</summary>
<keywords>Memory, association of ideas, habit, human mind, human body, affections, perception, psychological association, Spinoza's Ethics</keywords>

--- Page 60 ---
<meta>This page continues the transition from the Mind's perception of the Body to its perception of itself. It follows Proposition 19, which established that the Mind only knows the Body through the Body's "affections" (states or changes).</meta>

<page-num>45</page-num>
<header>ON THE MIND.</header>

...constitutes the mind, or the human Mind perceives those same affections (by Proposition 12 *of this part*); and consequently (by Proposition 16 *of this part*) it perceives the human Body itself, and that (by Proposition 17 *of this part*) as actually existing; therefore, the human Mind perceives the human Body itself only to that extent. <note>original: "Q. E. D." (Quod erat demonstrandum), a traditional closing for a logical proof meaning "which was to be demonstrated."</note>

### PROPOSITION XX.
There is also in God an idea or knowledge of the human Mind, which follows in God in the same way, and is referred to God in the same way, as the idea or knowledge of the human Body.

### DEMONSTRATION.
Thought is an attribute of God (by Proposition 1 *of this part*); and so (by Proposition 3 *of this part*) an idea of it, as well as of all its affections, must necessarily be given in God; and consequently (by Proposition 11 *of this part*) an idea of the human Mind must also be given. Next, this idea or knowledge of the Mind does not follow in God insofar as He is infinite, but insofar as He is affected by another idea of a singular thing (by Proposition 9 *of this part*). But the order and connection of ideas is the same as the order and connection of causes (by Proposition 7 *of this part*); therefore, this idea or knowledge of the Mind follows in God, and is referred to God in the same way, as the idea or knowledge of the Body. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### PROPOSITION XXI.
*This idea of the Mind is united to the Mind in the same way as the Mind itself is united to the Body.*

### DEMONSTRATION.
We have shown that the Mind is united to the Body from the fact that the Body is the <term>object</term> <note>In Spinoza's system, the "object" of the mind is the physical body that corresponds to it.</note> of the Mind (see Propositions 12 and 13 *of this part*): and so, by that same reasoning, the idea of the Mind must be united with its own object—that is, with the Mind itself—in the same way as the Mind itself is united to the Body. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### SCHOLIUM.
This Proposition is understood far more clearly from what was said in the Scholium to Proposition 7 *of this part*; for there we showed that the idea of the Body and the Body—that is (by Proposition 13 *of this part*), the Mind and the Body—are one and the same <term>Individual</term> <note>Spinoza argues that "Mind" and "Body" are not two different things, but the same thing described in two different ways.</note>, which is conceived now under the attribute of Thought, now under the attribute of Extension. Wherefore the idea of the Mind and the Mind itself are one and the same thing, which is conceived under one and the same attribute, namely, Thought. The idea of the Mind, I say, and the Mind itself follow in God by the same necessity and from the same power of thinking. For in reality the "idea of the Mind"—that is, the idea of the idea—is nothing other than the form of the idea, insofar as this is considered as a mode of thinking, without relation to the object. For as soon as someone knows something, by that very fact they know that they know it, and at the same time they know that they know that they know, and so on to infinity. But more on these matters later.

### PROPOSITION XXII.
*The human Mind perceives not only the affections of the Body, but also the ideas of these affections.*

### DEMONSTRATION.
The ideas of the ideas of the affections follow in God in the same way, and to...

<summary>Spinoza explains that because the Mind is a "mode of thought," there must be an idea of the Mind in God (self-consciousness). He argues that the Mind and the Body are actually the same thing viewed through different lenses, and that the "idea of an idea" is simply the mind's awareness of its own thoughts.</summary>
<keywords>self-consciousness, idea of the idea, attribute of thought, attribute of extension, individual, unity of mind and body, Spinoza</keywords>

...referred to God in the same way as the ideas of the affections themselves; this is demonstrated in the same manner as Proposition 20 of this part. But the ideas of the Body’s affections are in the human Mind (by Proposition 12 *of this part*), which is to say (by the Corollary to Proposition 11 *of this part*) they are in God insofar as He constitutes the essence of the human Mind. Therefore, the ideas of these ideas will be in God insofar as He has the knowledge—or the idea—of the human Mind; that is (by Proposition 21 *of this part*), they are in the human Mind itself, which for that reason perceives not only the affections of the Body, but also the ideas of those <term>affections</term><note>In Spinoza’s philosophy, an "affection" is a state, change, or "mode" of the body—how the body is affected by something else.</note>. <note>original: "Q. E. D." (Quod erat demonstrandum), a traditional closing for a logical proof meaning "which was to be demonstrated."</note>

### PROPOSITION XXIII.

->*The Mind does not know itself, except insofar as it perceives the ideas of the affections of the Body.*<-

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

The idea or knowledge of the Mind (by Proposition 20 *of this part*) follows in God in the same way, and is referred to God in the same way, as the idea or knowledge of the Body. But since (by Proposition 19 *of this part*) the human Mind does not know the human Body itself—that is (by the Corollary to Proposition 11 *of this part*), since the knowledge of the human Body is not referred to God insofar as He constitutes the nature of the human Mind—therefore, neither is the knowledge of the Mind referred to God insofar as He constitutes the essence of the human Mind. And so (by the same Corollary to Proposition 11 *of this part*), the human Mind to that extent does not know itself. Furthermore, the ideas of the affections by which the Body is affected involve the nature of the human Body itself (by Proposition 16 *of this part*); that is (by Proposition 13 *of this part*), they agree with the nature of the Mind. Therefore, the knowledge of these ideas will necessarily involve the knowledge of the Mind. But (by the preceding Proposition) the knowledge of these ideas is in the human Mind itself; therefore, the human Mind knows itself only to that extent. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### PROPOSITION XXIV.

->*The human Mind does not involve an adequate knowledge of the parts composing the human Body.*<-

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

The parts composing the human Body do not belong to the essence of the Body itself, except insofar as they communicate their motions to one another in a certain fixed proportion (see the Definition after the Corollary to Lemma 3), and not insofar as they can be considered as <term>individuals</term><note>Spinoza uses "Individual" to refer to any complex thing (like an organ or a cell) that functions as a single unit through the coordinated motion of its parts.</note> without relation to the human Body. For the parts of the human Body are (by Postulate 1) highly composite individuals, whose parts (by Lemma 4) can be separated from the human Body while its nature and form are preserved entirely, and can communicate their motions (see <margin>Early Printed Edition: *Axiom 2.*</margin> Axiom 1 after Lemma 3) to other bodies in a different way. And so (by Proposition 3 *of this part*) the idea or knowledge of any part will be in God, and indeed (by Proposition 9 *of this part*) insofar as God is considered to be affected by another idea of a singular thing, which singular thing is prior in the order of Nature to the part itself (by Proposition 7 *of this part*). This same point also holds true for every part of the individual component itself that makes up the human Body...

<summary>Spinoza argues that self-consciousness only arises through bodily experience and that our knowledge of our own biological components is inherently incomplete (inadequate) because those parts exist within a wider chain of natural causes.</summary>
<keywords>self-knowledge, bodily affections, adequate knowledge, individuality, mechanics of the body, Spinoza, perception</keywords>

...must be said; and so the knowledge of whatever part composing the human Body is in God, insofar as He is affected by many ideas of things, and not insofar as He has the idea of the human Body alone—that is (by Proposition 13 *of this part*), the idea which constitutes the nature of the human Mind. And so (by the Corollary to Proposition 11 *of this part*) the human Mind does not involve an <term>adequate</term><note>In Spinoza's system, an "adequate" idea is one that is complete and clearly understood in its own right, as God understands it, rather than a partial or "mutilated" human perception.</note> knowledge of the parts composing the human Body. <note>original: "Q. E. D." (Quod erat demonstrandum), meaning "which was to be demonstrated."</note>

### PROPOSITION XXV.

*The idea of any <term>affection</term><note>An "affection" (affectio) refers to a state or change in the body, specifically how it is "affected" by an encounter with something else.</note> of the human Body does not involve an adequate knowledge of an external body.*

#### DEMONSTRATION.

We have shown (see Proposition 16 *of this part*) that the idea of an affection of the human Body involves the nature of an external body only insofar as the external body itself determines the human Body in a certain specific way. But insofar as the external body is an individual thing that is not related to the human Body, its idea or knowledge is in God (by Proposition 9 *of this part*) insofar as God is considered to be affected by the idea of another thing which (by Proposition 7 *of this part*) is prior in nature to the external body itself. Therefore, the adequate knowledge of the external body is not in God insofar as He has the idea of the affection of the human Body; or, the idea of the affection of the human Body does not involve an adequate knowledge of the external body. <note>original: "Q. E. D."</note>

### PROPOSITION XXVI.

*The human Mind perceives no external body as actually existing, except through the ideas of the affections of its own Body.*

#### DEMONSTRATION.

If the human Body is in no way affected by an external body, then (by Proposition 7 *of this part*) neither the idea of the human Body—that is (by Proposition 13 *of this part*) the human Mind—is affected in any way by the idea of the existence of that body; nor does it perceive the existence of that external body in any way. But insofar as the human Body is affected by an external body in some way, it then (by Proposition 16 *of this part* with its Corollary) perceives the external body. <note>original: "Q. E. D."</note>

#### COROLLARIUM.

*Insofar as the human Mind <term>imagines</term><note>For Spinoza, "imagination" is not just creativity, but the mental process of forming images based on physical sensations. It is the lowest level of knowledge because it is always partial.</note> an external body, it does not have adequate knowledge of it.*

#### DEMONSTRATION.

When the human Mind contemplates external bodies through the ideas of the affections of its own Body, we say then that it imagines them (see the Scholium to Proposition 17 *of this part*); nor can the Mind in any other way (by the preceding Proposition) imagine external bodies as actually existing. And so (by Proposition 25 *of this part*), insofar as the Mind imagines external bodies, it does not have adequate knowledge of them. <note>original: "Q. E. D."</note>

### PROPOSITION XXVII.

*The idea of any affection of the human Body does not involve an adequate knowledge of the human Body itself.*

<summary>This page outlines Spinoza's argument that human knowledge is inherently limited and "inadequate" because we only perceive external objects and our own body through the sensory changes (affections) they produce, rather than through their true essential nature.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the logical progression of Part II of the Ethics, moving from the Mind's lack of adequate knowledge regarding external bodies (demonstrated on page 47) to the conclusion that our everyday perceptions of our bodies and the world are necessarily confused and incomplete.</meta>

<page-num>48</page-num>
<header>ETHICS PART II.</header>

### DEMONSTRATION.

Any idea of any <term>affection</term><note>In Spinoza’s philosophy, an "affection" is a state or change produced in the body by an interaction with another thing.</note> of the human Body involves the nature of the human Body to the extent that the human Body itself is considered to be affected in a certain specific way (*see Proposition 16 of this part*). But insofar as the human Body is an Individual that can be affected in many other ways, the idea of it, etc. *See the Demonstration of Proposition 25 of this part*.

## PROPOSITION XXVIII.

*The ideas of the affections of the human Body, insofar as they are related only to the human Mind, are not clear and distinct, but are confused.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

The ideas of the affections of the human Body involve the nature of both external bodies and of the human Body itself (*by Proposition 16 of this part*); and they must involve the nature not only of the human Body, but also of its parts; for the affections are modes (*by Postulate 3*) by which the parts of the human Body, and consequently the whole Body, are affected. But (*by Propositions 24 and 25 of this part*) the <term>adequate</term><note>An "adequate" idea is a complete, perfect idea as it exists in the mind of God, showing the true cause of a thing.</note> knowledge of external bodies, as well as of the parts composing the human Body, is not in God insofar as He is considered as the human Mind, but insofar as He is considered as affected by other ideas. Therefore, these ideas of the affections, insofar as they are related to the human Mind alone, are like "consequences without premises," <note>original: "consequentiæ absque præmissis"</note> that is (*as is self-evident*) they are confused ideas. <note>original: "Q. E. D." (Quod erat demonstrandum), meaning "which was to be demonstrated."</note>

### SCHOLIUM.

The idea that constitutes the nature of the human Mind is demonstrated in the same way not to be, considered in itself alone, clear and distinct; as is also the idea of the human Mind, and the ideas of the ideas of the affections of the human Body, insofar as they are related to the Mind alone, as anyone can easily see.

## PROPOSITION XXIX.

*The idea of the idea of any affection of the human Body does not involve an adequate knowledge of the human Mind.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

The idea of an affection of the human Body (*by Proposition 27 of this part*) does not involve an adequate knowledge of the Body itself, or, does not adequately express its nature; that is (*by Proposition 13 of this part*), it does not adequately agree with the nature of the Mind; and so (*by Axiom 6 of Part 1*) the idea of this idea does not adequately express the nature of the human Mind, or does not involve an adequate knowledge of it. <note>original: "Q. E. D."</note>

### COROLLARY.

From this it follows that the human Mind, whenever it perceives things from the common order of Nature, has not an adequate, but only a confused and <term>mutilated</term><note>Spinoza uses the term "mutilated" (mutilata) to describe knowledge that is fragmented, disconnected from its causes, or seen only from a limited perspective.</note> knowledge of itself, of its own Body, and of external bodies. For the Mind does not know itself, except insofar as it perceives the ideas of the affections of the Body (*by Proposition 23 of this part*). But it does not perceive its own Body (*by Proposition 19 of this part*) except through those same ideas of the affections, through which alone (*by Proposition 26 of this part*) it also perceives external bodies...

<summary>This page concludes that human sensory perception and self-awareness are naturally "confused" and "mutilated" because we perceive effects without fully understanding their causes. Spinoza argues that clear and distinct knowledge is only possible when the mind is not merely reacting to the "common order of Nature."</summary>
<keywords>confused ideas, adequate knowledge, affections of the body, human mind, common order of nature, mutilated knowledge, Spinoza</keywords>

<page-num>49</page-num>
<header>OF THE MIND.</header>
<sig>4</sig>

perceives; and so, to the extent that it has these ideas, it has an <term>adequate</term><note>In Spinoza's system, an "adequate" idea is one that is complete and shows the true cause of a thing, as God understands it.</note> knowledge neither of itself (*by Proposition 29 of this part*), nor of its Body (*by Proposition 27 of this part*), nor of external bodies (*by Proposition 25 of this part*), but only (*by Proposition 28 of this part with its Scholium*) a mutilated and confused knowledge. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### SCHOLIUM.

I say expressly that the Mind has an adequate knowledge neither of itself, nor of its Body, nor of external bodies, but only a confused one, whenever it perceives things from the common order of Nature; that is, whenever it is determined externally—namely, by the chance encounter of things—to contemplate this or that. This is in contrast to whenever it is determined internally—namely, by the fact that it contemplates several things at once—to understand their similarities, differences, and oppositions. For whenever the mind is disposed internally in this or another way, it then contemplates things clearly and distinctly, as I shall show later.

## PROPOSITION XXX.

*We can have only a very inadequate knowledge of the duration of our Body.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

The <term>duration</term><note>Spinoza distinguishes between "essence" (what a thing is eternally) and "duration" (how long it happens to exist in time).</note> of our body does not depend on its essence (*by Axiom 1 of this part*), nor even on the absolute nature of God (*by Proposition 21, Part 1*). Instead (*by Proposition 28, Part 1*), it is determined to exist and operate by causes which are themselves determined by others to exist and operate in a specific and determined way, and these again by others, and so on to infinity. Therefore, the duration of our Body depends on the common order of Nature and the constitution of things. However, an adequate knowledge of how things are constituted exists in God, insofar as He has the ideas of all of them, and not insofar as He has only the idea of the human Body (*by the Corollary to Proposition 9 of this part*). For this reason, the knowledge of the duration of our Body is very inadequate in God, insofar as He is considered only to constitute the nature of the human Mind; that is (*by the Corollary to Proposition 11 of this part*), this knowledge is very inadequate in our Mind. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

## PROPOSITION XXXI.

*We can have only a very inadequate knowledge of the duration of singular things that are outside of us.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

For every singular thing, just like the human Body, must be determined by another singular thing to exist and operate in a specific and determined way; and this again by another, and so on to infinity (*by Proposition 28, Part 1*). Since we have demonstrated in the preceding Proposition—based on this common property of singular things—that we have only a very inadequate knowledge of the duration of our Body, we must conclude the same regarding the duration of singular things: namely, that we can have only a very inadequate knowledge of it. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

<summary>Spinoza explains that everyday sensory experience leads to confused knowledge, and argues that because our existence is part of an infinite chain of causes, we cannot naturally know the lifespan of ourselves or external objects.</summary>
<keywords>adequate knowledge, duration, common order of nature, singular things, causality, human mind, human body, Spinoza</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the exploration of human knowledge from the previous page. Having established that our knowledge of the duration of bodies is inadequate, Spinoza now defines the nature of truth and the source of error, arguing that "falsity" is not a positive "thing" but merely a lack of information.</meta>

<page-num>50</page-num>
<header>THE ETHICS, PART II.</header>

### COROLLARY.

From this it follows that all particular things are <term>contingent</term><note>original: "contingentes"; In Spinoza’s view, things only seem "contingent" or "accidental" because we lack knowledge of the chain of causes that makes them necessary.</note> and corruptible. For we can have no adequate knowledge of their <term>duration</term><note>The length of time a thing exists.</note> (*by the preceding Proposition*), and this is what we must understand by the contingency of things and the possibility of their decay (*see Scholium 1 to Proposition 33 in Part 1*). For (*by Proposition 29 of Part 1*) besides this, nothing is given as contingent.

## PROPOSITION XXXII.

*All ideas, insofar as they are related to God, are true.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

For all ideas that are in God agree entirely with their <term>objects</term><note>original: "ideatis"; the actual thing that an idea represents.</note> (*by the Corollary to Proposition 7 of this part*), and thus (*by Axiom 6 of Part 1*) all are true. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

## PROPOSITION XXXIII.

*There is nothing positive in ideas because of which they are called false.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

If you deny this, conceive, if possible, a positive mode of thinking that would constitute the form of error or falsity. This mode of thinking cannot be in God (*by the preceding Proposition*), nor can it be, or be conceived, outside of God (*by Proposition 15 of Part 1*). And thus, there can be nothing positive in ideas because of which they are called false. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

## PROPOSITION XXXIV.

*Every idea that is absolute in us—that is, adequate and perfect—is true.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

When we say that we have an adequate and perfect idea within us, we say nothing else (*by the Corollary to Proposition 11 of this part*) than that in God, insofar as He constitutes the essence of our Mind, there is an adequate and perfect idea; and consequently (*by Proposition 32 of this part*), we say nothing else than that such an idea is true. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

## PROPOSITION XXXV.

*Falsity consists in the <term>privation</term><note>A "privation" is a lack of something that ought to be there; Spinoza argues that a "false" idea is just a partial or "mutilated" idea, not a different kind of thing from a "true" idea.</note> of knowledge which inadequate, or mutilated and confused, ideas involve.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

There is nothing positive in ideas that constitutes the form of falsity (*by Proposition 33 of this part*); but falsity cannot consist in absolute privation (for Minds, not Bodies, are said to err or be deceived), nor even in absolute ignorance; for to be ignorant and to err are different things. Therefore, it consists in the privation of knowledge which the inadequate knowledge of things—or inadequate and confused ideas—involve. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### SCHOLIUM.

In the Scholium to Proposition 17 of this Part, I explained in what way error consists in the privation of knowledge; but for a fuller explanation of this matter—

<summary>Spinoza defines truth as the total perspective of God and argues that "falsehood" is not a real thing, but simply a lack of complete information (inadequacy) in the human mind.</summary>
<keywords>contingency, truth, falsity, God, adequate ideas, privation of knowledge, error, essence</keywords>

--- Page 66 ---
<meta>This page continues Part II of The Ethics, "On the Nature and Origin of the Mind." Having just defined falsity as a "privation of knowledge" (a lack of information), Spinoza here illustrates how human "will" and sensory illusions (like the size of the sun) are misunderstood as freedom or truth simply because we do not see the underlying causes.</meta>

<page-num>51</page-num>
<header>ON THE MIND.</header>

I will give an example. To be sure, people are mistaken in thinking they are free; this opinion consists solely in this: that they are conscious of their actions and ignorant of the causes by which they are determined. This, then, is their idea of <term>freedom</term><note>Spinoza argues that "free will" is an illusion; true freedom is acting according to the necessity of one's own nature rather than being pushed by outside forces.</note>: that they know no cause for their actions. For what they say—that human actions depend on the <term>will</term>—are words of which they have no idea. For what the will is, and how it moves the Body, everyone is ignorant; those who boast otherwise imagine "seats" and "dwellings" for the soul, and usually provoke either laughter or disgust. 

So when we look at the sun, we imagine it to be about two hundred feet away from us; this error does not consist in this imagination alone, but in the fact that while we imagine it so, we are ignorant of its true distance and the cause of this imagination. For even if we later learn that it is more than 600 earth-diameters away from us <note>Spinoza is referencing the astronomical estimates of his time; he uses this to show that intellectual knowledge does not automatically delete sensory impressions.</note>, we will nonetheless imagine it to be near; for we do not imagine the sun to be so close because we are ignorant of its true distance, but because the <term>affection</term><note>original: "affectio"; a physical state or change in the body caused by an external object.</note> of our Body involves the <term>essence</term> of the sun insofar as the Body itself is affected by it.

## PROPOSITION XXXVI.

*Inadequate and confused ideas follow by the same necessity as adequate, or clear and distinct, ideas.*

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

All ideas are in God (*by Prop. 15, Part 1*); and, insofar as they are related to God, they are true (*by Prop. 32 of this part*) and (*by the Corollary to Prop. 7 of this part*) <term>adequate</term><note>An "adequate" idea is a complete one that contains all the logical causes of a thing.</note>; therefore, none are inadequate or confused except insofar as they are related to the individual Mind of someone (*on which point see Prop. 24 and 28 of this part*): and so all, both adequate and inadequate, follow by the same necessity (*by the Corollary to Prop. 6 of this part*). *Which was to be demonstrated.* <note>original: "Q. E. D." (Quod Erat Demonstrandum)</note>

## PROPOSITION XXXVII.

*That which is common to all (concerning this, see Lemma 2 above), and which is equally in the part and in the whole, does not constitute the essence of any particular thing.*

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

If you deny this, conceive, if possible, that it constitutes the essence of some particular thing; for example, the essence of B. Therefore (*by Def. 2 of this part*), it could neither be nor be conceived without B; yet this is contrary to the hypothesis <note>Since the property is "common to all," it must be able to exist and be understood without reference to one specific object B.</note>. Therefore, it does not belong to the essence of B, nor does it constitute the essence of any other particular thing. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

## PROPOSITION XXXVIII.

*Those things which are common to all, and which are equally in the part and in the whole, cannot be conceived except adequately.*

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

Let A be something that is common to all bodies, and which is equally in the part of any body and in the whole. I say that A cannot be conceived except adequately. For its idea (*by the Corollary to Prop. 7 of this part*) will necessarily be adequate in God, both insofar as he has the idea of the human Body...

<summary>Spinoza explains that human "free will" is merely a lack of knowledge regarding causes and argues that common properties of things are always understood perfectly (adequately) because they are universal.</summary>
<keywords>freedom, will, illusion, sun, adequate ideas, common properties, necessity, essence</keywords>

This page transitions into explaining how "adequate" (complete and clear) knowledge is possible, specifically through what Spinoza calls "common notions"—properties shared by all things, like extension and motion.</meta>

<page-num>52</page-num>
<header>PART II OF THE ETHICS.</header>

...body, except insofar as it has ideas of the <term>affections</term><note>The various states or changes the body undergoes when interacting with the world.</note> of the same, which (*per Proposition 16, 25, and 27 of this part*) involve in part the nature of both the human Body and external bodies; that is (*per Propositions 12 and 13 of this part*), this idea will necessarily be <term>adequate</term><note>A technical term meaning a clear and complete idea that shows the true cause of a thing, rather than just a partial "snapshot" from the senses.</note> in God, insofar as He constitutes the human Mind, or insofar as He has the ideas that are in the human Mind; therefore, the Mind (*per the Corollary to Proposition 11 of this part*) necessarily perceives A adequately, and that both insofar as it perceives itself, and insofar as it perceives its own body or any external body, nor can it be conceived in any other way. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### COROLLARY.

From this it follows that there are certain ideas, or notions, common to all men; for (*per Lemma 2*) all bodies agree in certain things, which (*per the preceding Proposition*) must be perceived adequately, or clearly and distinctly, by all.

## PROPOSITION XXXIX.

*That which is common to and peculiar to the human Body and certain external bodies by which the human Body is usually affected, and which is equally in the part of any of these as in the whole, the idea of this will also be adequate in the Mind.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

Let A be that which is common and peculiar to the human Body and certain external bodies, and which is equally in the human Body as in the same external bodies, and which, finally, is equally in the part of any external body as in the whole. An adequate idea of A itself will be given in God (*per the Corollary to Proposition 7 of this part*), both insofar as He has the idea of the human Body and insofar as He has the ideas of the external bodies mentioned. Now, let it be posited that the human Body is affected by an external body through that which it has in common with it, that is, by A: the idea of this affection will involve the property A (*per Proposition 16 of this part*), and therefore (*per the same Corollary to Proposition 7 of this part*) the idea of this affection, insofar as it involves property A, will be adequate in God, insofar as He is affected by the idea of the human Body; that is (*per Proposition 13 of this part*), insofar as He constitutes the nature of the human Mind; and so (*per the Corollary to Proposition 11 of this part*) this idea is also adequate in the human Mind. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### COROLLARY.

From this it follows that the Mind is the more capable of perceiving more things adequately as its Body has more things in common with other bodies.

## PROPOSITION XL.

*Whatever ideas follow in the Mind from ideas that are adequate in it are also adequate.*

### DEMONSTRATION

is evident. For when we say that in the human mind an idea follows from ideas that are adequate in it, we say nothing else (*per the Corollary to Proposition 11 of this part*) than that in the Divine intellect itself there is an idea of which God is the cause, not insofar as He is infinite, nor insofar as He is affected by the ideas of very many singular things, but only insofar as He constitutes the essence of the human Mind.

<summary>Spinoza explains that because certain physical properties are common to all bodies (such as motion or extension), our ideas of these properties are "adequate" or perfectly clear. This forms the basis for universal laws of logic and physics that all humans share.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Part II of Spinoza's Ethics, following the discussion of "common notions"—the objective properties like extension and motion that all people perceive accurately because they are present in every part of the universe. Here, Spinoza shifts to explaining why other general concepts, like "Man" or "Being," are often confused and subjective, unlike the common notions.</meta>

<page-num>53</page-num>
<header>CONCERNING THE MIND.</header>

### SCHOLIUM I.

I have now explained the cause of those notions called <term>Common Notions</term><note>original: "Communes"; these are basic properties shared by all things, such as extension or motion, which Spinoza believes we perceive perfectly.</note>, which are the foundations of our reasoning. However, there are other causes for certain axioms or notions that would be useful to explain using our method. From these, it would become clear which notions are more useful than others, and which are of hardly any use at all. Furthermore, it would show which notions are truly common, which are clear and distinct only to those not burdened by prejudices, and finally, which ones are poorly founded. Moreover, it would be clear where those notions called <term>Second Notions</term><note>original: "Secundas"; these are "notions of notions," or abstract logical categories like "genus" or "species" used to organize our thoughts.</note>—and consequently the axioms based on them—derive their origin, along with other things I have meditated on regarding this subject. But since I have dedicated these matters to another treatise, and also to avoid creating boredom through too much wordiness on this point, I have decided to skip over it here.

Nevertheless, so that I do not omit anything necessary to know, I will briefly add the causes from which the terms called <term>Transcendentals</term><note>original: "Trascendentales"; these are the most generic possible categories that seem to "transcend" specific types of things.</note> have derived their origin, such as <term>Being</term><note>original: "Ens"</note>, <term>Thing</term><note>original: "Res"</note>, and <term>Something</term><note>original: "Aliquid"</note>. These terms arise from the fact that the human Body, because it is limited, is only capable of forming a certain number of <term>images</term><note>original: "imago"; I explained what an image is in the Scholium to Proposition 17 of this part. For Spinoza, an image is a physical trace or impression in the body.</note> distinctly and simultaneously within itself. If this number is exceeded, these images begin to be confused; and if this number of images that the Body is capable of forming distinctly and simultaneously is far exceeded, they will all become completely confused with one another.

Since this is the case, it is evident from the Corollary to Proposition 17 and Proposition 18 of this part, that the human Mind can imagine as many bodies distinctly and simultaneously as there are images that can be formed simultaneously in its own Body. But when the images in the Body are completely confused, the Mind will also imagine all bodies confusedly without any distinction, and will comprehend them as if under one attribute—namely, under the attribute of *Being*, *Thing*, etc. This can also be deduced from the fact that images are not always equally strong, and from other causes analogous to these, which there is no need to explain here; for it is enough to consider only one for the goal we are aiming at. For all these causes come down to this: these terms signify ideas that are confused in the highest degree.

From similar causes, those notions called <term>Universals</term><note>original: "Universales"</note> arose, such as <term>Man</term><note>original: "Homo"</note>, <term>Horse</term><note>original: "Equus"</note>, and <term>Dog</term><note>original: "Canis"</note>. That is to say, because so many images (for example, of men) are formed simultaneously in the human Body that they surpass the power of imagining—not entirely, but to the point where the Mind cannot imagine the small differences of individuals (such as each person's color, size, etc.) or their specific number. Instead, the Mind imagines distinctly only that in which all of them agree, insofar as the Body is affected by them; for the Body was affected most forcefully by that common element, specifically by each individual instance. This common element it expresses by the name *man*, and it predicates this of an infinite number of individuals. For, as we said, it cannot imagine a fixed number of individuals.

But it should be noted that these notions are not formed by everyone in the same way, but vary in each person according to the thing by which the Body has been more frequently affected, and which the Mind imagines or remembers more easily. For example, those who have more often contemplated the stature of men with admiration will understand by the name *man* an animal of upright stature; but those who are accustomed to contemplating something else will form another common image of men—namely,

<meta>catchword: namely,</meta>

<summary>Spinoza explains that general terms like "Being" and universal concepts like "Man" are actually confused ideas that arise because the human body is physically limited in how many distinct images it can process at once. These concepts are subjective and vary from person to person based on their individual experiences and physical impressions.</summary>

<page-num>54</page-num>
<header>ETHICS PART II.</header>

...that man is a <term>laughing animal</term><note>original: "animal risibile"; a traditional Aristotelian definition of humans as the only creatures capable of laughter.</note>; a two-legged animal without feathers; a <term>rational animal</term><note>original: "animal rationale"; the classic definition of humanity used by Scholastic philosophers.</note>; and so on for the rest. Each person will form universal images of things according to the disposition of their own body. Therefore, it is no wonder that so many controversies have arisen among Philosophers who have tried to explain natural things through the images of things alone.

### SCHOLIUM II.

From everything said above, it clearly appears that we perceive many things and form universal notions:

I. From individual objects, represented to our intellect through the senses in a fragmented, confused, and disorganized way (*See the Corollary to Prop. 29 of this Part*). For this reason, I have become accustomed to calling such perceptions <term>knowledge from wandering experience</term><note>original: "cognitionem ab experientia vaga"; this refers to unorganized, casual observations that haven't been tested by reason.</note>.

II. From signs—for example, from the fact that having heard or read certain words, we remember things and form certain ideas of them similar to those through which we imagine the things (*See the Scholium to Prop. 18 of this Part*). Both of these ways of contemplating things I shall hereafter call <term>knowledge of the first kind</term>, **opinion**, or **Imagination**.

III. Finally, from the fact that we have common notions and adequate ideas of the properties of things (*See Corollaries to Prop. 38 and 39, with its Corollary, and Prop. 40 of this Part*). This I shall call **Reason** and <term>knowledge of the second kind</term>.

Besides these two kinds of knowledge, there exists, as I shall show in what follows, another, **third kind**, which we will call <term>Intuitive Science</term><note>original: "Scientia Intuitiva"; Spinoza’s highest form of knowledge, where one sees the essence of things directly through the nature of God.</note>. This kind of knowing proceeds from an adequate idea of the formal essence of certain attributes of God to the adequate knowledge of the essence of things.

I will explain all these with an example of a single thing. Suppose there are three numbers given, and a fourth is to be obtained, which is to the third as the second is to the first. 

*   **By the First Kind (Memory/Experience):** Merchants do not hesitate to multiply the second by the third and divide the product by the first; either because they have not yet forgotten what they heard from their teacher without any proof, or because they have often experienced this with the simplest numbers. 
*   **By the Second Kind (Reason):** Others use the force of the proof of Proposition 19 in Book 7 of Euclid, namely from the common property of proportionals. 
*   **By the Third Kind (Intuition):** But in the simplest numbers, none of this is necessary. For example, given the numbers 1, 2, and 3, everyone sees that the fourth proportional number is 6; and this is seen much more clearly because we conclude the fourth number from the very ratio which we see with a **single intuition** between the first and the second.

### PROPOSITION XLI.

*Knowledge of the first kind is the only cause of falsity; but knowledge of the second and third kind is necessarily true.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

In the preceding Scholium, we said that all those ideas which are inadequate and confused belong to knowledge of the first kind; and therefore (*by Prop. 35 of this Part*), this knowledge is the only cause of falsity. Next, we said that those ideas which are adequate belong to knowledge of the second and third kinds; and so (*by Prop. 34 of this Part*), it is necessarily true. <term>Q. E. D.</term><note>original: "Quod Erat Demonstrandum"; meaning "Which was to be demonstrated."</note>

### PROPOSITION XLII.

*Knowledge of the second and third kinds, and not of the first kind, teaches us to distinguish the true from the false.*

<summary>Spinoza classifies three levels of knowledge: Imagination (unreliable experience/signs), Reason (common notions), and Intuition (direct insight into essences), arguing that only the latter two guarantee truth.</summary>
<keywords>epistemology, kinds of knowledge, imagination, reason, intuitive science, truth and falsity, rule of proportion, Euclid, Spinoza Ethics</keywords>

<page-num>55</page-num>
<header>ON THE MIND.</header>

### DEMONSTRATION.

This Proposition is self-evident. For anyone who knows how to distinguish between the true and the false must have an <term>adequate idea</term><note>An "adequate idea" is one that perfectly represents all the properties and internal characteristics of its object, as opposed to a "confused" or "inadequate" idea gained from mere sensory experience.</note> of the true and the false; that is (<note>original: "per 2. Schol. Prop. 40. hujus"</note> *according to the second Scholium of Proposition 40 of this Part*), they must know the true and the false through the second or third kind of knowledge.

## PROPOSITION XLIII.

*He who has a true idea knows at the same time that he has a true idea, and cannot doubt the truth of the matter.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

A true idea in us is one which, insofar as it is explained through the nature of the human Mind, is adequate in God (<note>original: "per Coroll. Prop. 11. hujus"</note> *according to the Corollary of Proposition 11 of this Part*). Let us suppose, therefore, that there exists in God, insofar as He is explained through the nature of the human Mind, an adequate idea **A**. Of this idea, there must also necessarily exist in God an idea that relates to God in the same way as idea **A** (<note>original: "per Prop. 20. hujus, cujus Demonstratio universalis est"</note> *according to Proposition 20 of this Part, the demonstration of which is universal*). But idea **A** is supposed to relate to God insofar as He is explained through the nature of the human Mind; therefore, the idea of idea **A** must also relate to God in the same way. That is to say (<note>original: "per idem Coroll. Prop. 11. hujus"</note> *according to the same Corollary of Proposition 11 of this Part*), this adequate idea of idea **A** will be in the Mind itself which has the adequate idea **A**. And thus, he who has an adequate idea, or (<note>original: "per Prop. 34. hujus"</note> *according to Proposition 34 of this Part*) he who truly knows a thing, must at the same time have an adequate idea of his knowledge, or a true knowledge; that is (<note>original: "ut per se manifestum"</note> *as is self-evident*), he must at the same time be certain. **WHICH WAS TO BE DEMONSTRATED.**

### SCHOLIUM.

In the Scholium to Proposition 21 of this Part, I explained what an <term>idea of an idea</term><note>This refers to reflexive knowledge: the mind’s ability to think about its own thoughts, making the thought itself the object of a new thought.</note> is; but it should be noted that the preceding Proposition is sufficiently manifest through itself. For no one who has a true idea is unaware that a true idea involves the highest certainty; for to have a true idea signifies nothing other than to know a thing perfectly or in the best way. Indeed, no one can doubt this matter unless they think that an idea is something mute, like a picture on a panel, and not a <term>mode of thinking</term><note>Spinoza argues against the "picture theory" of ideas. For him, an idea is an active mental process of understanding, not a static image.</note>, namely, the act of understanding itself. And, I ask, who can know that they understand something unless they first understand it? That is, who can know that they are certain of a thing unless they are first certain of it? Furthermore, what can be granted that is clearer and more certain than a true idea, which may serve as a standard of truth? Surely, just as light reveals both itself and the darkness, so <term>truth is the standard of itself and of the false</term><note>original: "veritas norma sui et falsi est"; one of Spinoza's most famous maxims, suggesting that truth is its own internal guarantee and needs no external test.</note>. And by these points, I believe I have answered the following questions: namely, if a true idea is distinguished from a false one only insofar as it is said to "agree with its object," then a true idea has no more reality or perfection than a false one (since they are distinguished only by an external label), and consequently, neither would a man who has true ideas have any advantage over one who has only false ideas. Next, how does it happen that men have false ideas? And finally, how can someone know for certain that they have ideas which agree with their objects? To these questions, I say, I believe I have already responded. For as far as the difference between a true and a false idea is concerned, it is established from Proposition 35 of this Part that the former relates to the latter as <term>being</term> relates to <term>non-being</term>. The causes of falsity, however, from Proposition 19 all the way to...

<summary>Spinoza argues that truth is self-evident and reflexive: to know something truly is to know that you know it. He famously asserts that truth acts like light, revealing both itself and the nature of falsehood without needing an external test.</summary>
<keywords>certainty, adequate idea, idea of an idea, standard of truth, light and darkness, being vs non-being, reflexivity</keywords>
