<page-num>56</page-num>
<header>ETHICS PART II.</header>
<meta>catchword: Fluctuation (original: "Fluctuabi-")</meta>

I have clearly shown this in Proposition 35 along with its Scholium. From these points, it also becomes clear what the difference is between a person who possesses true ideas and one who possesses only false ones. Finally, as to the last point—namely, how a person can know that they have an idea which corresponds to its <term>object</term><note>original: "ideato"; the actual thing in reality that the idea represents</note>—I have just shown sufficiently and more than enough that this arises solely from the fact that he has an idea which corresponds to its object, or that truth is its own standard. To this, add that our Mind, insofar as it perceives things truly, is a part of the infinite intellect of God (<note>original: "per Coroll. Prop. 11. hujus"</note> *according to the Corollary of Proposition 11 of this Part*); and thus it is as necessary that the clear and distinct ideas of the Mind are true as it is that the ideas of God are true.

# PROPOSITION XLIV.

*It is not in the nature of Reason to perceive things as contingent, but as necessary.*

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

It is in the nature of Reason to perceive things truly (<note>original: "per Prop. 41. hujus"</note> *according to Proposition 41 of this Part*), namely (<note>original: "per Ax. 6. p. 1."</note> *according to Axiom 6 of Part 1*) as they are in themselves; that is (<note>original: "per Prop. 29. p. 1."</note> *according to Proposition 29 of Part 1*), not as <term>contingent</term><note>Something is "contingent" if we think it could either happen or not happen; Spinoza argues this is just a failure of our understanding.</note>, but as necessary. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

->COROLLARY I.<-

From this it follows that it depends on the <term>imagination</term><note>In Spinoza’s system, "imagination" refers to the lowest level of knowledge, based on sensory experience and memory rather than logical necessity.</note> alone that we perceive things, whether in respect to the past or the future, as contingent.

->SCHOLIUM.<-

I will explain briefly how this happens. We have shown above (<note>original: "Prop. 17. hujus cum ejus Coroll."</note> *in Proposition 17 of this Part and its Corollary*) that the Mind, even if things do not exist, always imagines them as present to itself, unless causes occur which exclude their present existence. Furthermore (<note>original: "Prop. 18. hujus"</note> *in Proposition 18 of this Part*), we showed that if the human Body was once affected by two external bodies at the same time, when the Mind later imagines one of them, it will immediately remember the other as well; that is, it will perceive both as present to itself, unless causes occur which exclude their present existence.

Moreover, no one doubts that we also imagine time, specifically because we imagine some bodies moving slower or faster than others, or with equal speed. Let us therefore suppose a boy who yesterday saw Peter for the first time in the morning, Paul at noon, and Simeon in the evening, and today sees Peter again in the morning. From Proposition 18 of this Part, it is clear that as soon as he sees the morning light, he will immediately imagine the sun traversing the same part of the sky as on the preceding day—that is, he imagines the whole day—and simultaneously with the morning time he will imagine Peter, with the noon time Paul, and with the evening time Simeon. That is to say, he will imagine the existence of Paul and Simeon in relation to future time. Conversely, if he sees Simeon in the evening, he will relate Paul and Peter to past time, by imagining them together with the past time. This will happen more consistently the more often he has seen them in this same order.

But if it should happen that on some other evening he sees James instead of Simeon, then the following morning, when he thinks of the evening time, he will imagine now Simeon, now James, but not both at once. For he is supposed to have seen only one or the other at that evening time, not both together. His mind will therefore [fluctuate]...

<summary>Spinoza argues that reason perceives things as necessary truths rather than by chance, and explains that our sense of "contingency" or "luck" is merely a product of how our imagination links memories and time.</summary>
<keywords>reason, necessity, contingency, imagination, association of ideas, perception of time, Peter and Paul example, certainty</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on how the mind perceives time and contingency, specifically concluding the demonstration for Proposition 44 before defining the "perspective of eternity."</meta>

<page-num>57</page-num>
<header>OF THE MIND.</header>

...his imagination will therefore be [fluctuating], and when he imagines now this and now that in the coming evening time, he will imagine neither with certainty, but will contemplate each as a <term>contingent</term><note>Spinoza uses "contingent" to describe things we think might or might not happen, but he argues this is only because we lack full information.</note> future. This fluctuation of the imagination will be the same if the imagination concerns things we contemplate in the same way with relation to past or present time; and consequently, we shall imagine things related to the present as well as the past or future as contingent.

### COROLLARY II.

->It is in the nature of Reason to perceive things under a certain perspective of eternity.<-

### DEMONSTRATION.

For it is in the nature of Reason to contemplate things as necessary, and not as contingent (<note>original: "per Prop. praeced."</note> *according to the preceding Proposition*). Moreover, it perceives this necessity of things truly (<note>original: "per Prop. 41. hujus"</note> *according to Proposition 41 of this Part*), which is to say (<note>original: "per Axiom. 6. p. 1."</note> *according to Axiom 6 of Part 1*) as it is in itself. But (<note>original: "per Prop. 16. p. 1."</note> *according to Proposition 16 of Part 1*) this necessity of things is the very necessity of God’s eternal nature; therefore, it is in the nature of Reason to contemplate things under this <term>perspective of eternity</term><note>original: "sub quadam aeternitatis specie"; this is one of Spinoza's most famous concepts, meaning to see things not as they happen in time, but as they follow logically from the laws of the universe.</note>. Add to this that the foundations of Reason are <term>notions</term><note>original: "notiones"; these are "Common Notions," or universal truths that apply to everything, like the laws of geometry or physics.</note> (<note>original: "per Prop. 38. hujus"</note> *according to Proposition 38 of this Part*) which explain those things common to all, and which (<note>original: "per Prop. 37. hujus"</note> *according to Proposition 37 of this Part*) do not explain the <term>essence</term><note>The unique, core nature of a specific thing.</note> of any singular thing; these must therefore be conceived without any relation to time, but under a certain perspective of eternity. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

## PROPOSITION XLV.

->*Each idea of any body, or of a singular thing actually existing, necessarily involves the eternal and infinite essence of God.*<-

### DEMONSTRATION.

The idea of a singular thing, actually existing, necessarily involves both the essence and the existence of the thing itself (<note>original: "per Coroll. Prop. 8. hujus"</note> *according to the Corollary of Proposition 8 of this Part*): But singular things (<note>original: "per Prop. 15. p. 1."</note> *according to Proposition 15 of Part 1*) cannot be conceived without God; and because (<note>original: "per Prop. 6. hujus"</note> *according to Proposition 6 of this Part*) they have God as their cause insofar as He is considered under the <term>attribute</term><note>A fundamental quality of reality, such as "Extension" (physical space) or "Thought."</note> of which the things themselves are <term>modes</term><note>A "mode" is a specific individual instance or "version" of an attribute—like a specific thought or a specific physical object.</note>, their ideas must necessarily (<note>original: "per Axiom. 4. p. 1."</note> *according to Axiom 4 of Part 1*) involve the concept of their attribute, which is to say (<note>original: "per Defin. 6. p. 1."</note> *according to Definition 6 of Part 1*) the eternal and infinite essence of God. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### SCHOLIUM.

Here, by existence, I do not mean <term>duration</term><note>original: "duratio"; the passage of time. Spinoza argues that true existence isn't about "how long" something lasts, but its logical necessity.</note>, that is, existence as it is conceived abstractly and as if it were a kind of quantity. For I am speaking of the very nature of existence, which is attributed to singular things because they follow from the eternal necessity of God’s nature in infinite ways (<note>original: "vide Prop. 16. p. 1."</note> *see Proposition 16 of Part 1*). I am speaking, I say, of the very existence of singular things insofar as they are in God. For even if each thing is determined by another singular thing to exist in a certain way, the force by which each one persists in existing follows from the eternal necessity of the nature of God. On this matter, see the Corollary to Proposition 24 of Part 1.

## PROPOSITION XLVI.

->*The knowledge of the eternal and infinite essence of God which each idea involves is adequate and perfect.*<-

<summary>Spinoza defines "Reason" as the ability to see things as necessary parts of an eternal system (sub specie aeternitatis) rather than as random or contingent events. He argues that because everything is caused by God, the idea of any specific thing must inherently include the perfect knowledge of God's eternal essence.</summary>
<keywords>sub specie aeternitatis, perspective of eternity, necessity vs contingency, essence of God, singular things, common notions, duration, Reason</keywords>

<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion on the nature of Reason and its capacity to perceive things as necessary and eternal. It transitions from the general proofs regarding the Mind's perception of God to a specific proposition about the human mind's inherent knowledge of the Divine.</meta>

<page-num>58</page-num>
<header>ETHICS PART II.</header>

## DEMONSTRATION.

The demonstration of the preceding universal Proposition is clear; whether a thing is considered as a part or as a whole, its idea—whether of the whole or of the part—will involve God’s eternal and infinite essence (<note>original: "per Prop. præced."</note> *according to the preceding Proposition*). Therefore, that which provides knowledge of the eternal and infinite essence of God is common to all things, and is equally in the part as in the whole; and thus (<note>original: "per Prop. 38. hujus"</note> *according to Proposition 38 of this Part*) this knowledge will be <term>adequate</term><note>In Spinoza's philosophy, "adequate" knowledge is a clear and complete understanding that reflects the true cause of a thing, rather than a partial or confused perception.</note>. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

# PROPOSITION 47.

**The human mind has an adequate knowledge of the eternal and infinite essence of God.**

## DEMONSTRATION.

The human mind has ideas (<note>original: "per Prop. 22. hujus"</note> *according to Proposition 22 of this Part*), from which (<note>original: "per Prop. 23. hujus"</note> *according to Proposition 23 of this Part*) it perceives itself, its own Body (<note>original: "per Prop. 19. hujus"</note> *according to Proposition 19 of this Part*), and external bodies (<note>original: "per Coroll. 1. Prop. 16. et per Prop. 17. hujus"</note> *according to Corollary 1 of Proposition 16 and Proposition 17 of this Part*) as actually existing; and therefore (<note>original: "per Prop. 45. et 46. hujus"</note> *according to Propositions 45 and 46 of this Part*) it has an adequate knowledge of the eternal and infinite essence of God. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

## SCHOLIUM.

From this we see that the infinite essence of God and his eternity are known to all. Since, however, all things are in God and are conceived through God, it follows that we can deduce a great many things from this knowledge which we may know adequately, and thus form that <term>third kind of knowledge</term><note>Spinoza identifies three types of knowledge: 1. Opinion/Imagination (unreliable), 2. Reason (logical), and 3. Intuition (the "third kind"), which is the direct understanding of things as they follow from God.</note> of which we spoke in the second Scholium to Proposition 40 of this Part, and of whose excellence and utility we shall have the opportunity to speak in the Fifth Part. 

But the reason why humans do not have a knowledge of God as clear as they have of common notions is because they cannot <term>imagine</term><note>To "imagine," for Spinoza, is to form mental images based on physical sensations. Since God is not a physical body, he cannot be truly "pictured" by the senses.</note> God as they do bodies, and because they have joined the name *God* to images of things they are accustomed to seeing. Humans can hardly avoid this, because they are continually affected by external bodies. Indeed, most errors consist simply in this: that we do not correctly apply names to things. For when someone says that the lines drawn from the center of a circle to its circumference are unequal, they certainly understand by a "circle" something different—at least in that moment—than what mathematicians understand. Similarly, when men make errors in calculation, they have some numbers in their mind while writing others on the paper. 

Therefore, if you look at their Mind, they are certainly not in error; yet they seem to err because we think they have the same numbers in their mind that they have on the paper. If this were not the case, we would not believe they were erring at all—just as I did not believe a certain person was in error whom I recently heard shouting that his <term>atrium</term><note>A central hall or courtyard in a house.</note> had flown into his neighbor's hen, because his meaning seemed sufficiently clear to me <note>Spinoza likely means the man was using words so bizarrely that he wasn't making a logical mistake, but rather a linguistic one, or was perhaps speaking in a metaphor that was clear in context.</note>. Most controversies arise from this: either because men do not correctly explain their own mind, or because they misinterpret the mind of another. For in reality, while they are most vehemently contradicting one another, they are either thinking the same thing or thinking of different things, so that the errors and absurdities they imagine to exist in another are not actually there.

---

1) Early Edition: *by Corollary to Proposition 16.*

<header>ON THE MIND.</header>

### PROPOSITION 48.

*In the Mind there is no absolute or free will; but the Mind is determined to will this or that by a cause, which has also been determined by another, and this again by another, and so on to infinity.*

## DEMONSTRATION.

The Mind is a certain and determined mode of thinking (<note>original: "per Prop. 11. hujus"</note> *according to Proposition 11 of this Part*), and therefore (<note>original: "per Coroll. 2. Prop. 17. p. 1."</note> *according to Corollary 2 of Proposition 17, Part 1*) it cannot be a free cause of its own actions, or it cannot have an absolute faculty of willing and not willing; but it must be determined to will this or that (<note>original: "per Prop. 28. p. 1."</note> *according to Proposition 28, Part 1*) by a cause, which is also determined by another, and this again by another, etc. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

## SCHOLIUM.

In this same way it is demonstrated that in the Mind there is no absolute faculty of understanding, conceiving, loving, etc. From this it follows that these and similar faculties are either entirely fictitious, or are nothing but <term>metaphysical entities</term><note>original: "entia Metaphysica"; abstract concepts treated as if they were real things</note>, or <term>universals</term><note>general categories like "Humanity" as opposed to specific people</note>, which we are accustomed to form from particulars. So much so, that the intellect and the will are related to this or that idea, or to this or that volition, in the same way as <term>stoniness</term><note>original: "lapideitas"; the abstract essence of being a stone</note> is related to this or that stone, or as "man" is related to <term>Peter</term> and <term>Paul</term>. Moreover, the cause why men think themselves to be free, we explained in the *Appendix to Part One*. 

But before I proceed further, it must be noted here that by "will" I understand the faculty of affirming and denying, and not desire; I understand, I say, the faculty by which the Mind affirms or denies what is true or what is false, and not the desire by which the Mind seeks after or avoids things. But after we have demonstrated that these faculties are <term>universal notions</term>, which are not distinguished from the <term>singulars</term><note>the specific, individual instances of thoughts or actions</note> from which we form them, we must now inquire whether the volitions themselves are anything beyond the very ideas of things. We must inquire, I say, whether there is any other affirmation and negation in the Mind besides that which the idea, insofar as it is an idea, involves; regarding this matter, see the following Proposition, as well as Definition 3 of this Part, so that thought does not fall into the trap of mental pictures. For I do not understand by "ideas" the images such as are formed at the back of the eye, and, if you like, in the middle of the brain, but the <term>concepts of Thought</term>.

### PROPOSITION 49.

*In the Mind no volition exists, that is, no affirmation and negation, except that which the idea, insofar as it is an idea, involves.*

## DEMONSTRATION.

In the Mind (<note>original: "per Prop. præced."</note> *according to the preceding Proposition*) there is no absolute faculty of willing and not willing, but only singular volitions, namely this and that affirmation, and this and that negation. Let us conceive, therefore, some singular volition, namely a mode of thinking by which the Mind affirms that the three angles of a triangle are equal to two right angles. This affirmation involves the concept or idea of the triangle, that is, it cannot be conceived without the idea of the triangle. For it is the same thing to say that A must involve concept B, as it is to say that A cannot be conceived without B. Next, this affirmation (<note>original: "per..."</note> *according to...*)

<header>ETHICS PART II.</header>

<note>original: "Axiom. 3. hujus"</note> *Axiom 3 of this Part*) it cannot even exist without the idea of the triangle. This affirmation, therefore, can neither exist nor be conceived without the idea of the triangle. Furthermore, this idea of the triangle must involve this very same affirmation—namely, that its three angles are equal to two right angles. <note>In Euclidean geometry, the sum of the angles in a triangle is 180 degrees, or "two right angles."</note> Wherefore, and conversely, this idea of the triangle can neither exist nor be conceived without this affirmation; and thus (<note>original: "per Def. 2. hujus"</note> *according to Definition 2 of this Part*) this affirmation belongs to the essence of the idea of the triangle, and is nothing other than the idea itself. And what we have said regarding this specific volition (since we chose it as an example at will), must also be said of every volition—namely, that it is nothing more than the idea itself. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

->COROLLARY<-

The will and the intellect are one and the same.

->DEMONSTRATION<-

The will and the intellect are nothing but the individual volitions and ideas themselves (<note>original: "per Prop. 48. hujus et ejusdem Schol."</note> *according to Proposition 48 of this Part and its Scholium*). But an individual volition and an idea (<note>original: "per Prop. præced."</note> *according to the preceding Proposition*) are one and the same; therefore, the will and the intellect are one and the same. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

->SCHOLIUM<-

By this, we have removed the cause that is commonly established as the source of error. Moreover, we have shown above that falsehood consists solely in the <term>privation</term><note>The lack or absence of something that should be there; Spinoza argues error isn't a "thing" but just a lack of complete information.</note> which mutilated and confused ideas involve. Wherefore, a false idea, insofar as it is false, does not involve certainty. When we say, therefore, that a man rests in false things and does not doubt them, we do not say that he is certain, but only that he does not doubt, or that he rests in false things because no causes are present to make his <term>imagination</term><note>The faculty of forming mental images of things not present; for Spinoza, this is a lower level of knowledge.</note> waver ¹).

Regarding this, see the Scholium to Proposition 44 of this Part. However much a man is supposed to adhere to false things, we will never say that he is certain. For by certainty, we understand something positive (<note>original: "vide Prop. 43. hujus cum ejusdem Schol."</note> *see Proposition 43 of this Part with its Scholium*), not the mere privation of doubt. But by the privation of certainty, we understand falsehood.

However, for a fuller explanation of the preceding Proposition, there remain some things to be cautioned. It remains, then, for me to respond to the objections that can be raised against this doctrine of ours; and finally, to remove every scrap of doubt, I have thought it worthwhile to indicate certain benefits of this doctrine. Certain benefits, I say; for the principal ones will be better understood from what we shall say in the Fifth Part.

I begin, therefore, with the first point. And I warn readers to distinguish accurately between an **Idea**, or a concept of the Mind, and the **Images** of things which we imagine. Next, it is necessary to distinguish between **ideas** and **Words**, by which we signify things. For because these three—namely, images, words, and ideas—are by many either completely confused, or not distinguished accurately enough, or finally, not distinguished with enough caution, they have remained completely ignorant of this doctrine of the will, which is entirely necessary to know, both for speculation and for ordering life wisely. Indeed, those who think that ideas...

<meta>This page continues the Scholium to Proposition 49 in Part II of the Ethics. Spinoza is addressing common misconceptions about the nature of the mind, specifically the relationship between the "will" and the "intellect," and is preparing to answer four major objections to his theory that they are identical.</meta>

# ON THE MIND.

...convince themselves that <insert>ideas</insert> consist in images formed in us by the encounter with bodies, they persuade themselves that those ideas of things for which we can form no similar image are not ideas at all, but only fictions which we forge by the free choice of the will. They therefore look upon ideas as if they were mute pictures on a panel, and, preoccupied by this prejudice, they do not see that an idea, insofar as it is an idea, involves affirmation or negation. Furthermore, those who confuse words with the idea, or with the very affirmation that the idea involves, think that they can "will" something contrary to what they feel, simply because they can affirm or deny something in words alone that contradicts their true internal perception. However, anyone who pays attention to the nature of Thought (<note>original: "Cogitationis"</note>)—which does not involve the concept of Extension (<note>original: "Extensionis"</note>) <note>In Spinoza’s system, "Extension" refers to the physical world of matter and space, while "Thought" refers to the mental realm. He argues they are distinct attributes that do not overlap in their definitions.</note>—will easily be able to cast off these prejudices. Such a person will clearly understand that an idea (since it is a mode of thinking) consists neither in the image of some thing nor in words. For the essence of words and images is constituted solely by bodily motions, which do not involve the concept of thought at all.

And let it suffice to have given these few warnings; I therefore move on to the aforementioned objections. The *first of these* is that they think it is certain that the will extends more widely than the intellect, and is therefore different from it. The reason they think the will extends more widely than the intellect is that they say they experience that they do not require a greater faculty of assenting—that is, of affirming or denying—to agree to an infinite number of other things which we do not perceive, than that which we already have; yet they certainly would require a greater faculty of understanding to do so. Therefore, the will is distinguished from the intellect because the latter is finite, while the former is infinite. *Secondly*, it can be objected against us that experience seems to teach nothing more clearly than that we can suspend our judgment so as not to assent to the things we perceive. This is further confirmed by the fact that no one is said to be deceived insofar as they perceive something, but only insofar as they assent or dissent. For example, someone who imagines a winged horse does not, for that reason, grant that a winged horse exists; that is, he is not deceived by it unless he simultaneously grants that a winged horse exists. Therefore, nothing seems to be taught more clearly by experience than that the will, or the faculty of assenting, is free and distinct from the faculty of understanding. *Thirdly*, it can be objected that one affirmation does not seem to contain more reality than another; that is, we do not seem to require a greater power to affirm that what is true is true, than to affirm that something false is true. However, we perceive that one idea has more reality, or perfection, than another; for just as some objects are more excellent than others, so too the ideas of those objects are more perfect than others. From this, the difference between the will and the intellect also seems to be established. *Fourthly*, it can be objected: if a man does not act from freedom of the will, what will happen if he is in a state of equilibrium, like Buridan's ass? <note>Buridan's ass is a classic philosophical paradox describing a donkey placed exactly halfway between two identical piles of hay. Without a reason to choose one over the other, the donkey would theoretically starve to death.</note> Will he perish of hunger and thirst? If I were to grant this, I would seem to be conceiving of an ass or a statue of a man, rather than a man; but if I deny it, then he will determine himself, and consequently possesses the faculty of going and doing whatever he wants. Besides these, other things can perhaps be objected; but since I am not bound to repeat everything that anyone might dream up, I will take care to respond only to these objections, and as briefly as possible. And indeed, to the *first*, I say that I grant that the will extends more widely...

<meta>This page continues Spinoza’s refutation of common objections to his theory that the "will" and the "intellect" are the same thing. On the previous page, he began addressing the first objection: the claim that the will is "infinite" while the intellect is "finite."</meta>

### ETHICS PART II.

...than the intellect, if by "intellect" they understand only clear and distinct ideas. But I deny that the **will** extends more widely than perceptions, or the faculty of conceiving. Indeed, I do not see why the faculty of willing should be called "infinite" any more than the faculty of sensing. For just as we can affirm an infinite number of things by the same faculty of willing (though only one after another, as we cannot affirm infinite things all at once), so too can we sense or perceive an infinite number of bodies (one after another) by the same faculty of sensing.

What if they say that there are infinite things given which we cannot perceive? I retort that we cannot reach those same things by any thought, and consequently by any faculty of willing. But they say: "if God wished to bring it about that we perceived those things as well, he would indeed have to give us a greater faculty of perceiving, but not a greater faculty of willing than he has already given." This is the same as saying that if God wished to bring it about that we understood an infinite number of other beings, it would be necessary for him to give us a greater intellect, but not a more universal idea of "being" than he has already given to embrace those same infinite beings. 

For we have shown that the **will** is a universal entity—or an idea—by which we explain all singular volitions (<note>That is, individual acts of willing or choosing.</note>), which is to say, that which is common to them all. Since they believe this common or universal idea of all volitions to be a "faculty," it is not at all surprising if they say that this faculty extends in infinitum (<note>To infinity</note>) beyond the limits of the intellect. For a universal term is applied equally to one, to many, or to infinite individuals.

To the *second* objection, I respond by denying that we have a free power to suspend judgment. For when we say that someone "suspends judgment," we are saying nothing other than that he sees that he does not perceive the thing adequately (<note>In Spinoza's philosophy, "adequate" knowledge is complete, clear, and certain, whereas "inadequate" knowledge is partial or confused.</note>). Therefore, the suspension of judgment is in reality a perception, and not a free act of will. 

To understand this clearly, let us conceive of a boy imagining a horse, and perceiving nothing else. Since this imagination involves the existence of the horse (*by the Corollary to Proposition 17 of this part*), and the boy perceives nothing that takes away the horse’s existence, he will necessarily contemplate the horse as present. Nor will he be able to doubt its existence, although he is not certain of it. 

We experience this every day in our dreams, and I do not believe there is anyone who thinks that while dreaming he has the free power of suspending judgment concerning those things he dreams, or of bringing it about that he does not dream what he sees himself dreaming. And yet, it does happen that we suspend judgment even in dreams—namely, when we dream that we are dreaming. 

Furthermore, I grant that no one is deceived insofar as he perceives; that is, I grant that the imaginations of the Mind, considered in themselves, involve no error (*see the Scholium to Proposition 17 of this part*). But I deny that a human being affirms nothing insofar as he perceives. For what else is perceiving a winged horse than affirming wings of a horse? For if the Mind perceived nothing else besides the winged horse, it would contemplate it as present to itself; it would have no cause for doubting its existence, nor any faculty of dissenting—unless the imagination of the winged horse were joined to an idea that takes away the existence of that same horse, or unless the Mind perceives that the idea of the winged horse which it has is inadequate. In those cases, it will either necessarily deny the existence of the horse or necessarily doubt it. 

And with these points, I think I have also responded to the *third* objection, namely, that the wi...

<summary>Spinoza argues that the "will" is not a special infinite power but a general concept for individual acts of thought, and that suspending judgment is a result of perceiving incomplete information rather than a free choice.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Spinoza’s refutation of objections to his theory that the will and the intellect are the same. He is currently arguing that "Will" is merely a universal term we use to describe individual acts of affirmation.</meta>

# ON THE MIND.

...will <note>original: "voluntas"</note> is a universal thing which is predicated of all ideas, and which signifies only that which is common to all ideas—namely, affirmation. Therefore, its adequate essence, insofar as it is conceived abstractly, must be in every single idea, and in this way alone is it the same in all of them; but not insofar as it is considered to constitute the essence of the idea, for in that respect, singular affirmations differ as much from one another as the ideas themselves do. For example, the affirmation involved in the idea of a circle differs as much from the affirmation involved in the idea of a triangle as the idea of a circle itself differs from the idea of a triangle. 

Furthermore, I absolutely deny that we require an equal power of thinking to affirm that what is true is true as we do to affirm that what is false is true. For these two affirmations, if you look at the mind, relate to each other as being relates to non-being <note>original: "ens ad non-ens"</note>; for there is nothing positive in ideas that constitutes the form of falsehood (*see Proposition 35 of this part with its Scholium, and the Scholium to Proposition 47*). Therefore, it must be especially noted here how easily we are deceived when we confuse universal concepts with singular ones, and confuse beings of reason <note>mental constructs used for categorization</note> and abstractions with real things. 

Finally, regarding the **fourth** objection <note>The famous "Buridan's Ass" thought experiment, where a subject is placed exactly between two equal rewards.</note>, I say that I entirely concede that a person placed in such an equilibrium (namely, one who perceives nothing other than hunger and thirst, and such food and such drink as are equally distant from him) will perish of hunger and thirst. If they ask me whether such a person should not be considered an ass rather than a human, I say that I do not know—just as I do not know how to value a man who hangs himself, or how to value children, fools, the insane, and so on.

It remains, finally, to indicate how much the knowledge of this doctrine contributes to the conduct of life, which we shall easily perceive from the following points:

**I.** Insofar as it teaches us that we act by the will <note>original: "nutu", literally "at the nod" or command</note> of God alone, and that we are partakers of the divine nature; we are more so as we perform more perfect actions, and as we understand God more and more. This doctrine, therefore, besides making the soul entirely calm, also teaches us wherein our highest happiness or blessedness <note>original: "beatitudo"</note> consists: namely, in the knowledge of God alone, by which we are led to do only those things that love and piety suggest. From this we clearly understand how far they wander from the true estimation of virtue who expect to be decorated by God with the highest rewards for their virtue and best actions, as if for the harshest servitude—as if virtue itself and the service of God were not happiness itself and the highest freedom.

**II.** Insofar as it teaches how we should conduct ourselves regarding "matters of fortune"—those things which are not in our power, or which do not follow from our own nature. It teaches us to expect and bear both faces of fortune with an even mind <note>original: "æquo animo"</note>; because all things follow from the eternal decree of God with the same necessity as it follows from the essence of a triangle that its three angles are equal to two right angles.

**III.** This doctrine contributes to social life, insofar as it teaches us to hate no one, to despise no one, to mock no one, to be angry with no one, and to envy no one. Furthermore, insofar as it teaches that everyone should be content with their own things, and helpful to their neighbor $^1$)—not out of...

# ETHICS PART II.

...womanish <note>The OCR text begins with "liebri," a fragment of the Latin *muliebri*. In the 17th century, "womanish pity" was a technical term used to describe an impulsive, irrational emotional response as opposed to deliberate, rational compassion.</note> pity, partiality, or superstition, but by the sole guidance of **Reason** <term>Reason: For Spinoza, this is "knowledge of the second kind," which involves understanding the shared properties of things and acting according to logical necessity rather than random impulse.</term>, according as time and circumstance require, as I shall show in the third Part. **IV.** Finally, this doctrine also contributes not a little to the common society, insofar as it teaches the manner in which citizens are to be governed and led: namely, not so that they should be slaves, but so that they may freely do those things which are best.

And with this, I have completed what I intended to address in this Scholium, and I hereby bring this second Part of ours to an end. In it, I believe I have explained the nature of the human Mind and its properties at sufficient length and—as far as the difficulty of the subject allows—with clarity. I have also handed down such principles from which many excellent, highly useful, and necessary things to know can be concluded, as will be partly evident from what follows.

<meta>This page begins Part III of the Ethics. In the previous page, Spinoza concluded Part II, which focused on the nature and origin of the human mind. He now transitions from the cognitive and physical foundations of human existence to the study of human psychology and the passions.</meta>

# ETHICS

## PART THREE,

### ON THE ORIGIN AND NATURE OF THE EMOTIONS. <term>Emotions: Spinoza uses the Latin word *Affectus*. While often translated as "Affects," it refers to the ways the body and mind are "affected" by external forces, increasing or decreasing our power to act.</term>

Most people who have written about the **Emotions** and the human way of life seem to treat not of natural things, which follow the common laws of *Nature*, but of things that are outside of *Nature*. Indeed, they seem to conceive of man in *Nature* as a "kingdom within a kingdom." <note>original: "imperium in imperio." This is one of Spinoza’s most famous critiques, arguing that humans are not a special exception to the laws of the universe.</note> For they believe that man disturbs the order of *Nature* rather than following it, and that he has absolute power over his own actions, being determined by nothing other than himself. They then attribute the cause of human weakness and instability not to the common power of *Nature*, but to some supposed defect in human nature, which they therefore bewail, mock, despise, or—as is most often the case—detest. And whoever knows how to criticize the weakness of the human *Mind* more eloquently or cleverly is regarded as if they were divine. There have not been lacking excellent men, however (to whose labor and industry we confess we owe much), who have written many outstanding things about the right way of life, and have given advice full of wisdom to mortals; but the nature and power of the **Emotions**, and what the *Mind* can do in turn to moderate them, no one, as far as I know, has determined.

I am indeed aware that the most celebrated *Descartes* <note>René Descartes (1596–1650), the French philosopher Spinoza frequently engages with. Descartes attempted to explain the passions through the pineal gland.</note>, although he also believed that the *Mind* has absolute power over its actions, nevertheless sought to explain human **Emotions** through their primary causes, and at the same time to show a way by which the *Mind* might have absolute command over the **Emotions**. But, in my opinion, he showed nothing but the sharpness of his great intellect, as I shall demonstrate in its proper place. For I wish to return to those who prefer to detest or mock human **Emotions** and actions rather than understand them. To them it will doubtless seem strange that I should undertake to treat human vices and follies in a **Geometric** manner <term>Geometric manner: Spinoza writes his philosophy like a geometry book, using definitions and axioms to prove his points. He believes human behavior is as predictable as the lines and angles of a triangle.</term>, and that I wish to demonstrate by certain reasoning those things which they cry out are contrary to **Reason**, and are vain, absurd, and horrific. But this is my reasoning: Nothing happens in *Nature* that can be attributed to a defect in it; for *Nature* is always the same, and everywhere its virtue and power of acting are one and the same; that is, the laws and rules of *Nature*, according to which all things happen and are changed from one form into another, are everywhere and always the same, and therefore there must also be one and the same 

<summary>Spinoza begins Part III by critiquing philosophers who treat human emotions as "supernatural" or as flaws in human nature. He announces his intention to study human behavior and emotions using the same rigorous, logical laws that govern the rest of the natural world.</summary>
<keywords>Emotions (Affects), Laws of Nature, Human Nature, Descartes, Geometric Method, Rationalism</keywords>

--- Page 81 ---
<meta>This page continues the Preface to Part III of the Ethics. Spinoza has just finished criticizing previous philosophers for treating human emotions as if they were outside the laws of nature. He now asserts his intention to study human behavior using the same objective, geometric rigor applied to mathematics or physics.</meta>

# ETHICS PART III.

...way of understanding the nature of any things whatsoever, namely through the universal laws and rules of *Nature*. The **emotions** <term>Emotion: Spinoza uses the word *Affectus*. In his system, an emotion is not just a feeling, but a physical and mental change that increases or decreases our power to function.</term> therefore, of hatred, anger, envy, etc., considered in themselves, follow from the same necessity and power of *Nature* as other singular things; and therefore they acknowledge certain causes through which they are understood, and they have certain properties just as worthy of our knowledge as the properties of any other thing, in the mere contemplation of which we take delight. Therefore, regarding the nature and power of the **emotions**, and the power of the *Mind* over them, I shall proceed by the same method as I did in the previous parts concerning *God* and the *Mind*, and I will consider human actions and appetites just as if the question were about lines, planes, or bodies. <note>This is Spinoza's famous "Geometric Method." He believes human psychology is governed by laws as fixed as those of geometry.</note>

### DEFINITIONS.

I. I call an **adequate cause** one whose effect can be clearly and distinctly perceived through it. I call an **inadequate** or **partial cause**, however, one whose effect cannot be understood through it alone.
II. I say that we **act** when something happens, either within us or outside us, of which we are the adequate cause—that is (*by the preceding Definition*), when something follows from our nature, either within us or outside us, which can be clearly and distinctly understood through that nature alone. On the other hand, I say that we **suffer** <note>original: "pati." In a philosophical context, this refers to being "passive" or "acted upon" by outside forces, rather than experiencing pain.</note> when something happens in us, or something follows from our nature, of which we are only a partial cause.
III. By **Emotion** I understand the affections of the *Body* by which the *Body’s* power of acting is increased or diminished, aided or restrained, and at the same time, the ideas of these affections.
*If, therefore, we can be the adequate cause of any of these affections, then by Emotion I understand an **action**; otherwise, a **passion**.*

### POSTULATES.

I. The human *Body* can be affected in many ways by which its power of acting is increased or diminished, and also in other ways which make its power of acting neither greater nor less.
*This Postulate or Axiom relies on Postulate 1 and Lemmas 5 and 7, which see after Prop. 13, Part 2.*
II. The human *Body* can undergo many changes and nevertheless retain the impressions or traces of objects (*on which see Post. 5, Part 2*), and consequently the same images of things; *for the definition of which, see the Scholium to Prop. 17, Part 2.*

### PROPOSITION I.

*Our Mind does some things (acts), but suffers others; namely, insofar as it has adequate ideas, it necessarily does certain things, and insofar as it has inadequate ideas, it necessarily suffers certain things.*

#### DEMONSTRATION.

*In any human Mind, some ideas are adequate, while others are fragmented and confused (by the Scholium to Prop. 40, Part 2). However, ideas that are adequate in someone's Mind are adequate in God, insofar as He constitutes the essence of that same Mind (by the Corollary to Prop. 11, Part 2), and*

<summary>Spinoza establishes the foundational definitions for his study of human emotions, arguing that human behavior follows universal laws of nature and can be studied geometrically. He defines the distinction between "acting" (being the adequate cause of one's own changes) and "suffering" (being acted upon by external forces).</summary>
<keywords>adequate cause, inadequate cause, action, passion, emotion, affectus, geometric method, nature, mind-body relationship</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the demonstration of Proposition 1 in Part III of the Ethics. The previous page established the definitions of "action" (when we are the adequate cause of our state) and "passion" (when we are only a partial cause). Spinoza is now completing the proof that the mind acts when it has clear ideas and is passive when it has confused ones.</meta>

# CONCERNING THE EMOTIONS.

<note>original header: "DE AFFECTIBUS." Spinoza uses "Affects" to describe both active emotions and passive feelings.</note>

...which are then inadequate in the Mind, are also adequate in God (*by the same Corollary* <note>Referring to Part 2, Prop. 11, Coroll.</note>)—not insofar as He contains only the essence of that same Mind, but insofar as He contains the Minds of other things in Himself at the same time. Next, from any given idea some effect must necessarily follow (*by Prop. 36, Part 1*), of which effect God is the **adequate cause** (*see Def. 1 of this part*), not insofar as He is infinite, but insofar as He is considered as affected by that given idea (*see Prop. 9, Part 2*). 

But of that effect, of which God is the cause insofar as He is affected by an idea that is **adequate** in someone’s Mind, that same Mind is the **adequate cause** (*by Coroll. Prop. 11, Part 2*). Therefore our Mind (*by Def. 2 of this part*), insofar as it has **adequate ideas**, necessarily **acts**; which was the first point. 

Next, whatever necessarily follows from an idea that is **adequate** in God—not insofar as He has the Mind of one man only, but insofar as He has the Minds of other things in Himself along with the Mind of that same man—of that effect, that man’s Mind is not the **adequate cause**, but a **partial cause** (*by the same Coroll. Prop. 11, Part 2*); and consequently (*by Def. 2 of this part*) the Mind, insofar as it has **inadequate ideas**, necessarily **undergoes** <term>Undergoes: Latin *patitur*, the root of "passion." For Spinoza, we are "passive" when something happens to us that we don't fully understand or control.</term> certain things; which was the second point. Therefore our Mind, etc. **Which was to be demonstrated.** <note>original: "Q. E. D." (Quod Erat Demonstrandum)</note>

### COROLLARY.

From this it follows that the Mind is subject to more **passions** the more **inadequate ideas** it has, and conversely, it **acts** more the more **adequate ideas** it has.

## PROPOSITION II.

->*Neither can the **Body** determine the **Mind** to thinking, nor can the **Mind** determine the **Body** to motion, nor to rest, nor to anything else (if there be any such thing).*<-

### DEMONSTRATION.

All modes of **thinking** have God as a cause insofar as He is a **thinking thing**, and not insofar as He is explained through another **attribute** <term>Attribute: A fundamental way of perceiving reality, such as "Thought" or "Extension" (physical matter).</term> (*by Prop. 6, Part 2*); therefore, that which determines the Mind to think is a mode of **Thinking** and not of **Extension**; that is (*by Def. 1, Part 2*), it is not the **Body**; which was the first point. 

Next, the motion and rest of the **Body** must arise from another body, which was also determined to motion or rest by another, and absolutely, whatever arises in a body must have arisen from God, insofar as He is considered as affected by some mode of **Extension**, and not insofar as He is affected by some mode of **Thinking** (*by the same Prop. 6, Part 2*); that is, it cannot arise from the **Mind**, which (*by Prop. 11, Part 2*) is a mode of thinking; which was the second point. Therefore neither does the **Body** [determine] the **Mind** etc. **Which was to be demonstrated.**

### SCHOLIUM.

These things are understood more clearly from what was said in the Scholium to Proposition 7 of Part 2: namely, that the **Mind** and the **Body** are one and the same thing, which is conceived now under the attribute of **Thought**, now under the attribute of **Extension**. From this it follows that the order or connection of things is one, whether **Nature** be conceived under this or that attribute; consequently, the order of **actions** and **passions** of our **Body** is simultaneous in nature with the order of **actions** and **passions** of the **Mind**; which is also evident from the way in which Pro-
<meta>catchword: Pro-</meta>

<summary>Spinoza concludes that the Mind's power to act is tied to its clarity of thought (adequate ideas). He then introduces the famous principle of Mind-Body Parallelism, arguing that the mind cannot cause physical movement and the body cannot cause thoughts, because they are simply two different ways of viewing the same reality.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the Scholium to Proposition 2 of Part III. In the preceding section, Spinoza argued that the Mind and Body are one and the same thing, conceived under different attributes (Thought and Extension). He is now addressing the common resistance to this idea, specifically the belief that the Mind "commands" the Body.</meta>

### ETHICS PART III.

...demonstrated in Proposition 12 of Part 2. <note>Proposition 12 of Part 2 states that whatever happens in the Body must be perceived by the Mind.</note> However, even though these things are so, such that no reason for doubting remains, I hardly believe that men can be induced to weigh these matters with a calm mind unless I verify the matter through experience; so firmly are they persuaded that the Body moves or rests solely at the Mind’s command <note>original: "nutu," meaning a nod or command</note>, and does many things that depend solely on the Mind's will and its art of thinking. For indeed, no one has yet determined what the Body is capable of—that is, experience has taught no one so far what the Body can do solely by the laws of Nature (insofar as Nature is considered only as corporeal), and what it cannot do unless it is determined by the Mind. For no one has yet known the structure of the Body so accurately as to be able to explain all its functions—not to mention that many things are observed in **Animals** <note>original: "Brutis." Spinoza often uses animals as examples of complex "machines" that function without human-like reasoning.</note> which far exceed human ingenuity, and that sleepwalkers do many things in their sleep which they would not dare while awake. This shows clearly enough that the Body itself, solely by the laws of its own nature, can do many things that its own Mind wonders at. 

Next, no one knows in what manner or by what means the Mind moves the Body, nor how many degrees of motion it can impart to the Body, nor with what speed it can move it. It follows from this that when men say this or that action of the Body arises from the Mind, which has command over the Body, they do not know what they are saying; they are doing nothing more than admitting, in fancy words, that they are ignorant of the true cause of that action and find it remarkable. 

But they will say that, whether or not they know by what means the Mind moves the Body, they nonetheless experience that unless the human Mind were fit for thinking, the Body would be inert. Next, they experience that it is in the Mind’s power alone both to speak and to be silent, and many other things which they therefore believe depend on the Mind’s decree. But as for the *first* point, I ask them: does experience not also teach that if the Body is inert, the Mind is simultaneously unfit for thinking? For when the Body is at rest in sleep, the Mind remains localized with it in a stupor, and does not have the power of thinking as it does when awake. Next, I believe everyone has experienced that the Mind is not always equally fit for thinking about the same subject; rather, as the Body is more fit for the image of this or that object to be stirred in it, so the Mind is more fit for contemplating this or that object. 

But they will say that from the laws of Nature alone (insofar as it is considered only as corporeal) it is impossible for the causes of buildings, paintings, and things of this sort—which are made only by human art—to be deduced; nor would the human Body be capable of building any temple unless it were determined and guided by the Mind. But I have already shown that they do not know what the Body can do, or what can be deduced from the mere contemplation of its nature alone. They themselves experience that many things happen solely by the laws of Nature which they would never have believed could happen except by the direction of the Mind (such as the things sleepwalkers do in their sleep, which they themselves wonder at when awake). I add here the very structure of the human Body, which far surpasses in craftsmanship <note>original: "artificio"</note> everything made by human art—not to mention what I have shown above: that from Nature, considered under any attribute, infinite things follow. 

Furthermore, as for the *second* point, human affairs would certainly be much more fortunately conducted if it were equally in man's power to be silent as it is to speak. But experience teaches more than enough that humans...

<summary>Spinoza challenges the idea that the Mind controls the Body, arguing that we do not yet know the full extent of the Body's physical capabilities and that mental states are deeply dependent on physical ones.</summary>

--- Page 84 ---
<meta>This page continues the Scholium to Proposition 2 in Part III of the Ethics. Spinoza is furthering his argument that the "decrees" of the Mind are actually identical to the physical "appetites" of the Body, seen from a different perspective.</meta>

# ON THE AFFECTS. <note>original: "De Affectibus." Spinoza uses "Affects" to describe both our emotions and the physical changes in our power to act.</note>

...that they have nothing less in their power than their tongue, and that they can do nothing less than moderate their appetites; hence it has happened that most people believe we only act freely in those matters which we seek lightly, because the appetite for such things can easily be lessened by the memory of another thing which we frequently recall; but that we are least free in those things which we seek with great emotion, and which cannot be calmed by the memory of something else. But truly, unless they had experienced that we do many things for which we afterward feel regret, and that we often—namely, when we are conflicted by contrary emotions—"see the better but follow the worse," <note>Spinoza is quoting the Roman poet Ovid: "Video meliora proboque, deteriora sequor." It is a classic description of the lack of self-control.</note> nothing would prevent them from believing that we do everything freely. 

Thus an infant believes it freely desires milk, while an angry boy believes he wants revenge, and a timid man believes he wants to flee. Next, a drunkard believes that he speaks from a free decree of the **Mind** those things which he later, when sober, wishes he had kept silent; so too the delirious person, the chatterbox, the child, and many others of this sort believe they speak by a free decree of the Mind, when in fact they are unable to restrain the impulse to speak that they have. 

Thus experience itself teaches no less clearly than Reason that men believe themselves to be free for this reason alone: that they are conscious of their actions, and yet ignorant of the causes by which they are determined. Furthermore, it shows that the <term>decrees of the Mind</term> <note>Spinoza defines a "decree" (decretum) as a mental decision or choice.</note> are nothing other than the appetites themselves, which vary according to the varying disposition of the **Body**. For everyone governs everything according to their own emotion, and those who are conflicted by contrary emotions do not know what they want; those, however, who are moved by none are easily driven here and there by the slightest momentum. 

All these things certainly show clearly that the decree of the Mind, the appetite, and the <term>determination of the Body</term> <note>A "determination" is a physical state or direction resulting from the laws of motion.</note> are simultaneous by nature, or rather are one and the same thing, which we call a **Decree** when it is considered under the attribute of <term>Thought</term> <note>Spinoza's term for the mental realm of reality.</note> and explained through it, and which we call a **Determination** when it is considered under the attribute of <term>Extension</term> <note>Spinoza's term for the physical, spatial realm of reality.</note> and deduced from the laws of motion and rest; this will become even clearer from what is about to be said. 

For there is something else I would especially like to be noted here: namely, that we can do nothing by a decree of the Mind unless we remember it. For example, we cannot speak a word unless we remember it. Furthermore, it is not within the free power of the Mind to remember or to forget anything. Therefore, it is believed to be within the power of the Mind alone that we can either keep silent or speak about the thing we remember, based solely on a decree of the Mind. But when we dream that we are speaking, we believe we are speaking from a free decree of the Mind, and yet we do not speak; or, if we do speak, that happens from a spontaneous movement of the Body. We dream, next, that we are hiding certain things from people, and this by the same decree of the Mind by which, while awake, we keep silent about those things we know. Finally, we dream that we do certain things by a decree of the Mind which we would not dare to do while awake. 

And so I would very much like to know whether there are two kinds of decrees in the Mind: one "Fantastic" and the other "Free"? But if we do not wish to go so far into madness, it must necessarily be conceded that this decree of the Mind, which is believed to be free, is not distinguished from the imagination or the memory itself, and is nothing other than that affirmation which the idea, insofar as it is an idea, necessarily involves (*see Part 2, Proposition 49*). And so these decrees of the Mind arise in the Mind with the same necessity as the ideas of things actually existing. Those, therefore, who believe that they speak, or keep silent, or do anything from a free decree of the Mind, dream with their eyes open.

<summary>Spinoza argues that "free will" is an illusion caused by our awareness of our actions but ignorance of their underlying physical causes. He concludes that mental decisions and physical appetites are actually the same event described in two different ways.</summary>
<keywords>free will, determinism, mind-body identity, appetite, decree, memory, dreams, consciousness, Spinoza</keywords>

<meta>This page continues from the discussion in Part III of the Ethics regarding the relationship between the mind and the body. Spinoza has just argued that the "decrees" of the mind are the same as the "appetites" of the body. He now begins to distinguish between when the mind is "active" (acting from its own power) and "passive" (affected by outside forces).</meta>

# ETHICS PART III.

### PROPOSITION III.

*The actions of the **Mind** arise from adequate ideas alone; however, its passions depend on inadequate ideas alone.* <note>Spinoza uses the term "actions" to describe things we do because of our own nature, while "passions" (from the Latin *passio*, meaning "suffering" or "undergoing") are things that happen to us because of outside influences.</note>

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

First, that which constitutes the essence of the **Mind** is nothing other than the idea of an actually existing **Body** (*per Proposition 11 and 13, Part 2*). This idea (*per Proposition 15, Part 2*) is composed of many others, some of which (*per Corollary to Proposition 38, Part 2*) are **adequate**, while others are **inadequate** <note>An "adequate idea" is a complete, clear, and distinct understanding of a cause; an "inadequate idea" is like looking at a fragment of a picture without knowing the whole context.</note> (*per Corollary to Proposition 29, Part 2*). Therefore, whatever follows from the nature of the **Mind**, and of which the **Mind** is the proximate cause through which it must be understood, must necessarily follow from an adequate or an inadequate idea. But insofar as the **Mind** (*per Proposition 1 of this Part*) has inadequate ideas, it necessarily undergoes change (it is passive); therefore, the actions of the **Mind** follow from adequate ideas alone, and the **Mind** is passive only because it has inadequate ideas. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

->SCHOLIUM.<-

We see, therefore, that passions are not related to the **Mind** except insofar as it contains something that involves negation—that is, insofar as the **Mind** is considered a part of **Nature** which cannot be perceived clearly and distinctly on its own without the help of other parts. By this same reasoning, I could show that passions are related to individual things in the same way they are to the **Mind**, and cannot be understood in any other way; but my purpose is to treat only the human **Mind**.

### PROPOSITION IV.

*Nothing can be destroyed except by an external cause.*

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

This Proposition is self-evident; for the definition of any thing affirms the essence of that thing, but does not deny it; or, it poses the essence of the thing, but does not take it away. Therefore, while we attend only to the thing itself, and not to external causes, we will be able to find nothing in it that can destroy it. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### PROPOSITION V.

*Things are of a contrary nature—that is, they cannot exist in the same subject—insofar as one is able to destroy the other.*

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

For if they could agree with each other, or exist simultaneously in the same subject, there could then exist in that subject something that could destroy it, which (*per the preceding Proposition*) is absurd. Therefore, things, etc. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### PROPOSITION VI.

*Each thing, as far as it lies within itself, strives to persevere in its being.* <note>This "striving" is known as <term>conatus</term>, the fundamental drive of every being to keep existing and functioning.</note>

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

For individual things are modes by which the attributes of **God** are expressed in a certain and determined way (*per Corollary to Proposition 25, Part 1*); that is (*per Proposition 34, Part 1*), they are things that express the power of **God**, by which **God** is and acts, in a certain and determined way. Nor does any thing have anything in itself...

<summary>Spinoza establishes that the mind is active when it has clear (adequate) ideas and passive when it has confused (inadequate) ideas, leading into his famous "Conatus" principle: that all things naturally strive to continue existing.</summary>
<keywords>adequate ideas, passions, essence, external cause, contrary nature, conatus, perseverance in being, Baruch Spinoza</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Spinoza’s geometric deduction of human psychology. Having established that things naturally resist destruction, he now introduces the "conatus"—the fundamental drive for self-preservation—and explains how this manifests as Will and Appetite in human beings.</meta>

<page-num>71</page-num>

# ON THE AFFECTS.
<note>original: "DE AFFECTIBUS." Spinoza uses "Affects" to describe both our emotions and the physical changes in our bodies that increase or decrease our power to act.</note>

by which it could be destroyed, or which might take away its existence (*by Proposition 4 of this Part*); but on the contrary, it is opposed to everything that can take away its existence (*by the preceding Proposition*); and thus, as far as it can and as it is in itself, it strives to persevere in its being. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

## PROPOSITION VII.

*The striving* <term>conatus: the innate drive of a thing to persist and maintain itself</term> *by which each thing strives to persevere in its being is nothing but the actual essence of the thing itself.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

From the given essence of any thing, certain things follow necessarily (*by Proposition 36, Part 1*), and things can do nothing else than what follows necessarily from their determined nature (*by Proposition 29, Part 1*); therefore, the power or striving of any thing, by which it acts or strives to act—either alone or with others—that is (*by Proposition 6 of this Part*), the power or striving by which it strives to persevere in its being, is nothing but the given or actual essence of the thing itself. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

## PROPOSITION VIII.

*The striving by which each thing strives to persevere in its being involves no finite time, but an indefinite duration.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

For if it involved a limited time, which determined the duration of the thing, then from the very power by which the thing exists, it would follow that after that limited time the thing could not exist, but would have to be destroyed; but this (*by Proposition 4 of this Part*) is absurd. Therefore, the striving by which a thing exists involves no defined time; but on the contrary, since (*by the same Proposition 4 of this Part*), if it is destroyed by no external cause, it will continue to exist by the same power by which it already exists, this striving involves an indefinite duration. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

## PROPOSITION IX.

*The **Mind**, both insofar as it has clear and distinct ideas and insofar as it has confused ideas, strives to persevere in its being for an indefinite duration, and it is conscious of this striving of its own.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

The essence of the **Mind** is constituted by adequate and inadequate ideas <note>See previous page: adequate ideas are clear understandings, while inadequate ideas are partial or "confused" perceptions.</note> (*as we showed in Proposition 3 of this Part*); and thus (*by Proposition 7 of this Part*), both insofar as it has the former and insofar as it has the latter, it strives to persevere in its being; and this (*by Proposition 8 of this Part*) for an indefinite duration. But since the **Mind** (*by Proposition 23, Part 2*) is necessarily conscious of itself through the ideas of the affections of the **Body**, it follows (*by Proposition 7 of this Part*) that the **Mind** is conscious of its striving. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### SCHOLIUM.

This striving, when it is related to the **Mind** alone, is called ***Will***; but when it is related to both the **Mind** and the **Body** together, it is called ***Appetite***. This appetite is therefore nothing other than the very essence of man, from whose nature those things that serve his preservation necessarily follow; and 

<summary>Spinoza defines "conatus" as the fundamental striving for self-preservation that constitutes a thing's essence. He argues that this drive is indefinite in time and, in humans, manifests as "Will" (mental) or "Appetite" (mental and physical).</summary>

<meta>This page continues from the discussion of the "conatus" or striving for self-preservation. Spinoza has just explained that when this striving is related to the mind alone, it is called Will; when related to both mind and body, it is called Appetite. He now moves to define Desire and the primary emotions of Joy and Sadness.</meta>

<page-num>72</page-num>
<header>ETHICS PART III.</header>

...to that extent, a human being is determined to act in those ways. Furthermore, there is no difference between Appetite and Desire, except that <term>Desire: the conscious version of our natural drive</term> is generally attributed to humans insofar as they are conscious of their appetite; therefore, it can be defined as follows: *Desire is appetite together with the consciousness of it*. It is clear from all this, then, that we do not strive for, will, seek, or desire anything because we judge it to be good; but on the contrary, we judge something to be good because we strive for, will, seek, and desire it. <note>This is a revolutionary claim in philosophy: Spinoza argues that our values are not objective truths we discover, but are instead labels we give to things based on our own biological and psychological drives.</note>

## PROPOSITION X.

*An idea that excludes the existence of our Body cannot be present in our Mind, but is contrary to it.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

Whatever can destroy our Body cannot be present within it (*by Proposition 5 of this Part*), and therefore the idea of such a thing cannot be present in God insofar as He possesses the idea of our Body (*by the Corollary to Proposition 9, Part 2*); that is (*by Propositions 11 and 13, Part 2*), the idea of that thing cannot be present in our Mind. On the contrary, since (*by Propositions 11 and 13, Part 2*) the first thing that constitutes the essence of the Mind is the idea of a body actually existing, the first and principal striving of our Mind is (*by Proposition 7 of this Part*) to affirm the existence of our Body; and thus, an idea that denies the existence of our Body is contrary to our Mind. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

## PROPOSITION XI.

*Whatever increases or diminishes, aids or restrains our Body's power of acting, the idea of that same thing increases or diminishes, aids or restrains our Mind's power of thinking.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

This Proposition is evident from Proposition 7 of Part 2, or even from Proposition 14 of Part 2.

### SCHOLIUM.

We have seen, then, that the Mind can undergo great changes, passing now to a greater and now to a lesser perfection. These passions explain for us the affects of <term>Joy</term> <note>original: "Lætitia"</note> and <term>Sadness</term> <note>original: "Tristitia"</note>. By <term>Joy</term>, therefore, I shall understand in what follows the passion *by which the Mind passes to a greater perfection*. By <term>Sadness</term>, however, I shall understand the passion *by which it passes to a lesser perfection*. Furthermore, when the affect of <term>Joy</term> is related to both the Mind and the Body at once, I call it <term>Titillation</term> <note>original: "Titillatio"; a localized pleasure</note> or <term>Cheerfulness</term> <note>original: "Hilaritas"</note>; but <term>Sadness</term> I call <term>Pain</term> <note>original: "Dolor"</note> or <term>Melancholy</term> <note>original: "Melancholia"</note>. 

But it should be noted that <term>Titillation</term> and <term>Pain</term> are attributed to a person when one part of them is affected more than the rest; whereas <term>Cheerfulness</term> and <term>Melancholy</term> are used when all parts are affected equally. What <term>Desire</term> is, I have already explained in the Scholium to Proposition 9 of this Part; and besides these three <note>Desire, Joy, and Sadness</note>, I recognize no other primary affect ¹); I shall show in what follows that all others arise from these three. But before I proceed further, I wish to explain Proposition 10 of this Part more fully...

<summary>Spinoza defines the three primary emotions—Desire, Joy, and Sadness—as shifts in an individual's "power of acting." He argues that our concept of "good" is merely a reflection of our natural desires, rather than an inherent property of objects.</summary>

--- Page 88 ---
<meta>This page continues the demonstration of how the mind and body interact. Having established that the mind's essence is bound to the body's existence, Spinoza is now explaining how certain ideas can be "contrary" to one another based on whether they support or threaten that existence.</meta>

<page-num>73</page-num>
<header>ON THE AFFECTS.</header>
<meta>Digitized by Google watermark at bottom right</meta>

...to explain [this] Part, so that it may be more clearly understood in what way one idea is contrary to another idea.

In the Note to Proposition 17 of Part 2, we showed that the idea which constitutes the essence of the Mind involves the existence of the Body for as long as the Body itself exists. Next, from what we showed in the Corollary to Proposition 8 of Part 2 and in its Note, it follows that the present existence of our Mind depends on this alone: namely, that the Mind involves the actual existence of the Body. Finally, we showed (*see Propositions 17 and 18 of Part 2 with their Note*) that the power of the Mind, by which it imagines things and remembers them, also depends on this: that it involves the actual existence of the Body. From these things it follows that the present existence of the Mind and its power of imagining are taken away as soon as the Mind ceases to affirm the present existence of the Body. But the cause why the Mind ceases to affirm this existence of the Body cannot be the Mind itself (*by Proposition 4 of this Part*), nor even because the Body ceases to exist. For (*by Proposition 6 of Part 2*) the cause why the Mind affirms the existence of the Body is not because the Body began to exist; therefore, by the same reasoning, it does not cease to affirm the existence of the Body simply because the Body ceases to exist; but (*by Proposition 17 <gloss>1)</gloss> of Part 2*) this arises from another idea which excludes the present existence of our Body, and consequently of our Mind, and which is therefore contrary to the idea that constitutes the essence of our Mind. <note>Spinoza is arguing that the mind's natural state is to affirm the body's life. Only an external force or a "contrary idea"—like the realization of a fatal injury—can stop the mind from affirming that the body exists.</note>

### PROPOSITION XII.

*The Mind, as far as it can, strives to imagine those things that increase or assist the Body's power of acting.*

#### DEMONSTRATION.

As long as the human Body is affected in a way that involves the nature of some external body, the human Mind will regard that same body as present (*by Proposition 17 of Part 2*), and consequently (*by Proposition 7 of Part 2*), as long as the human Mind regards any external body as present—that is (*by the Note to Proposition 17 <gloss>2)</gloss> of the same Part*), imagines it—the human Body is affected in a way that involves the nature of that same external body; and so, as long as the Mind imagines those things which increase or assist our Body’s power of acting, the Body is affected in ways that increase or assist its power of acting (*see Postulate 1 of this Part*); and consequently (*by Proposition 11 of this Part*), the Mind’s power of thinking is increased or assisted; and therefore (*by Proposition 6 or 9 of this Part*), the Mind, as far as it can, strives to imagine these same things. *Which was to be demonstrated.* <note>This is the psychological basis for "positive thinking": Spinoza argues that because the mind and body are two sides of the same coin, the mind naturally seeks out thoughts that make the body more capable and active.</note>

### PROPOSITION XIII.

*When the Mind imagines those things which diminish or constrain the Body’s power of acting, it strives, as far as it can, to remember things that exclude the existence of these things.* <note>In other words, when we encounter something that makes us feel weak or sad, our mind naturally tries to find reasons or thoughts that prove that "bad thing" isn't real or isn't present.</note>

---

1) First Edition: *by Proposition 8 of Part 2.*
2) First Edition: *by the Note of the same Proposition.*

<summary>Spinoza explains that the mind naturally strives to entertain thoughts that increase the body's vitality and power, while conversely attempting to push away or disprove thoughts that weaken the body.</summary>
<keywords>power of acting, imagination, mind-body connection, affirmation, self-preservation, affects, psychology</keywords>
