<meta>This page continues Spinoza’s analysis of the emotions, specifically how the mind reacts to things that decrease its vitality. It transitions from the mechanics of the body and mind to the formal definitions of Love and Hate, which Spinoza identifies as psychological states linked to external causes.</meta>

<page-num>74</page-num>
<header>ETHICS PART III.</header>

### DEMONSTRATION.

As long as the Mind imagines anything of this sort, the power of the Mind and Body is diminished or restrained (<note>original: "ut in præced. Prop. demonstravimus"</note> *as we demonstrated in the preceding Proposition*); and nevertheless, the Mind will continue to imagine this until it imagines something else that excludes the present existence of the former thing (<note>original: "per Prop. 17. p. 2."</note> *according to Proposition 17 of Part 2*). This means (<note>original: "ut modo ostendimus"</note> *as we have just shown*) that the power of the Mind and Body is diminished or restrained until the Mind imagines something else which excludes the existence of that thing. Therefore, the Mind (<note>original: "per Prop. 9. hujus"</note> *according to Proposition 9 of this Part*), as much as it can, will strive to imagine or remember those things [which exclude the existence of the power-diminishing thing]. Which was to be demonstrated.

### COROLLARY.

From this it follows that the Mind is averse to imagining those things which diminish or restrain its own power and the power of the Body.

### SCHOLIUM.

From these points, we clearly understand what <term>Love</term> (<note>original: "Amor"</note>) and <term>Hate</term> (<note>original: "Odium"</note>) are. Specifically, ***Love is nothing other than Joy accompanied by the idea of an external cause;*** and ***Hate is nothing other than Sadness accompanied by the idea of an external cause.*** We see, then, that he who loves necessarily strives to have and to preserve the thing he loves present before him; and conversely, he who hates strives to remove and destroy the thing he hates. But we shall speak of all these things more at length in what follows.

## PROPOSITION XIV.

->*If the Mind has once been affected by two <term>affects</term> (<note>Spinoza uses "affects" to mean changes or modifications in the body's power to act, and the corresponding ideas of those changes.</note>) at the same time, when it is later affected by one of them, it will also be affected by the other.*<-

### DEMONSTRATION.

If the human Body has been affected by two bodies at the same time, when the Mind later imagines one of them, it will immediately remember the other as well (<note>original: "per Prop. 18. p. 2."</note> *according to Proposition 18 of Part 2*). But the imaginations of the Mind indicate the affects of our Body more than the nature of external bodies (<note>original: "per Coroll. 2. Prop. 16. p. 2."</note> *according to Corollary 2 of Proposition 16 in Part 2*). Therefore, if the Body—and consequently the Mind (<note>original: "vid. Defin. 3. hujus"</note> *see Definition 3 of this Part*)—was once affected by two affects at the same time, when it is later affected by one of them, it will also be affected by the other. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

## PROPOSITION XV.

->*Anything can be the cause of <term>Joy</term> (<note>Lætitia: a transition to a greater perfection or power.</note>), <term>Sadness</term> (<note>Tristitia: a transition to a lesser perfection or power.</note>), or <term>Desire</term> (<note>Cupiditas</note>) by accident.*<-

### DEMONSTRATION.

Suppose the Mind is affected by two affects at the same time: one which neither increases nor diminishes its power of acting, and another which either increases or diminishes it (<note>original: "vide Post. 1. hujus"</note> *see Postulate 1 of this Part*). From the preceding Proposition, it is clear that when the Mind is later affected by the first affect—arising from its true cause, which (by hypothesis) by itself neither increases nor diminishes its power of thinking—it will immediately be affected by the second one as well, which does increase or diminish its power of thinking. That is (<note>original: "per Schol. Prop. 11. hujus"</note> *according to the Note to Proposition 11 of this Part*), it will be affected by Joy or Sadness. And thus, that thing will be the cause of Joy or Sadness, not through itself, but <term>by accident</term> (<note>original: "per accidens"; meaning incidentally or by association rather than by its own inherent nature.</note>). By this same path, it can easily be shown that a thing can be the cause of Desire by accident. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

<summary>This page defines Love and Hate as Joy or Sadness linked to an external cause and introduces the principle of association, explaining how neutral things can trigger emotions if they were previously experienced alongside something significant.</summary>
<keywords>Love, Hate, Joy, Sadness, Desire, Association of Ideas, Accidental Cause, Power of the Mind, Spinoza, Ethics</keywords>

--- Page 90 ---
<meta>This page continues Part III of the Ethics, moving from the definitions of Love and Hate into the "laws of association" that govern our feelings. Spinoza is explaining how the mind links emotions to objects not because of the objects themselves, but because of memory, similarity, or coincidence.</meta>

<page-num>75</page-num>
<header>ON THE EMOTIONS.</header>

->COROLLARY.<-

From the mere fact that we have contemplated a thing with an emotion of Joy or Sadness, even if that thing is not the <term>efficient cause</term> (<note>The direct, acting cause that produces an effect.</note>) of the emotion, we can still come to love or hate it.

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

This happens simply because (<note>original: "per Prop. 14. hujus"</note> *according to Proposition 14 of this Part*) the Mind, in imagining this thing later, is affected by the emotion of Joy or Sadness. This means (<note>original: "per Schol. Prop. 11. hujus"</note> *according to the Note to Proposition 11 of this Part*) that the power of the Mind and Body is increased or diminished, and so on. Consequently (<note>original: "per Prop. 12. hujus"</note> *according to Proposition 12 of this Part*), the Mind will desire to imagine the thing or (<note>original: "per Coroll. Prop. 13. hujus"</note> *according to the Corollary to Proposition 13 of this Part*) feel an aversion to it; which is to say (<note>original: "per Schol. Prop. 13. hujus"</note> *according to the Note to Proposition 13 of this Part*), it will love or hate the thing. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

->SCHOLIUM.<-

From this we understand how it is possible for us to love or hate certain things without any cause known to us, but only out of what are called <term>Sympathy</term> (<note>original: "Sympathia"</note>) and <term>Antipathy</term> (<note>original: "Antipathia"</note>). To this we should also refer those objects which affect us with Joy or Sadness simply because they have some similarity to objects that usually affect us with those same emotions, as I will show in the following Proposition. I am well aware that the authors who first introduced these names, Sympathy and Antipathy, intended them to signify certain "hidden qualities" of things; nevertheless, I believe we are permitted to understand them through known or manifest qualities as well.

## PROPOSITION XVI.

*From the mere fact that we imagine a thing to have something similar to an object that usually affects the Mind with Joy or Sadness, we will love or hate that thing, even if that in which the thing is similar to the object is not the efficient cause of these emotions.*

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

We have contemplated that which is similar to the object in the object itself (<note>original: "per hypothesin"</note> *by hypothesis*) along with the emotion of Joy or Sadness; and therefore (<note>original: "per Prop. 14. hujus"</note> *according to Proposition 14 of this Part*), when the Mind is affected by the image of this similarity, it will immediately be affected by this or that emotion as well. Consequently, the thing which we perceive to have this same quality will be (<note>original: "per Prop. 15. hujus"</note> *according to Proposition 15 of this Part*) an <term>accidental cause</term> (<note>A cause that produces an effect by chance or association rather than by its own nature.</note>) of Joy or Sadness. And so (<note>original: "per præced. Coroll."</note> *according to the preceding Corollary*), even though that in which it is similar to the object is not the efficient cause of these emotions, we will nonetheless love or hate it. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

## PROPOSITION XVII.

*If we imagine that a thing <insert>1)</insert> which usually affects us with the emotion of Sadness has something similar to another thing which usually affects us with an equally great emotion of Joy, we will hate it and love it at the same time.*

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

For this thing is (<note>original: "per hypothesin"</note> *by hypothesis*) a cause of Sadness in its own right, and (<note>original: "per Schol. Prop. 13. hujus"</note> *according to the Note to Proposition 13 of this Part*), insofar as we imagine it with this emotion,

<summary>Spinoza explains how emotions like love and hate can be triggered by mere association or similarity, giving rise to the concepts of sympathy and antipathy. He introduces the idea of emotional ambivalence, where a single object can trigger both love and hate simultaneously due to conflicting associations.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Part III of the Ethics, concluding the explanation of how we can feel conflicting emotions (love and hate) toward the same object. It then introduces the concept of how time—past, present, or future—affects our emotional state, arguing that the mind fundamentally perceives all images with equal intensity regardless of when they occur.</meta>

<page-num>76</page-num>
<header>ETHICS PART III.</header>

...we hate that same thing; and insofar as we also imagine it to have something similar to another thing that usually affects us with an equally great emotion of Joy, we will love it with an equally great striving for Joy (<note>original: "per Prop. praec."</note> *according to the preceding Proposition*); and thus we will hate it and love it at the same time. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

## SCHOLIUM.

This state of the Mind, which arises from two contrary emotions, is called <term>Vacillation of the Mind</term> (<note>original: "Animi Fluctuatio"</note>). It relates to emotion in the same way that doubt relates to the imagination (<note>original: "vid. Schol. Prop. 44. p. 2."</note> *see the Note to Proposition 44, Part 2*); indeed, Vacillation of the Mind and doubt only differ from each other by degree. 

But it should be noted that in the preceding Proposition I derived these vacillations from causes which are the cause of one emotion in themselves, and of the other by accident. I did this because they could be more easily derived from what came before; not because I deny that vacillations often arise from an object which is the direct cause of both emotions. For the human Body (<note>original: "per Post. 1. p. 2."</note> *according to Postulate 1, Part 2*) is composed of many <term>individual components</term> (<note>original: "individuis"</note>) of different natures, and thus (<note>original: "per Ax. 1. post Lem. 3."</note> *according to Axiom 1 after Lemma 3, which you may see after Proposition 13, Part 2*) it can be affected by one and the same body in many different ways; and conversely, because one and the same thing can be affected in many ways, it can also affect one and the same part of the Body in many different ways. From this we can easily conceive that one and the same object can be the cause of many contrary emotions.

# PROPOSITION XVIII.

*A person is affected by the image of a past or future thing with the same emotion of Joy or Sadness as they would be by the image of a present thing.*

## DEMONSTRATIO.

As long as a person is affected by the image of a thing, they will contemplate the thing as present, even if it does not exist (<note>original: "per Prop. 17. p. 2. cum ejusdem Coroll."</note> *according to Proposition 17, Part 2, along with its Corollary*). They do not imagine it as past or future except insofar as its image is joined to the image of past or future time (<note>original: "vid. Schol. Prop. 44. p. 2."</note> *see the Note to Proposition 44, Part 2*). Therefore, the image of a thing, considered in itself, is the same whether it refers to future, past, or present time; that is (<note>original: "per Coroll. 2. Prop. 16. p. 2."</note> *according to Corollary 2 of Proposition 16, Part 2*), the state of the Body, or its emotion, is the same whether the image is of a past, future, or present thing. Consequently, the emotion of Joy or Sadness is the same whether the image is of a past, future, or present thing. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

## SCHOLIUM I.

I call a thing past or future here insofar as we have been affected or will be affected by it. For example, insofar as we have seen or will see it, it has refreshed or will refresh us, it has harmed or will harm us, and so on. Insofar as we imagine it in this way, we are affirming its existence; that is, the Body is not affected by any emotion that excludes the existence of the thing, and therefore (<note>original: "per Prop. 17. p. 2."</note> *according to Proposition 17, Part 2*) the Body is affected by the image of that thing in the same way as if the thing itself were present. However, because it often happens that those who have more experience vacillate as long as they contemplate a thing as future or past, and regarding the outcome of the thing for the most part...

<summary>Spinoza defines 'Vacillation of the Mind' as the emotional state of experiencing conflicting feelings like love and hate simultaneously. He also demonstrates that our bodies and minds respond to memories or future expectations with the same emotional intensity as present reality.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 76 regarding the "vacillation of the mind" (emotional conflict). It transitions from explaining how we can feel both love and hate for the same object to defining specific emotions based on uncertainty—such as hope and fear—and the logic of how we react to the fate of the things we love or hate.</meta>

<page-num>77</page-num>
<header>ON THE EMOTIONS.</header>

...doubt it (<note>original: "vid. Schol. Prop. 44. p. 2."</note> *see the Note to Proposition 44, Part 2*); hence it happens that the emotions which arise from similar images of things are not very constant, but are mostly disturbed by the images of other things, until men become more certain of the outcome of the event.

### SCHOLIUM II.

From what has just been said, we understand what <term>Hope</term>, <term>Fear</term>, <term>Confidence</term>, <term>Despair</term>, <term>Gladness</term>, and <term>Remorse</term> are. For **Hope** (<note>original: "Spes"</note>) is nothing other than an inconstant **Joy**, arising from the image of a future or past thing, about whose outcome we doubt. **Fear** (<note>original: "Metus"</note>), on the other hand, is an inconstant **Sadness**, also arising from the image of a doubtful thing. Furthermore, if the doubt regarding these emotions is removed, Hope becomes **Confidence** (<note>original: "Securitas"</note>), and Fear becomes **Despair** (<note>original: "Desperatio"</note>); namely, a Joy or Sadness arising from the image of the thing we feared or hoped for. **Gladness** (<note>original: "Gaudium"</note>) is then a Joy arising from the image of a past thing, about whose outcome we had doubted ¹). Finally, **Remorse** (<note>original: "Conscientiæ morsus"</note>) is the Sadness that is the opposite of Gladness.

### PROPOSITION XIX.

*He who imagines that what he loves is being destroyed will be saddened; however, if he imagines it is being preserved, he will rejoice.*

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

The Mind, as much as it can, strives to imagine those things that increase or aid the Body's power of acting (<note>original: "per Prop. 12. hujus"</note> *according to Proposition 12 of this Part*); that is (<note>original: "per Schol. Prop. 13. hujus" ²</note> *according to the Note to Proposition 13 of this Part*), those things which it loves. But the imagination is aided by those things which posit the existence of a thing, and on the contrary, it is restrained by those things which exclude the existence of a thing (<note>original: "per Prop. 17. p. 2."</note> *according to Proposition 17, Part 2*); therefore, the images of things which posit the existence of the loved thing aid the Mind's striving by which it tries to imagine the loved thing, that is (<note>original: "per Schol. Prop. 11. hujus"</note> *according to the Note to Proposition 11 of this Part*), they affect the Mind with Joy; and those things which, on the contrary, exclude the existence of the loved thing, restrain that same striving of the Mind, that is (<note>original: "per idem Schol."</note> *according to the same Note*), they affect the Mind with Sadness. Therefore, he who imagines that what he loves is being destroyed will be saddened, etc. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### PROPOSITION XX.

*He who imagines that what he hates is being destroyed will rejoice.*

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

The Mind (<note>original: "per 13. Prop. hujus"</note> *according to Proposition 13 of this Part*) strives to imagine those things which exclude the existence of things by which the Body's power of acting is diminished or restrained; that is (<note>original: "per Schol. ejusdem Prop."</note> *according to the Note to the same Proposition*), it strives to imagine those things which exclude the existence of things it hates; and thus the image of a thing which excludes the existence of that which the Mind hates aids this striving of the Mind, that is (<note>original: "per Schol. Prop. 11. hujus"</note> *according to the Note to Proposition 11 of this Part*), it affects the Mind with Joy. Therefore, he who imagines that what he hates is being destroyed will rejoice. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### PROPOSITION XXI.

*He who imagines that what he loves is affected by Joy or Sadness, will—*

<meta>This page continues the logical progression of Spinoza’s "geometry of the emotions" from page 77. Specifically, it concludes the proof that we share the feelings of those we love and begins to explain our feelings toward third parties who affect the things we love or hate.</meta>

<page-num>78</page-num>
<header>ETHICS PART III.</header>

[will also be affected by Joy] or **Sadness**; and each of these <term>affects</term> <note>original: "affectus"; Spinoza uses this term to describe emotions as "states" or "modifications" of the mind and body.</note> will be greater or lesser in the lover, just as each is greater or lesser in the thing loved.

### DEMONSTRATION.

Images of things (as we demonstrated in *Proposition 19 of this part*) which affirm the existence of the thing loved, assist the Mind’s <term>striving</term> <note>original: "conatus"; the inner drive or effort of any thing to persist in its own being.</note> by which it tries to imagine the loved thing itself. But **Joy** <note>original: "Lætitia"</note> affirms the existence of the joyful thing, and more so as the affect of Joy is greater: for it is (according to the *Note to Proposition 11 of this part*) a transition to a greater perfection. Therefore, the image of Joy in the thing loved assists the Mind’s striving in the lover; that is (according to the *Note to Proposition 11 of this part*), it affects the lover with Joy, and the greater the Joy, the greater this affect will have been in the thing loved. This was the first point. Next, insofar as any thing is affected by **Sadness**, it is to that extent destroyed, and more so as it is affected by a greater Sadness (by the same *Note to Proposition 11*); and so (by *Proposition 19 of this part*) one who imagines that which they love to be affected by Sadness, will also be affected by Sadness, and the greater the Sadness, the greater this affect will have been in the thing loved. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

## PROPOSITION XXII.

*If we imagine someone giving Joy to a thing we love, we will be affected by Love toward them. If, on the contrary, we imagine them giving Sadness to that same thing, we will conversely be affected by Hate toward them.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

He who gives Joy or Sadness to the thing we love, also affects us with Joy or Sadness, provided that we imagine the loved thing to be affected by that Joy or Sadness (by the *preceding Proposition*). But this Joy or Sadness is supposed to occur in us accompanied by the idea of an external cause; therefore (by the *Note to Proposition 13 of this part*), if we imagine someone giving Joy or Sadness to a thing we love, we will be affected by Love or Hate toward that person. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### SCHOLIUM.

Proposition 21 explains to us what <term>Pity</term> <note>original: "Commiseratio"</note> is, which we can define as a Sadness arising from another's harm. By what name we should call the Joy that arises from another's good, I do not know. Furthermore, we shall call **Love toward one who has done well for another, Approval** <note>original: "Favorem"</note>, and conversely, **Hate toward one who has done evil to another, Indignation** <note>original: "Indignationem"</note>. Finally, it should be noted that we do not only feel pity for a thing we have loved (as we showed in *Proposition 21*), but also for that which we have previously regarded with no emotion, provided we judge it to be similar to ourselves (as I shall show below); and so we also feel approval toward someone who has done well for a similar being, and conversely, feel indignation toward one who has brought harm to a similar being.

## PROPOSITION XXIII.

*One who imagines that which they hate to be affected by Sadness will rejoice; if, on the contrary, they imagine it to be affected by Joy, they will be saddened; and each of these affects will be greater or lesser, as its contrary is greater or lesser in that which they hate.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

Insofar as a hated thing is affected by Sadness, it is to that extent destroyed, and the more so,

Therefore, one who <note>original: "per Prop. 20. hujus"; according to Proposition 20 of this part.</note> imagines the thing they hate to be affected by **Sadness**, will conversely be affected by **Joy**; and the greater the **Sadness** they imagine the hated thing to be affected by, the greater that **Joy** will be. ### NOTE.

This **Joy** can hardly be solid and without any mental conflict. For (as I will show immediately in Proposition 27 of this part) insofar as one imagines a thing similar to oneself to be affected by an <term>affect</term> <note>original: "affectu"; Spinoza uses this to mean a state of being or emotion that increases or decreases our power.</note> of **Sadness**, one must to that extent feel sad; and conversely, if one imagines the same thing to be affected by **Joy**. But here we are attending only to **Hatred**.

### PROPOSITION XXIV.

*If we imagine someone to affect with **Joy** a thing we hate, we will also be affected by **Hatred** toward them. If, on the contrary, we imagine them to affect the same thing with **Sadness**, we will be affected by **Love** toward them.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

This Proposition is demonstrated in the same way as Proposition 22 of this part, which you may see.

### NOTE.

These and similar affects of **Hatred** are referred to as **Envy**, which is therefore nothing other than ***Hatred* itself, insofar as it is considered to dispose a person in such a way that they rejoice in the misfortune of another and, conversely, feel sadness at another's good fortune.**

### PROPOSITION XXV.

*We strive to affirm everything about ourselves and the thing we love which we imagine affects us or the loved thing with **Joy**; and conversely, we strive to deny everything that we imagine affects us or the loved thing with **Sadness**.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

That which we imagine affects the loved thing with **Joy** or **Sadness**, affects us with **Joy** or **Sadness** <note>original: "per Prop. 21. hujus"; according to Proposition 21 of this part.</note>. But the Mind <note>original: "per Prop. 12. hujus"; according to Proposition 12 of this part.</note> strives as much as it can to imagine those things that affect us with **Joy**, which is to say <note>original: "per Prop. 17. p. 2. et ejus Coroll."; according to Proposition 17 of Part 2 and its Corollary.</note> to contemplate them as present; and conversely <note>original: "per Prop. 13. hujus"; according to Proposition 13 of this part.</note>, it strives to exclude the existence of those things which affect us with **Sadness**; therefore, we strive to affirm everything about ourselves and the loved thing that we imagine affects us or the loved thing with **Joy**, and vice versa. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### PROPOSITION XXVI.

*We strive to affirm everything about a thing we hate which we imagine affects it with **Sadness**, and conversely to deny everything which we imagine affects it with **Joy**.*

<meta>This page continues from the discussion of how we are affected by the joys and sorrows of those we love or hate. It transitions from how we perceive our own value and the value of others into the fundamental principle of empathy—the "imitation of affects"—whereby we naturally mirror the emotions of those we perceive as similar to ourselves.</meta>

<page-num>80</page-num>
<header>PART III OF THE ETHICS</header>

#### DEMONSTRATION.

This Proposition follows from Proposition 23 <note>original: "ex Propositione 23."</note>, just as the preceding one followed from Proposition 21 of this part.

#### NOTE.

From these points, we see how easily it happens that a person thinks more highly of themselves and of the thing they love than is just, and conversely, thinks less of a thing they hate than is just. When this imagination concerns the person themselves who thinks more of themselves than is fair, it is called **Pride** <note>original: "Superbia"</note>. It is a species of **Delirium** <note>original: "Delirii"</note>, because the person dreams with their eyes open, imagining they can do all those things which they achieve only in their imagination. They regard these fantasies as real and exult in them, so long as they cannot imagine those things which would exclude the existence of these fantasies and which would determine the person's actual <term>power of acting</term> <note>original: "agendi potentiam"; for Spinoza, our ability to exist and cause effects.</note>. Therefore, **Pride** is *Joy arising from the fact that a person thinks more of themselves than is just.* Furthermore, the **Joy** that arises from thinking more of another than is just is called **Overestimation** <note>original: "Existimatio"</note>; and finally, that which arises from thinking less of another than is just is called **Disdain** <note>original: "Despectus"</note>.

### PROPOSITION XXVII.

*If we imagine a thing similar to ourselves—and toward which we have felt no emotion—to be affected by some <term>affect</term>, we are by that very fact affected by a similar emotion.* <note>original: "affectu affici... simili affectu afficimur"; this is the basis of Spinoza's theory of empathy.</note>

#### DEMONSTRATION.

The images of things are <term>affections</term> <note>original: "affectiones"; physical impressions or states</note> of the human Body, the ideas of which represent external bodies as present to us <note>original: "per Schol. Prop. 17. p. 2."; according to the Note to Proposition 17, Part 2.</note>; that is <note>original: "per Prop. 16. p. 2."; according to Proposition 16, Part 2.</note>, their ideas involve the nature of our own Body and, at the same time, the present nature of the external body. If, therefore, the nature of the external body is similar to the nature of our own Body, then the idea of the external body we imagine will involve an affection of our own Body similar to the affection of the external body. Consequently, if we imagine someone similar to ourselves to be affected by some emotion, this imagination will express an affection of our own Body similar to that emotion. Therefore, from the fact that we imagine a thing similar to ourselves to be affected by an emotion, we are affected by a similar emotion along with it. But if we should hate a thing similar to ourselves, we will—to that extent—be affected by an emotion contrary to it, rather than a similar one <note>original: "per Prop. 23. hujus"; according to Proposition 23 of this part.</note>. *Which was to be demonstrated.* <note>original: "Q. E. D."</note>

#### NOTE.

This **imitation of affects** <note>original: "affectuum imitatio"</note>, when it relates to **Sadness**, is called **Compassion** <note>original: "Commiseratio"; often translated as Pity (see Note to Prop. 22 of this part)</note>. However, when it relates to **Desire**, it is called **Emulation** <note>original: "Æmulatio"</note>, which is nothing other than the **Desire** for something generated in us because we imagine others similar to ourselves to have that same **Desire**.

### COROLLARY I.

If we imagine someone toward whom we have felt no emotion to affect a thing similar to ourselves with **Joy**, we shall be affected by **Love** toward that person. If, on the contrary, we imagine them to affect the same thing with **Sadness**, we shall be affected by **Hate** toward them.

#### DEMONSTRATION.

This is demonstrated from the preceding Proposition in the same way that Proposition 22 of this part was demonstrated from Proposition 21.

<summary>Spinoza defines pride, overestimation, and disdain as distortions of value, and introduces the "imitation of affects," explaining how we naturally mirror the emotions and desires of those we perceive as similar to ourselves.</summary>
<keywords>Pride, Overestimation, Disdain, Empathy, Imitation of Affects, Compassion, Emulation, Psychology, Human Nature</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the deductions from Proposition 27, which established the "imitation of affects"—our tendency to feel what others feel. Here, Spinoza explores the practical consequences of pity and how our pursuit of joy drives our social actions.</meta>

<page-num>81</page-num>
<header>ON THE EMOTIONS.</header>

->SECOND COROLLARY.<-

**We cannot hate a thing that we pity, specifically because its misery affects us with Sadness.**

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

If we could hate that thing for that reason, then (according to Proposition 23 of this Part <note>original: "per Prop. 23. hujus"</note>) we would feel joy from its sadness; however, this contradicts our starting premise <note>original: "hypothesin"</note>, which is that we are feeling pity.

->THIRD COROLLARY.<-

**We will strive, as far as we are able, to free the thing we pity from its misery.**

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

Whatever affects the thing we pity with **Sadness** <note>original: "Tristitia"</note> affects us with a similar **Sadness** as well (according to the preceding Proposition). Therefore, we will strive to devise anything that removes the existence of that thing, or which destroys it (according to Proposition 13 of this Part); that is (according to the Note to Proposition 9 of this Part), we will have an <term>appetite</term> <note>original: "appetemus"; a natural, often unconscious drive toward an object.</note> to destroy it, or we will be determined toward its destruction. Consequently, we will strive to free the thing we pity from its misery. *Which was to be demonstrated.* <note>original: "Q. E. D."</note>

->NOTE.<-

This will or appetite for doing good, which arises from our pity for the thing upon which we wish to confer a benefit, is called **Benevolence** <note>original: "Benevolentia"</note>. This is, therefore, nothing other than **Desire arising from pity**. Regarding the **Love** and **Hate** we feel toward someone who has done well or poorly toward a thing we imagine to be similar to ourselves, see the Note to Proposition 22 of this Part.

->PROPOSITION XXVIII.<-

*Everything that we imagine to lead to Joy, we strive to promote so that it may happen; but whatever we imagine to oppose Joy, or to lead to Sadness, we strive to remove or destroy.*

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

We strive, as much as we can, to imagine that which we believe leads to **Joy** <note>original: "Lætitiam"</note> (according to Proposition 12 of this Part). This means (according to Proposition 17 of Part 2) we will strive as much as possible to contemplate it as present or as actually existing. But the Mind's effort <note>original: "conatus"</note> or power in thinking is, by nature, equal to and simultaneous with the Body's effort or power in acting (as clearly follows from the Corollaries to Proposition 7 and Proposition 11 of Part 2). Therefore, we strive absolutely for that thing to exist, or (which is the same thing, according to the Note to Proposition 9 of this Part) we desire it and intend it. This was the first point. 

Next, if we imagine that which we believe to be the cause of **Sadness**—that is (according to the Note to Proposition 13 of this Part), that which we hate—is being destroyed, we will rejoice (according to Proposition 20 of this Part). Therefore (according to the first part of this proof), we will strive to destroy it, or (according to Proposition 13 of this Part) to remove it from us, so that we do not contemplate it as present. This was the second point. Therefore, everything that leads to **Joy**, etc. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

->PROPOSITION XXIX.<-

*We shall also strive to do everything that we imagine* ¹) *men look upon with Joy...*

<header>ETHICS PART III.</header>

*...we imagine they look upon with joy, and on the contrary, we will be averse to doing that which we imagine men are averse to.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

From the fact that we imagine men love or hate something, we will love or hate the same thing (according to Proposition 27 of this Part <note>original: "per Prop. 27. hujus"</note>); that is (according to the Note to Proposition 13 of this Part), by that very fact we will feel joy or sadness at the presence of that thing; and so (according to the preceding Proposition) we will strive to do everything that we imagine men love or look upon with **Joy** <note>original: "Lætitia"</note>, and so on. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### NOTE.

This striving to do something (and also to omit doing something) solely for the sake of pleasing men is called **Ambition** <note>original: "Ambitio"</note>, especially when we strive so eagerly to please the common people that we do or omit certain things to our own or another's detriment; otherwise, it is usually called **Humanity** <note>original: "Humanitas"; here meaning courtesy or social politeness</note>. Next, the **Joy** with which we imagine the action of another, by which he has tried to delight us, I call **Praise** <note>original: "Laudem"</note>; whereas the **Sadness** with which we are averse to another's action, I call **Blame** <note>original: "Vituperium"</note> <margin>1) Elsewhere in the Political Treatise 2. § 24.</margin>.

## PROPOSITION XXX.

*If someone has done something which they imagine affects others with Joy, they will be affected by Joy accompanied by the idea of themselves as the cause; or, they will regard themselves with Joy. If, on the contrary, they have done something which they imagine affects others with Sadness, they will regard themselves with Sadness.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

One who imagines that they affect others with Joy or Sadness will (according to Proposition 27 of this Part) be affected by Joy or Sadness themselves. However, since a human being (according to Proposition 19, Part 2) is conscious of themselves through the <term>affections</term> <note>original: "affectiones"; the changes or "modes" the body undergoes as it interacts with the world.</note> by which they are determined to act; therefore, one who has done something that they imagine affects others with Joy will be affected by Joy along with a consciousness of themselves as the cause, or will regard themselves with Joy, and vice versa. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### NOTE.

Since **Love** (according to the Note to Proposition 13 of this Part) is Joy accompanied by the idea of an external cause, and **Hate** is Sadness accompanied by the idea of an external cause, this Joy and Sadness will therefore be species of Love and Hate. But because Love and Hate are referred to external objects, we shall signify these **Affects** <note>original: "Affectus"; the emotions or states of being.</note> by other names. Namely, we shall call Joy accompanied by the idea of an external cause <margin>2) The Dutch version and Camerer read: "internal," comparing the Explanation of Emotion 24. But see above, Note 2 to Proposition 8, Part 1, for what "external cause" signifies; nothing prevents it from having its seat in the same individual as the effect.</margin> **Glory** <note>original: "Gloriam"</note>, and the Sadness contrary to it **Shame** <note>original: "Pudorem"</note>. This is to be understood when the Joy or Sadness arises from the fact that a person believes themselves to be praised or blamed. Otherwise, I shall call Joy accompanied by the idea of an external cause **Self-Contentment** <note>original: "Acquiescentiam in se ipso"; literally "resting in oneself."</note>, and the Sadness contrary to it **Repentance** <note>original: "Pœnitentiam"</note>. Then, because (according to the Corollary to Proposition 17, Part 2)...

<meta>This page continues Spinoza’s exploration of how social interaction shapes our emotions. Following his definition of Ambition as the desire to please others, he now examines how we instinctively seek to align the preferences of others with our own, leading to both social cohesion and interpersonal conflict.</meta>

<page-num>83</page-num>
<header>ON THE EMOTIONS.</header>

it can happen that the **Joy** <note>original: "Lætitia"; the increase in a person's power or well-being</note> with which someone imagines themselves affecting others is only imaginary, and (*according to Proposition 25 of this Part*) everyone strives to imagine about themselves whatever they imagine affects them with **Joy**; thus it can easily happen that a boastful man is actually proud and imagines himself to be pleasing to everyone, when in fact he is a nuisance to everyone.

## PROPOSITION XXXI.

*If we imagine that someone loves, desires, or hates something that we ourselves love, desire, or hate, we will love the thing more constantly, and so on. If, however, we imagine that they are averse to what we love, or vice versa, then we will suffer a fluctuation of the mind.*

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

From the mere fact that we imagine someone loves something, we will love that same thing (*according to Proposition 27 of this Part*). But we are already assuming that we love it without this extra cause <margin>1) The Dutch version: "we suppose that we." — Correction in the first edition: *supponimur* (we are supposed).</margin>; therefore, a new cause is added to our **Love**, by which it is nourished; and consequently, we will love what we love more constantly for this reason. Furthermore, from the fact that we imagine someone to be averse to something, we will also be averse to it (*according to the same Proposition*). But if we assume that we love that same thing at the same time, we will therefore love and be averse to the same thing at once; or (*see the Note to Proposition 17 of this Part*) we will suffer a fluctuation of the mind. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

->COROLLARY.<-

From this and from Proposition 28 of this Part, it follows that everyone strives, as much as they can, to make everyone else love what they themselves love, and hate what they themselves hate; hence that line from the Poet <note>original: "Poëtæ"; referring to Ovid</note> <margin>2) Ovid, *Amores* II. 19, with the order of the lines changed.</margin>:

> Let us as lovers hope together and fear together;
> he is made of iron who loves that which another permits.
> <note>original: "Speremus pariter, pariter metuamus amantes; / Ferreus est, si quis, quod sinit alter, amat." Spinoza is quoting Ovid to show that we want our partners/friends to share our emotional stakes.</note>

->NOTE.<-

This effort to make everyone approve of what we ourselves love or hate is truly **Ambition** <note>original: "Ambitio"; here meaning a drive for social validation or conformity</note> (*see the Note to Proposition 29 of this Part*); and so we see that everyone by nature desires that others should live according to their own temperament; and while everyone desires this equally, they are equally a hindrance to one another; and while everyone wishes to be praised or loved by everyone, they end up hating one another.

## PROPOSITION XXXII.

*If we imagine someone enjoying something that only one person can possess, we will strive to ensure that they do not possess it.*

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

From the mere fact that we imagine someone enjoying something (*according to Proposition 27 and its first Corollary*), we will love that thing and desire to enjoy it. But (*by the hypothesis*) we imagine that their enjoyment of this thing stands in the way of our own **Joy** <margin>3) Saisset and Camerer: *potiatur* (possesses).</margin>; therefore (*according to Proposition 28*), we will strive to prevent them from possessing it. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

<summary>This page explains how humans naturally seek emotional validation by trying to make others love and hate the same things they do, a drive Spinoza identifies as the root of "Ambition." He also notes that this social mirroring leads to conflict when two people desire the same exclusive object.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Spinoza’s analysis of the social nature of human emotions. On the previous page, he established that we are naturally inclined to love what others love and hate what others hate. Here, he explains the origins of pity, envy, and the desire for mutual love, leading into his famous psychological analysis of jealousy.</meta>

<page-num>84</page-num>
<header>ETHICS, PART III.</header>

# SCHOLIUM <note>original: "Scholium"; a conversational note or explanatory discussion following a formal proof.</note>

We see, therefore, that human nature is generally so constituted that people feel pity for those who are faring poorly, and envy for those who are faring well; and (*by the preceding Proposition*) they do so with a greater **Hate** <note>original: "Odio"</note> the more they love the thing which they imagine the other person to possess. We see further, from the same property of human nature from which it follows that men are compassionate, that it also follows that they are envious and ambitious.

Finally, if we wish to consult experience itself, we will find that it teaches all these things—especially if we turn our attention to the first years of our lives. For we find that children, because their bodies are constantly in a kind of equilibrium, as it were, laugh or cry for the sole reason that they see others laugh or cry. Furthermore, whatever they see others doing, they immediately desire to imitate; and finally, they desire for themselves all those things by which they imagine others are delighted. This is because the images of things, as we have said, are the very affections <note>original: "affectiones"; the way the body is "affected" or changed by an external force</note> of the human Body, or the modes <note>original: "modi"; specific states or expressions of being</note> by which the human Body is affected by external causes and disposed to do this or that.

# PROPOSITION XXXIII.

*When we love a thing similar to ourselves, we strive, as much as we can, to bring it about that it loves us in return.*

# DEMONSTRATION.

We strive, as much as we can, to imagine the thing we love above all others (*by Proposition 12 of this Part*). If, therefore, the thing is similar to ourselves, we will strive to affect it with **Joy** <note>original: "Lætitia"; the transition to a greater state of perfection or well-being</note> above all others (*by Proposition 29 of this Part*). In other words, we will strive, as much as we can, to bring it about that the loved thing is affected with **Joy** accompanied by the idea of ourselves—which is to say (*by the Scholium to Proposition 13 of this Part*), that it loves us in return. Which was to be demonstrated.

# PROPOSITION XXXIV.

*The greater the emotion with which we imagine a loved thing to be affected toward us, the more we will glory in ourselves.*

# DEMONSTRATION.

We strive (*by the preceding Proposition*), as much as we can, for the loved thing to love us in return; that is (*by the Scholium to Proposition 13 of this Part*), for the loved thing to be affected with **Joy** accompanied by the idea of ourselves. Therefore, the greater the **Joy** with which we imagine the loved thing to be affected on our account, the more this striving <note>original: "conatus"; the fundamental drive of a thing to persist in its own being</note> is aided; that is (*by Proposition 11 of this Part and its Scholium*), the greater the **Joy** with which we ourselves are affected. But since we feel joy because we have affected another person similar to ourselves with **Joy**, we then contemplate ourselves with **Joy** (*by Proposition 30 of this Part*). Therefore, the greater the emotion with which we imagine the loved thing to be affected toward us, the greater the **Joy** with which we will contemplate ourselves, or (*by the Scholium to Proposition 30 of this Part*) the more we will glory <note>original: "gloriabimur"; to feel a sense of self-esteem or pride based on the approval of others</note> in ourselves. Which was to be demonstrated.

# PROPOSITION XXXV.

*If someone imagines that the object of their love is joining another person to themselves by the same or a tighter bond of friendship than that which he alone previously enjoyed, he will be affected with Hate toward the loved thing and will envy that other person.*

<summary>Spinoza explains that human emotions like pity and envy arise from our natural tendency to imitate others. He then proves that we inherently seek mutual love and gain self-esteem (glory) from being loved, setting the stage for his definition of jealousy.</summary>

<keywords>pity, envy, ambition, imitation in children, mutual love, glory, self-esteem, conatus, psychology of love, jealousy</keywords>

--- Page 100 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page, Spinoza is exploring how our emotions are linked to the people we love and the rivals we fear. Having just discussed envy and ambition, he now moves into a psychological breakdown of jealousy, showing how it arises from a mix of love, hate, and the fear of losing a "beloved object" to someone else.</meta>

<header>ON THE AFFECTS.</header>
<page-num>85</page-num>

### PROOF.

The more one imagines that the beloved thing is moved by love toward oneself, the more one will feel **Pride** <note>original: "gloriabitur"; here meaning a sense of self-esteem or joy in being valued</note> (*by the preceding Proposition*), which is to say (*by the Scholium to Proposition 30 of this part*) one will feel **Joy** <note>original: "lætabitur"</note>; and therefore (*by Proposition 28 of this part*) one will strive, as much as possible, to imagine the beloved thing bound to oneself as closely as possible. This striving or appetite is encouraged if one imagines that someone else desires the same thing for themselves (*by Proposition 31 of this part*). But this striving or appetite is assumed to be hindered by the image of the beloved thing itself accompanied by the image of that person whom the beloved thing joins to itself; therefore (*by the Scholium to Proposition 11 of this part*) one will be affected by **Sadness** <note>original: "Tristitia"; often translated as "Pain" or "Sorrow" in Spinoza’s system</note>, accompanied by the idea of the beloved thing as the cause, and simultaneously by the image of the other person. That is (*by the Scholium to Proposition 13 of this part*), one will be affected by **Hate** toward the beloved thing, and simultaneously toward that other person (*by the Corollary to Proposition 15 of this part*), whom one will consequently envy (*by Proposition 23 of this part*) because they are delighted by the beloved thing. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### SCHOLIUM.

This **Hate** toward the beloved thing joined with **Envy** is called **Jealousy** <term>Zelotypia: Spinoza defines this not just as a feeling, but as a "fluctuation of the mind" caused by competing emotions.</term>, which is therefore nothing other than a <term>fluctuation of the mind</term> <note>an unstable state where the mind shifts rapidly between different emotions</note> arising from **Love** and **Hate** together, accompanied by the idea of another who is envied.

Furthermore, this **Hate** toward the beloved thing will be greater in proportion to the **Joy** with which the jealous person used to be affected by the reciprocal love of the beloved, and also in proportion to the affect with which they were moved toward the person they imagine the beloved is joining to themselves. For if they hated that person, they will for that very reason (*by Proposition 24 of this part*) hate the beloved thing, because they imagine the beloved is affecting with Joy that which they themselves hate; and also (*by the Corollary to Proposition 15 of this part*) from the fact that they are forced to join the image of the beloved thing to the image of the person they hate. This reasoning mostly applies to Love toward a woman; for he who imagines that the woman he loves gives herself to another will not only be saddened because his own desire is hindered, but also, because he is forced to connect the image of the beloved thing to the <note>original: "pudendis et excrementis"; Spinoza uses blunt physiological terms to describe the physical repulsion felt during sexual jealousy</note> private parts and wastes of another, he feels an aversion toward her. To this is finally added the fact that the jealous person is no longer received with the same look the beloved was accustomed to show him; for this reason also the lover is saddened, as I shall now show.

## PROPOSITION XXXVI.

*Whoever remembers a thing by which they were once delighted desires to possess it under the same circumstances as when they were first delighted by it.*

### PROOF.

Whatever a person saw simultaneously with the thing that delighted them will all (*by Proposition 15 of this part*) be an accidental cause of **Joy**; and thus (*by Proposition 28 of this part*) they will desire to possess all of it together with the thing that delighted them, or they will desire to possess the thing with all the same circumstances as when they were first delighted by it. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### COROLLARY.

Therefore, if the lover finds one of these circumstances to be missing, they will be saddened.

### PROOF.

For insofar as they find some circumstance to be missing, to that extent...

<meta>This page continues Spinoza’s geometric-style proof regarding the human emotions (affects). On the previous page, he concluded a proof about the internal conflict of jealousy. Here, he defines "Longing," establishes that the intensity of our desires is proportional to the intensity of our feelings, and begins a provocative argument about why we hate former loved ones more intensely than people we never loved at all.</meta>

<header>ETHICS PART III.</header>
<page-num>86</page-num>

...imagines something that excludes the existence of that thing. However, since they are full of desire for that thing or circumstance (*by the preceding Proposition*) because of **Love**, therefore (*by Proposition 19 of this Part*), to the extent that they imagine the same thing to be lacking, they will feel **Sadness** <note>original: "Tristitia"; often translated as "Pain" or "Sorrow" in Spinoza’s system, representing a decrease in a person's power</note>. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

->S C H O L I U M.<-

This **Sadness**, insofar as it regards the absence of what we love, is called *Longing* <note>original: "Desiderium"; Spinoza uses this specific term for the sadness felt when a beloved object is missing</note>.

### P R O P O S I T I O N   XXXVII.

*The **Desire** <note>original: "Cupiditas"</note> that arises from **Sadness** or **Joy**, and from **Hate** or **Love**, is greater as the affect itself is greater.*

->P R O O F.<-

**Sadness** diminishes or restrains a person’s power of acting (*by the Scholium to Proposition 11 of this Part*); that is (*by Proposition 7 of this Part*), it diminishes or restrains the <term>effort|conatus; the innate drive of every being to persist in its own existence</term> by which a person strives to persevere in their own being. Therefore (*by Proposition 5 of this Part*), **Sadness** is contrary to this effort; and whatever a person affected by **Sadness** strives for, it is to remove that **Sadness**. But (*by the definition of Sadness*), the greater the **Sadness**, the greater the part of the person’s power of acting it must necessarily oppose; therefore, the greater the **Sadness**, the greater the power of acting with which the person will strive to remove that **Sadness**. This means (*by the Scholium to Proposition 9 of this Part*) that they will strive to remove the **Sadness** with a greater **Desire** or appetite.

Next, since **Joy** (*by the same Scholium to Proposition 11 of this Part*) increases or assists a person’s power of acting, it is easily demonstrated by the same path that a person affected by **Joy** desires nothing other than to preserve it, and does so with a **Desire** that is greater as the **Joy** itself is greater.

Finally, since **Hate** and **Love** are themselves affects of **Sadness** or **Joy**, it follows in the same way that the effort, appetite, or **Desire** which arises from **Hate** or **Love** will be greater in proportion to that **Hate** and **Love**. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### P R O P O S I T I O N   XXXVIII.

*If someone begins to hate a beloved thing, so that their **Love** is entirely destroyed, they will regard it with greater **Hate**, for the same cause, than if they had never loved it; and this Hate will be greater the greater the Love was before.*

->P R O O F.<-

For if someone begins to hate the thing they love, more of their appetites are restrained than if they had not loved it. For **Love** is **Joy** (*by the Scholium to Proposition 13 of this Part*) which a person, as much as they can (*by Proposition 28 of this Part*), strives to preserve; they do this (*by the same Scholium*) by contemplating the beloved thing as present, and (*by Proposition 21 of this Part*) by affecting the beloved thing with **Joy** as much as possible. This effort (*by the preceding Proposition*) is greater as the **Love** is greater, as is the effort to bring it about that the beloved thing loves them in return (*see Proposition 33 of this Part*). 

But these efforts are restrained by **Hate** toward the beloved thing (*by the Corollary to Proposition 13 and by Proposition 23 of this Part*); therefore the lover (*by the Scholium to Proposition 11 of this Part*) will also be affected by **Sadness** for this reason, and it will be greater as the **Love** was greater. That is, besides the **Sadness** which was the cause of the **Hate**, another **Sadness** arises from the fact that they loved the thing; and consequently, they will regard the beloved thing with a greater affect of **Sadness**...

<summary>This page explains that the intensity of our desires is directly proportional to the strength of the emotions (Joy, Sadness, Love, Hate) triggering them. Spinoza also begins to prove that hating someone we once loved is more painful and intense than hating a stranger, because the loss of the previous joy adds an extra layer of sadness.</summary>
<keywords>Desire, Sadness, Joy, Love, Hate, Longing, power of acting, effort, conatus, psychology</keywords>

--- Page 102 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical progression of Part III of Spinoza’s Ethics. On the previous page, Spinoza argued that longing is a form of sadness and that our desires are scaled to our emotions. On this page, he moves into the social and moral consequences of these emotions: how hatred leads us to seek another's harm, and how we subjectively define "good" and "evil" based on our own personal desires.</meta>

<header>ON THE AFFECTS.</header>

...will contemplate it; that is (by the *Scholium to Proposition 13 of this Part*) they will pursue it with a greater **Hatred** <note>original: "Odio"</note> than if they had not loved that same thing, and with a hatred all the greater as the **Love** had previously been greater. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

## PROPOSITION XXXIX.

*Whoever hates someone will strive to inflict harm on them, unless they fear that a greater harm to themselves will arise from it; and conversely, whoever loves someone will strive, by the same law, to benefit them.*

### PROOF.

To hate someone is (by the *Scholium to Proposition 13 of this Part*) to imagine someone as a cause of **Sadness**; and so (by *Proposition 28 of this Part*) one who hates someone will strive to remove or destroy them. But if from this action they fear something sadder, or (what is the same thing) a greater harm to themselves, and they believe they can avoid this by not inflicting the harm they intended upon the one they hate, then they will desire to abstain from inflicting that harm (by the same *Proposition 28*). They will do so (by *Proposition 37 of this Part*) with a greater effort than the one by which they were held to inflict the harm; and therefore this second impulse will prevail, just as we intended to show. The proof of the second part proceeds in the same way. Therefore, whoever hates someone, etc. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### SCHOLIUM.

By **Good** <note>original: "Bonum"</note> here, I understand every kind of **Joy** <note>original: "Laetitiae"</note>, and whatever leads to it—and especially that which satisfies a <term>longing|desiderio; a specific type of craving or missing for something absent</term>, of whatever kind that may be. By **Evil** <note>original: "Malum"</note>, however, I understand every kind of **Sadness**, and especially that which frustrates a longing. For we showed above (in the *Scholium to Proposition 9 of this Part*) that we do not desire anything because we judge it to be good; but on the contrary, we call that thing "good" which we desire, and consequently, we call that which we turn away from "evil."

Therefore, every person judges or estimates according to their own **Affect** <note>original: "affectu"; a state of being or emotion that increases or decreases one's power</note> what is good, what is evil, what is better, what is worse, and finally what is best or what is worst. Thus, the Miser judges an abundance of money to be the best thing, and its lack to be the worst. The Ambitious person desires nothing so much as Glory, and conversely, fears nothing so much as **Shame** <note>original: "Pudorem"</note>. To the Envious person, nothing is more pleasant than the unhappiness of another, and nothing more troublesome than another’s happiness. And so, everyone judges a thing to be good or evil, useful or useless, based on their own emotion.

Furthermore, this affect by which a person is so disposed that they do not want what they want, or want what they do not want, is called **Fear** <note>original: "Timor"</note>. Fear is therefore nothing other than **Dread** <note>original: "Metus"</note>, insofar as a person is disposed by it to avoid a harm they judge to be *future* by enduring a smaller harm (*See Proposition 28 of this Part*). But if the evil they fear is **Shame**, then the fear is called **Modesty** <note>original: "Verecundia"</note>. Finally, if the **Desire** to avoid a *future* evil is restrained by the fear of another evil, such that the person does not know which they would prefer, then that dread is called **Consternation** <note>original: "Consternatio"</note>, especially if both the evils they fear are among the greatest.

## PROPOSITION XL.

*Whoever imagines they are hated by someone, and believes they have given that person no cause for hatred, will hate that person in return.*

<summary>Spinoza defines "Good" and "Evil" as subjective labels based on personal desire and emotion, and explores how fear and hatred drive human interactions and self-preservation.</summary>
<keywords>subjective value, good and evil, fear, shame, consternation, hatred, Spinoza, Ethics, psychology of emotion</keywords>

--- Page 103 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical progression of Part III of Spinoza’s Ethics, specifically focusing on the reciprocity of emotions—how we react when we perceive others’ feelings toward us. It concludes the proof of Proposition 40 (on reciprocating hatred) and introduces Proposition 41 (on reciprocating love).</meta>

<header>ETHICS, PART III.</header>

#### PROOF.

Anyone who imagines that someone is affected by hatred toward them will, by 그 very fact, also be affected by hatred (*by Proposition 27 of this Part*); that is (*by the Scholium to Proposition 13 of this Part*), they will feel a **Sadness** <note>original: "Tristitia"</note> accompanied by the idea of an external cause. But (*by the hypothesis*) they imagine no cause for this Sadness other than the person who hates them; therefore, from the fact that they imagine themselves to be hated by someone, they will be affected by a Sadness accompanied by the idea of the one who hates them—or (*by the same Scholium*) they will hate that person in return. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

#### SCHOLIUM.

But if they imagine they have provided a just cause for that Hatred, they will then be affected by **Shame** <note>original: "Pudore"; Spinoza defines shame as sadness accompanied by the idea of some action of our own that we imagine others find fault with</note> (*by Proposition 30 of this Part and its Scholium*). However, this (*by Proposition 25 of this Part*) rarely happens. Furthermore, this reciprocation of Hatred can also arise from the fact that Hatred is followed by an effort to inflict evil on the one who is hated (*by Proposition 39 of this Part*). Therefore, anyone who imagines they are hated by someone will imagine that person as the cause of some evil or Sadness; and thus they will be affected by Sadness, or **Fear** <note>original: "Metu"</note>, accompanied by the idea of the one who hates them as the cause. That is, they will be affected by hatred in return, as stated above.

#### COROLLARY I.

Anyone who imagines that someone they love is affected by hatred toward them will be conflicted by Hatred and Love at the same time. For insofar as they imagine themselves to be hated by that person, they are determined (*by the preceding Proposition*) to hate them in return. But (*by the hypothesis*) they nevertheless love that person; therefore, they will be conflicted by Hatred and Love at the same time.

#### COROLLARY II.

If someone imagines that an evil has been inflicted upon them out of Hatred by someone toward whom they previously felt no emotion, they will immediately strive to return that same evil to that person.

#### PROOF.

Anyone who imagines that someone is affected by Hatred toward them will hate that person in return (*by the preceding Proposition*), and (*by Proposition 26 of this Part*) will strive to devise everything that can affect that person with Sadness, and will study to inflict it upon them (*by Proposition 39 of this Part*). But (*by the hypothesis*) the first thing of this kind they imagine is the evil inflicted upon themselves; therefore, they will immediately strive to inflict that same thing upon the other person. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

#### SCHOLIUM.

The effort to inflict evil on the one we hate is called **Anger** <note>original: "Ira"</note>; while the effort to return an evil inflicted on us is called **Vengeance** <note>original: "Vindicta"</note>.

### PROPOSITION XLI.

*If someone imagines they are loved by another, and does not believe they have given any cause for it (which can happen by the Corollary to Proposition 15 and by Proposition 16 of this Part), they will love that person in return.*

#### PROOF.

This Proposition is demonstrated in the same way as the preceding one. See also its Scholium.

<summary>Spinoza explains the psychological mechanics of reciprocity, arguing that we naturally tend to hate those who hate us and love those who love us, unless we feel we deserve their dislike. He defines Anger and Vengeance as specific strivings to return harm to those we perceive as hating us.</summary>
<keywords>reciprocity of affects, hatred, love, anger, vengeance, shame, fear, Spinoza, psychological imitation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the logical progression of Spinoza’s analysis of the reciprocal nature of human emotions. Having previously discussed how we react to being hated, Spinoza now turns to the experience of being loved, the nature of gratitude, and the complexities of social interactions driven by the desire for approval.</meta>

->ON THE EMOTIONS<-

### S C H O L I U M <note>A "Scholium" is an explanatory note or commentary that follows a formal proof.</note>

But if someone believes they have provided a just cause for **Love** <note>original: "Amoris"</note>, they will feel **Glory** <note>original: "gloriabitur"; Spinoza defines "Glory" or "Pride" as Joy accompanied by the idea of some action of our own which we imagine others praise</note> (*by Proposition 30 of this Part and its Scholium*). This, indeed, happens quite frequently (*by Proposition 25 of this Part*), and we have said the opposite occurs when someone imagines they are hated by another (*see the Scholium to the preceding Proposition*). Furthermore, this reciprocal Love—and consequently (*by Proposition 39 of this Part*) the effort to do good for the one who loves us and who (*by the same Proposition 39*) tries to do good for us—is called **Favor** or **Gratitude** <note>original: "Gratia seu Gratitudo"</note>. Thus it appears that humans are far readier for **Revenge** <note>original: "Vindictam"</note> than for returning a benefit.

### C O R O L L A R I U M <note>A "Corollary" is a logical consequence that follows immediately from a proven proposition.</note>

Anyone who imagines they are loved by someone they hate will be conflicted by **Hatred** and **Love** at the same time. This is demonstrated by the same reasoning as the first Corollary of the preceding Proposition.

### S C H O L I U M.

But if Hatred prevails, they will try to inflict evil on the one by whom they are loved; this emotion is called **Cruelty** <note>original: "Crudelitas"</note>, especially if it is believed that the lover has provided no common cause for Hatred.

## P R O P O S I T I O N XLII.

*Anyone who has conferred a benefit on another, moved by Love or the hope of Glory, will be saddened if they see that benefit received with an ungrateful spirit.*

### D E M O N S T R A T I O N.

Anyone who loves something similar to themselves tries, as much as they can, to ensure they are loved by it in return (*by Proposition 33 of this Part*). Therefore, whoever has conferred a benefit on someone out of love does so with the desire to be loved in return—that is (*by Proposition 34 of this Part*), with the hope of **Glory** or (*by the Scholium to Proposition 30 of this Part*) **Joy**. And so (*by Proposition 12 of this Part*) they will try to imagine this cause of Glory as much as possible, or to contemplate it as actually existing. But (*by the hypothesis*) they imagine something else that excludes the existence of that cause; therefore (*by Proposition 19 of this Part*), they will by that very fact be saddened. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

## P R O P O S I T I O N XLIII.

*Hatred is increased by reciprocal hatred, and can, on the contrary, be extinguished by Love.*

### D E M O N S T R A T I O N.

When someone imagines that a person they hate is affected by Hatred toward them in return, a new Hatred arises (*by Proposition 40 of this Part*) while (*by the hypothesis*) the first Hatred still persists. But if, on the contrary, they imagine that person to be affected by Love toward them, then insofar as they imagine this, they contemplate themselves with Joy (*by Proposition 30 of this Part*), and to that extent (*by Proposition 29 of this Part*) they will try to please that person; that is (*by Proposition 41 <sup>1)</sup> of this Part*), they try not to hate the person and not to affect them with any **Sadness** <note>original: "Tristitia"</note>. This effort (*by Proposition 37 of this Part*) will be greater or lesser in proportion to the love felt.

<summary>Spinoza defines gratitude and cruelty, explaining how our desire for reciprocal love leads to sadness when met with ingratitude. He concludes that while hatred feeds on itself, it can be overcome by the transformative power of love.</summary>

# ETHICS, PART III.

by reason of the emotion from which it arises; and thus, if it be greater than that which arises from **Hatred** <note>original: "Odio"</note>, and by which he endeavors to affect the thing he hates with **Sadness** <note>original: "Tristitiâ"; Spinoza uses this term to denote any decrease in a person's power or vitality</note> (*by Proposition 26 of this Part*), it will prevail over it and erase the Hatred from the soul. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

## PROPOSITION XLIV.

*Hatred that is completely conquered by Love passes into Love; and for that reason, the Love is greater than if Hatred had not preceded it.*

### DEMONSTRATION

This proceeds in the same manner as Proposition 38 of this Part. For he who begins to love a thing which he used to hate, or which he used to contemplate with **Sadness**, rejoices by the very fact that he loves; and to this **Joy** <note>original: "Lætitiæ"</note> which Love involves (*see its definition in the Scholium to Proposition 13 of this Part*), there is also added that which arises from the fact that the effort to remove the Sadness which Hatred involves (*as we showed in Proposition 37 of this Part*) is thoroughly assisted, accompanied by the idea of the one he hated as the cause.

### SCHOLIUM.

Although this is the case, no one will nonetheless endeavor to hate anything, or to be affected with Sadness, in order to enjoy this greater Joy; that is, no one will desire to be harmed in the hope of recovering from the loss, nor long to be sick in the hope of convalescing. For every individual will always endeavor to preserve their own being and to remove Sadness as much as they can. If, on the contrary, it could be conceived that a man could desire to hate someone so that he might afterward be filled with a greater Love for them, then he would always desire to hate that person. For the greater the Hatred has been, the greater the Love will be; and thus he would always desire the Hatred to be more and more increased. For the same reason, a man would endeavor to be more and more sick, so that he might afterward enjoy a greater Joy from the restoration of his health; and thus he would always endeavor to be sick, which (*by Proposition 6 of this Part*) is absurd <note>Proposition 6 is Spinoza's famous "Conatus" principle: "Everything, in so far as it is in itself, endeavors to persist in its own being."</note>.

## PROPOSITION XLV.

*If anyone imagines that someone like themselves is affected by Hatred toward a thing like themselves which they love, they will hate that person.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

For the thing loved hates in return the one who hates it (*by Proposition 40 of this Part*); and thus the lover, who imagines someone to hate the thing loved, by that very fact imagines the thing loved to be affected by Hatred, that is (*by the Scholium to Proposition 13 of this Part*), by **Sadness**, and consequently (*by Proposition 21 of this Part*) he is saddened, and that with the accompanying idea of the one who hates the thing loved as the cause; that is (*by the Scholium to Proposition 13 of this Part*), he will hate that person. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

## PROPOSITION XLVI.

*If anyone has been affected with Joy or Sadness by someone from a certain* <gloss>1)</gloss> <note>The Latin text here has a correction note: the First Edition reads "cujusdam" (a certain), while the Corrigenda suggests "ejusdem" (the same). The translator follows the sense of "a certain class or nation."</note> *class or nation different from their own, accompanied by the idea of that person under the name of*

<header>ON THE EMOTIONS.</header>

*...of a universal class or nation, as a cause: he will love or hate not only that individual, but everyone of the same class or nation.* <note>Spinoza is concluding a point from the previous page: if we associate a person with a broader group, our feelings toward that person transfer to the whole group.</note>

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

The proof of this is evident from Proposition 16 of this Part.

### PROPOSITION XLVII.

***Joy** <note>original: "Lætitia"; in Spinoza's system, this is an increase in a person's power of acting</note>, which arises from the fact that we imagine a thing we hate to be destroyed or affected by some other evil, does not arise without some **Sadness** <note>original: "Tristitia"; a decrease in a person's power or vitality</note> of mind.*

->DEMONSTRATION<-

is evident from Proposition 27 of this part. For insofar as we imagine a thing like ourselves to be affected with Sadness, we are to that extent saddened. <note>Spinoza argues that because we share a common nature with other humans, we naturally "mirror" their pain, even if we think we hate them.</note>

->SCHOLIUM.<-

This Proposition can also be demonstrated from the Corollary of Proposition 17, Part 2. Whenever we remember a thing, even if it does not actually exist, we contemplate it as present, and the Body is affected in the same way; therefore, as long as the memory of the thing is strong, the human is determined to contemplate it with Sadness. This determination, while the image of the thing remains, is indeed restrained by the memory of those things which exclude its existence, but it is not removed. Thus, the person rejoices only insofar as this determination is restrained; and hence it happens that this Joy, which arises from the evil of the thing we hate, is repeated as often as we remember that thing. For, as we said, when the image of the thing is excited, because it involves the existence of the thing itself, it determines the person to contemplate the thing with the same Sadness with which he used to contemplate it when it existed. 

But because he has joined other images to the image of this thing which exclude its existence, this determination toward Sadness is immediately restrained, and the person rejoices anew, and this happens as often as this repetition occurs. And this is the same reason why people rejoice whenever they remember some past evil, and why they delight in telling of dangers from which they have been delivered. For when they imagine some danger, they contemplate it as if it were still in the future, and are determined to fear it; which determination is again restrained by the idea of freedom, which they joined to the idea of this danger when they were delivered from it, and which makes them feel secure once more; and thus they rejoice anew.

### PROPOSITION XLVIII.

***Love** and **Hatred** <note>original: "Amor et Odium"</note> toward, for example, Peter, is destroyed if the Sadness involved in the latter, or the Joy involved in the former, is joined to the idea of another cause; and each is diminished to the extent that we imagine Peter was not the sole cause of either.*

->DEMONSTRATION<-

is evident from the definition of Love and Hatred alone; see the Scholium to Proposition 13 of this Part. For Joy is called Love, and Sadness is called Hatred, toward Peter solely because Peter is considered to be the cause of this or that emotion. Therefore, if this is removed entirely or in part, the emotion toward Peter is also entirely or partially diminished. *Which was to be demonstrated.*
