<meta>This page continues Spinoza’s analysis of how the human mind attributes cause and effect to emotions. Specifically, it explores why we feel more intensely toward other humans (whom we imagine to be "free agents") than toward inanimate objects or necessary events, and how the instability of our feelings leads to superstition.</meta>

<page-num>92</page-num>
<header>ETHICS PART III.</header>

# PROPOSITION XLIX.

*Love and Hate toward a thing which we imagine to be free must, for the same cause, both be greater than toward a necessary thing.*

## DEMONSTRATION.

A thing which we imagine to be free must <note>According to Definition 7, Part 1: something is "free" if it exists and acts solely by the necessity of its own nature, rather than being forced by something else.</note> be perceived through itself without others. If, therefore, we imagine it to be the cause of **Joy** or **Sadness**, we will, by that very fact <note>according to the Note on Proposition 13 of this part</note>, love or hate it, and that Love or Hate will be the greatest <note>according to the preceding Proposition</note> that can arise from that given emotion. But if we imagine the thing that is the cause of that same emotion to be necessary, then <note>according to the same Definition 7, Part 1</note> we will imagine it to be the cause of the emotion not alone, but together with other things, and consequently <note>according to the preceding Proposition</note> the Love or Hate toward it will be less. Which was to be demonstrated.

## SCHOLIUM.

From this it follows that humans, because they think themselves to be free, hold one another in greater Love or Hate than they do other things; to this is added the "imitation of emotions," <note>Spinoza's theory that we tend to feel what we perceive others are feeling.</note> concerning which see Propositions 27, 34, 40, and 43 of this part.

# PROPOSITION L.

*Any thing whatever can be an accidental cause of Hope or Fear.*

## DEMONSTRATION.

This Proposition is demonstrated in the same way as Proposition 15 of this part, which see, together with the Note to Proposition 18 of this part.

## SCHOLIUM.

Things which are accidental causes of **Hope** or **Fear** are called good or bad omens. Furthermore, insofar as these same omens are the cause of Hope or Fear, they are to that extent <note>according to the Definition of Hope and Fear in Note 2, Proposition 18 of this part</note> causes of **Joy** or **Sadness**; consequently <note>according to the Corollary of Proposition 15</note>, we love or hate them to that extent, and <note>according to Proposition 28</note> we endeavor to use them as means to those things we hope for, or to remove them as obstacles or causes of Fear. Moreover, from Proposition 25 of this part, it follows that we are so constituted by nature that we easily believe the things we hope for, but believe with difficulty the things we fear, and that we think more or less of them than is just. From these facts have arisen the **Superstitions** by which humans everywhere are troubled. For the rest, I do not think it worth the effort to show here the fluctuations of the mind that arise from Hope and Fear; since it follows from the definition of these emotions alone that there is no Hope without Fear, nor Fear without Hope (as we shall explain more fully in its proper place). Furthermore, insofar as we hope for or fear something, we love or hate it to that same extent; and thus, whatever we have said about Love and Hate, anyone can easily apply to Hope and Fear.

# PROPOSITION LI.

*Different humans can be affected in different ways by one and the same object, and one and the same human can be affected in different ways by one and the same object at different times.*

<summary>Spinoza explains that we feel more intense love or hate toward those we consider "free" (like other humans) and describes how accidental associations lead to omens and the psychological roots of superstition.</summary>
<keywords>Freedom vs. Necessity, Love and Hate, Hope and Fear, Superstition, Omens, Imitation of Affects, Human Nature</keywords>

--- Page 108 ---
<meta>This page continues the demonstration of Proposition 51, exploring how different people—or even the same person at different times—can react differently to the same object. This leads into an analysis of human judgment and the specific emotions of repentance and self-contentment.</meta>

<page-num>93</page-num>
<header>ON THE EMOTIONS.</header>

### DEMONSTRATION.

The human body (according to *Postulate 3 of Part 2*) is affected by external bodies in very many ways. Therefore, two humans can be affected in different ways at the same time; and thus (according to *Axiom 1*, which follows *Lemma 3* after *Proposition 13 of Part 2*) they can be affected in different ways by one and the same object. Furthermore (according to the same *Postulate*), the human body can be affected now in this way, now in another; and consequently (according to the same *Axiom*) it can be affected in different ways by one and the same object at different times. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### SCHOLIUM.

We see, therefore, that it can happen that what one person loves, another hates; what one fears, another does not; and that the same person may now love what he previously hated, and now dare what he previously feared, etc. Furthermore, because everyone judges from their own <term>affect<note>Latin: "affectus." Spinoza uses this term to describe both the body's physical changes and the mind's ideas of those changes—commonly translated as "emotions" or "feelings."</note></term> what is good, what is evil, what is better, and what is worse (*see the Scholium to Proposition 39 of this part*), it follows that humans can vary as much in their judgment as in their emotions. From this it happens that when we compare people to one another, we distinguish them solely by the difference of their emotions, calling some "intrepid," others "timid," and others by yet another name. 

For example, I shall call a person *intrepid* if they despise an evil that I am accustomed to fear; and if I further notice that their <term>Desire<note>Latin: "Cupiditas." For Spinoza, desire is the very essence of a human being insofar as they are determined to act.</note></term> to do harm to someone they hate, or to do good to someone they love, is *not* restrained by the fear of an evil that usually holds me back, I shall call that person *bold*. On the other hand, a person will seem *timid* to me if they fear an evil that I am accustomed to despise; and if I further notice that their Desire is restrained by the fear of an evil that cannot hold me back, I shall say they are *fainthearted*; and thus everyone will judge. 

Finally, because of this human nature and the inconstancy of judgment—since humans often judge things solely by their emotions, and because the things they believe lead to **Joy** or **Sadness**, and which they therefore strive to promote or remove (*according to Proposition 28 of this part*), are often merely imaginary (not to mention other things we showed in Part 2 regarding the uncertainty of things)—we can easily conceive that a person can often be the cause of both their own sadness and their own joy. That is, they are affected by both **Sadness** and **Joy** accompanied by the idea of themselves as the cause. Thus we can easily understand what <term>Repentance<note>Latin: "Pœnitentia." Spinoza views repentance as a "Sadness" and therefore not a virtue, though it can lead toward a better life in those not yet ruled by reason.</note></term> is, and what <term>Self-contentment<note>Latin: "Acquiescentia in se ipso." Also translated as "Self-approval" or "Self-satisfaction." It is a joy arising from a person contemplating themselves and their power of acting.</note></term> is. 

Specifically, *Repentance is Sadness accompanied by the idea of oneself as the cause, and Self-contentment is Joy accompanied by the idea of oneself as the cause*; these emotions are extremely powerful because humans believe themselves to be free (*see Proposition 49 of this part*).

## PROPOSITION LII.

***An object which we have previously seen together with others, or which we imagine to have nothing except what is common to many things, we shall not contemplate as long as one which we imagine to have something singular.***

---

<margin>1) **N. B.** <note>Note Well</note> We showed in the Scholium to Proposition 13 [First Edition: 17. Perhaps: Corollary to Proposition 11] of Part 2 that this can happen even if the human Mind were a part of the divine intellect.</margin>
<margin>2) First Edition: *in itself.*</margin>

<summary>This page concludes Proposition 51, explaining why individuals have different emotional responses to the same objects and defining "Repentance" and "Self-contentment" as emotions where the self is viewed as the cause of joy or sadness. Proposition 52 begins an investigation into wonder and how unique objects command more attention than common ones.</summary>

<keywords>emotions, affects, judgment, intrepidity, boldness, timidity, faintheartedness, repentance, self-contentment, joy, sadness, singularity</keywords>

--- Page 109 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion on how individuals differ in their judgments and emotions. It moves into Proposition 52 and its Scholium, where Spinoza explains the mechanics of "Wonder" (Admiratio) and how our minds react to singular objects versus common ones, ultimately providing a taxonomy of complex emotions like Veneration, Devotion, and Contempt.</meta>

<page-num>94</page-num>
<header>ETHICS PART III.</header>

### DEMONSTRATION.

As soon as we imagine an object that we have seen alongside others, we immediately remember the others as well (according to *Proposition 18 of Part 2; see also its Scholium*), and thus from the contemplation of one, we immediately fall into the contemplation of another. The same principle applies to an object which we imagine to have nothing but what is common to many things. For by that very fact, we suppose that we are contemplating nothing in it which we have not seen before in other things. 

But when we suppose that we imagine something singular in an object, which we have never seen before, we are saying nothing other than that the Mind, while it contemplates that object, has no other idea in itself into which it can pass from the contemplation of that object; and therefore, the Mind is determined to contemplate that object alone. Therefore, the object, etc. <note>original: "Q. E. D." (Quod erat demonstrandum)—Which was to be demonstrated.</note>

### SCHOLIUM.

This <term>affection of the Mind<note>Latin: "affectio." A state or change in the mind.</note></term>, or this imagination of a singular thing—insofar as it alone occupies the Mind—is called **Wonder** <note>original: "Admiratio." In 17th-century philosophy, this refers to being struck by novelty or surprise, not necessarily "liking" the object.</note>. If this wonder is triggered by an object we fear, it is called **Consternation**, because the Wonder at an evil keeps a person so suspended in the sole contemplation of it that they are unable to think of other things by which they might avoid that evil. 

But if that which we wonder at is a person's wisdom, industry, or something of this sort, we call that Wonder **Veneration** <note>original: "Veneratio."</note>, because by that very act we contemplate the person as far surpassing us. Otherwise, we call it **Horror**, if we wonder at a person's anger, envy, and so forth. Furthermore, if we wonder at the wisdom or industry of a person we love, our Love will (by *Proposition 12 of this part*) be greater because of it; and this Love joined to Wonder or Veneration we call **Devotion**. 

In this same way, we can also conceive of Hate, Hope, Security, and other <term>Affects<note>Latin: "Affectus." Often translated as "emotions" or "feelings," referring to the mind's power being increased or decreased.</note></term> joined to Wonder; and thus we could derive more Affects than those usually indicated by accepted words. From this it appears that the names of the Affects have been invented more from their common usage than from an accurate knowledge of them.

Opposed to Wonder is **Contempt**, the cause of which is usually this: because we see someone wondering at, loving, or fearing something, or because something at first sight appears similar to things we wonder at, love, or fear (according to *Proposition 15 with its Corollary, and Proposition 27 of this part*), we are inclined to wonder at, love, or fear that same thing. But if, from the presence of the thing itself or a more accurate contemplation of it, we are forced to deny everything about it that could be a cause of Wonder, Love, Fear, etc., then the Mind remains determined by the presence of the thing to think more about what is *not* in the object than what *is* in it; whereas normally, the presence of an object causes one to think primarily of what is in the object. 

Furthermore, just as Devotion arises from the Wonder at a thing we love, so **Derision** <note>original: "Irrisio."</note> arises from the Contempt of a thing we hate or fear, and **Scorn** <note>original: "Dedignatio."</note> arises from the Contempt of folly, just as Veneration arises from the Wonder at wisdom. Finally, we can conceive of Love, Hope, Glory, and other Affects joined to Contempt, and from there derive yet more Affects, which we are not accustomed to distinguish from others by any singular word.

<summary>Spinoza defines Wonder as the mind's focus on a singular, novel object and explores how this state combines with love or fear to create complex emotions like Veneration, Devotion, and Contempt.</summary>
<keywords>Wonder, Admiration, Contempt, Veneration, Devotion, Consternation, Derision, Scorn, singular objects, psychology of emotion</keywords>

--- Page 110 ---
<meta>This page continues the exploration of human emotions (affects) from the previous discussion on Wonder. Here, Spinoza shifts focus to how the Mind's contemplation of its own "power of acting" leads to Joy, while the contemplation of its own weakness leads to Sadness. This provides the psychological basis for self-esteem, humility, and the desire for praise.</meta>

<page-num>95</page-num>
<header>ON THE AFFECTS.</header>

## PROPOSITION 53.

*When the Mind contemplates itself and its power of acting, it rejoices; and it does so the more, the more distinctly it imagines itself and its power of acting.*

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

A human being does not know themselves except through the <term>affections<note>Latin: "affectiones." These are changes or states of the body and the corresponding ideas in the mind.</note></term> of their own Body and the ideas of these affections (according to *Propositions 19 and 23 of Part 2*). When it happens, therefore, that the Mind can contemplate itself, it is by that very fact assumed to pass to a greater perfection—that is (according to the *Scholium of Proposition 11 of this part*), it is affected with **Joy** <note>original: "Lætitia." For Spinoza, Joy is the emotion we feel when our "power of acting" increases.</note>. This Joy is greater the more distinctly the Mind can imagine itself and its power of acting. <note>original: "Q. E. D." (Quod erat demonstrandum)—Which was to be demonstrated.</note>

->COROLLARITY.<-

This Joy is fostered more and more as a person imagines themselves to be praised by others. For the more one imagines being praised by others, the more one imagines others to be affected with Joy by oneself, and this is accompanied by the idea of oneself (according to the *Scholium of Proposition 29 of this part*). Therefore (according to *Proposition 27 of this part*), the person is themselves affected with a greater Joy accompanied by the idea of themselves. Which was to be demonstrated.

## PROPOSITION 54.

*The Mind strives to imagine only those things that posit its power of acting.*

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

The striving or power of the Mind is the very essence of the Mind itself (according to *Proposition 7 of this part*). However, the essence of the Mind (as is self-evident) affirms only that which the Mind is and can do—not that which it is not or cannot do. Therefore, it strives to imagine only what affirms or <term>posits<note>To "posit" means to state or establish the presence of something.</note></term> its power of acting. Which was to be demonstrated.

## PROPOSITION 55.

*When the Mind imagines its own lack of power, it is saddened by that very fact.*

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

The essence of the Mind affirms only what the Mind is and can do; or, it is of the nature of the Mind only to imagine those things that posit its power of acting (according to the *preceding Proposition*). Therefore, when we say that the Mind, while contemplating itself, imagines its own <term>impotence<note>original: "impotentiam." This refers to a lack of power or a weakness.</note></term>, we are saying nothing other than that while the Mind strives to imagine something that posits its power of acting, this striving is checked, or (according to the *Scholium of Proposition 11 of this part*) the Mind is saddened. Which was to be demonstrated.

->COROLLARITY.<-

This Sadness is fostered more and more if one imagines being blamed by others. This is demonstrated in the same way as the *Corollary of Proposition 53 of this part*.

->SCHOLIUM.<-

This Sadness, when accompanied by the idea of our own weakness, is called **Humility**. On the other hand, the Joy that arises from the contemplation of ourselves is called **Self-Love** <note>original: "Philautia" (Greek: φίλαυτία). Spinoza uses this term to denote a natural regard for oneself.</note> or **Self-Satisfaction** <note>original: "Acquiescentia in se ipso." Literally, "rest or satisfaction in oneself." This is the contentment one feels when recognizing their own abilities.</note>. And since this Joy is repeated as often as a person contemplates their virtues or their power of acting, it follows that everyone is eager to tell of their own deeds and show off...

<meta>Digitized by Google watermark at bottom of page</meta>

<summary>Spinoza argues that the mind naturally seeks to contemplate its own strengths, resulting in joy, while acknowledging its own weaknesses causes sadness. These dynamics form the basis for self-satisfaction (self-love), humility, and our sensitivity to the praise or blame of others.</summary>
<keywords>Joy, Sadness, Power of Acting, Self-Love, Humility, Self-Satisfaction, Praise, Blame, Ethics, Spinoza</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 95 regarding the Mind's contemplation of its own power. Having established that self-contemplation leads to Joy, Spinoza now examines the social consequences: how comparing our own power to that of others leads to the "natural" human tendencies of envy and competition.</meta>

<page-num>96</page-num>
<header>ETHICS PART III.</header>

...he yearns to display the powers of both body and mind, and that for this reason men are troublesome to one another. From which it follows again that men are by nature envious (see the <term>Scholium<note>A "Scholium" is an explanatory note or commentary added to a formal proof.</note></term> of *Proposition 24* and the *Scholium of Proposition 32 of this part*), or rather, they rejoice in the weakness of their equals and, conversely, are saddened by their virtues. For as often as anyone imagines his own actions, so often is he affected with **Joy** (*by Proposition 53 of this part*), and the more so, the more those actions express perfection and the more distinctly he imagines them; that is (*by what was said in Scholium 1 of Proposition 40, Part 2*), the more he can distinguish them from others and contemplate them as <term>singular things<note>Individual, specific entities or events rather than abstract categories.</note></term>. Therefore, everyone will rejoice most from the contemplation of himself when he contemplates something in himself which he denies of others. 

But if he refers what he affirms of himself to the universal idea of "man" or "animal," he will not rejoice as much; and conversely, he will be saddened if he imagines his own actions, compared to the actions of others, to be weaker. This **Sadness** (*by Proposition 28 of this part*) he will endeavor to remove, and that by wrongly interpreting the actions of his equals, or by adorning his own as much as possible. It appears, therefore, that men are naturally prone to Hatred and Envy, to which education itself is added. For parents are accustomed to incite children to <term>virtue<note>Latin: "virtus." In Spinoza's usage, this refers to a thing's "power" or "excellence" of acting, not just moral rectitude.</note></term> solely by the stimulus of honor and Envy. But perhaps a doubt remains, because we not infrequently admire the virtues of men and venerate them. To remove this, therefore, I shall add the following Corollary.

### COROLLARY.

No one envies the virtue of anyone unless they are an equal.

### DEMONSTRATION.

Envy is <term>Hatred<note>original: "Odium."</note></term> itself (*see Scholium of Proposition 24 of this part*), or (*by the Scholium of Proposition 13 of this part*) **Sadness**, that is (*by the Scholium of Proposition 11 of this part*), an <term>affection<note>A state of being affected; a change in the body's power or the mind's idea of that power.</note></term> by which a man’s power of acting or striving is restrained. But a man (*by the Scholium of Proposition 9 of this part*) strives to do nothing, nor does he desire anything, except what can follow from his given nature; therefore, a man will not desire that any power of acting or (what is the same thing) virtue be predicated of himself which is proper to another's nature and alien to his own. Thus, his **Desire** cannot be restrained—that is (*by the Scholium of Proposition 11 of this part*), he himself cannot be saddened—by the fact that he contemplates some virtue in someone unlike himself, and consequently he cannot envy him. But he can indeed envy his equal, who is supposed to be of the same nature as himself. <note>original: "Q. E. D." (Quod erat demonstrandum)—Which was to be demonstrated.</note>

### SCHOLIUM.

Since, therefore, we said above in the Scholium of Proposition 52 of this Part that we venerate a man because we admire his wisdom, courage, etc., this happens (*as is evident from the Proposition itself*) because we imagine these virtues to be in him uniquely, and not as common to our nature; and so we will no more envy him for them than we do trees for their height, or lions for their courage, etc.

## PROPOSITION 56.

*There are as many species of **Joy**, **Sadness**, and **Desire**, and consequently of every affect composed of these (such as <term>vacillation of mind<note>original: "animi Fluctuationis." A state of emotional uncertainty or conflict.</note></term>) or derived from them...*

<summary>Spinoza argues that envy is a natural human result of comparing one's own power to that of equals, but we do not envy those we perceive as fundamentally different from ourselves. He explains that we only feel sadness (envy) when an equal possesses a "virtue" or power that we lack.</summary>

<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion regarding how individual affects (emotions) are shaped by our perceptions. Having established that humans are naturally prone to envy and comparison, Spinoza now moves to prove that the variety of human emotions is as vast as the variety of objects in the world that trigger them.</meta>

<header>ON THE AFFECTS.</header>
<sig>7</sig>

...is derived, namely of **Love**, **Hatred**, **Hope**, **Fear**, and so on, there are as many species as there are species of objects by which we are affected.

### P R O O F

**Joy** and **Sadness**, and consequently the <term>affects<note>Spinoza uses "affect" to describe any change in the body’s power to act, alongside the idea of that change. Think of them as "emotions" or "states of being" that move us toward or away from something.</note></term> which are composed of these or derived from them, are passions (*by the Note to Proposition 11 of this part*); however (*by Proposition 1 of this part*), we necessarily undergo passions only insofar as we have <term>inadequate ideas<note>Ideas that are incomplete or confused because they are based on external impressions rather than our own clear, logical reasoning.</note></term>; and insofar as we have these same ideas (*by Proposition 3 of this part*), only to that extent do we undergo passions. That is (*see Note 1 to Proposition 40, Part 2*), we only necessarily suffer insofar as we **imagine**, or (*see Proposition 17, Part 2, with its Note*) insofar as we are affected by an affect that involves both the nature of our own Body and the nature of an external body. Therefore, the nature of every passion must necessarily be explained in such a way that the nature of the object by which we are affected is expressed.

Namely, the **Joy** that arises from object *A* involves the nature of object *A* itself, and the **Joy** that arises from object *B* involves the nature of object *B* itself; and so these two affects of **Joy** are different in nature because they arise from causes of a different nature. Likewise, the affect of **Sadness** which arises from one object is different in nature from the **Sadness** which arises from another cause; this must also be understood regarding **Love**, **Hatred**, **Hope**, **Fear**, **Fluctuation of the Mind**, and so on. Consequently, there are necessarily as many species of **Joy**, **Sadness**, **Love**, **Hatred**, etc., as there are species of objects by which we are affected.

But **Desire** is the very essence or nature of each individual, insofar as it is conceived as determined to doing something by whatever constitution it currently has (*see the Note to Proposition 9 of this part*); therefore, according as each person is affected by external causes with this or that species of **Joy**, **Sadness**, **Love**, **Hatred**, etc.—that is, according as his nature is constituted in this or that way—so his **Desire** must be of one kind or another. The nature of one **Desire** must differ from the nature of another as much as the affects from which each arises differ from one another. Thus, there are as many species of **Desire** as there are species of **Joy**, **Sadness**, **Love**, etc., and consequently (*by what has just been shown*) as many as there are species of objects by which we are affected. <note>original: "Q. E. D." (Quod erat demonstrandum)</note> *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### N O T E

Among the species of affects, which (*by the preceding Proposition*) must be very numerous, the most notable are *Gluttony, Drunkenness, Lust, Avarice*, and *Ambition*. These are nothing other than notions of **Love** or **Desire** which explain the nature of each of these affects through the objects to which they refer. For by **Gluttony**, **Drunkenness**, **Lust**, **Avarice**, and **Ambition**, we understand nothing other than an immoderate **Love** or **Desire** for feasting, drinking, sexual intercourse, wealth, and glory.

Furthermore, these affects—insofar as we distinguish them from others solely by the object to which they refer—do not have contraries. For **Temperance**, which we are accustomed to oppose to *Gluttony*; **Sobriety**, which we oppose to *Drunkenness*; and finally **Chastity**, which we oppose to *Lust*, are not affects or passions, but rather indicate a **power of the mind** which moderates these affects. As for the remaining species of Affects, I can neither explain them here (because there are as many as there are species of objects), nor, if I could, is it necessary. For in order to determine what we intend—namely, the strength of the affects and the Mind's power over them—it is sufficient for us...

<summary>Spinoza demonstrates that because our emotions (affects) are triggered by external objects, there are as many distinct types of Joy, Sadness, and Desire as there are types of objects in the world. He also clarifies that virtues like Temperance and Sobriety are not emotions themselves, but rather signs of the mind's power to control emotions.</summary>

<meta>Continuing from page 97, where Spinoza established that affects (emotions) vary according to the objects that cause them, this page shifts focus to the individual. It argues that because every individual has a unique essence, their internal experience of an emotion is fundamentally different from anyone else’s experience of that same emotion.</meta>

<header>ETHICS PART III.</header>

...the general definition of each affect. It is enough, I say, for us to understand the common properties of the **Affects** and the **Mind**, so that we can determine the nature and extent of the **Mind's** power in moderating and restraining the **Affects**. Even if there is a great difference between this or that affect of **Love**, **Hatred**, or **Desire**—for example, between the **Love** toward children and the **Love** toward a wife—it is not necessary for us to know every single difference or to investigate the nature and origin of the affects any further.

## PROPOSITION 57.

*Every affect of each individual differs from the affect of another only as much as the essence of the one differs from the essence of the other.*

### PROOF.

This Proposition is clear from Axiom 1 (which see after Lemma 3, following the Note to Proposition 13, Part 2). However, we will demonstrate it using the definitions of the three primary affects.

All affects relate to **Desire**, **Joy**, or **Sadness**, as shown by the definitions we have provided. But **Desire** is the very nature or <term>essence<note>Spinoza defines essence as the very core of what a thing is; without it, the thing can neither be nor be conceived.</note></term> of each individual (*see its definition in the Note to Proposition 9 of this part*); therefore, the **Desire** of one individual differs from the **Desire** of another only as much as the nature or essence of the one differs from the essence of the other. Next, **Joy** and **Sadness** are passions by which the power or <term>striving<note>Original: *conatus*. This is the fundamental drive of everything to continue existing and functioning.</note></term> of each individual to persist in its being is increased or diminished, aided or restrained (*by Proposition 11 of this part and its Note*). But by the "striving to persist in its being," insofar as it relates to both the **Mind** and **Body** at once, we mean **Appetite** and **Desire** (*see the Note to Proposition 9 of this part*); therefore, **Joy** and **Sadness** are simply **Desire** or **Appetite** itself, insofar as it is increased or diminished by external causes. That is (*by the same Note*), it is the very nature of each individual. Thus, the **Joy** or **Sadness** of one individual differs from that of another only as much as their nature or essence differs; and consequently, any affect of one individual differs from the affect of another, etc. <note>original: "Q. E. D." (Quod erat demonstrandum) — Which was to be demonstrated.</note>

### NOTE.

From this it follows that the affects of animals, which are called irrational (for we cannot doubt that animals feel, now that we know the origin of the **Mind**), differ from the affects of humans only as much as their nature differs from human nature. A horse and a man are both driven by a <term>lust<note>Original: *Libido*. Here Spinoza refers to the biological drive for procreation.</note></term> to procreate; but the horse is driven by a horse-like **Lust**, and the man by a human one. So also the **Lusts** and **Appetites** of insects, fish, and birds must each be different from one another. Although each individual lives content with its own nature and rejoices in it, that life and that joy are nothing other than the <term>idea or soul<note>In Spinoza’s system, the "soul" or "mind" is the mental counterpart to the physical body.</note></term> of that individual. Thus, the joy of one differs in nature from the joy of another as much as their essences differ. Finally, it follows from the previous Proposition that there is a significant difference between the joy that a drunkard feels, for example, and...

<summary>Spinoza argues that emotions are not universal experiences but are uniquely shaped by an individual's specific nature or 'essence.' This logic extends to animals, whose feelings are real but fundamentally different from human emotions based on their biological nature.</summary>

<meta>Continuing from page 98, which discussed how the nature of our emotions depends on our individual essence, Spinoza now moves from discussing "passions"—emotions where we are acted upon by the outside world—to "actions"—emotions that arise from our own power of reasoning.</meta>

<header>ON THE AFFECTS.</header>

...the joy which the Philosopher possesses, which I wanted to mention here in passing. And these are the things I wished to say concerning the <term>affects<note>Original: *affectibus*. In Spinoza's philosophy, these are transitions in a person's power of acting, commonly called emotions or feelings.</note></term> related to a human being insofar as they are passive <note>Original: *patitur*. This refers to "passions," where the cause of the feeling lies outside the person.</note>. It remains for me to add a few things about those affects related to the same person insofar as they act.

### PROPOSITION 58.

*Besides **Joy** and **Desire**, which are passions, there are other affects of **Joy** and **Desire** that relate to us insofar as we act.*

#### DEMONSTRATION.

When the Mind conceives of itself and its own power of acting, it rejoices (*by Proposition 53 of this part*). However, the Mind necessarily contemplates itself whenever it conceives a true or <term>adequate idea<note>An "adequate idea" is a clear and distinct thought that is fully understood through the power of the mind alone, without external confusion.</note></term> (*by Proposition 43, Part 2*). But the Mind does conceive of certain adequate ideas (*by the second Note to Proposition 40, Part 2*). Therefore, it also rejoices insofar as it conceives adequate ideas; that is (*by Proposition 1 of this part*), insofar as it acts. Furthermore, the Mind strives to persevere in its own being both insofar as it has clear and distinct ideas and insofar as it has confused ideas (*by Proposition 9 of this part*). But by "striving" we mean **Desire** (*by the Note to that same Proposition*); therefore, **Desire** relates to us even insofar as we understand, or (*by Proposition 1 of this part*) insofar as we act. Which was to be demonstrated.

### PROPOSITION 59.

*Among all the affects related to the Mind insofar as it acts, there are none except those related to **Joy** or **Desire**.*

#### DEMONSTRATION.

All affects relate to **Desire**, **Joy**, or **Sadness**, as the definitions we have given show. By **Sadness**, however, we mean that the Mind's power of thinking is diminished or restrained (*by Proposition 11 of this part and its Note*); and so, insofar as the Mind is saddened, its power of understanding—which is its power of acting (*by Proposition 1 of this part*)—is diminished or restrained. Therefore, no affects of **Sadness** can be related to the Mind insofar as it acts; but only affects of **Joy** and **Desire**, which (*by the preceding Proposition*) are also related to the Mind in that capacity. Which was to be demonstrated.

#### SCHOLIUM.

All actions that follow from affects related to the Mind insofar as it understands, I attribute to <term>**Fortitude**<note>Original: *Fortitudo*. For Spinoza, this is the general strength of character that arises from reason.</note></term>, which I distinguish into <term>**Courage**<note>Original: *Animositas*. This is the rational desire to maintain one's own well-being.</note></term> and <term>**Generosity**<note>Original: *Generositas*. This is the rational desire to help others and join with them in friendship.</note></term>. For by **Courage**, I mean the **Desire** by which each person strives to preserve their own being solely by the dictate of Reason. By **Generosity**, I mean the **Desire** by which each person, solely by the dictate of Reason, strives to help other people and join them to themselves in friendship. Therefore, those actions which aim only at the benefit of the agent I relate to **Courage**, and those which also aim at the benefit of another I relate to **Generosity**. Thus, **Temperance**, **Sobriety**, and **Presence of Mind** in dangers, etc., are types of **Courage**; while **Modesty**, **Clemency**, etc., are types of **Generosity**. And by these, I think I have explained the principal affects and fluctuations of the mind, which arise from the combination of the three primary—

<summary>Spinoza argues that while some emotions are passive (passions), Joy and Desire can also be active expressions of the mind's power through reason. He categorizes these rational actions under the umbrella of Fortitude, split into self-focused Courage and other-focused Generosity.</summary>
<keywords>active affects, Joy, Desire, Fortitude, Courage, Generosity, Reason, adequate ideas, self-preservation, ethics</keywords>

--- Page 115 ---
<meta>Continuing from page 99, which established that the Mind's power of acting can be increased or diminished, Spinoza now synthesizes how these fundamental shifts—Joy, Sadness, and Desire—interact with the external world and lead into a formal list of definitions.</meta>

<header>ETHICS PART III.</header>

I have explained that these <term>affects<note>Original: *affectuum*. In Spinoza's system, an affect is a change in the body's power to act, along with the mind's idea of that change. They are more than just "emotions"; they are shifts in our very state of being.</note></term>, namely **Desire**, **Joy**, and **Sadness**, arise from and are shown through their primary causes. From these, it is clear that we are tossed about by external causes in many ways and that, just like the waves of the sea driven by contrary winds, we waver, unaware of our outcome and our fate. However, I stated that I have shown only the principal conflicts of the mind, not every conflict that can exist. For by proceeding in the same way as above, we can easily show that **Love** is joined to **Repentance**, **Scorn**, **Shame**, and so on. Indeed, I believe it is clearly established from what has been said that affects can be combined with one another in so many ways, and so many variations can arise from this, that they cannot be defined by any number. But for my purpose, it is enough to have enumerated only the principal ones; for the others I have omitted would be more a matter of curiosity than of utility.

Nevertheless, one thing remains to be noted about **Love**: it very often happens that while we are enjoying the thing we desired, the Body acquires a new constitution from that enjoyment, by which it is determined differently. Other images of things are stirred up in the body, and at the same time, the Mind begins to imagine other things and to desire other things. For example, when we imagine something that usually delights us with its flavor, we desire to enjoy it—that is, to eat it. But as long as we are enjoying it, the stomach is filled, and the Body is constituted differently. If, therefore, while the Body is now differently disposed, the image of that same food is encouraged because the food is present—and consequently the effort or **Desire** to eat it is also encouraged—that new constitution of the body will resist this Desire or effort. Consequently, the presence of the food we once desired will be hateful; and this is what we call **Satiety** <note>Original: *Fastidium*. This can also be translated as "disgust" or "loathing" resulting from over-indulgence.</note> and **Weariness**. 

Furthermore, I have ignored the external modifications of the Body observed in the affects—such as trembling, paleness, sobbing, laughter, etc.—because they relate to the Body alone without any relation to the Mind. Finally, certain things must be noted regarding the definitions of the affects; I shall therefore repeat them here in order and insert what needs to be observed in each one.

## DEFINITIONS OF THE AFFECTS.

### I.

->**Desire** is the very essence of man, insofar as it is conceived as determined to do something by any given modification of itself.<-

### EXPLANATION.

We said above, in the Note to Proposition 9 of this Part, that **Desire** is appetite together with the consciousness of it; whereas <term>appetite<note>Original: *appetitum*. This refers to the fundamental drive to exist.</note></term> is the very essence of man, insofar as it is determined to do those things that serve his self-preservation. But in that same Note, I also warned that I recognize no real difference between human appetite and **Desire**. For whether a man is conscious of his appetite or not, the appetite remains one and the same. Therefore, lest I should seem to commit a tautology, I did not want to explain **Desire** through appetite, but rather studied to define it in such a way that I might encompass all the efforts of human nature—which we signify by the names **appetite**, **will**, **desire**, or **impulse**—in one definition. For I could have said that **Desire** is the very essence of man, insofar as it is conceived as determined to do something;

<summary>Spinoza concludes his general discussion of how human emotions are buffeted by external forces and introduces "Satiety" as a physical change. He then begins his formal "Definitions of the Affects," defining Desire as the fundamental essence of a human being's drive to act.</summary>

<meta>Continuing from the discussion on the three primary affects—Desire, Joy, and Sadness—Spinoza now refines his definitions to account for the Mind's consciousness and the nature of transitions in our state of being.</meta>

# ON THE AFFECTS

...but from this definition <note>original: "ex hac definitione"</note> (by Proposition 23, Part 2), it would not follow that the Mind could be conscious of its own **Desire** or appetite. Therefore, in order to involve the cause of this consciousness, it was necessary (by the same Proposition) to add: *insofar as it is determined by any given affection of itself*, etc. For by an <term>affection<note>An "affection" (*affectio*) is a specific state or mode of the body or mind resulting from an interaction.</note></term> of the human essence, we understand any constitution of that same essence, whether it be innate, whether it be conceived through the attribute of **Thought** alone or **Extension** alone, or finally, whether it be referred to both at once. Here, then, under the name of **Desire**, I understand any of a person's strivings, impulses, appetites, and volitions, which vary according to that person’s changing constitution, and are not infrequently so opposed to one another that the person is pulled in different directions and does not know where to turn.

->II.<-

**Joy** is a person’s transition from a lesser to a greater perfection.

->III.<-

**Sadness** is a person’s transition from a greater to a lesser perfection.

->EXPLANATION<-

I say a *transition*. For **Joy** is not perfection itself. If a person were born with the perfection to which they pass, they would possess it without the affect of **Joy**; this appears more clearly from the affect of **Sadness**, which is its opposite. For no one can deny that **Sadness** consists in a transition to a lesser perfection, rather than in the lesser perfection itself, since a person cannot feel sad insofar as they partake in any perfection. Nor can we say that **Sadness** consists in the privation of a greater perfection; for <term>privation<note>Spinoza argues that "privation" is a human concept of what something "lacks" compared to an ideal, whereas "transition" is a real, active change in power.</note></term> is nothing, but the affect of **Sadness** is an act, which therefore can be nothing else than the act of passing to a lesser perfection—that is, an act by which a person’s power of acting is diminished or restrained (see the Scholium to Proposition 11 of this part). For the rest, I omit the definitions of **Cheerfulness**, **Titillation**, **Melancholy**, and **Pain**, because they relate chiefly to the **Body**, and are nothing but species of **Joy** or **Sadness**.

->IV.<-

**Wonder** <note>original: *Admiratio*. Often translated as "Wonder" or "Astonishment," this refers to a state of being struck by the novelty of an object.</note> is an imagination of some thing in which the Mind remains fixed, because this singular imagination has no connection with others. *See Proposition 52 of this part, with its Scholium.*

->EXPLANATION<-

In the Scholium to Proposition 18 of Part 2, we showed the cause why the Mind, from the contemplation of one thing, immediately falls into the thought of another: namely, because the images of those things are linked together and so ordered that one follows the other. This cannot be conceived when the image of a thing is new; rather, the Mind will be held in the contemplation of the same thing until it is determined by other causes to think of other things. Considered in itself, the imagination of a new thing is of the same nature as others; for this reason, I do not count **Wonder** among the affects, nor do I see why I should do so, since this [distraction] of the Mind...

<summary>Spinoza defines Joy and Sadness not as static states, but as active transitions in a person's level of perfection or "power of acting." He also introduces Wonder as a cognitive state where the mind is fixed on a single image due to a lack of mental associations.</summary>

<keywords>Desire, Joy, Sadness, Wonder, transition, perfection, power of acting, consciousness, human essence</keywords>

<meta>Continuing from the definitions of the primary affects—Joy, Sadness, and Desire—Spinoza now addresses how other common emotions are derived from these three and critiques traditional definitions of Love and Hate.</meta>

# THE ETHICS PART III.

...distraction arises from no positive cause that pulls the Mind away from other things, but only from the fact that the cause—the reason why the Mind is determined to move from the contemplation of one thing to thinking of others—is lacking. Therefore (as I noted in the Scholium to Prop. 11 of this part), I recognize only three primitive or primary affects: **Joy**, **Sadness**, and **Desire**. I have spoken of **Wonder** <note>original: "Admiratione"</note> for no other reason than that it has become common usage for certain affects derived from the three primitives to be called by other names when they refer to objects we wonder at. This same reasoning moves me to also add the definition of **Contempt** to these.

->V.<-

***Contempt*** *is the representation of some thing that touches the Mind so slightly that the Mind is moved by the presence of that thing more to imagine what is NOT in the thing than what IS in it. See the Scholium to Prop. 52 of this part.*

I am omitting the definitions of **Veneration** and **Disdain** <note>original: "Venerationis et Dedignationis"</note> here, because no affects, as far as I know, take their names from these.

->VI.<-

***Love*** *is **Joy** accompanied by the idea of an external cause.*

->E X P L A N A T I O N<-

This definition explains the essence of **Love** clearly enough. However, the definition used by those Authors who describe **Love** as *the will of the lover to join themselves to the thing loved* expresses not the essence of Love, but a property of it. Because these Authors did not sufficiently perceive the essence of Love, they could not have a clear concept of its properties either; this is why everyone has judged their definition to be quite obscure. 

It must be noted, however, that when I say it is a property in a lover to join themselves by "will" to the thing loved, I do not understand by <term>will<note>original: "voluntatem"</note></term> a consent, a deliberation of the mind, or a free decree (for we demonstrated that such things are fictions in Proposition 48 of Part 2). Nor do I mean the **Desire** of joining oneself to the thing loved when it is absent, or of staying in its presence when it is near; for love can be conceived without either of these Desires. Rather, by "will" I understand the <term>Contentment<note>original: "Acquiescentiam"; a state of peaceful satisfaction or rest.</note></term> that the Lover feels because of the presence of the thing loved, which strengthens or at least encourages the lover's **Joy**.

->VII.<-

***Hate*** *is **Sadness** accompanied by the idea of an external cause.*

->E X P L A N A T I O N<-

The points to be noted here are easily understood from what was said in the Explanation of the preceding Definition. *See also the Scholium to Prop. 13* ^1) *of this part.*

->VIII.<-

***Inclination*** <note>original: "Propensio"</note> *is **Joy** accompanied by the idea of some thing that is an accidental cause of Joy.*

---

1) Bruder. — First Edition: *Schol. Prop. 11.*

<summary>Spinoza defines Contempt, Love, Hate, and Inclination, arguing that Love and Hate are essentially Joy or Sadness linked to an external cause. He critiques the traditional view of Love as a "will to join," redefining "will" as the contentment found in the object's presence.</summary>

--- Page 118 ---
<meta>Continuing the definitions of the affects from the previous page, specifically moving from the primary definitions of Love and Hate to more complex emotional states such as Devotion, Mockery, Hope, and Fear.</meta>

<header>ON THE AFFECTS.</header>

### IX.

**Aversion** <note>original: "Aversio"</note> is **Sadness** accompanied by the idea of something that is the accidental cause of that **Sadness**. *Regarding this, see the Scholium to Proposition 15 of this part.*

### X.

**Devotion** <note>original: "Devotio"</note> is **Love** toward someone whom we wonder at <note>Spinoza uses "wonder" (admiratio) to mean the focused attention we give to something new or singular</note>.

->EXPLANATION.<-

I showed in Proposition 52 of this part that **Wonder** arises from the novelty of a thing. Therefore, if it happens that we frequently imagine <sup>1)</sup> <note>The editors Gfrörer, Bruder, and Riedel suggest "frequently imagine"; the First Edition of 1677 reads "we imagine."</note> that which we wonder at, we will cease to wonder at it; and so we see that the affect of **Devotion** easily degrades into simple **Love**.

### XI.

**Mockery** <note>original: "Irrisio"</note> is **Joy** arising from the fact that we imagine something we despise is present in a thing we hate.

->EXPLANATION.<-

Insofar as we despise a thing we hate, we deny its existence <note>to "despise" here means to think the thing has no qualities worth the mind's attention</note> (*see the Scholium to Proposition 52 of this part*), and to that extent (*by Proposition 20 of this part*), we feel joy. But since we assume the person still hates what they mock, it follows that this **Joy** is not solid or lasting. *See the Scholium to Proposition 47 of this part.*

### XII.

**Hope** <note>original: "Spes"</note> is an inconstant **Joy**, arising from the idea of a future or past thing, about whose outcome we are to some extent in doubt.

### XIII.

**Fear** <note>original: "Metus"</note> is an inconstant **Sadness**, arising from the idea of a future or past thing, about whose outcome we are to some extent in doubt. *Regarding these, see Scholium 2 to Proposition 18 of this part.*

->EXPLANATION.<-

It follows from these definitions that there is no **Hope** without **Fear**, nor **Fear** without **Hope**. For whoever is suspended in **Hope** and doubts the outcome of a thing, is assumed to imagine something that excludes the existence of that future thing; thus, to that extent, they are saddened (*by Proposition 19 of this part*), and consequently, while they are suspended in **Hope**, they fear that the thing may happen. Conversely, whoever is in a state of **Fear**—that is, whoever doubts the outcome of a thing they hate—also imagines something that excludes the existence of that same thing; thus (*by Proposition 20 of this part*), they feel joy, and consequently, to that extent, they have **Hope** that it will not happen.

### XIV.

**Confidence** <note>original: "Securitas"; in this context, a state of mind where doubt is removed</note> is **Joy** arising from the idea of a future or past thing, regarding which the cause for doubt has been removed.

---

1) Gfrörer, Bruder, Riedel. — First Edition: *we imagine*.

<summary>This page continues Spinoza’s definitions of the human emotions, specifically exploring how Joy and Sadness are modified by doubt and wonder. He famously argues that Hope and Fear are psychologically inseparable because both involve uncertainty about an outcome.</summary>
<keywords>Aversion, Devotion, Mockery, Hope, Fear, Confidence, Joy, Sadness, Wonder, Uncertainty, Ethics</keywords>

--- Page 119 ---
<meta>This page continues the definitions of the affects in Part III of the Ethics. The previous page ended mid-definition of Fear (Metus). We now transition into the definitions of Despair, Gladness, Remorse, Pity, Favor, and Indignation.</meta>

<header>ETHICS PART III.</header>

## XV.

*Despair* <note>original: "Desperatio"</note> *is **Sadness** arising from the idea of a future or past thing regarding which the cause for doubt has been removed.*

### EXPLANATION.

From **Hope** arises **Confidence** <note>original: "Securitas"</note>, and from **Fear** arises **Despair**, when the cause for doubting the outcome of a thing is removed. This happens either because a person imagines a past or future thing to be present and contemplates it as if it were right before them, or because they imagine other things that exclude the existence of the factors that were causing them to doubt. For although we can never be certain of the outcome of things (*by the Corollary to Proposition 31, Part 2*), it is still possible for us not to doubt their outcome. We have shown (*see the Scholium to Proposition 49, Part 2*) that it is one thing not to doubt a thing, and another to possess certainty of it. And so it is possible for us to be affected by the same emotion of **Joy** or **Sadness** from the image of a past or future thing as from the image of a present thing, as we demonstrated in Proposition 18 of this part; see that proposition along with its Scholium.

## XVI.

*Gladness* <note>original: "Gaudium"</note> *is **Joy** accompanied by the idea of a past thing which has happened beyond hope.*

## XVII.

*Remorse* <note>original: "Conscientiæ morsus"; literally 'the bite of conscience'</note> *is **Sadness** accompanied by the idea of a past thing which has happened beyond hope.*

## XVIII.

*Pity* <note>original: "Commiseratio"</note> *is **Sadness** accompanied by the idea of an evil which has happened to another whom we imagine to be like ourselves. See the Scholium to Proposition 22 and the Scholium to Proposition 27 of this part.*

### EXPLANATION.

There seems to be no difference between **Pity** and **Compassion** <note>original: "Misericordia"</note>, except perhaps that **Pity** refers to an individual instance of the emotion, whereas **Compassion** refers to the habit or disposition of that emotion.

## XIX.

*Favor* <note>original: "Favor"</note> *is **Love** toward someone who has benefited another.*

## XX.

*Indignation* <note>original: "Indignatio"</note> *is **Hatred** toward someone who has done evil to another.*

### EXPLANATION.

I am aware that in common usage these names signify something else. But my purpose is not to explain the meaning of words, but the nature of things, and to label them with those terms whose meaning—as established by common usage—is not entirely at odds with the meaning I wish to employ; let it suffice to have mentioned this once. Furthermore, regarding the cause of these emotions, see Corollary 1 of Proposition 27 and Scholium 1 <sup>1)</sup> of Proposition 22 of this Part.

---

1) <note>original: "Vers. Belg. — Ed. Pr.: Scholiis."</note> The Dutch version and the First Edition read: "Scholia" (plural).

<summary>Spinoza defines several complex emotions (affects) derived from Joy and Sadness, including Despair, Gladness, Remorse, Pity, Favor, and Indignation, while explaining his method of redefining common terms to suit a psychological and philosophical framework.</summary>
<keywords>Despair, Confidence, Gladness, Remorse, Pity, Favor, Indignation, Joy, Sadness, Certainty, Spinoza, Ethics, Human Emotions</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the definitions of the affects in Part III of Spinoza's Ethics. Following the definitions of Pity and Favor on the previous page, we now move to the concepts of overestimation, envy, and the internal reflections of the mind such as self-contentment and humility.</meta>

<header>ON THE AFFECTS.</header>

### XXI.

*Overestimation* <note>original: "Existimatio"</note> *is to think more highly of someone than is just, out of **Love**.*

### XXII.

*Disparagement* <note>original: "Despectus"</note> *is to think less highly of someone than is just, out of **Hatred**.*

#### EXPLANATION.

**Overestimation** is therefore an effect or property of **Love**, and **Disparagement** an effect or property of **Hatred**. Thus, **Overestimation** can also be defined as **Love**, insofar as it so affects a person that they think more highly of the beloved thing than is just. Conversely, **Disparagement** can be defined as **Hatred**, insofar as it so affects a person that they think less highly of the one they hate than is just. See the Scholium to Proposition 26 of this part for more on these.

### XXIII.

*Envy* <note>original: "Invidia"</note> *is **Hatred**, insofar as it so affects a person that they are saddened by another’s happiness and, conversely, rejoice in another’s misfortune.*

#### EXPLANATION.

**Envy** is commonly opposed to **Compassion** <note>original: "Misericordia"</note>, which therefore—despite the usual meaning of the word—can be defined as follows:

### XXIV.

*Compassion is **Love**, insofar as it so affects a person that they rejoice in another’s good and, conversely, are saddened by another’s misfortune.*

#### EXPLANATION.

Furthermore, concerning **Envy**, see the Scholium to Proposition 24 and the Scholium to Proposition 32 of this part. These are the affects of **Joy** and **Sadness** which are accompanied by the idea of an external thing as their cause, whether essentially or accidentally. From here, I pass on to others which are accompanied by the idea of an internal thing as their cause.

### XXV.

*Self-Contentment* <note>original: "Acquiescentia in se ipso"; literally 'rest or satisfaction in oneself'</note> *is **Joy** arising from the fact that a person contemplates themselves and their own power of acting.*

### XXVI.

*Humility* is **Sadness** arising from the fact that a person contemplates their own lack of power or weakness.*

#### EXPLANATION.

**Self-Contentment** is opposed to **Humility**, insofar as we understand it as the **Joy** that arises from contemplating our power of acting. However, insofar as we also understand it as **Joy** accompanied by the idea of some deed which we believe ourselves to have performed by a free decree of the **Mind**, then it is opposed to **Repentance**, which we define as follows:

### XXVII.

*Repentance* <note>original: "Pœnitentia"</note> *is **Sadness** accompanied by the idea of some deed which we believe ourselves to have performed by a free decree of the **Mind**.*

<summary>This page defines social affects like envy and compassion alongside internal reflections such as self-contentment and humility. Spinoza characterizes these emotions based on whether they stem from external causes or the mind's internal contemplation of its own power and actions.</summary>
<keywords>overestimation, disparagement, envy, compassion, self-contentment, humility, repentance, power of acting, Joy, Sadness</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the definitions of the affects in Part III of Spinoza's Ethics. It moves from the affects directed toward others (Overestimation and Disparagement) to those directed toward the self, specifically focusing on how our upbringing and self-perception shape our moral emotions.</meta>

<header>ETHICS PART III.</header>

#### EXPLANATION.
We have shown the causes of these affects <note>Spinoza uses "affects" to describe emotions or patterns of feeling that increase or decrease our power to act</note> in the Scholium <note>A "Scholium" is an explanatory note or commentary following a mathematical-style proof</note> to Proposition 51 of this part, and in Propositions 53, 54, and 55 of this part and its Scholium. Regarding the free decree of the Mind, see the Scholium to Proposition 35, Part 2. 

But it should be noted here besides, that it is not surprising that Sadness <note>original: "Tristitia"</note> follows all those acts which out of custom are called *wicked*, and Joy <note>original: "Lætitia"</note> follows those called *upright*. We can easily understand from what has been said above that this depends chiefly on education. Parents, by blaming the former and often scolding their children for them, and conversely by persuading and praising the latter, have ensured that the stirrings of Sadness are joined to the one, and the stirrings of Joy to the other. This is even confirmed by experience itself. For custom and Religion are not the same for everyone; on the contrary, what is sacred to some is profane to others, and what is honorable to some is shameful to others. Therefore, according to how each person has been educated, they either repent of a certain deed or glory in it.

### XXVIII.
->*Pride is thinking more highly of oneself than is just, out of Love of self.*<-

#### EXPLANATION.
**Pride** <note>original: "Superbia"</note> therefore differs from **Overestimation**, because the latter refers to an external object, while **Pride** refers to the person himself, thinking more highly of himself than is just. Furthermore, just as **Overestimation** is an effect or property of **Love**, so **Pride** is an effect or property of **Self-love** <note>original: "Philautia"</note>. For this reason, it can also be defined as *Self-love, or Self-contentment, insofar as it so affects a person that they think more highly of themselves than is just* (see the Scholium to Proposition 26 of this part). 

There is no affect contrary to this one. For no one thinks less highly of themselves than is just out of Hatred of themselves; indeed, no one thinks less of themselves than is just insofar as they imagine that they cannot do this or that. For whatever a person imagines they cannot do, they necessarily imagine it, and are so disposed by this imagination that they truly cannot do what they imagine they cannot. For as long as they imagine they cannot do this or that, they are not determined to act, and consequently, it is impossible for them to do it as long as that imagination lasts. 

However, if we look at those things which depend on opinion alone, we can conceive it possible for a person to think less of themselves than is just. For it can happen that someone, while sadly contemplating their own weakness, imagines that they are despised by everyone, even while others are thinking of nothing less than despising them. Furthermore, a person can think less of themselves than is just if they deny something about themselves in the present in relation to a future time of which they are uncertain; for instance, if they deny that they can conceive of anything certain, or that they can desire or do anything but what is wicked or shameful, etc. Finally, we can say someone thinks less of themselves than is just when we see that, out of excessive fear of shame, they do not dare to do those things which others, their equals, do dare. We can therefore oppose this affect to **Pride**, and I shall call it **Despondency** <note>original: "Abjectio"</note>; for as **Pride** arises from **Self-contentment**, so **Despondency** arises from **Humility**, which we therefore define as follows.

### XXIX.
->*Despondency is thinking less highly of oneself than is just, out of Sadness.*<-

<summary>Spinoza explains how education and social custom link certain actions to joy or sadness, then defines Pride and Despondency as distorted forms of self-evaluation arising from self-love and humility respectively.</summary>
<keywords>pride, despondency, self-love, education, moral relativity, humility, joy, sadness, Spinoza, psychology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the definitions of the affects in Spinoza's Ethics, Part III. It follows the discussion on Pride and moves into the definitions of Glory, Shame, and the psychological mechanism of Longing.</meta>

# ON THE AFFECTS #
<meta>catchword: cohibe-</meta>

-> EXPLANATION. <-

We are, however, often accustomed to oppose Humility to Pride, but in doing so we attend more to the effects of each than to their actual nature. For we are accustomed to call "proud" one who boasts too much (see the Scholium to Proposition 30 of this part), who relates only his own virtues and only the vices of others, who wishes to be preferred above all, and who finally walks with that gravity and adornment usually adopted by others who are positioned far above him. Conversely, we call "humble" one who blushes often, who confesses his own vices and relates the virtues of others, who yields to everyone, and who finally walks with a bowed head and neglects to adorn himself. Furthermore, these affects <note>Spinoza uses "affects" to describe emotions or states of mind that increase or decrease our power to act</note>, namely Humility and Despondency <note>original: "Abjectio"; often translated as "Self-abasement," meaning thinking less of oneself than is just</note>, are very rare. For human nature, considered in itself, strives against them as much as it can (see Propositions 13 and 54 of this part); and therefore, those who are believed to be the most despondent and humble are generally the most ambitious and envious.

-> XXX. <-

**Glory** <note>original: "Gloria"</note> is Joy accompanied by the idea of some action of our own which we imagine others to praise.

-> XXXI. <-

**Shame** <note>original: "Pudor"</note> is Sadness accompanied by the idea of some action of our own which we imagine others to blame.

-> EXPLANATION. <-

Concerning these, see the Scholium to Proposition 30 of this Part. But here a difference must be noted between **Shame** <note>original: "Pudor"</note> and **Modesty** <note>original: "Verecundia"</note>. For Shame is the Sadness that follows a deed of which one is ashamed. Modesty, however, is the Fear or Dread of Shame, which restrains a person from committing anything disgraceful. To Modesty, **Impudence** is usually opposed, which in reality is not an affect, as I shall show in its proper place; but the names of the affects (as I have already warned) regard their common usage more than their nature. And with this, I have completed the affects of Joy and Sadness which I had proposed to explain. I proceed, therefore, to those which I refer to **Desire**.

-> XXXII. <-

**Longing** <note>original: "Desiderium"</note> is a Desire or Appetite to possess something, which is encouraged by the memory of that thing and, at the same time, is constrained by the memory of other things which exclude the existence of the thing desired.

-> EXPLANATION. <-

When we remember something, as we have already said many times, by that very fact we are disposed to contemplate it with the same affect as if the thing were present; but this disposition, or striving, while we are awake, is generally restrained by the images of things which exclude the existence of that which we remember. Therefore, when we remember a thing that affects us with some kind of Joy, we strive by that very fact to contemplate it as present with the same affect of Joy; which striving is indeed immediately restrained [by the memory of things that exclude its existence]...

---

1) Schmidt, Auerbach. — First Edition, Kirchmann: 15 and 34. — Corrections, Dutch Version, Gfrörer, Bruder, Saisset: 15 and 54. <note>This footnote reflects various scholarly editions of Spinoza's work and corrections to the internal cross-references.</note>

<summary>Spinoza defines Glory and Shame as social forms of Joy and Sadness, distinguishes between Shame and Modesty, and begins explaining Longing as a conflicted state of memory and desire.</summary>
<keywords>Pride, Humility, Despondency, Glory, Shame, Modesty, Longing, Desire, Spinoza, Ethics, Psychology of Emotion</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the specific definitions of the affects in Part III of Spinoza's Ethics. It concludes the definition of Longing (Desiderium) and moves through several interpersonal affects including Emulation, Gratitude, and Anger, concluding with the definition of Fear as a specific type of desire.</meta>

# ETHICS PART III. #

...the memory of things that exclude its existence is restrained. Therefore, **Longing** <note>original: "Desiderium"</note> is really a Sadness, which is opposed to that Joy that arises from the absence of a thing we hate, concerning which see the Scholium to Proposition 47 of this part. But because the name *Longing* seems to refer to **Desire** <note>Spinoza here uses "Cupiditas," his technical term for the very essence of a human being as a striving force</note>, I therefore refer this affect to the affects of Desire.

### XXXIII. ###

**Emulation** <note>original: "Aemulatio"</note> is a Desire for something, which is generated in us because we imagine others to have that same Desire.

#### -> EXPLANATION. <- ####

He who flees because he sees others fleeing, or he who fears because he sees others fearing; or even he who, from the fact that he sees someone has burned his hand, pulls back his own hand and moves his body as if his own hand were being burned; we would say that such a person indeed **imitates** the affect of another, but does not **emulate** it. We say this not because we recognize one cause for emulation and another for imitation, but because usage has brought it about that we call only him an "emulator" who imitates what we judge to be honorable, useful, or pleasant. For the cause of Emulation, see Proposition 27 of this Part with its Scholium. As for why Envy is usually joined to this affect, see Proposition 32 of this part with its Scholium.

### XXXIV. ###

**Thankfulness** or **Gratitude** <note>original: "Gratia seu Gratitudo"</note> is a Desire, or a zeal of Love, by which we strive to benefit someone who has conferred a benefit upon us with an equal affect of love. *See Prop. 39 with the Scholium to Prop. 41 of this part.*

### XXXV. ###

**Benevolence** <note>original: "Benevolentia"</note> is the Desire of doing good for someone for whom we feel pity. *See the Scholium to Prop. 27 of this part.*

### XXXVI. ###

**Anger** <note>original: "Ira"</note> is a Desire by which we are incited by **Hatred** to bring evil upon the one we hate. *See Prop. 39 of this part.*

### XXXVII. ###

**Revenge** <note>original: "Vindicta"</note> is a Desire by which we are stirred up by mutual **Hatred** to bring evil upon someone who, with an equal affect, has inflicted damage upon us. *See Corollary 2 of Prop. 40 of this part with its Scholium.*

### XXXVIII. ###

**Cruelty** or **Savagery** <note>original: "Crudelitas seu Saevitia"</note> is a Desire by which someone is stirred up to bring evil upon one whom we love, or for whom we feel pity.

#### -> EXPLANATION. <- ####

Opposed to Cruelty is **Clemency** <note>original: "Clementia"</note>, which is not a passion, but a power of the mind by which a person moderates anger and revenge.

### XXXIX. ###

**Fear** <note>original: "Timor"</note> is a Desire of avoiding a greater evil, which we fear, by means of a lesser one. *See the Scholium to Prop. 39 of this part.*

<summary>This page defines several social and reactive "affects" or emotions, specifically categorizing them as forms of "Cupiditas" (Desire/Appetite) directed toward others.</summary>
<keywords>Emulation, Gratitude, Benevolence, Anger, Revenge, Cruelty, Clemency, Fear, Spinoza's Ethics, psychological definitions</keywords>

--- Page 124 ---
<meta>This page continues Spinoza’s systematic "Definitions of the Affects" from Part III of the Ethics. The previous page ended with the definition of Gratitude; this page defines various social and personal drives, ranging from courage and fear to social climbing and physical excess.</meta>

<header>ON THE AFFECTS.</header>

### XL. ###

**Boldness** <note>original: "Audacia"</note> is a **Desire** <term>Cupiditas: for Spinoza, this is the very essence of a person as they strive to persist in their own being</term> by which someone is incited to perform an action involving danger, which their peers are afraid to undergo.

### XLI. ###

**Faint-heartedness** <note>original: "Pusillanimitas"</note> is said of a person whose Desire is restrained by the fear of a danger which their peers dare to undergo.

#### -> EXPLANATION. <- ####

Faint-heartedness is, therefore, nothing other than the Fear of some evil which most people are not accustomed to fearing; for this reason, I do not classify it among the affects of Desire. However, I wanted to explain it here because, insofar as we look at Desire, it is truly the opposite of the affect of **Boldness**.

### XLII. ###

**Consternation** <note>original: "Consternatio"</note> is said of a person whose Desire to avoid an evil is restrained by **amazement** <note>original: "admiratione"</note> at the evil which they fear.

#### -> EXPLANATION. <- ####

Consternation is thus a species of Faint-heartedness. But because Consternation arises from a double Fear, it can be more conveniently defined as: *a Fear that keeps a person so stupefied or wavering that they are unable to remove the evil.* I say *stupefied*, insofar as we understand their Desire to remove the evil to be restrained by amazement. I say *wavering*, insofar as we conceive that same Desire to be restrained by the Fear of another evil which equally torments them; from this, it happens that the person does not know which of the two to turn away from. Regarding these, see the Scholium to Proposition 39 and the Scholium to Proposition 52 of this Part. Moreover, regarding Faint-heartedness and Boldness, see the Scholium to Proposition 51 of this Part.

### XLIII. ###

**Courtesy** <note>original: "Humanitas"</note> or **Modesty** is the Desire to do those things that please people, and to omit those that displease them.

### XLIV. ###

**Ambition** is an immoderate Desire for glory.

#### -> EXPLANATION. <- ####

Ambition is a Desire by which all the affects (*according to Propositions 27 and 31 of this Part*) are fostered and strengthened; and therefore, this affect can hardly be overcome. For as long as a person is held by any Desire, they are necessarily held by this one as well. *Every best man,* says Cicero ^1), *is led most of all by glory. Even philosophers, in the books they write about despising glory, inscribe their own names, etc.*

### XLV. ###

**Gluttony** <note>original: "Luxuria"</note> is an immoderate Desire for, or even Love of, feasting.

### XLVI. ###

**Drunkenness** is an immoderate Desire for, and Love of, drinking.
