1) From Cicero's speech *Pro Archia*, section 11.

<summary>This page defines a series of "affects" or emotions related to social standing and physical desire, including boldness, faint-heartedness, and ambition. Spinoza emphasizes how social emotions like ambition are deeply rooted in human nature and difficult to suppress.</summary>
<keywords>Boldness, Faint-heartedness, Consternation, Courtesy, Ambition, Gluttony, Drunkenness, Cicero, Desire, Fear</keywords>

--- Page 125 ---
<meta>This page continues Spinoza’s concluding section of Part III, titled "Definitions of the Affects." Having previously defined specific social and emotional states, Spinoza now moves to physical drives and concludes with a comprehensive, "general" definition of what an emotion or affect actually is in his philosophical system.</meta>

<header>ETHICS PART III.</header>

->XLVII.<-
**Avarice is an immoderate Desire and Love for riches.**

->XLVIII.<-
**Lust is also a Desire and Love in joining bodies together.** <note>original: "in commiscendis corporibus," referring to sexual intercourse</note>

->EXPLANATION.<-

Whether this Desire for sexual intercourse is moderate or not, it is usually called Lust. Furthermore, these five affects <note>referring to Gluttony, Drunkenness, Lust, Avarice, and Ambition</note> (*as I noted in the Scholium to Proposition 56 of this part*) have no opposites. For **Modesty** <note>original: "Modestia"</note> is a species of Ambition, regarding which see the Scholium to Proposition 29 of this part; I have also already noted that **Temperance**, **Sobriety**, and **Chastity** indicate a power of the Mind <term>Mentis potentiam: the active ability of the mind to follow reason</term>, rather than a passion. And although it can happen that a greedy, ambitious, or fearful man refrains from excessive food, drink, and sex, **Avarice**, **Ambition**, and **Fear** are not opposites to luxury, drunkenness, or lust ¹). For the greedy man usually desires to gorge himself on another's food and drink. The ambitious man, moreover, provided he hopes it will remain secret, will not restrain himself in anything; and if he lives among drunkards and the lustful, precisely because he is ambitious, he will be more inclined to these same vices. Finally, the fearful man does what he does not want to do. For although he may throw his riches into the sea to avoid death, he remains greedy; and if a lustful man is sad because he cannot indulge his inclinations, he does not for that reason cease to be lustful. In an absolute sense, these affects do not so much regard the acts themselves of feasting, drinking, etc., as they do the Appetite and Love itself. Therefore, nothing can be opposed to these affects except **Generosity** and **Courage** <note>original: "Animositatem," meaning high-spiritedness or strength of character</note>, which will be discussed in what follows.

I pass over the definitions of **Jealousy** and the other vacillations of the mind in silence, both because they arise from the combination of affects we have already defined, and because most of them do not have specific names; this shows that for the practical use of life, it is sufficient to know them only in a general way. Moreover, from the Definitions of the affects which we have explained, it is clear that they all arise from **Desire**, **Joy**, or **Sadness**, or rather, that they are nothing besides these three, each of which is usually called by various names because of their various relations and external denominations. If we now wish to attend to these primary affects, and to what we said above about the nature of the Mind, we can define the affects, insofar as they refer to the Mind alone, in this way:

# GENERAL DEFINITION OF THE AFFECTS.

An affect, which is called a **passion** <note>original: "Pathema," a Greek-derived term for a state of being acted upon or suffering</note> of the mind, is a **confused idea** by which the Mind affirms a greater or lesser force of existence of its Body, or of some part of it, than before, and by which, when it is given, the Mind itself is determined to think of this rather than that.

->EXPLANATION.<-

I say first, that an Affect or passion of the mind is a *confused idea*. For we have shown (*see Prop. 3 of this part*) that the Mind only undergoes a passion <note>original: "pati," to be acted upon</note> insofar as it has inadequate or confused ideas. I say next, *by which*...

---

1) Schmidt, Saisset, Auerbach. — All editions. Dutch version, Kirchmann: *to chastity*. <note>This footnote tracks a variation in different historical editions where some editors substituted "chastity" for "lust" in the list of opposites.</note>

<summary>Spinoza defines Avarice and Lust, arguing they are obsessive states without true "opposites" among the passions, and concludes by providing a formal "General Definition" of an affect as a confused idea related to the body's power.</summary>

--- Page 126 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion on the specific definitions of human emotions. Having just finished the list of forty-eight distinct affects (emotions), Spinoza now provides a "General Definition of the Affects" to explain the underlying mechanics of how the mind and body interact during an emotional experience. This concludes Part III of the Ethics.</meta>

<header>CONCERNING THE AFFECTS.</header>

The Mind affirms a greater or lesser force of existing for its Body, or for some part of it, than it did before. For all the ideas of bodies that we have indicate the actual state of our own Body (*according to Corollary 2 of Proposition 16, Part 2*), rather than the nature of an external body; but this idea, which constitutes the form of an affect, must indicate or express the state of the Body, or some part of it, which the Body itself or some part of it has, because its power of acting, or its force of existing, is increased or diminished, aided or restrained. 

But it should be noted that when I say, *a greater or lesser force of existing than before*, I do not mean that the Mind compares the present state of the Body with a past state, but rather that the idea which constitutes the form of the affect affirms something of the Body which actually involves more or less reality than before. And since the essence of the Mind consists in this (*according to Propositions 11 and 13, Part 2*), that it affirms the actual existence of its Body, and we understand by "perfection" the very essence of a thing; it follows, therefore, that the Mind passes to a greater or lesser perfection when it happens to affirm something of its Body, or some part of it, which involves more or less reality than before. 

When, therefore, I said above that the Mind’s power of thinking is increased or diminished, I meant nothing else than that the Mind has formed an idea of its Body, or of some part of its Body, which expresses more or less reality than it had previously affirmed of its Body. For the excellence of ideas and the actual power of thinking are measured by the excellence of the object. Finally, I added, *and by the presence of which the Mind itself is determined to think of one thing rather than another*, so that, besides the nature of Joy <note>original: "Lætitiæ"</note> and Sadness <note>original: "Tristitiæ"</note>, which the first part of the definition explains, I might also express the nature of Desire <note>original: "Cupiditatis"</note>.

->*The End of the Third Part.*<-

<summary>This page concludes Part III of the Ethics by providing a general definition of emotions as changes in the Body's power of existence, which the Mind perceives as a transition to a greater or lesser state of perfection. Spinoza emphasizes that an emotion is not a comparison of states, but an immediate affirmation of the body's current increased or decreased "reality."</summary>

<meta>This page marks the beginning of Part IV of the Ethics. In the previous part, Spinoza provided a detailed map of the human emotions (affects). Here, he begins to apply that psychology to the human condition, specifically why we often feel "enslaved" by our passions despite our better judgment.</meta>

# ETHICS

## PART FOUR,

### CONCERNING HUMAN BONDAGE,

->*OR THE STRENGTH OF THE EMOTIONS.*<-

->PREFACE.<-

I call human lack of power in moderating and restraining the <term>Affects</term> <note>Spinoza uses "affects" (affectuum) to describe not just feelings, but any change in the body’s power to act, along with the mind’s idea of that change.</note> Bondage; for a person who is subject to emotions is not their own master, but is at the mercy of fortune. They are under fortune's power to such an extent that they are often forced, even though they see what is better for themselves, to follow what is worse. <note>Spinoza is referencing a famous line by the Roman poet Ovid: "I see the better and I approve it, but I follow the worse."</note> I have proposed to demonstrate the cause of this state, and what the emotions have of good or evil, in this Part. But before I begin, I would like to say a few words by way of preface regarding perfection and imperfection, and good and evil.

Anyone who has decided to make something and has finished it will say their work is perfect—not only the person themselves, but everyone who correctly knows the mind of the Author of that work and its goal, or believes they know it. For example, if someone sees a work (which I suppose is not yet finished) and knows the goal of the Author of that work is to build a house, they will say the house is imperfect; conversely, they will call it perfect as soon as they see the work brought to the conclusion its Author had decided to give it. But if someone sees a work the likes of which they have never seen before, and does not know the mind of the craftsman, they certainly cannot know whether that work is perfect or imperfect. And this seems to have been the original meaning of these words. 

But after humans began to form universal ideas, and to devise models for houses, buildings, towers, etc., and to prefer some models of things over others, it happened that everyone called that "perfect" which they saw agreed <note>original: "convenire"</note> with the universal idea they had formed of that kind of thing; and conversely, they called that "imperfect" which they saw agreed less with their conceived model, even if, in the opinion of the craftsman, it was fully com—

---

1) <note>These are names of various editors of Spinoza: Schmidt, Auerbach, Saisset. The First Edition (Ed. Pr.) reads "viderat" (had seen) instead of "videret" (would see).</note>

<summary>In this preface to Part IV, Spinoza introduces the concept of "Human Bondage"—the state of being ruled by emotions rather than reason—and begins to critique the subjective ways we define perfection and imperfection based on human-made models rather than nature itself.</summary>
<keywords>Bondage, Emotions, Perfection, Imperfection, Universal Ideas, Fortune, Ethics</keywords>

--- Page 128 ---
<meta>This page continues the Preface to Part IV. Spinoza is refining his definitions of perfection and imperfection, arguing that these are human comparisons rather than inherent qualities of things in Nature. He is connecting this to his earlier argument that God/Nature does not act with a "purpose" or "final cause."</meta>

### CONCERNING HUMAN BONDAGE.

...would be completely finished. Nor does there seem to be any other reason why people commonly call natural things—those not made by human hands—*perfect or imperfect*. For humans are accustomed to forming universal ideas of both *natural and artificial things*, which they treat as models for things. They believe that Nature (which they think acts for the sake of some purpose) looks upon these ideas and sets them before itself as *models* <note>original: "exemplaria"</note>. Therefore, when they see something happen in Nature that does not match the model they have conceived for that kind of thing, they believe that Nature itself has failed or blundered, and has left that thing imperfect. We see, then, that humans have become accustomed to calling natural things *perfect* or *imperfect* more from prejudice than from true knowledge of them.

For we showed in the *Appendix to the First Part* that Nature does not act for the sake of a purpose; *for that eternal and infinite Being, whom we call God or Nature, acts by the same necessity by which it exists.* For we have shown that it acts from the same necessity of nature from which it exists (<term>Proposition</term> 16, Part 1). Therefore, the reason or cause why God or Nature acts, and why it exists, is one and the same. Just as it exists for the sake of no purpose, it acts for the sake of no purpose; but as it has no beginning or end of existing, so it has none for acting.

*However, what is called a <term>final cause</term> is nothing but human appetite itself, insofar as it is considered as the starting point or primary cause of something.* <note>In traditional philosophy, a "final cause" is the goal or purpose for which something exists. Spinoza argues that purposes are just human desires projected onto the world.</note> *For example*, when we say that habitation was the final cause of this or that house, we surely understand nothing else than that a person, because they imagined the comforts of domestic life, had an appetite for building a house. Therefore, *habitation, insofar as it is considered as a final cause, is nothing but this particular appetite, which is in truth an <term>efficient cause</term>* <note>An "efficient cause" is the actual mechanism or force that produces an effect—in this case, the human desire that triggers the building process.</note>; it is considered as the first cause because people are generally ignorant of the causes of their own appetites. For, as I have often said, they are indeed conscious of their actions and appetites, but ignorant of the causes by which they are determined to want something.

Furthermore, as for the common saying that Nature sometimes fails or blunders and produces imperfect things, I count this among the fictions I dealt with in the Appendix to the First Part. *Therefore, perfection and imperfection are in reality only modes of thinking—that is, notions we are accustomed to creating because we compare individuals of the same species or genus with one another.* And for this reason I said above (Definition 6, Part 2) that I understand *reality* and *perfection* to be the same thing. For we are accustomed to referring all individual things in Nature to one genus, which is called the most general; namely, to the notion of <term>Being</term> <note>original: "Entis"</note>, which belongs to absolutely all individual things in Nature. Insofar, then, as we refer the individuals of Nature to this genus and compare them with one another, and find that some have more being or reality than others, to that extent we say some are more perfect than others; and insofar as we attribute to them something that involves negation, such as a limit, an end, lack of power, etc., to that extent we call them imperfect—not because they lack anything that belongs to them, or because Nature has blundered, but because they do not affect our <term>Mind</term> as strongly as those things we call perfect. For nothing belongs to the nature of anything except what follows from the necessity of the nature of its efficient cause, and whatever follows from the necessity of the nature of the efficient cause necessarily happens.

8

<summary>Spinoza explains that "perfection" and "imperfection" are subjective human labels based on comparisons, arguing that Nature acts by necessity rather than toward a goal or "final cause." He redefines the final cause as nothing more than human desire (appetite) and equates reality with perfection.</summary>
<keywords>final cause, efficient cause, perfection, imperfection, appetite, necessity of nature, Being, reality, modes of thinking</keywords>

--- Page 129 ---
<meta>This page continues from the Preface of Part IV, where Spinoza has been arguing that perfection and imperfection are not inherent qualities of things in nature, but rather labels humans apply when comparing things to a mental "model."</meta>

### ETHICS PART IV.

*Regarding good and evil, they also indicate nothing positive in things—considered, that is, in themselves—nor are they anything other than modes of thinking, or notions which we form because we compare things to one another. For one and the same thing can be at the same time good, evil, and even indifferent. For example, music is good for a person in a melancholy state, bad for one who is mourning; but for a deaf person, it is neither good nor bad. However, even though this is the case, we must still retain these words. For since we desire to form an idea of man as a model <note>original: "exemplar"</note> of human nature which we may look toward, it will be useful for us to retain these same words in the sense I have mentioned. Therefore, in what follows, I shall understand by "good" that which we certainly know to be a means for us to approach nearer and nearer to the model of human nature we set before ourselves. By "evil," on the other hand, I shall understand that which we certainly know to prevent us from reflecting that same model. Further, we shall say that men are more perfect or less perfect insofar as they approach nearer to or further from this same model. For it must be especially noted that when I say someone passes from a lesser to a greater perfection, and vice versa, I do not mean that he is changed from one essence or form into another. For example, a horse is destroyed just as much if it were changed into a human as if it were changed into an insect; rather, we conceive that his power of acting <note>Spinoza uses "power of acting" to describe a being's capacity to persist and thrive</note>, insofar as this is understood through his own nature, is increased or diminished. Finally, by perfection in general I shall understand reality, as I have said; that is, the essence of any thing insofar as it exists and operates in a certain way, without taking any account of its duration. For no singular thing can be called more perfect because it has persisted in existing for a longer time; indeed, the duration of things cannot be determined from their essence, since the essence of things involves no certain and determined time of existing. Instead, any thing—whether it be more perfect or less—will always be able to persist in existing by the same force by which it began to exist, so that in this regard all things are equal.*

->DEFINITIONS.<-

I. *By **good**, I shall understand that which we certainly know is useful to us.*
II. *By **evil**, however, I shall understand that which we certainly know prevents us from being masters of <note>or "attaining"</note> some good.*
(Regarding these, see the end of the preceding preface.)
III. *I call singular things **contingent** insofar as, while we attend to their essence alone, we find nothing that necessarily posits their existence, or that necessarily excludes it.*
IV. *I call the same singular things **possible** insofar as, while we attend to the causes by which they must be produced, we do not know whether those causes are determined to produce them.*
(In the first <term>Scholium</term> <note>A "Scholium" is an explanatory note or commentary added to a mathematical or logical proof</note> of Proposition 33, Part 1, I made no difference between "possible" and "contingent," because there was no need to distinguish them accurately there.)
V. *By **contrary affects** <note>In Spinoza’s vocabulary, an "affect" is an emotion or a state of being acted upon</note> in what follows, I shall understand those which pull a person in different directions, even though they are of the same kind—such as luxury and greed, which are species of love; they are not contrary by nature, but by accident.*
VI. *What I understand by an **affect toward a future, present, and past thing**, I have explained in the first and second Scholiums of Proposition 18, Part 3, which see.*

<summary>Spinoza defines "good" and "evil" as relative human concepts based on a "model of human nature," and clarifies that "perfection" is synonymous with "reality" or a thing's "power of acting." He also provides definitions for contingency, possibility, and conflicting emotions (affects) that will be used throughout Part IV.</summary>

<meta>This page continues from the Definitions section of Part IV, "Of Human Bondage." Spinoza is defining the terms he will use to explain how human emotions and the limits of our reason lead to a state of "bondage" or lack of power.</meta>

-># ON HUMAN BONDAGE<-

<header>ON HUMAN BONDAGE.</header>

(But it must be noted here, furthermore, that just as we can only distinctly imagine the distance of space up to a certain limit, we have a similar limit for the distance of time. That is, just as we are accustomed to imagine that all those objects which are more than two hundred feet away from us—or whose distance from our current location exceeds what we can imagine distinctly—are equally distant from us and as if they were on the same plane; so too, we imagine that all objects whose time of existence is separated from the present by a longer interval than we are accustomed to imagine distinctly, are equally far from the present, and we refer them, as it were, to a single moment of time.)

VII. By an **end** <note>original: "finem"; here meaning a goal or purpose</note> for the sake of which we do something, I understand **appetite** <note>original: "appetitum"; the internal drive or desire toward an object</note>.

VIII. By **virtue** and **power**, I understand the same thing; that is (*by Proposition 7, Part 3*), virtue, insofar as it relates to man, is the very essence or nature of man, insofar as he has the power of bringing about certain things which can be understood through the laws of his nature alone.

### A X I O M

No single thing exists in the Nature of things such that there does not exist another more powerful and stronger than it. Rather, whatever thing is given, there is another more powerful given by which that thing can be destroyed.

### P R O P O S I T I O N I

*Nothing that a false idea possesses of a positive nature is removed by the presence of the true, insofar as it is true.*

### D E M O N S T R A T I O N

Falsity consists only in the privation <note>meaning a lack or absence</note> of knowledge which inadequate ideas involve (*by Proposition 35, Part 2*), nor do they have anything positive in them because of which they are called false (*by Proposition 33, Part 2*); but on the contrary, insofar as they are related to God, they are true (*by Proposition 32, Part 2*). If, therefore, that which a false idea has of a positive nature were removed by the presence of the true, insofar as it is true, then a true idea would be removed by itself, which (*by Proposition 4, Part 3*) is absurd. Therefore, nothing that a false idea, etc. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### S C H O L I U M

This Proposition is understood more clearly from the second Corollary to Proposition 16, Part 2. For the **imagination** is an idea which indicates the present constitution of the human Body more than it indicates the nature of an external body; not indeed distinctly, but confusedly; from which it happens that the Mind is said to err. For example, when we look at the sun, we imagine it to be about two hundred feet away from us; in this we are deceived for only so long as we are ignorant of its true distance; but once the distance is known, the error is indeed removed, but not the imagination—that is, the idea of the sun, which explains its nature only insofar as the Body is affected by it. And so, although we may know its true distance, we will nonetheless imagine it to be near us. For as we said in the Scholium to Proposition 35, Part 2, we do not imagine the sun to be so near because we are ignorant of its true distance, but because the Mind conceives the size of the sun insofar as the Body is affected by it. Thus, when the rays of the sun, falling upon the surface of water, are reflected to our eyes, we imagine it just as if it were in the water

<summary>Spinoza defines virtue as power and explains that knowledge of the truth does not automatically eliminate sensory "imaginations," using the example of the sun appearing close even when we know it is far.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the demonstration of Proposition 1 in Part IV, discussing why knowing the truth does not automatically dispel false perceptions or "imaginations." It then moves into the fundamental laws of human existence within the vast system of Nature.</meta>

<header>ETHICS PART IV.</header>

...[the sun] would be, we imagine it [at that distance], even though we have known its true place; and so other imaginations by which the Mind is deceived—whether they indicate the natural constitution of the Body, or indicate that its power of acting is increased or diminished—are not contrary to the truth, nor do they vanish in the presence of the truth. It does happen, when we fear some evil falsely, that the fear vanishes upon hearing a true report; but it also happens, on the contrary, when we fear an evil that is certainly going to come, that the fear also vanishes upon hearing a false report. And thus imaginations do not vanish because of the presence of the true, insofar as it is true, but because other stronger imaginations occur, which exclude the present existence of the things we imagine, as we showed in Proposition 17, Part 2. <note>Spinoza’s point is that our perceptions are physical states of the body. Knowing a scientific fact (the truth) doesn't stop our eyes from seeing the sun as a small disk in the sky; only a more powerful mental image can shift our focus.</note>

### PROPOSITION II.

*We are passive <note>original: "patimur"; to be "passive" or to "suffer" a passion means to be acted upon by external forces rather than being the sole cause of our own actions</note> insofar as we are a part of Nature, which cannot be conceived through itself without others.*

#### DEMONSTRATION.

We are said to be passive when something arises in us of which we are only the partial cause (by Definition 2, Part 3), that is (by Definition 1, Part 3), something which cannot be deduced from the laws of our nature alone. We are passive, therefore, insofar as we are a part of Nature which cannot be conceived through itself without others. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### PROPOSITION III.

*The force by which a human persists in existing is limited, and is infinitely surpassed by the power of external causes.*

#### DEMONSTRATION.

This is clear from the Axiom of this part. For given a human, something else is given—say A—which is more powerful; and given A, another is then given—say B—more powerful than A itself, and so on to infinity. And therefore the power of a human is defined by the power of another thing, and is infinitely surpassed by the power of external causes. *Which was to be demonstrated.* <note>This is a humbling reminder of human vulnerability: no matter how strong we are, the universe around us is always infinitely stronger.</note>

### PROPOSITION IV.

*It is impossible for a human not to be a part of Nature, and for them to be unable to undergo any changes except those which can be understood through their own nature alone, and of which they are the adequate cause.*

#### DEMONSTRATION.

The power by which singular things, and consequently a human, preserves their being, is the very power of God or Nature (by the Corollary of Proposition 24, Part 1 <insert>1</insert>), not insofar as it is infinite, but insofar as it can be explained through the actual human essence (by Proposition 7, Part 3). Therefore, the power of a human, insofar as it is explained through their own actual essence, is part of the infinite power of God or Nature—that is (by Proposition 34, Part 1), its essence. This was the first point. Next, if it were possible <insert>2</insert> for a human to undergo <insert>2</insert> no changes except those which could be understood through the nature of the human alone, it would follow (by Propositions 4 and 6, Part 3) that they could not <insert>2</insert> perish, but would always necessarily exist; and this would have to follow from a cause...

2) First Edition: "might be" <note>original: "possit"; the editors suggest "posset" for better grammatical alignment with the hypothetical statement.</note>

<summary>Spinoza explains that humans are inevitably part of a larger natural system, meaning we are constantly affected by external forces that are far more powerful than ourselves. He argues that it is impossible for a human to be entirely self-sufficient or immortal, as we are subject to changes we cannot fully control.</summary>

--- Page 132 ---
<meta>This page concludes the demonstration of Proposition 4 of Part IV, which establishes that humans are necessarily a part of Nature and subject to its changes. It then proceeds to Propositions 5, 6, and 7, which analyze how the strength of our emotions (passions) is determined by external forces rather than our own willpower alone.</meta>

<header>ON HUMAN BONDAGE.</header>

...whether its power is finite or infinite; namely, either from the power of man alone, who would be able to remove other changes that could arise from external causes; or from the infinite power of Nature, by which all singular things <note>Individual objects or beings</note> would be so directed that man could suffer no other changes except those that serve his own preservation. But the first (by the preceding Proposition, the demonstration of which is universal and can be applied to all singular things) is absurd. Therefore, if it were possible <note>original: "posset"; see footnote 1</note> that man should suffer no changes except those that could be understood through the nature of man alone, and consequently (as we have already shown) that he should always necessarily exist; this would have to follow from the infinite power of God; and consequently (by Proposition 16, Part 1), from the necessity of the divine nature, insofar as it is considered affected by the idea of some man, the order of the whole of Nature, insofar as it is conceived under the attributes of Extension <note>The physical world</note> and Thought, would have to be deduced; and thus (by Proposition 21, Part 1 <note>original: "p. 1"; see footnote 2</note>) it would follow that man would be infinite, which (by the first part of this demonstration) is absurd. It is therefore impossible that man should suffer no other changes except those of which he himself is the <term>adequate cause</term> <note>An "adequate cause" is one where the effect can be clearly and distinctly understood through that cause alone; here, it means being the sole master of one's own changes.</note>. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### COROLLARY.

From this it follows that man is necessarily always subject to passions, follows the common order of Nature, and obeys the same, and adapts himself to it as far as the nature of things demands.

### PROPOSITION V.

*The force and growth of any passion <note>An emotion caused by external influence</note>, and its perseverance in existing, is defined not by the power with which we strive to persevere in existing, but by the power of an external cause compared with our own.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

The essence of a passion cannot be explained through our essence alone (by Definitions 1 and 2, Part 3), that is (by Proposition 7, Part 3), the power of a passion cannot be defined by the power with which we strive to persevere in our being, but (as was shown in Proposition 16, Part 2) it must necessarily be defined by the power of an external cause compared with our own. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### PROPOSITION VI.

*The force of any passion, or emotion <note>original: "affectûs"</note>, can surpass the rest of a man’s actions, or power, so that the emotion adheres stubbornly to the man.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

The force and growth of any passion, and its perseverance in existing, is defined by the power of an external cause compared with our own (by the preceding Proposition); and therefore (by Proposition 3 of this Part) it can surpass the power of man, etc. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### PROPOSITION VII.

*An emotion can only be restrained or removed by an emotion contrary to and stronger than the emotion to be restrained.*

<summary>Spinoza demonstrates that humans are inherently part of a larger natural system, meaning our emotions and survival are often dominated by external forces rather than our internal will. He concludes that a passion can only be overcome by an even stronger, opposing emotion.</summary>

--- Page 133 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical sequence of Spinoza's "Ethics," moving from the external forces that act upon human beings to how those forces manifest as emotions and our perception of "good" and "evil." It follows the demonstration that humans are necessarily subject to the laws of nature.</meta>

-># PART IV OF THE ETHICS<-

### DEMONSTRATION.

An <term>affect</term> <note>Spinoza uses "affect" (affectus) to describe both a physical change in the body and the corresponding idea of that change in the mind—essentially what we call an emotion or a feeling of being moved.</note>, insofar as it is related to the Mind, is an idea by which the Mind affirms a greater or lesser force of existence for its Body than it had before (*by the General Definition of the Affects, which is found near the end of the Third Part*). Therefore, when the Mind is troubled by some emotion, the Body is simultaneously affected by a state through which its power of acting is increased or diminished. Furthermore, this state of the Body (*by Proposition 5 of this Part*) receives from its own cause the force to persist in its being; consequently, it can neither be restrained nor removed except by a physical cause (*by Proposition 6, Part 2*), which affects the Body with a state that is contrary to it (*by Proposition 5, Part 3*) and stronger than it (*by the Axiom of this Part*). And so (*by Proposition 12, Part 2*) the Mind will be affected by the idea of a state that is stronger and contrary to the previous one—that is (*by the General Definition of the Affects*), the Mind will be affected by an emotion that is stronger and contrary to the previous one, which will exclude or remove the existence of the former. Therefore, an emotion can neither be removed nor restrained except by a contrary and stronger emotion. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### COROLLARY.

An emotion, insofar as it is related to the Mind, can neither be restrained nor removed except by the idea of a contrary state of the Body that is stronger than the emotion we are suffering. For the emotion we suffer can neither be restrained nor removed except by an emotion stronger than it and contrary to it (*by the preceding Proposition*), which is to say (*by the General Definition of the Affects*), except by the idea of a state of the Body that is stronger and contrary to the state we are suffering.

## PROPOSITION VIII.

*The knowledge of good and evil is nothing other than the emotion of Joy or Sadness, insofar as we are conscious of it.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

We call "good" or "evil" that which is useful for, or an obstacle to, the preservation of our being (*by Definitions 1 and 2 of this Part*), which is to say (*by Proposition 7, Part 3*), that which increases or diminishes, assists or restrains, our power of acting. Insofar, therefore, as we perceive that a certain thing affects us with <term>Joy</term> or <term>Sadness</term> <note>Spinoza uses these terms technically: Joy (Lætitia) is the transition to a greater perfection/power, and Sadness (Tristitia) is the transition to a lesser one.</note> (*see the Definitions of Joy and Sadness in the Scholium to Proposition 11, Part 3*), we call that thing good or evil; and thus the knowledge of good and evil is nothing other than the idea of Joy or Sadness, which necessarily follows from the emotion of Joy or Sadness itself (*by Proposition 22, Part 2*). But this idea is united to the emotion in the same way that the Mind is united to the Body (*by Proposition 21, Part 2*); that is (*as shown in the Scholium to that same Proposition*), this idea is not actually distinguished from the emotion itself—or (*by the General Definition of the Affects*) from the idea of the Body's state—except by a conceptual distinction. Therefore, this knowledge of good and evil is nothing other than the emotion itself, insofar as we are conscious of it. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

## PROPOSITION IX.

*An emotion whose cause we imagine to be present to us is stronger than if we imagined that cause not to be present.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

Imagination is the idea by which the Mind contemplates a thing as present (*see*

Therefore, an <term>affect</term> <note>Spinoza’s term for an emotion or a physical/mental reaction.</note> (*by the General Definition of Affects*) is an <term>imagination</term>, insofar as it indicates the constitution of the body. ### SCHOLIUM.

When I said above, in Proposition 18 of Part 3, that we are affected by the image of a future or past thing with the same affect as if the thing we imagine were present, I expressly warned that this is true only insofar as we attend to the image of the thing itself; for the image is of the same nature, whether we have imagined the thing [as existing] or not. However, I did not deny that this image is rendered weaker when we contemplate other things present to us which exclude the present existence of the future thing. I neglected to point this out then because I had decided to deal with the powers of the affects in this Part.

### COROLLARY.

The image of a future or past thing—that is, a thing which we contemplate with relation to a future or past time, excluding the present—is, all other things being equal, weaker than the image of a present thing; and consequently, an affect toward a future or past thing is, all other things being equal, more moderate than an affect toward a present thing.

## PROPOSITIO X.

*We are affected more intensely toward a future thing which we imagine will soon be present, than if we imagined its time of existing to be further distant from the present; we are also affected more intensely by the memory of a thing which we imagine has not long passed, than if we imagined it to have passed long ago.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

For insofar as we imagine a thing as soon to be present, or as having not long passed, we imagine by that very fact something that excludes the presence of the thing less than if we were to imagine that the time of its future existence is further distant from the present, or that it passed long ago (*as is self-evident*); and so (*by the preceding Proposition*), we will to that extent be more intensely affected toward it. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### SCHOLIUM.

From what we noted at Definition 6 of this Part <note>This refers to how humans perceive time and duration through the limit of their sensory imagination.</note>, it follows that we are affected equally weakly toward objects which are distant from the present by a longer interval of time than we can determine by imagining, even if we understand them to be separated from one another by a long interval of time.

## PROPOSITIO XI.

*The affect toward a thing which we imagine as <term>necessary</term> is, all other things being equal, more intense than toward a <term>possible</term> or <term>contingent</term> (that is, non-necessary) thing.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

## PROPOSITION XII.

->***An affect toward a thing which we know does not exist in the present, and which we imagine as possible, is, all other things being equal, more intense than toward a contingent thing.***<-

### DEMONSTRATION.

Insofar as we imagine a thing as <term>contingent</term> <note>Spinoza defines "contingent" as something we don't know for sure to be necessary or impossible based on its essence alone.</note>, we are affected by no image of another thing that posits the thing's existence (*by Definition 3 of this part*): but on the contrary (*according to the hypothesis*), we imagine certain things which exclude its present existence. But insofar as we imagine a thing to be <term>possible</term> <note>Spinoza distinguishes "possible" from "contingent" here: a possible thing is one where we imagine causes that might produce it, though we aren't certain.</note> in the future, to that extent we imagine certain things which posit its existence (*by Definition 4 of this part*), that is (*by Proposition 18, Part 3*), things which foster Hope or Fear; and thus an affect toward a possible thing is more vehement. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### COROLLARY.

->***An affect toward a thing which we know does not exist in the present, and which we imagine as contingent, is much more moderate than if we were to imagine the thing as present to us.***<-

### DEMONSTRATION.

An affect toward a thing which we imagine to exist in the present is more intense than if we were to imagine it as future (*by the Corollary to Proposition 9 of this part*), and it is much more vehement if we were to imagine the future time to be far 1) from the present (*by Proposition 10 of this part*). Therefore, an affect toward a thing whose time of existing we imagine to be far distant from the present is much more moderate than if we were to imagine it as present; and nevertheless (*by the preceding Proposition*), it is more intense than if we were to imagine the same thing as contingent; and thus the affect toward a contingent thing will be much more moderate than if we were to imagine the thing as present to us. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### PROPOSITION XIV.

*A true knowledge of good and evil, insofar as it is true, cannot restrain any affect, but only insofar as it is considered as an affect.*

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

An affect is an idea by which the Mind affirms a greater or lesser force of existence for its Body than before (*by the general Definition of the Affects*); and thus (*by Proposition 1 of this part*) it has nothing positive which could be removed by the presence of truth; and consequently a true knowledge of good and evil, insofar as it is true, can restrain no affect. But insofar as it is an affect (*see Proposition 8 of this part*), if it is stronger than the affect to be restrained, to that extent only (*by Proposition 7 of this part*) will it be able to restrain the affect. <note>Spinoza is arguing that "reason" or "truth" alone has no power over our emotions unless that reason itself becomes a powerful emotional experience.</note> *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### PROPOSITION XV.

*Desire, which arises from a true knowledge of good and evil, can be extinguished or restrained by many other Desires which arise from the affects by which we are conflicted.*

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

From the true knowledge of good and evil, insofar as this (*by Proposition 8 of this part*) is an affect, there necessarily arises <term>Desire</term> <note>original: *Cupiditas*; for Spinoza, this is the very essence of a person as they strive to persist in their own being.</note> (*by the 1st Definition of the Affects*), which is greater as the affect from which it arises is greater (*by Proposition 37, Part 3*). But because this Desire (*by hypothesis*) arises from the fact that we truly understand something, it follows that it exists in us insofar as we act (*by Proposition 1 <insert>per Prop. 3.</insert> <note>The editor notes that while the original edition cited Proposition 1, the scholar Sigwart suggests Proposition 3 is more accurate here.</note> of Part 3*), and therefore must be understood through our essence alone (*by Definition 2, Part 3*); and consequently (*by Proposition 7, Part 3*) its force and growth must be defined by human power alone. Furthermore, the Desires which arise from the affects by which we are conflicted are greater as these affects are more vehement; and thus their force and growth (*by Proposition 5 of this part*) must be defined by the power of external causes, which, if compared to ours, indefinitely surpasses our power (*by Proposition 3 of this part*). And so Desires arising from similar affects can be more vehement than that which arises from a true knowledge of good and evil, and therefore (*by Proposition 7 of this part*) they will be able to restrain or extinguish it. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### PROPOSITION XVI.

*The Desire which arises from a knowledge of good and evil, insofar as this knowledge regards the future, can more easily be restrained or extinguished by the Desire for things that are pleasant in the present.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

The <term>affect</term> <note>Spinoza uses "affect" to describe both a physical state and the mental idea of that state—essentially what we call an emotion or a "feeling" that changes our capacity to act.</note> toward a thing which we imagine to be in the future is weaker than toward a thing which is present (*by the Corollary to Proposition 9 of this part*). ## PROPOSITION XVII.

*Desire which arises from a true knowledge of good and evil, insofar as this knowledge concerns <term>contingent</term> <note>A "contingent" thing is one we do not currently see as necessary or impossible; it is something that might or might not happen.</note> things, can be restrained much more easily by a Desire for things that are present.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

This Proposition is demonstrated in the same way as the preceding Proposition, using the Corollary to Proposition 12 of this part.

### SCHOLIUM.

With these points, I believe I have shown the cause why humans are moved more by opinion than by true Reason, and why the true knowledge of good and evil excites disturbances of the mind and often yields to every kind of lust. From this arises that saying of the Poet: 1) <note>original: "Video meliora proboque, deteriora sequor." Ovid, Metamorphoses, VII. 20–21. This is a famous literary expression of the "weakness of will."</note> *I see the better path and approve of it, yet I follow the worse.* The author of Ecclesiastes 2) <note>Ecclesiastes 1:18.</note> also seems to have had the same thing in mind when he said, *He who increases knowledge increases sorrow.* <note>original: "Qui auget scientiam, auget dolorem."</note>

I do not say these things for the purpose of concluding that it is better to be ignorant than to know, or that there is no difference between a wise man and a fool in governing their affects; rather, I say them because it is necessary to know both the power and the <term>lack of power</term> <note>Spinoza uses the word "impotentia" (lack of power or "bondage") to describe when we are ruled by external circumstances rather than our own reason.</note> of our nature. We must do this so that we may determine what Reason can and cannot do in governing the affects. I have already said that in this Part I would deal only with human lack of power; for I have decided to treat the power of Reason over the affects separately <note>This refers to Part V of the Ethics, where Spinoza discusses human freedom.</note>.

## PROPOSITION XVIII.

*Desire which arises from Joy is, all other things being equal, stronger than Desire which arises from Sadness.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

Desire is the very essence of a human being (*by the 1st Definition of the Affects*); that is (*by Proposition 7, Part 3*), the effort <note>Often referred to as "conatus"—the innate drive of every being to continue existing.</note> by which a human being strives to persevere in their being. Therefore, Desire which arises from Joy is assisted or increased by the affect of Joy itself (*by the Definition of Joy, which see in the Scholium to Proposition 11, Part 3*); on the other hand, that which arises from Sadness is diminished or restrained by the affect of Sadness itself (*by the same Scholium*). And so the force of Desire arising from Joy must be defined by both human power and the power of an external cause; whereas that arising from Sadness must be defined by human power alone. Therefore, the former is stronger than the latter. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### SCHOLIUM.

In these few words, I have explained the causes of human lack of power and inconstancy, and why

->OF HUMAN BONDAGE.<-

...why humans do not follow the precepts of Reason, I have explained. It now remains for me to show what it is that Reason prescribes to us, and which <term>affects</term> <note>Spinoza uses "affects" to refer to emotions or feelings that increase or decrease our body's power to act.</note> agree with the rules of human Reason, and which, on the contrary, are opposed to them. But before I begin to demonstrate these things in our lengthy <term>Geometric order</term> <note>Spinoza wrote his "Ethics" like a math textbook, using "propositions," "demonstrations," and "axioms" to prove his points logically.</note>, I wish first to briefly show the dictates of Reason themselves here, so that what I feel may be more easily perceived by everyone. 

Since Reason demands nothing against Nature, it therefore demands that everyone love themselves, seek their own <term>advantage</term> <note>original: "utile." This refers to what is truly beneficial for a person’s well-being and growth.</note> (that which is truly advantageous), and desire everything that truly leads a person to greater perfection; and absolutely, that everyone should endeavor to preserve their own being, as far as it lies within them. This is indeed as necessarily true as that the whole is greater than its part (*See Proposition 4, Part 3*). 

Next, since virtue (*by Definition 8 of this part*) is nothing other than acting according to the laws of one's own nature, and no one endeavors to preserve their being (*by Proposition 7, Part 3*) except according to the laws of their own nature; it follows from this: 
*First*, that the foundation of virtue is the very <term>striving</term> <note>original: "conatus." This is a central concept for Spinoza—the innate drive of every thing to continue existing and flourishing.</note> to preserve one's own being, and that happiness consists in the fact that a person is able to preserve their being. 
*Secondly*, it follows that virtue is to be desired for its own sake, and that there is nothing more excellent or more useful to us than virtue, for the sake of which it should be desired. 
*Thirdly* and finally, it follows that those who kill themselves are weak in mind and are entirely conquered by external causes that are contrary to their nature. 

Furthermore, from Postulate 4 of Part 2, it follows that we can never bring it about that we need nothing outside ourselves to preserve our being, or that we live in such a way that we have no dealings with things outside of us. Indeed, if we consider our Mind, our intellect would be more imperfect if the Mind were alone and understood nothing besides itself. There are, therefore, many things outside of us that are useful to us and which should therefore be desired. Of these, none can be thought of as more excellent than those which entirely agree with our nature. For if, for example, two individuals of entirely the same nature are joined together, they compose an individual twice as powerful as each one alone. 

To a human being, therefore, nothing is more useful than another human being. Humans, I say, can wish for nothing more excellent to preserve their being than that everyone should so agree in all things that the Minds and Bodies of all compose, as it were, one Mind and one Body; and that all together, as much as they can, should endeavor to preserve their being, and all together seek the common advantage of all. From this it follows that humans who are governed by Reason—that is, humans who seek their own advantage under the guidance of Reason—desire nothing for themselves that they do not also desire for the rest of humanity, and therefore they are just, faithful, and honest.

These are those dictates of Reason which I proposed to show here in a few words before I began to demonstrate them in a longer order. I have done this so that, if possible, I might gain the attention of those who believe that this principle—namely, that everyone is bound to seek their own advantage—is the foundation of impiety, rather than of virtue and piety. Therefore, after having briefly shown that the matter is quite the opposite, I proceed to demonstrate the same thing by the same path we have followed until now.

<summary>Spinoza argues that Reason dictates self-love and the pursuit of one's own advantage as the foundation of virtue, and that this pursuit naturally leads to social harmony and justice.</summary>
<keywords>Reason, virtue, self-preservation, conatus, advantage, social contract, ethics, human nature, Spinoza</keywords>

--- Page 139 ---
<meta>This page continues from the discussion of the dictates of Reason. Spinoza is establishing that the foundation of virtue is the drive for self-preservation, and that no one acts against their own life except through external force.</meta>

# ETHICS PART IV.

## PROPOSITION XIX.

***Each person, by the laws of their own nature, necessarily desires what they judge to be good and avoids what they judge to be evil.***

### DEMONSTRATION.

The knowledge of good and evil is (*by Proposition 8 of this part*) the very <term>affect</term> <note>Spinoza uses "affect" to describe an emotion or feeling that changes one’s power to act.</note> of Joy or Sadness, insofar as we are conscious of it. Therefore (*by Proposition 28, Part 3*), each person necessarily desires what they judge to be good, and conversely avoids what they judge to be evil. But this <term>appetite</term> <note>original: "appetitus." This is the drive or desire that constitutes a person's very essence.</note> is nothing other than the very essence or nature of a human being (*see the Definition of Appetite in the Note to Proposition 9, Part 3, and the first Definition of the Affects*). Therefore, each person, solely from the laws of their own nature, necessarily desires or avoids these things, etc. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

## PROPOSITION XX.

***The more each person strives—and is able—to seek their own advantage, which is to say, to preserve their own being, the more they are endowed with virtue; and conversely, insofar as each person neglects their own advantage, that is, neglects to preserve their own being, they are weak.***

### DEMONSTRATION.

<term>Virtue</term> <note>original: "virtus." For Spinoza, virtue is synonymous with "power"—specifically the power to act according to one's own nature.</note> is human power itself, which is defined by the human essence alone (*by Definition 8 of this part*); that is (*by Proposition 7, Part 3*), it is defined by the <term>striving</term> <note>original: "conatus." The innate drive of every living thing to persist in its own existence.</note> alone whereby a person endeavors to persist in their own being. Therefore, the more each person strives and is able to preserve their own being, the more they are endowed with virtue. Consequently (*by Propositions 4 and 6, Part 3*), insofar as someone neglects to preserve their own being, they are weak. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### SCHOLIUM.

No one, therefore, neglects to seek their own advantage or to preserve their own being unless they are overcome by external causes that are contrary to their own nature. No one, I say, avoids food or kills themselves out of the necessity of their own nature; rather, they are compelled by external causes. This can happen in many ways. For example, someone might kill themselves because they are forced by another who twists their right hand (which happened to be holding a sword) and forces them to direct the blade toward their heart. Or, they might be compelled by the command of a tyrant, as <term>Seneca</term> <note>Seneca the Younger was a Roman Stoic philosopher and advisor to Emperor Nero, who eventually ordered Seneca to commit suicide.</note> was, to open his own veins—that is, he desired to avoid a greater evil by choosing a lesser one. Or finally, it may happen because hidden external causes so influence a person's imagination and affect their body that it takes on a different nature contrary to its original one, the <term>idea</term> <note>An "idea" here refers to the mental representation of the body's state.</note> of which cannot exist in the mind (*by Proposition 10, Part 3*). However, that a person should, by the necessity of their own nature, strive not to exist or to be changed into another form, is as impossible as for something to come from nothing. Anyone can see this with even a small amount of reflection.

## PROPOSITION XXI.

***No one can desire to be blessed*** <note>1) original: "beatum." In early modern philosophy, "blessedness" refers to the highest state of human happiness and mental peace.</note> ***, to act well, and to live well, without at the same time desiring to be, to act, and to live—that is, to actually exist.***

---

1) Gfrörer: *blessed*. <note>A textual variant from the historian A.F. Gfrörer's edition.</note>

<summary>Spinoza argues that the fundamental law of human nature is self-preservation, which he equates with virtue and power. He asserts that suicide is never a result of a person's own nature but is always caused by external pressures or the breakdown of the body's original state.</summary>
<keywords>self-preservation, conatus, virtue, power, suicide, Seneca, joy and sadness, human essence, blessedness</keywords>

--- Page 140 ---
<meta>This page continues Spinoza’s geometric proof regarding human nature. He is transitioning from the definition of virtue as power to the specific claim that self-preservation is the logical and ontological starting point for all human excellence and rationality.</meta>

->ON HUMAN BONDAGE.<-

### DEMONSTRATION.

The demonstration of this Proposition—or rather, the matter itself—is self-evident, and also follows from the definition of <term>Desire</term> <note>original: "Cupiditas." For Spinoza, this is the conscious drive or "appetite" of a human being.</note>. For Desire (*by Definition 1 of the Affects*) for living happily or well, acting, etc., is the very essence of a human being; that is (*by Proposition 7, Part 3*), the <term>striving</term> <note>original: "conatus." The inherent tendency of every being to persist in its own existence.</note> by which each person strives to preserve their own being. Therefore, no one can desire [to be happy or act well without also desiring to exist], etc. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

## PROPOSITION XXII.

***No virtue can be conceived prior to this one (namely, the striving to preserve oneself).***

### DEMONSTRATION.

The striving to preserve oneself is the very essence of a thing (*by Proposition 7, Part 3*). If, therefore, any virtue could be conceived prior to this one—namely, this striving—then the very essence of the thing would be conceived as prior to itself (*by Definition 8 of this part*), which (*as is self-evident*) is absurd. Therefore, no virtue [can be conceived prior], etc. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### COROLLARARY.

The striving to preserve oneself is the first and only foundation of virtue. For no other principle can be conceived prior to this one (*by the preceding Proposition*), and without it (*by Proposition 21 of this part*), no virtue can be conceived.

## PROPOSITION XXIII.

***A human being, insofar as they are determined to perform an action because they have <term>inadequate ideas</term> <note>Confused or incomplete thoughts that arise from being acted upon by external things, rather than from our own clear logic.</note>, cannot be said absolutely to act from virtue; they can only be said to do so insofar as they are determined by what they understand.***

### DEMONSTRATION.

Insofar as a human being is determined to act because they have inadequate ideas, they are to that extent <term>passive</term> <note>original: "patitur." This indicates a state of suffering or being "acted upon" by the outside world.</note> (*by Proposition 1, Part 3*); that is (*by Definitions 1 and 2, Part 3*), they do something that cannot be perceived through their essence alone, which is to say (*by Definition 8 of this part*) it does not follow from their virtue. But insofar as they are determined to act because they understand, they to that extent <term>act</term> (*by the same Proposition 1, Part 3*); that is (*by Definition 2, Part 3*), they do something that is perceived through their essence alone, or (*by Definition 8 of this part*) which follows <term>adequately</term> <note>An effect is "adequate" when it can be fully explained by the person's own nature as the cause.</note> from their virtue. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

## PROPOSITION XXIV.

***To act absolutely from virtue is nothing else in us but to act, to live, and to preserve one’s being (these three signify the same thing) by the guidance of <term>Reason</term> <note>original: "Ratio." The mental capacity to perceive things clearly and see their necessary connections.</note>, based on the foundation of seeking what is useful to oneself.***

### DEMONSTRATION.

To act absolutely from virtue is nothing else (*by Definition 8 of this part*) than to act by the laws of one's own nature. But we act only insofar as we understand (*by Proposition 3, Part 3*); therefore, to act from virtue is nothing else in us but to act, to live, and to preserve one's being by the guidance of Reason, and that (*by the Corollary to Proposition 22 of this part*) based on the foundation of seeking what is useful to oneself. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

## PROPOSITION XXV.

***No one strives to preserve their being for the sake of any other thing.***

<summary>Spinoza argues that the fundamental drive for self-preservation (conatus) is the primary basis of all virtue and that acting "virtuously" is synonymous with living according to the guidance of Reason.</summary>
<keywords>self-preservation, conatus, virtue, Reason, inadequate ideas, essence, human bondage</keywords>

--- Page 141 ---
<meta>This page continues the demonstrations of Part IV of the Ethics, specifically concluding the proof for Proposition 25 and establishing the relationship between reason, understanding, and the knowledge of God as the ultimate human good.</meta>

# ETHICS PART IV.

->D E M O N S T R A T I O N.<-

The <term>striving</term> <note>original: "Conatus." The internal force or drive that defines a thing’s essence.</note> by which each thing strives to persevere in its own being is defined solely by the essence of the thing itself (*by Proposition 7, Part 3*); and given that essence alone, and not the essence of another thing, it follows necessarily (*by Proposition 6, Part 3*) that each person strives to preserve their own being. Furthermore, this Proposition is evident from the Corollary to Proposition 22 of this Part. For if a human being strove to preserve their being for the sake of another thing, then that other thing would be the primary foundation of virtue (*as is self-evident*), which (*by the aforementioned Corollary*) is absurd. Therefore, no one [strives to preserve] his own being [for the sake of another thing], etc. <note>original: "Q. E. D." stands for "Quod Erat Demonstrandum," or "Which was to be demonstrated."</note>

### P R O P O S I T I O N  X X V I.

*Whatever we strive for through <term>Reason</term> <note>original: "Ratio." In Spinoza's system, this is the second level of knowledge, characterized by clear and distinct ideas and logical progression.</note> is nothing other than understanding; nor does the <term>Mind</term>, insofar as it uses Reason, judge anything else to be useful to itself except that which leads to understanding.*

->D E M O N S T R A T I O N.<-

The striving to preserve oneself is nothing but the very essence of the thing (*by Proposition 7, Part 3*), which, insofar as it exists as such, is conceived to have the power to persevere in existing (*by Proposition 6, Part 3*) and to do those things that necessarily follow from its given nature (*see the Definition of Appetite in the Scholium to Proposition 9, Part 3*). But the essence of Reason is nothing other than our Mind, insofar as it understands clearly and distinctly (*see its definition in the 2nd Scholium to Proposition 40, Part 2*); Therefore (*by Proposition 40, Part 2*), whatever we strive for through Reason is nothing other than understanding. Furthermore, since this striving of the Mind, by which the Mind—insofar as it reasons—strives to preserve its being, is nothing other than understanding (*by the first part of this proof*); this striving for understanding is therefore (*by the Corollary to Proposition 22 of this Part*) the first and only foundation of virtue; nor shall we strive to understand things for the sake of any other end (*by Proposition 25 of this Part*). On the contrary, the Mind, insofar as it reasons, can conceive nothing as being good for itself except that which leads to understanding (*by Definition 1 of this Part*). *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### P R O P O S I T I O N  X X V I I.

*We know nothing for certain to be good or evil, except that which truly leads to understanding, or that which can prevent us from understanding.*

->D E M O N S T R A T I O N.<-

The Mind, insofar as it reasons, desires nothing other than to understand, nor does it judge anything else to be useful to itself except that which leads to understanding (*by the preceding Proposition*). But the Mind (*by Propositions 41 and 43, Part 2, and also see its Scholium*) has no certainty of things except insofar as it has adequate ideas, or (*which is the same thing by the 2nd Scholium to Proposition 40, Part 2*) insofar as it reasons. Therefore, we know nothing for certain to be good except that which truly leads to understanding; and conversely, [we know] that to be evil which can prevent us from understanding. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### P R O P O S I T I O N  X X V I I I.

*The highest good of the Mind is the knowledge of God, and the highest virtue of the Mind is to know God.*

<summary>Spinoza argues that since the essence of reason is understanding, the mind's primary utility and virtue lie in what facilitates understanding. He concludes that the highest form of this understanding, and thus the supreme human good, is the knowledge of God.</summary>
<keywords>Reason, Understanding, Knowledge of God, Highest Good, Virtue, Self-Preservation, Logic, Spinoza</keywords>

--- Page 142 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical proofs of Part IV. While the previous page discussed the drive for self-preservation and the role of reason, this page provides the demonstration for Proposition 28 (regarding the knowledge of God) and establishes how our relationships with external things are governed by shared nature (Propositions 29–31).</meta>

# ON HUMAN BONDAGE.

### DEMONSTRATION.
The highest thing that the Mind can understand is God, which is (by *Definition 6, Part 1*) a Being absolutely infinite, and without whom (by *Proposition 15, Part 1*) nothing can be nor be conceived; and therefore (by *Propositions 26 and 27 of this Part*) the highest utility of the Mind, or (by *Definition 1 of this Part*) its highest good, is the knowledge of God. Furthermore, the Mind acts only insofar as it understands (by *Propositions 1 and 3, Part 3*), and only to that extent (by *Proposition 23 of this Part*) can it be said absolutely to act from <term>virtue</term> <note>original: "virtus." For Spinoza, virtue is not a moral trait but the power or ability of a thing to act according to its own nature.</note>. Therefore, the absolute virtue of the Mind is to understand. But the highest thing that the Mind can understand is God (as we have just demonstrated); therefore, the highest virtue of the Mind is to understand or know God. <note>original: "Q. E. D." (Quod erat demonstrandum) — which was to be demonstrated.</note>

## PROPOSITION XXIX.
*Any <term>singular thing</term> <note>original: "Res singularis." An individual, finite object or being in the world, such as a specific person, animal, or rock.</note> whose nature is entirely different from our own can neither aid nor restrain our power of acting, and absolutely no thing can be good or bad for us unless it has something in common with us.*

### DEMONSTRATION.
The power by which any singular thing—and consequently (by the *Corollary to Proposition 10, Part 2*) a human being—exists and operates is determined only by another singular thing (by *Proposition 28, Part 1*), whose nature (by *Proposition 6, Part 2*) must be understood through the same <term>attribute</term> <note>original: "attributum." The fundamental characteristics of reality, like "extension" (physicality) or "thought."</note> through which human nature is conceived. Therefore, our power of acting, however it is conceived, can be determined—and consequently aided or restrained—by the power of another singular thing that has something in common with us, and not by the power of a thing whose nature is entirely different from ours. And because we call that "good" or "bad" which is the cause of Joy or Sadness (by *Proposition 8 of this Part*), which is to say (by the *Scholium to Proposition 11, Part 3*) that which increases or diminishes, aids or restrains our power of acting; therefore, a thing whose nature is entirely different from our own can be neither good nor bad for us. Which was to be demonstrated.

## PROPOSITION XXX.
*No thing can be bad through that which it has in common with our nature; but insofar as it is bad for us, it is to that extent contrary to us.*

### DEMONSTRATION.
We call "bad" that which is the cause of Sadness (by *Proposition 8 of this Part*), which is (by the definition found in the *Scholium to Proposition 11, Part 3*) that which diminishes or restrains our power of acting. If, therefore, some thing were bad for us through that which it has in common with us, then that thing could diminish or restrain the very thing it has in common with us, which (by *Proposition 4, Part 3*) is absurd <note>Spinoza argues that nothing can contain the principle of its own destruction; commonality implies a shared essence that wouldn't "fight" itself.</note>. Therefore, no thing can be bad for us through that which it has in common with us; but on the contrary, insofar as it is bad—that is (as we have just shown), insofar as it can diminish or restrain our power of acting—it is to that extent (by *Proposition 5, Part 3*) contrary to us. Which was to be demonstrated.

## PROPOSITION XXXI.
*Insofar as a thing agrees with our nature, it is to that extent necessarily good.*

<summary>This page argues that the highest human good is the knowledge of God and explains that we are only affected (helped or hindered) by things that share our fundamental nature.</summary>
<keywords>knowledge of God, highest good, virtue, singular things, common nature, power of acting, joy and sadness, ethics</keywords>

--- Page 143 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical progression of Part IV, specifically focusing on Propositions 31 through 33. It builds upon the idea that things are "good" for us only when they harmonize with our nature, and begins to explore how human passions (emotions where we are passive) lead to discord and inconstancy rather than harmony.</meta>

# PART IV OF THE ETHICS.

### DEMONSTRATION.

Insofar as a thing agrees with our nature, it cannot (*by the preceding Proposition*) be bad. It will therefore necessarily be either good or indifferent. If the latter is assumed—namely, that it is neither good nor bad—then (*by Definition 1*<sup>1)</sup> *of this Part*) nothing would follow from its nature that serves the preservation of our nature; that is (*by the hypothesis*), nothing that serves the preservation of the thing’s own nature. But this is absurd (*by Proposition 6, Part 3* <note>Proposition 6 of Part 3 states that "Everything, insofar as it is in itself, endeavors to persist in its own being."</note>); therefore, insofar as it agrees with our nature, it must necessarily be good. <note>original: "Q. E. D." (Quod erat demonstrandum) — which was to be demonstrated.</note>

### COROLLARARY.

From this it follows that the more a thing agrees with our nature, the more useful or better it is for us; and conversely, the more useful a thing is for us, the more it agrees with our nature. For insofar as it does not agree with our nature, it will necessarily be different from our nature or contrary to it. If it is different, then (*by Proposition 29 of this Part*) it can be neither good nor bad; if, however, it is contrary, it will also be contrary to that which agrees with our nature—that is (*by the preceding Proposition*), contrary to the good, or bad. Nothing, therefore, can be good except insofar as it agrees with our nature; and thus, the more a thing agrees with our nature, the more useful it is, and vice versa. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

## PROPOSITION XXXII.

*Insofar as humans are subject to <term>passions</term> <note>original: "passiones." For Spinoza, passions are emotions or states where the cause lies outside ourselves, making us "passive" rather than active participants in our lives.</note>, they cannot to that extent be said to agree in nature.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

Things that are said to agree in nature are understood to agree in power (*by Proposition 7, Part 3*); but not in <term>impotence</term> <note>original: "impotentia." This refers to a lack of power or a state of being acted upon, rather than acting from one's own internal logic.</note> or negation, and consequently (*see the Scholium to Proposition 3, Part 3*<sup>2)</sup>) nor even in passion; therefore, humans, insofar as they are subject to passions, cannot be said to agree in nature. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### SCHOLIUM.

The matter is also evident through itself; for he who says that white and black agree only in the fact that neither is red, is asserting absolutely that white and black agree in nothing. So also, if someone says that a stone and a human agree only in this: that both are finite, weak, or that neither exists by the necessity of its own nature, or finally that both are indefinitely surpassed by the power of external causes—he is asserting entirely that a stone and a human agree in nothing. For things that agree only in a negation, or in what they do not have, truly agree in nothing at all.

## PROPOSITION XXXIII.

*Humans can differ in nature insofar as they are conflicted by affects that are passions; and to that extent, one and the same human is changeable and inconstant.*

---

2) Dutch Version — First Edition: *Part 2.*

<summary>This page demonstrates that things are only beneficial to humans when they share a common nature, and argues that human passions represent a lack of power that prevents people from truly "agreeing" with one another.</summary>
