<meta>This page concludes the proof of Proposition 33 and begins Proposition 34. It marks a transition from discussing how things that agree with our nature are good, to how human passions (emotions where we are passive) cause conflict and make people contrary to one another.</meta>

<page-num>129</page-num>
<header>OF HUMAN BONDAGE.</header>
<sig>9</sig>

### DEMONSTRATION.

The nature or essence of the affects <note>Spinoza uses "affects" (affectus) to describe emotions or states of being that change our power to act.</note> cannot be explained through our essence or nature alone (*by Definitions 1 and 2, Part 3*); rather, it must be defined by the power—that is (*by Proposition 7, Part 3*), the nature—of external causes compared with our own. From this it follows that there are as many species of each affect as there are species of objects by which we are affected (*see Proposition 56, Part 3*); and that humans can be affected by one and the same object in different ways (*see Proposition 51, Part 3*), and to that extent they differ in nature; and finally, that one and the same person (*by the same Proposition 51, Part 3*) can be affected in different ways toward the same object, and to that extent be inconstant, etc. <note>original: "Q. E. D." (Quod erat demonstrandum) — which was to be demonstrated.</note>

## PROPOSITION XXXIV.

***Insofar as men are torn by affects that are passions, they can be contrary to one another.***

### DEMONSTRATION.

For example, a man—let us say Peter—can be the cause of Paul feeling sadness, because Peter has something similar to a thing Paul hates (*by Proposition 16, Part 3*); or because Peter alone possesses a thing that Paul also loves (*see Proposition 32, Part 3 with its Scholium <note>A "scholium" is an explanatory note or commentary added to a logical proof.</note>*), or for other causes (*see the main ones in the Scholium to Proposition 55, Part 3*). And so it will happen (*by Definition 7 of the Affects*) that Paul hates Peter; and consequently it will easily follow (*by Proposition 40, Part 3 with its Scholium*) that Peter, in turn, hates Paul. Therefore (*by Proposition 39, Part 3*), they will strive to inflict evil on each other—that is (*by Proposition 30 of this Part*), they are contrary to one another. But the affect of Sadness is always a passion (*by Proposition 59, Part 3*); therefore, insofar as men are torn by affects that are passions, they can be contrary to one another. <note>original: "Q. E. D."</note>

### SCHOLIUM.

I said that Paul hates Peter because he imagines that Peter possesses the same thing that Paul also loves. At first glance, it seems to follow from this that these two, because they love the same thing—and consequently because they agree in nature—are harmful to one another. If this were true, Propositions 30 and 31 of this Part would be false. 

But if we wish to examine the matter with an even hand, we will see that these points are entirely consistent. For these two are not troublesome to one another insofar as they agree in nature—that is, insofar as both love the same thing—but rather insofar as they differ from one another. For insofar as both love the same thing, the love of each is fostered by that very fact (*by Proposition 31, Part 3*); that is (*by Definition 6 of the Affects*), the Joy of each is fostered. Therefore, it is far from the case that they are troublesome to one another insofar as they love the same thing and agree in nature. 

Rather, the cause of this conflict, as I said, is nothing other than the fact that they are assumed to differ in nature. For we assume that Peter has the idea of the loved thing already possessed, while Paul, on the contrary, has the idea of the loved thing as lost. As a result, the latter is affected by Sadness, and the former by Joy, and to that extent they are contrary to one another. In this way, we can easily show that the remaining causes of hate depend solely on this: that men differ in nature, and not on that in which they agree.

<summary>Spinoza argues that human conflict does not arise from our shared nature or common loves, but from the differing ways our passions (like jealousy or loss) make us react to external objects. He uses the example of Peter and Paul to show that while they both love the same thing, it is the difference in their possession of it that creates enmity.</summary>

<keywords>passions, affects, human conflict, Peter and Paul, discord, joy, sadness, shared nature, essence</keywords>

Here, he pivots to the solution: how Reason acts as a unifying force that brings human beings into harmony.</meta>

<header>ETHICS PART IV.</header>

### PROPOSITION XXXV.
*Insofar as men live under the guidance of Reason, to that extent only do they always necessarily agree in nature.*

#### DEMONSTRATION.
Insofar as men are conflicted by affects <note>Spinoza uses "affects" (affectus) to mean emotions or states that increase or decrease our power.</note> that are passions, they can be different in nature (*by Proposition 33 of this part*) and contrary to one another (*by the preceding Proposition*). But men are said to act only insofar as they live under the guidance of Reason (*by Proposition 3, Part 3*); and thus, whatever follows from human nature, insofar as it is defined by Reason, must be understood through human nature alone as its proximate cause (*by Definition 2, Part 3*). But because everyone, by the laws of their own nature, desires what they judge to be good and tries to remove what they judge to be evil (*by Proposition 19 of this part*); and since, moreover, what we judge to be good or evil by the dictate of Reason is necessarily good or evil (*by Proposition 41, Part 2*); therefore, men, insofar as they live under the guidance of Reason, do only those things which are necessarily good for human nature, and consequently for every man; that is (*by the Corollary to Proposition 31 of this part*), things that agree with the nature of every man. And thus men also always necessarily agree among themselves, insofar as they live under the guidance of Reason. <note>original: "Q. E. D." (Quod erat demonstrandum) — which was to be demonstrated.</note>

#### COROLLARY I.
There is no individual thing in Nature more useful to a man than a man who lives under the guidance of Reason. For that which most agrees with his nature is most useful to a man (*by the Corollary to Proposition 31 of this part*), which is (as is self-evident) another man. But a man acts absolutely from the laws of his own nature when he lives under the guidance of Reason (*by Definition 2, Part 3*), and to that extent only does he always necessarily agree with the nature of another man (*by the preceding Proposition*); therefore, among individual things, nothing is more useful to a man than a man, etc. Which was to be demonstrated.

#### COROLLARY II.
When each man most seeks his own advantage for himself, then men are most useful to one another. For the more each one seeks his own advantage and tries to preserve himself, the more he is endowed with virtue (*by Proposition 20 of this part*), or, what is the same thing (*by Definition 8 of this part*), the greater power he has to act from the laws of his own nature, that is (*by Proposition 3, Part 3*), to live under the guidance of Reason. But men agree most in nature when they live under the guidance of Reason (*by the preceding Proposition*); therefore (*by the preceding Corollary*), men will be most useful to one another when each one most seeks his own advantage. Which was to be demonstrated.

#### SCHOLIUM <note>A scholium is an informal explanatory note or commentary added to a formal proof.</note>.
What we have just shown is also daily attested by experience with so many and such clear examples that it is on nearly everyone's lips: "Man is a God to man." <note>original: "hominem homini Deum esse"</note> Yet it rarely happens that men live under the guidance of Reason; instead, they are so constituted that they are mostly envious and a nuisance to one another. Nevertheless, they can hardly lead a solitary life...

<summary>Spinoza argues that while emotions can divide people, living according to Reason creates necessary harmony and cooperation. He concludes that nothing is more beneficial to a human being than another human being who lives rationally.</summary>

<keywords>Reason, harmony, human nature, cooperation, virtue, self-preservation, social utility, Spinoza</keywords>

--- Page 146 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that human beings are most useful to one another when they live according to Reason. Having established that Reason leads to harmony, Spinoza now argues that the "highest good" found through Reason is not a finite resource to be fought over, but a shared benefit that increases as more people participate in it.</meta>

<page-num>131</page-num>
<header>OF HUMAN BONDAGE.</header>

...cannot, so that to most people that definition—that man is a **social animal** <note>original: "animal sociale". This is a famous definition of humanity originating with Aristotle, suggesting that humans naturally thrive in communities.</note>—has been very pleasing; and in truth, the matter is such that many more advantages than disadvantages arise from the common society of men. Therefore, let the **Satirists** <note>Writers who mock human folly and vice.</note> laugh as much as they like at human affairs, let the **Theologians** detest them, and let the **Melancholics** <note>In the 17th century, "melancholy" was seen as a temperament that led people to withdraw from society and view the world gloomily.</note> praise as much as they can a wild and uncultivated life, despising men and admiring beasts; they will still find by experience that men can much more easily provide for the things they need through mutual help, and that only by joined forces can they avoid the dangers that threaten everywhere. I will not even mention that it is much more excellent, and more worthy of our knowledge, to contemplate the deeds of men than those of beasts. But of these things, more at length elsewhere.

### PROPOSITION XXXVI.

*The **highest good** <note>original: "Summum bonum". The ultimate goal or greatest possible benefit for a human being.</note> of those who follow virtue is common to all, and all can enjoy it equally.*

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

To act from **virtue** <note>original: "virtus". For Spinoza, virtue is synonymous with power and the ability to act according to one's own nature.</note> is to act under the guidance of **Reason** <note>original: "Ratio"</note> (*by Proposition 24 of this part*), and whatever we strive to do from Reason is to understand (*by Proposition 26 of this part*); and so (*by Proposition 28 of this part*) the highest good of those who follow virtue is to know God, that is (*by Proposition 47, Part 2 and its Scholium*) a good which is common to all men, and can be possessed equally by all men, insofar as they are of the same nature. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

->SCHOLIUM.<-

But if anyone should ask: what if the highest good of those who follow virtue were not common to all? Would it not then follow, as before (*see Proposition 34 of this part*), that men who live under the guidance of Reason—that is (*by Proposition 35 of this part*), men insofar as they agree in nature—would be contrary to one another? To this let the answer be: it arises not by accident, but from the very nature of Reason, that man’s highest good is common to all, because it is deduced from the very **essence** <note>original: "essentia"</note> of humanity, insofar as it is defined by Reason; and because man could neither be nor be conceived if he did not have the power of enjoying this highest good. For it belongs (*by Proposition 47, Part 2*) to the essence of the human Mind to have an adequate knowledge of the eternal and infinite essence of God.

### PROPOSITION XXXVII.

*The good which everyone who follows virtue seeks for himself, he will also desire for the rest of mankind, and the more so, the greater the knowledge of God he possesses.*

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

Men, insofar as they live under the guidance of Reason, are most useful to man (*by Corollary 1, Proposition 35 of this part*); and thus (*by Proposition 19 of this part*) under the guidance of Reason we will necessarily strive to bring it about that men live under the guidance of Reason. But the good which everyone who lives by the dictate of Reason—that is (*by Proposition 24 of this part*), who follows virtue—seeks for himself, is to understand (*by Proposition 26 of this part*); therefore, the good which everyone who follows virtue seeks for himself, he will also desire for the rest of mankind. Furthermore, **Desire** <note>original: "Cupiditas". One of the primary affects in Spinoza's system, representing the striving of a being to persist in its existence.</note>, insofar as it is related to the Mind, is the Mind’s very...

<summary>Spinoza argues that the highest good—knowledge of God—is inherently sharable and that rational individuals naturally desire for others to achieve the same virtuous, rational state they seek for themselves.</summary>
<keywords>social animal, highest good, Reason, virtue, knowledge of God, human essence, desire, mutual aid</keywords>

--- Page 147 ---
<meta>This page concludes the proof of Proposition 36 and begins Scholium 1 of Proposition 37. It transitions from the abstract proof that the highest good is shared by all to a practical discussion of how this manifests in human society through religion, piety, and social bonds.</meta>

<page-num>132</page-num>
<header>ETHICS PART IV.</header>

...essence (by the *1st Definition of the Emotions*); but the essence of the Mind consists in knowledge (by *Prop. 11, Part 2*), which involves the knowledge of God (by *Prop. 47, Part 2*), and without which (by *Prop. 15, Part 1*), it can neither be nor be conceived; and therefore, the more the essence of the Mind involves the knowledge of God, the greater will be the **Desire** <term>original: Cupiditas. For Spinoza, desire is not just a feeling, but the very essence of a person insofar as it is determined to act.</term> by which he who pursues virtue desires for another the good which he seeks for himself. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

->*Alternatively.*<-

The good which a man seeks for himself and loves, he will love more constantly if he sees others love the same (by *Prop. 31, Part 3*); and so (by the *Corollary of the same Prop.*) he will endeavor that the rest should love the same; and because this good (by the *preceding Prop.*) is common to all, and all can enjoy it, he will therefore endeavor (by the same reasoning) that all may enjoy the same, and (by *Prop. 37, Part 3*) the more so, the more he enjoys this good. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

->SCHOLIUM I.<-

He who strives from emotion <term>original: affectus. Spinoza uses this term to describe changes in our power to act, triggered by interactions with the world.</term> alone that others should love what he loves, and that others should live according to his own temperament, acts from impulse alone, and is therefore hateful—especially to those who find pleasure in other things, and who therefore also strive and endeavor with the same impulse that others should, on the contrary, live according to *their* temperament. Furthermore, since the highest good which men seek from emotion is often such that only one person can possess it, it happens that those who love are not at peace with themselves; while they delight in recounting the praises of the thing they love, they fear being believed. 

But he who strives to lead others by **Reason** <note>Reason, in Spinoza’s view, is our ability to understand the world through logical connections and common properties rather than just reacting to external stimuli.</note> acts not by impulse, but humanely and kindly, and is most at peace with himself. Furthermore, whatever we desire and do of which we are the cause <sup>1)</sup> insofar as we have the idea of God, or insofar as we know God, I refer to **Religion**. Moreover, the desire to do good which is generated by our living under the guidance of Reason, I call **Piety**. Next, the desire by which a man who lives under the guidance of Reason is bound to join others to himself in friendship, I call **Honesty** <note>original: "Honestas". In this context, it refers to "honorableness" or the quality of being socially respectable and forming stable bonds.</note>, and that which men who live under the guidance of Reason praise, I call **honorable**; and on the contrary, that which is repugnant to the formation of friendship, I call **base** <note>original: "turpe". Often translated as "shameful" or "dishonorable."</note>. 

Besides these things, I have also shown what the foundations of the state are. Then, the difference between true virtue and **impotence** <note>original: "impotentia". A lack of power or self-control; the state of being ruled by external circumstances rather than one’s own internal reason.</note> is easily perceived from what has been said above: namely, that true virtue is nothing other than living by the guidance of Reason alone; and thus impotence consists in this alone—that a man allows himself to be led by things which are outside him, and is determined by them to do those things which the common constitution of external things, and not his own nature considered in itself, demands. 

And these are the things which I promised to demonstrate in the Scholium of Proposition 18 of this Part, from which it appears that the law against slaughtering beasts is founded more on vain superstition and womanish compassion <note>original: "muliebri misericordia". A 17th-century idiom used here to contrast sentimental pity with what Spinoza considers the "masculine" or rigorous application of Reason. He argues that we have more right over animals than they have over us because of our superior nature.</note> than on sound Reason. Reason indeed teaches us to seek our own utility <sup>2)</sup>, the necessity <sup>3)</sup> of joining with men...

---

1) First Edition: *for the sake of which.*
2) Dutch Version: *Reason teaches us to seek our utility, the necessity...* (cf. below Scholium to Prop. 10, Part 5). — Schmidt, Auerbach, Kirchmann: *Reason, for the sake of seeking our utility, [teaches] the necessity with...*
3) Saisset: *Reason [teaches] that the necessity of seeking our utility joins us with men.*

<summary>Spinoza argues that the highest good (knowledge of God) is shared and social, defining key ethical terms like Religion, Piety, and Honesty as products of Reason. He also distinguishes between true virtue (self-governance by Reason) and impotence (being controlled by external forces), dismissing sentimental treatment of animals in favor of human utility.</summary>
<keywords>Knowledge of God, Reason, Piety, Honesty, Virtue vs Impotence, Religion, Ethics of animals, Social bonds</keywords>

--- Page 148 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Scholium 1 for Proposition 37, focusing on the relationship between humans and animals, before transitioning into Scholium 2, which lays the groundwork for Spinoza's political theory and the transition from a state of nature to a civil state.</meta>

<page-num>133</page-num>
<header>ON HUMAN BONDAGE.</header>

to join; but not with animals <term>original: brutis. Spinoza uses this to refer to non-human animals which, in his view, lack the capacity for human reason.</term>, or things whose nature is different from human nature. Rather, we have the same right over them that they have over us. Indeed, because each person’s right is defined by their virtue or power, humans have a far greater right over animals than animals have over humans. I do not deny that animals feel; but I deny that we are therefore forbidden from looking out for our own benefit, using them as we please, and treating them in whatever way suits us best; since they do not agree with us in nature, and their emotions <term>original: affectus. These are the states of being affected by external things that increase or decrease our power to act.</term> are by nature different from human emotions (see the Scholium to Proposition 57, Part 3). It remains for me to explain what is just, what is unjust, what is sin, and finally what is merit. But on these matters, see the following Scholium.

### SCHOLIUM II.

In the Appendix to Part One, I promised to explain what praise and blame, what merit and sin, and what just and unjust are. As for praise and blame, I explained them in the Scholium to Proposition 29, Part 3; as for the rest, this will now be the place to speak of them. But first, a few things must be said *concerning the natural and civil state of man*.

Each individual exists by the supreme right of Nature, and consequently, by the supreme right of Nature, each person does those things that follow from the necessity of their own nature; and thus, by the supreme right of Nature, each individual judges what is good and what is evil, looks out for their own advantage according to their own disposition (see Propositions 19 and 20 of this part), avenges themselves <note>The Latin *sese vindicat* can mean both to claim one's rights and to take revenge; in this context of the "state of nature," it implies taking justice into one's own hands.</note> (see Corollary 2 of Proposition 40, Part 3), and endeavors to preserve what they love and destroy what they hate (see Proposition 28, Part 3). Now, if humans lived according to the guidance of Reason, each would enjoy (by the 1st Corollary to Proposition 35 of this part) this right of theirs without any harm to another. But because they are subject to emotions (by the Corollary to Proposition 4 of this part), which far surpass human power or virtue (by Proposition 6 of this part), they are therefore often drawn in different directions (by Proposition 33 of this part) and are contrary to one another (by Proposition 34 of this part), even while they stand in need of mutual help (by the Scholium to Proposition 35 of this part). In order, therefore, that humans may be able to live in harmony and be of help to one another, it is necessary for them to give up their natural right and reassure one another that they will do nothing that could result in harm to another. How this can be done—namely, how humans, who are necessarily subject to emotions (by the Corollary to Proposition 4 of this part), and are inconstant and changeable (by Proposition 33 of this part), can reassure one another and have faith in one another—is clear from Proposition 7 of this part and Proposition 39 of Part 3. Namely, that no emotion can be restrained except by a stronger emotion contrary to the one being restrained, and that everyone abstains from causing harm out of fear of a greater harm. By this law, therefore, a Society <term>original: Societas. This refers to the organized community or the "State."</term> can be established, provided it claims for itself <margin>1) First Edition: *vendicet* (claims), as in other places, although just now [it used] *vindicat* and soon [will use] *vindicandi*.</margin> the right that each individual has to avenge themselves and to judge what is good and evil; and thus possesses the power to prescribe a common way of life and to make laws—strengthening them not by Reason, which cannot restrain emotions (by the Scholium to Proposition 17 of this part), but by threats. This Society, [maintaining] itself by laws and power...

<summary>Spinoza argues that humans have a natural right over animals due to their greater power and differing natures, then transitions into a discussion of the social contract, explaining that society is formed when individuals trade their natural rights for collective security maintained by the fear of punishment.</summary>
<keywords>natural right, animal ethics, social contract, emotions, state of nature, civil state, justice, law, power, Spinoza</keywords>

--- Page 149 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous page's Scholium to Proposition 37, where Spinoza distinguishes between the "state of nature"—a hypothetical condition without laws—and the "civil state" or organized society.</meta>

<page-num>134</page-num>
<header>PART IV OF THE ETHICS.</header>

the power of preserving [society] is called the *State* <note>original: "Civitas"</note>, and those who are protected by its law are called *Citizens* <note>original: "Cives"</note>. From this, we can easily understand that in the state of nature, nothing exists that is good or evil by universal agreement; for everyone in the state of nature looks only to their own advantage, deciding what is good or evil based on their own temperament and only as far as they consider their own benefit, and is not bound by any law to obey anyone but themselves. Therefore, in the state of nature, sin <note>Spinoza uses the word "peccatum," which can mean sin, error, or a legal "wrongdoing."</note> cannot be conceived. But it can be conceived in a civil state, where what is good and what is evil is decided by common consent, and everyone is bound to obey the State. Therefore, *sin* is nothing other than disobedience, which is punished by the law of the State alone. On the other hand, obedience is considered *merit* in a citizen, because they are judged worthy of enjoying the benefits of the State. Furthermore, in the state of nature, no one is the master of anything by common consent, nor is there anything in Nature that can be said to belong to one person and not another; rather, all things belong to everyone. Consequently, in the state of nature, we cannot conceive of a desire to "give everyone their own" or to take away from someone what belongs to them. That is, in the state of nature, nothing happens that can be called *just* or *unjust*. But this does happen in the civil state, where it is decided by common consent what belongs to one person and what belongs to another. From this, it is clear that just and unjust, sin and merit, are external notions <note>They are social constructs based on human agreement, not inherent properties of nature.</note>, not attributes that explain the nature of the Mind. But enough about these matters.

## PROPOSITION XXXVIII.

*Whatever so disposes the human Body that it can be affected in more ways, or which makes it capable of affecting external bodies in more ways, is useful to man; and it is more useful as the Body is rendered more capable of being affected in more ways and of affecting other bodies; conversely, what makes the Body less capable of these things is harmful.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

The more the Body is rendered capable of these things, the more the Mind is rendered capable of perceiving (by Proposition 14, Part 2); and therefore, that which disposes the Body in this way, and makes it capable of these things, is necessarily good or useful (by Propositions 26 and 27 of this part), and it is more useful as it can make the Body more capable of these things; and conversely (by the same Proposition 14, Part 2, inverted, and Propositions 26 and 27 of this part), it is harmful if it makes the Body less capable of these things. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

## PROPOSITION XXXIX.

*Those things are good which cause the ratio of motion and rest, which the parts of the human Body have to one another, to be preserved; and conversely, those things are evil which cause the parts of the human Body to have a different ratio of motion and rest to one another.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

The human Body needs many other bodies to be preserved (by Postulate 4, Part 2). But that which constitutes the form of the human Body consists in this: that its parts communicate their motions to one another in a certain fixed ratio (by the Definition before Lemma 4, which see after...

<summary>Spinoza defines the State, Citizens, and legal concepts like sin and justice as social constructs rather than natural laws, then argues that physical health and the preservation of the body's internal balance are essential for the Mind's power.</summary>

<meta>This page continues from the discussion of the "civil state" and the definition of justice. It concludes Proposition 39, which defines good and evil in terms of the physical preservation of the body's structural integrity, and begins the discussion of social harmony and the emotional states of joy and sadness.</meta>

<page-num>135</page-num>
<header>ON HUMAN BONDAGE.</header>

(referring to Proposition 13, Part 2). Therefore, those things which bring it about that the proportion of motion and rest, which the parts of the human body have toward one another, is preserved, also preserve the form <note>In Spinoza’s physics, "form" refers to the specific structural pattern or "ratio" of movement that makes a body what it is.</note> of the human body. Consequently, they ensure (by *Postulates 3 and 6 of Part 2*) that the human body can be affected in many ways, and that it can affect external bodies in many ways; thus (by the *preceding Proposition*), these things are good. On the other hand, those things which cause the parts of the human body to acquire a different proportion of motion and rest also cause (by the same *Definition in Part 2*) the human body to take on another form. This means (*as is self-evident, and as we warned at the end of the preface to this part*) that the human body is destroyed, and consequently rendered entirely unfit to be affected in many ways. Therefore (by the *preceding Proposition*), these things are evil. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### SCHOLIUM.

To what extent these things can harm or benefit the Mind will be explained in the Fifth Part. But here it should be noted that I understand the body to die when its parts are so disposed that they acquire a different proportion of motion and rest toward one another. For I do not dare to deny that the human body—while the circulation of the blood and other functions for which a body is thought to be alive are retained—can nonetheless be changed into another nature entirely different from its own. No reason compels me to establish that a body does not die unless it is changed into a corpse; indeed, experience itself seems to suggest otherwise. For it sometimes happens that a person undergoes such changes that I would not easily call him the same man. I have heard stories of a certain Spanish Poet <note>This likely refers to the poet Luis de Góngora, who suffered a loss of memory later in life.</note> who had been seized by illness, and although he recovered from it, he remained so oblivious to his past life that he did not believe the plays and tragedies he had written were his own; he truly could have been considered a grown-up infant if he had also forgotten his native tongue. And if this seems incredible, what shall we say of infants? A man of advanced age believes their nature to be so different from his own that he could not be persuaded he was ever an infant, were it not for the conjecture he makes about himself based on others. But, lest I provide material for the superstitious to raise new questions, I prefer to leave these matters in the open.

## PROPOSITION XL.

*Those things which lead to the common society of men, or which bring it about that men live in harmony, are useful; conversely, those things are evil which bring discord into the State <note>original: "Civitas"</note>.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

For those things which cause men to live in harmony also cause them to live under the guidance of Reason (by *Prop. 35 of this part*), and are therefore (by *Props. 26 and 27 of this part*) good. Conversely (by the same reasoning), those things are evil which stir up discord. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

## PROPOSITION XLI.

*Joy <note>original: "Lætitia"</note> is not directly evil, but good; Sadness <note>original: "Tristitia"</note>, however, is directly evil.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

Joy (by *Prop. 11, Part 3, with its Scholium*) is an affect by which

<meta>This page continues the exploration of human affects (emotions) from a physical and ethical perspective. Having defined "good" as that which preserves the body’s proportion of motion and rest, Spinoza now evaluates specific emotional states—Cheerfulness, Melancholy, Titillation, and Pain—based on how they affect the body's overall balance and power of acting.</meta>

<page-num>136</page-num>
<header>ETHICS PART IV.</header>

the body’s power of acting is increased or aided; Sadness, on the other hand, is an affect by which the body’s power of acting is diminished or restrained; and therefore (*by Proposition 38 of this part*) Joy is directly good, etc. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### PROPOSITION XLII.

*Cheerfulness <note>original: "Hilaritas"</note> cannot have excess, but is always good; and conversely, Melancholy <note>original: "Melancholia"</note> is always evil.*

#### DEMONSTRATION.

Cheerfulness (*see its definition in the Scholium to Proposition 11, Part 3*) is Joy which, insofar as it relates to the Body, consists in this: that all parts of the Body are equally affected. This means (*by Proposition 11, Part 3*) that the Body’s power of acting is increased or aided such that all its parts maintain the same proportion of motion and rest <term>motion and rest: the physical relationship between parts of a body that determines its identity and health</term> toward one another. And so (*by Proposition 39 of this part*), Cheerfulness is always good and cannot have excess. But Melancholy (*whose definition can also be found in the same Scholium to Proposition 11, Part 3*) is Sadness which, insofar as it relates to the Body, consists in this: that the Body’s power of acting is absolutely diminished or restrained; and therefore (*by Proposition 38 of this part*) it is always evil. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### PROPOSITION XLIII.

*Titillation <note>original: "Titillatio"; a localized pleasure or excitement</note> can have excess and be evil; but Pain <note>original: "Dolor"; localized physical or mental suffering</note> can be good to the extent that Titillation or Joy is evil.*

#### DEMONSTRATION.

Titillation is Joy which, insofar as it relates to the Body, consists in one or several of its parts being affected more than the rest (*see its definition in the Scholium to Proposition 11, Part 3*). The power of this affect can be so great that it overcomes the other actions of the Body (*by Proposition 6 of this part*) and clings to it stubbornly. This prevents the Body from being capable of being affected in many other ways; and therefore (*by Proposition 38 of this part*) it can be evil. Next, Pain—which on the contrary is Sadness—cannot be good when considered in itself alone (*by Proposition 41 of this part*). But because its force and increase are defined by the power of an external cause compared with our own (*by Proposition 5 of this part*), we can conceive of infinite degrees and modes of the forces of this affect (*by Proposition 3 of this part*). Thus, we can conceive of a degree of Pain that is able to restrain Titillation so that it does not have excess, and to that extent (*by the first part of this Proposition*) ensure the Body is not rendered less capable; and consequently, to that extent, it will be good. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### PROPOSITION XLIV.

*Love and Desire can have excess.*

#### DEMONSTRATION.

Love is Joy (*by Definition 6 of the Affects*) accompanied by the idea of an external cause. Therefore, Titillation (*by the Scholium to Proposition 11, Part 3*) accompanied by the idea of an external cause is Love; and so Love (*by the preceding Proposition*) can have excess. Furthermore, Desire is greater as the affect from which it arises is greater (*by Proposition 37, Part 3*). Therefore, just as an affect (*by Proposition 6 of this part*) can overcome the rest of a man's actions, so too can

<summary>Spinoza distinguishes between "Cheerfulness," which affects the whole body and is always good, and "Titillation," which is localized and can be harmful if it dominates other functions. He argues that localized pain can be beneficial if it checks excessive local pleasure and restores the body's overall balance.</summary>

--- Page 152 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of how specific emotions affect the human body and mind. It moves from discussing "Titillation"—a localized pleasure that can be excessive—to the broader social and moral implications of Hatred and Joy.</meta>

<page-num>137</page-num>
<header>ON HUMAN BONDAGE.</header>

Desire <note>original: "Cupiditas"</note> which arises from that same affect <note>referring back to Titillation from the previous page</note> can exceed all other Desires and can therefore have the same excess that we showed Titillation to have in the preceding Proposition. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### SCHOLIUM.

Cheerfulness <note>original: "Hilaritas"</note>, which I have said is good, is more easily conceived <note>understood as a concept</note> than observed in reality. For the affects <note>emotions or physical states</note> with which we struggle daily are usually related to some one part of the Body that is affected more than the rest. Therefore, these affects usually have an excess and hold the Mind so focused on the contemplation of a single object that it cannot think of others. Although humans are subject to many affects, and it is rare to find someone always struggling with one and the same emotion, there are nevertheless those to whom one particular affect stubbornly clings. We see, for instance, men so affected by a single object that even when it is not present, they believe they have it right before them. When this happens to someone who is not asleep, we say they are "delirious" or "insane." 

Similarly, those who burn with Love and dream night and day only of a mistress or a harlot are also believed to be insane, because they usually provoke laughter in others. However, when a greedy man <note>original: "avarus"</note> thinks of nothing but profit or money, or an ambitious man thinks only of glory, they are not believed to be delirious. This is because they are usually a nuisance and are judged worthy of Hate. But in reality, Greed, Ambition, and Lust <note>original: "Libido"</note> are species of delirium <note>a state of mental confusion or obsession</note>, even though they are not categorized as "diseases" by common medical standards.

## PROPOSITION XLV.

*Hatred can never be good.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

We strive to destroy the person whom we hate (*by Proposition 39, Part 3*); that is, we strive for something that is evil (*by Proposition 37 of this part*). Therefore, Hatred can never be good. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### SCHOLIUM.

Note that here and in the following, by "Hatred" I mean only that which is directed toward human beings.

### COROLLARIUM I.

Envy, Derision, Contempt, Anger, Revenge, and the remaining affects that relate to Hate or arise from it, are evil. This is also clear from Proposition 39, Part 3 and Proposition 37 of this part.

### COROLLARIUM II.

Whatever we desire as a result of being affected by hate is base <note>morally low or shameful</note> and, in a State <term>State: "Civitas," referring to a politically organized community or civil society</term>, is unjust. This is also clear from Proposition 39, Part 3 and from the definitions of "base" and "unjust" found in the Scholia of Proposition 37 of this part.

### SCHOLIUM.

Between Derision <note>mockery or ridicule</note> (which I said was evil in the first Corollary) and laughter, I recognize a great difference. For laughter, as well as joking, is pure Joy <note>original: "Lætitia"</note>; and so, provided it is not excessive, it is good in itself (*by Proposition 41 of this part*). Surely nothing but a grim and gloomy superstition forbids us from being delighted. For why should it be more fitting to extinguish hunger and thirst than to drive away melancholy? This is my reasoning, and this is how I have settled my mind.

<summary>Spinoza argues that localized obsessions like greed or lust are forms of madness, and that hatred is always ethically bad because it seeks destruction. He distinguishes between harmful mockery and healthy laughter, defending the latter as a natural good.</summary>
<keywords>hatred, madness, greed, ambition, laughter, joy, civil justice, ethics, human bondage</keywords>

--- Page 153 ---
<meta>This page concludes the discussion on "Hilaritas" (Cheerfulness) and transitions into Spinoza’s practical ethics for social living. It specifically addresses how a rational person should handle negative emotions from others and why certain emotions like hope and fear are inherently flawed.</meta>

<page-num>138</page-num>
<header>ETHICS PART IV.</header>

No divinity, nor anyone but an envious person, takes pleasure in my lack of power or my misfortune; nor does such a being credit as a virtue our tears, sobs, fear, and other things of this kind, which are signs of an impotent mind <note>original: "animi impotentis"—here meaning a mind lacking self-control or power over its circumstances</note>. On the contrary, the greater the Joy <note>original: "Lætitia"</note> we are affected by, the more we pass to a greater perfection, which is to say, the more we necessarily participate in the divine nature. Therefore, to make use of things and to delight in them as much as possible—though not to the point of disgust, for that is not delight—is the mark of a wise man. It is, I say, the mark of a wise man to refresh and restore himself with moderate and pleasant food and drink, as well as with scents, the beauty of green plants, fine clothing, music, sports, theater, and other things of this kind, which everyone can use without harm to anyone else. For the human body is composed of many parts of a diverse nature, which constantly require new and varied nourishment so that the whole body may be equally capable of everything that can follow from its nature, and consequently, so that the Mind may also be equally capable of understanding many things at once. This system of living, therefore, agrees best both with our principles and with common practice. Thus, if any way of life is best, this one is, and it is to be commended in every way; nor is there any need to treat these matters more clearly or at greater length.

# PROPOSITION XLVI.

->*He who lives by the guidance of Reason endeavors, as much as he can, to repay another’s Hatred, Anger, Contempt, etc., toward him with Love, or Nobility.*<-

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

All affects of Hatred are evil (*by the first Corollary of the preceding Proposition*); and so, he who lives by the guidance of Reason will endeavor, as much as he can, to ensure he is not troubled by affects of Hatred (*by Proposition 19 of this part*), and consequently (*by Proposition 37 of this part*) he will also endeavor to ensure that no one else suffers those same affects. But Hatred is increased by reciprocal Hatred, and on the contrary, can be extinguished by Love (*by Proposition 43, Part 3*), so that Hatred passes into Love (*by Proposition 44, Part 3*). Therefore, he who lives by the guidance of Reason will endeavor to repay another's Hatred, etc., with Love in return, that is, with Nobility <note>original: "Generositate"—Spinoza defines this specifically as the desire to join others in friendship and aid them through reason</note> (*see the definition in the Scholium to Proposition 59, Part 3*). Which was to be demonstrated.

->SCHOLIUM.<-

He who wishes to avenge injuries with reciprocal Hatred lives a truly miserable life. But he who, on the contrary, strives to conquer Hatred with Love certainly fights with joy and confidence; he resists one person or many with equal ease, and requires the help of fortune as little as possible. Those whom he conquers yield joyfully, not because they lack strength, but because their strength has increased. All these things follow so clearly from the definitions of Love and Intellect alone, that there is no need to demonstrate them individually.

# PROPOSITION XLVII.

->*The affects of Hope and Fear cannot be good in themselves.*<-

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

The affects of Hope and Fear do not exist without Sadness <note>original: "Tristitia"—often translated as Pain or Sorrow</note>. For Fear is (*by the 13th Definition of the Affects*) Sadness, and Hope (*see the Explanation of the 12th and 13th Definitions*) [cannot exist without Fear...]

<summary>Spinoza argues that a wise life involves moderate enjoyment of worldly pleasures to maintain a healthy body and mind. He also demonstrates that responding to hatred with love (nobility) is the most rational and effective way to live among others.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the analysis of specific emotions (affects) and their relationship to Reason. It focuses on why emotions rooted in Sadness, like Hope, Fear, Pity, and Remorse, are considered weaknesses or "evils" in Spinoza's ethical system, which prioritizes the power of the mind and the guidance of Reason.</meta>

<page-num>139</page-num>
<header>OF HUMAN BONDAGE.</header>

<note>This text begins mid-sentence, concluding a previous proof regarding Hope and Fear.</note>
...Definition of the Affects) <note>original: "Affect. Defin." — referring to the definitions at the end of Part 3</note> is not given without Fear; and therefore (*by Proposition 41 of this part*) these emotions cannot be good in themselves, but only insofar as they can restrain an excess of Joy (*by Proposition 43 of this part*). Which was to be demonstrated.

### SCHOLIUM.

To this it may be added that these emotions indicate a lack of knowledge and a lack of power in the Mind <note>original: "Mentis impotentiam" — a state where the mind is acted upon by external forces rather than acting from its own nature</note>; and for this reason, Confidence, Despair, Gladness, and Remorse are also signs of an impotent mind. For, although Confidence and Gladness <note>original: "Securitas et Gaudium" — here specifically meaning the joy that arises when a cause of doubt is removed</note> are emotions of Joy, they nevertheless suppose that Sadness has preceded them, namely Hope and Fear. Therefore, the more we endeavor to live by the guidance of Reason, the more we endeavor to depend less on Hope and to free ourselves from Fear, to command fortune as much as we can, and to direct our actions by the certain counsel of Reason.

# PROPOSITION XLVIII.

*The emotions of Overestimation and Disdain are always evil.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

For these emotions (*by Definitions 21 and 22 of the Affects*) are contrary to Reason; and therefore (*by Propositions 26 and 27 of this part*) they are evil. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

# PROPOSITION XLIX.

*Overestimation easily makes the man who is overestimated proud.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

If we see that someone, out of love, thinks more of us than is just, we will easily become glorified (*by the Scholium to Proposition 41, Part 3*), or be affected with Joy (*by Definition 30 of the Affects*); and we will easily believe the good that we hear predicated of ourselves (*by Proposition 25, Part 3*); and thus, out of love for ourselves, we will think more of ourselves than is just, which is to say (*by Definition 28 of the Affects*) we will easily become proud. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

# PROPOSITION L.

*Pity, in a man who lives by the guidance of Reason, is in itself evil and useless.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

For Pity <note>original: "Commiseratio"</note> (*by Definition 18 of the Affects*) is Sadness; and therefore (*by Proposition 41 of this part*) is in itself evil; but the good which follows from it—namely, that we endeavor to free the man we pity from his misery (*by Corollary 3 to Proposition 27, Part 3*)—we desire to do solely from the dictates of Reason (*by Proposition 37 of this part*); and only by the dictates of Reason can we do anything that we certainly know to be good (*by Proposition 27 of this part*); and thus pity, in a man who lives by the guidance of Reason, is in itself evil and useless. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### COROLLARARY.

From this it follows that a man who lives by the dictates of Reason endeavors, as much as he can, to ensure he is not touched by pity.

<header>ETHICS PART IV.</header>

### SCHOLIUM

He who rightly understands that all things follow from the necessity of the divine nature, and happen according to the eternal laws and rules of Nature, will indeed find nothing worthy of Hatred, Laughter, or Contempt, nor will he pity anyone; but, as much as human virtue allows, he will endeavor to do well and to rejoice. <note>original: "lætari" — This refers to Spinoza's concept of Joy as an increase in one's power of acting.</note>
To this we may add that he who is easily touched by the emotion of Pity <note>original: "Commiserationis" — Spinoza views pity as a weakness because it involves feeling the sadness of another, thereby decreasing one's own power.</note>, and is moved by the misery or tears of another, often does something of which he later repents; both because we do nothing out of emotion that we certainly know to be good, and because we are easily deceived by false tears. And here I speak expressly of the man who lives according to the guidance of Reason. For he who is moved neither by Reason nor by Pity to help others is rightly called inhuman, for (*by Proposition 27, Part 3*) he seems to be unlike a human being.

## PROPOSITION LI.

*Approval is not contrary to Reason, but can agree with it and arise from it.* <note>original: "Favor" — In this context, Favor means a positive regard or approval for someone who has helped another.</note>

### DEMONSTRATION.

For Approval is Love toward someone who has done good to another (*by Definition 19 of the Affects*); and therefore it can be referred to the Mind insofar as the Mind is said to act (*by Proposition 59, Part 3*), that is (*by Proposition 3, Part 3*), insofar as it understands; and consequently it agrees with Reason, etc. Which was to be demonstrated.

### Another Proof.

He who lives according to the guidance of Reason desires for another the good which he seeks for himself (*by Proposition 37 of this part*); therefore, from the fact that he sees someone doing good to another, his own effort to do good is aided, that is (*by the Scholium to Proposition 11, Part 3*) <note>The printed edition notes a reference to Prop. 11, Part 3</note> he will rejoice, and that (*by hypothesis*) accompanied by the idea of him who has done good to the other; and therefore (*by Definition 19 of the Affects*) he favors him. Which was to be demonstrated.

### SCHOLIUM.

Indignation, as it is defined by us (see Definition 20 of the Affects), is necessarily evil (*by Proposition 45 of this part*); but it should be noted that when the supreme power <note>original: "summa potestas" — the sovereign or the state authority</note>, held by the desire to preserve peace, punishes a citizen who has done an injury to another, I do not say that the state is "indignant" with the citizen; for it is not moved by Hatred to destroy the citizen, but is moved by Piety <note>original: "pietas" — here meaning a sense of duty or devotion to the public good</note> to punish him.

## PROPOSITION LII.

*Self-contentment can arise from Reason, and only that self-contentment which arises from Reason is the highest that can exist.* <note>original: "Acquiescentia in se ipso" — This is the satisfaction or peace of mind one feels when contemplating oneself and one's own power.</note>

### DEMONSTRATION.

Self-contentment is Joy arising from the fact that a man contemplates himself and his own power of acting (*by Definition 25 of the Affects*). But a man’s true power of acting, or virtue, is Reason itself (*by Proposition 3, Part 3*), which the man contemplates clearly and distinctly (*by Propositions 40 and 43, Part 2*); therefore, Self-contentment arises from Reason. Furthermore...

<header>ON HUMAN BONDAGE.</header>

...a person, while contemplating themselves, perceives clearly and distinctly, or adequately, nothing except those things that follow from their own power of acting (*by Definition 2, Part 3*), which is to say (*by Proposition 3, Part 3*) those things that follow from their power of understanding; and therefore, from this contemplation alone, the highest **Self-contentment** <note>original: "Acquiescentia" — Often translated as "self-satisfaction" or "peace of mind," this is the joy of recognizing one's own internal capacity to act and understand.</note> that can exist arises. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

->SCHOLIUM.<-

**Self-contentment** is in truth the highest thing we can hope for. For (*as we showed in Proposition 25 of this part*) no one strives to preserve their being for the sake of any other end; and because this Contentment is fostered and strengthened more and more by praise (*by Corollary to Proposition 53, Part 3*), and on the contrary (*by Corollary to Proposition 55, Part 3*) is more and more disturbed by blame, we are therefore most strongly guided by Glory, and can hardly bear a life lived in disgrace.

### PROPOSITION LIII.

*Humility is not a virtue; that is, it does not arise from Reason.*

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

**Humility** is a **Sadness** <note>original: "Tristitia" — In Spinoza's system, Sadness is defined as any transition of the mind to a state of lesser perfection or power.</note> which arises from a person contemplating their own lack of power (*by Definition of the Emotions*). However, insofar as a person knows themselves through true **Reason**, they are assumed to understand their own essence—that is (*by Proposition 7, Part 3*), their own power. Therefore, if a person, while contemplating themselves, perceives some lack of power, this does not come from the fact that they understand themselves, but (*as we showed in Proposition 55, Part 3*) from the fact that their power of acting is being restrained. If we suppose that a person conceives of their own lack of power because they understand something more powerful than themselves—by the knowledge of which they limit their own power of acting—then we conceive of nothing other than that the person understands themselves distinctly (*by Proposition 26 of this part*), <note>1)</note> and by this their power of acting is actually aided. Therefore, Humility, or the Sadness that arises from a person contemplating their own lack of power, does not arise from true contemplation or Reason; it is not a virtue, but a passion. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### PROPOSITION LIV.

*Repentance is not a virtue; that is, it does not arise from Reason. Rather, he who repents of an act is twice miserable or powerless.*

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

The first part of this is demonstrated like the preceding Proposition. The second part, however, is evident from the mere Definition of this emotion (*see Definition 27 of the Emotions*). For first, he allows himself to be conquered by an evil Desire, and then by Sadness.

->SCHOLIUM.<-

Because men rarely live according to the dictates of Reason, these two emotions—namely, Humility and Repentance—and besides these, **Hope** and **Fear**, bring more utility than harm. And so, if one must sin, it is better to sin in that direction. For if all those who are powerless in mind were equally proud, they would be ashamed of nothing, and they...

<header>ETHICS PART IV.</header>

### SCHOLIUM.

One who rightly knows that all things follow from the necessity of the divine nature, and happen according to the eternal laws and rules of Nature, will surely find nothing worthy of **Hate**, **Laughter**, or **Contempt**, nor will he feel pity for anyone. Instead, as far as human virtue allows, he will strive *to act well*, as they say, *and to rejoice*. <note>original: "bene agere... et lætari" — This is a famous Spinozistic maxim; rather than dwelling on the suffering of others (pity), the rational person focuses on positive action and the joy found in understanding.</note> Added to this is the fact that a person who is easily touched by the affect of **Pity** <note>original: "Commiseratio" — Spinoza views pity as a form of sadness, and since sadness diminishes our power to act, he argues it is not a virtue in itself, though it may be useful for those who do not live by reason.</note>, and moved by another's misery or tears, often does something for which he later repents; both because we do nothing from an affect that we certainly know to be good, and because we are easily deceived by false tears. Here I am speaking specifically of the person who lives according to the guidance of **Reason**. For one who is moved neither by Reason nor by Pity to help others is rightly called inhuman, for (*by Proposition 27, Part 3*) he seems unlike a human being.

## PROPOSITION LI.

*Favor is not contrary to Reason, but can agree with it and arise from it.* <note>term: "Favor" — Spinoza defines this specifically as Love toward someone who has benefited another.</note>

### DEMONSTRATION.

For **Favor** is **Love** toward one who has done good to another (*by Definition 19 of the Affects*); and therefore it can be related to the Mind insofar as the Mind is said to act (*by Proposition 59, Part 3*), which is to say (*by Proposition 3, Part 3*), insofar as it understands. Consequently, it agrees with Reason, etc. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

->*Another Proof.*<-

One who lives under the guidance of Reason desires for another the good he wants for himself (*by Proposition 37 of this part*). Therefore, when he sees someone doing good to another, his own effort to do good is aided—that is (*by the Scholium to Proposition 11, Part 3* <note>The first edition (Ed. Pr.) notes this refers to Proposition 11, Part 3.</note>), he will feel joy, and this (*by hypothesis*) will be accompanied by the idea of the one who did good to the other. Therefore (*by Definition 19 of the Affects*), he favors that person. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### SCHOLIUM.

**Indignation**, as it is defined by us (*see Definition 20 of the Affects*), is necessarily evil (*by Proposition 45 of this part*). <note>term: "Indignation" — Defined by Spinoza as hatred toward someone who has done evil to another.</note> But it should be noted that when the sovereign power, moved by the desire to preserve peace, punishes a citizen who has committed an injury against another, I do not say that the sovereign is "indignant" with the citizen. This is because the sovereign is not moved by Hate to destroy the citizen, but is moved by **Piety** <note>original: "pietas" — Here meaning a sense of duty or devotion to the welfare of the state/community.</note> to punish him.

## PROPOSITION LII.

*Self-contentment can arise from Reason, and only that self-contentment which arises from Reason is the highest that can exist.* <note>term: "Self-contentment" (Acquiescentia in se ipso) — A central concept for Spinoza, referring to the peace of mind or satisfaction that comes from recognizing one's own power and virtue.</note>

### DEMONSTRATION.

**Self-contentment** is **Joy** arising from the fact that a person contemplates himself and his own power of acting (*by Definition 25 of the Affects*). But a person’s true power of acting, or virtue, is Reason itself (*by Proposition 3, Part 3*), which the person contemplates clearly and distinctly (*by Propositions 40 and 43, Part 2*). Therefore, Self-contentment arises from Reason. Next...

He defines "Favor" as a rational approval of good deeds and introduces "Self-contentment" as the highest joy, stemming from the mind's recognition of its own rational power.</summary>

a human being, while contemplating themselves, perceives nothing clearly and distinctly, or adequately, except those things which follow from their own power of acting (*by Definition 2, Part 3*), that is (*by Proposition 3, Part 3*), which follow from their power of understanding; and therefore from this contemplation alone arises the highest **Self-contentment** <note>original: "Acquiescentia" — Often translated as "self-satisfaction" or "peace of mind," Spinoza defines this as a joy that arises when a person contemplates their own power to act and understand.</note> that can exist. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

#### S C H O L I U M.

**Self-contentment** is in fact the highest thing we can hope for. For (*as we showed in Proposition 25 of this part*) no one strives to preserve their being for the sake of any other end; and because this **Self-contentment** is more and more fostered and strengthened by praise (*by the Corollary to Proposition 53, Part 3*), and on the contrary (*by the Corollary to Proposition 55, Part 3*) more and more disturbed by blame, we are therefore guided mostly by glory, and can hardly bear a life lived in disgrace.

### PROPOSITION LIII.

->*Humility is not a virtue; that is, it does not arise from Reason.*<-

#### D E M O N S T R A T I O N.

**Humility** is a form of **Sadness** <note>original: "Tristitia" — In Spinoza's philosophy, "sadness" is a technical term for any state that decreases a person's power or perfection.</note> which arises from a person contemplating their own lack of power (*by Definition 26 of the Affects*). However, insofar as a person knows themselves through true Reason, they are assumed to understand their own essence, which is (*by Proposition 7, Part 3*) their power. Therefore, if a person, while contemplating themselves, perceives some lack of power, it is not because they understand themselves, but (*as we showed in Proposition 55, Part 3*) because their power of acting is restrained. But if we suppose that a person conceives of their lack of power because they understand something more powerful than themselves, by the knowledge of which they determine their own power of acting, then we conceive of nothing other than that the person understands themselves distinctly (*by Proposition 26 of this part*), <insert>by which [or: and by which]</insert> <note>original footnote: "Fortasse: quo vel quodque" — This is a translator's or editor's note regarding a variation in the original Latin manuscript's phrasing.</note> their power of acting is aided. Therefore **Humility**, or the Sadness that arises from a person contemplating their own lack of power, does not arise from true contemplation or Reason; it is not a virtue, but a passion. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### PROPOSITION LIV.

->*Repentance is not a virtue; that is, it does not arise from Reason; instead, he who repents of an act is twice miserable or powerless.*<-

#### D E M O N S T R A T I O N.

The first part of this is demonstrated like the preceding Proposition. The second part is evident from the definition of this affect alone (*see Definition 27 of the Affects*). For first he allows himself to be overcome by a base Desire, and then by Sadness.

#### S C H O L I U M.

Because humans rarely live according to the dictate of **Reason**, these two affects—namely **Humility** and **Repentance**—and besides these **Hope** and **Fear**, bring more benefit than harm. Thus, if one must sin, it is better to sin in that direction. For if all people who were powerless in mind were equally proud, they would be ashamed of nothing, nor would they...

<header>ETHICS PART IV.</header>

anything to fear by which they could be joined and bound together? The common people <note>original: "vulgus" — Spinoza uses this term not just for the poor, but for anyone—of any class—who is governed by their passions rather than by reason.</note> are terrifying if they are not afraid; therefore it is no wonder that the Prophets, who looked out for the common utility and not just for the few, so greatly recommended Humility, Repentance, and Reverence. And indeed, those who are subject to these emotions can be led much more easily than others, so that they might eventually live according to the guidance of Reason—that is, so they may be free and enjoy the life of the blessed.

### PROPOSITION LV.

**Maximum Pride or Self-debasement is the maximum ignorance of oneself.**

#### DEMONSTRATION.

This is clear from *Definitions of the Emotions 28 and 29.* <note>In Spinoza’s system, Pride is thinking more highly of oneself than is just, while Self-debasement (Abjectio) is thinking less of oneself than is just. Both are errors of self-knowledge.</note>

### PROPOSITION LVI.

**Maximum Pride or Self-debasement indicates maximum lack of power of the mind.**

#### DEMONSTRATION.

The first foundation of virtue is to preserve one's own being (*by the Corollary to Proposition 22 of this part*), and to do so under the guidance of Reason (*by Proposition 24 of this part*). Therefore, he who is ignorant of himself is ignorant of the foundation of all virtues, and consequently is ignorant of all virtues. Furthermore, to act from virtue is nothing other than to act under the guidance of Reason (*by Proposition 24 of this part*); and he who acts under the guidance of Reason must necessarily know that he acts under the guidance of Reason (*by Proposition 43, Part 2*). He, therefore, who is most ignorant of himself, and consequently (*as we have just shown*) of all virtues, acts least from virtue—that is (*as is clear from Definition 8 of this part*), he is most lacking in mental power. And so (*by the preceding Proposition*), maximum Pride or Self-debasement indicates maximum lack of power of the mind. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

#### COROLLARARY.

From this it follows most clearly that the proud and the self-debased are most subject to the passions.

#### SCHOLIUM.

Self-debasement <note>original: "Abjectio" — Also translated as "despondency," this is the state of feeling one is less capable than they actually are.</note> can nevertheless be corrected more easily than pride; since pride is an emotion of Joy, while self-debasement is one of Sadness, and therefore (*by Proposition 18 of this part*) the former is stronger than the latter.

### PROPOSITION LVII.

**A proud person loves the presence of parasites or flatterers, but hates the presence of noble-minded people.**

#### DEMONSTRATION.

Pride is Joy arising from the fact that a person thinks more highly of themselves than is just (*by Definitions of the Emotions 28 and 6*); a proud person will try to foster this opinion as much as they can (*see the Scholium to Proposition 13, Part 3*); and therefore

For *quo* is the ablative case of the pronoun *quid* (what/anything).</margin>

<meta>This page continues Part IV of the Ethics, completing the demonstration that the proud avoid those who see them honestly. It provides a detailed Scholium on the psychological mechanics of pride and its opposite, self-debasement, before moving to Proposition 58 regarding the nature of Glory.</meta>

<page-num>143</page-num>
<header>ON HUMAN BONDAGE.</header>

...they will love the presence of parasites or flatterers (<note>original: "parasitorum vel adulatorum" — those who use false praise for their own gain</note>), and they will flee from noble-minded people who think of them as is fitting. Which was to be demonstrated. <note>original: "Q. E. D." — Quod erat demonstrandum</note>

### SCHOLIUM.

It would be too long to list all the evils of Pride here, since the proud are susceptible to all emotions; but they are susceptible to none less than the emotions of Love and Pity. But I must not remain silent here regarding the fact that a person is also called proud if they think less of others than is just; and so, in this sense, Pride is defined as **Joy** arising from the false opinion by which a man considers himself to be above others. And **Self-debasement** <note>original: "Abjectio" — thinking too little of oneself; the inverse of pride.</note>, which is the opposite of this Pride, would be defined as **Sadness** arising from the false opinion by which a man believes himself to be below others. 

Given this, we easily conceive that the proud man is necessarily envious (<note>see the Scholium to Prop. 55, Part 3</note>), and that he especially hates those who are most praised for their virtues; nor is his hatred easily overcome by Love or benefits (<note>see the Scholium to Prop. 41, Part 3</note>); and he takes pleasure only in the presence of those who humor his weak mind and turn him from a fool into a madman.

Although Self-debasement is the opposite of Pride, the self-debased man is very close to the proud one. For, since his Sadness arises from the fact that he judges his own lack of power by the power or virtue of others, his Sadness will be relieved—that is, he will feel joy—if his imagination is occupied with contemplating the faults of others. From this comes that well-known proverb: *it is a comfort to the wretched to have had companions in misfortune.* <note>original: "solamen miseris socios habuisse malorum"</note> On the other hand, he will be the more saddened the more he believes himself to be below others; hence it happens that none are more prone to Envy than the Self-debased. They especially try to observe the deeds of men, more for the sake of finding fault than for correcting them, until finally they praise only Self-debasement and glory in it—yet in such a way that they still appear self-debased. 

These things follow from this emotion as necessarily as it follows from the nature of a triangle that its three angles are equal to two right angles. I have already said that I call these and similar emotions "evil" only insofar as I attend to human utility. But the laws of Nature respect the common order of Nature, of which man is a part; I wanted to mention this in passing, lest anyone think I intended to narrate the vices and absurd deeds of men, rather than demonstrate the nature and properties of things. For, as I said in the Preface to Part 3, I consider human emotions and their properties in the same way as other natural things. And certainly, human emotions indicate the power and craftsmanship of Nature no less than many other things that we wonder at and in the contemplation of which we take delight. But I proceed to note those things regarding the emotions which bring utility to men, or which bring them harm.

## PROPOSITION LVIII.

->*Glory is not contrary to Reason, but can arise from it.*<-

### DEMONSTRATION.

This is clear from the 30th *Definition of the Emotions* and from the definition of **the Honorable** <note>original: "Honesti" — that which is worthy of honor or morally right</note>, which see in the 1st Scholium of Proposition 37 of this part.

<summary>Spinoza defines pride and self-debasement as mirror-image errors of self-judgment and explains their social consequences, such as envy and the rejection of honest friends. He concludes by beginning an analysis of "Glory" as an emotion potentially compatible with reason.</summary>

--- Page 161 ---
<meta>This page continues Spinoza’s analysis of human emotions in Part IV of the Ethics. It transitions from a discussion on Pride and Self-debasement to the nature of Vainglory and Shame, eventually introducing Proposition 59, which argues that Reason can achieve anything that passive emotions can.</meta>

<page-num>144</page-num>
<header>THE ETHICS, PART IV.</header>

### SCHOLIUM.

What is called **Vainglory** is a form of self-contentment <note>original: "acquiescentia in se ipso" — a state of being satisfied with oneself</note> that is fostered solely by the opinion of the common people. When that opinion is withdrawn, the self-contentment itself ceases—which is to say (according to the Scholium of Proposition 52 of this Part), the highest good that everyone loves ceases. From this, it follows that whoever glories in the opinion of the common people struggles with daily, anxious care, acting and experimenting to preserve his reputation. For the common people are fickle and inconstant; therefore, unless a reputation is actively maintained, it quickly fades. Moreover, because everyone desires to capture the applause of the crowd, each person easily suppresses the reputation of others. Since they are competing for what they consider the highest good, a massive lust arises to oppress one another by any means necessary. He who finally emerges as the victor glories more in having hindered another than in having benefited himself. Therefore, this Glory, or self-contentment, is truly **Vain**, because it is effectively nothing.

What should be noted regarding **Shame** <note>original: "Pudor"</note> can be easily gathered from what we have said about Pity and Repentance. I will only add this: that just as Pity is not a virtue, neither is Shame; however, it is "good" insofar as it indicates that the person who blushes with Shame possesses a desire to live honorably. In the same way, physical pain is called "good" insofar as it indicates that the injured part of the body has not yet rotted. Therefore, although a man who is ashamed of an action is indeed feeling Sadness <note>original: "tristis"</note>, he is still more perfect than a shameless man who has no desire to live honorably.

These are the points I intended to note concerning the emotions of **Joy** and **Sadness**. As for **Desires** <note>original: "Cupiditates"</note>, these are certainly good or evil insofar as they arise from good or evil emotions. But in truth, all desires are blind insofar as they are generated in us by emotions that are passions <note>original: "passiones" — states where the mind is passive and acted upon by external forces</note> (as can be easily gathered from what we said in the Scholium to Proposition 44 of this Part). Nor would they be of any use if men could be easily led to live by the dictate of **Reason** <note>original: "Ratio"</note> alone, as I shall now briefly show.

## PROPOSITION LIX.

->*To all actions to which we are determined by an emotion which is a passion, we can be determined by Reason without it.*<-

### DEMONSTRATION.

To act from Reason is nothing else (by Proposition 3 and Definition 2 of Part 3) than to perform those actions that follow from the necessity of our nature, considered in itself alone. But **Sadness** is evil insofar as it diminishes or restrains this power of acting (by Proposition 41 of this Part); therefore, we can be determined to no action by this emotion that we could not also perform if we were led by Reason. Furthermore, **Joy** is evil only insofar as it prevents a man from being fit to act (by Propositions 41 and 43 of this Part); and thus, to that extent, we can be determined to no action that we could not perform if we were led by Reason. Finally, insofar as Joy is good, it agrees with Reason (for it consists in the fact that a man's power of acting is increased or assisted); nor is it a passion, except insofar as the man's power of acting is not increased to the point where he adequately conceives himself and his actions (by Proposition 3, Part 3, with its Scholium). Therefore, if a man affected by Joy were to

<summary>Spinoza defines Vainglory as a fragile self-satisfaction dependent on public opinion and introduces the idea that any positive action driven by passion can be achieved more effectively through Reason.</summary>
<keywords>Vainglory, Shame, Reason, Joy, Sadness, passions, power of acting, reputation, honor</keywords>

--- Page 162 ---
<meta>This page continues Spinoza’s argument in Proposition 59, demonstrating that actions driven by passions can also be performed by Reason. It then transitions to Proposition 60, discussing the balance of the body’s parts.</meta>

<page-num>145</page-num>
<header>ON HUMAN BONDAGE.</header>
<sig>10</sig>

...would be brought to such perfection that it would have an adequate conception of itself and its actions, and would be capable—indeed, more capable—of performing those same actions to which it is currently driven by emotions that are passions. But all emotions are related to Joy, Sadness, or Desire <note>original: "Lætitiam, Tristitiam, vel Cupiditatem"</note> (see the explanation of the fourth Definition of the Affects), and Desire (by the 1st Definition of the Affects) is nothing other than the very effort to act; therefore, for all actions to which we are determined by an emotion that is a passion, we can be led by Reason alone without it. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### Alternative Proof.

Any action is called bad <note>original: "mala"</note> insofar as it arises from the fact that we are affected by Hate or some other evil emotion (see Corollary 1 to Proposition 45 of this part). But no action, considered in itself, is good or evil (as we showed in the Preface to this part), but one and the same action is now good, now evil; therefore, toward that same action which is now evil, or which arises from some evil emotion, we can be led by Reason (by Proposition 19 of this part). *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### S C H O L I U M.

These things are explained more clearly by an example. Namely, the action of striking, insofar as it is considered physically—and we attend only to the fact that a man raises his arm, closes his hand, and moves his whole arm downward with force—is a virtue <note>original: "virtus" — here meaning a power or capacity of the body's physical structure</note> which is conceived from the structure of the human Body. If, therefore, a man moved by Anger or Hate is determined to close his hand or move his arm, this happens (as we showed in the Second Part) because one and the same action can be joined to any images of things whatever. Thus, we can be determined to one and the same action both by those images of things which we conceive confusedly and by those which we conceive clearly and distinctly. It appears, therefore, that every Desire arising from an emotion that is a passion would be of no use if men could be led by Reason. Let us now see why the Desire that arises from an emotion which is a passion is called "blind" <note>original: "cœca"</note> by us.

## P R O P O S I T I O N LX.

*Desire that arises from Joy or Sadness which is related to one or to some, but not to all parts of the Body, does not take into account the advantage of the whole man.*

### D E M O N S T R A T I O N.

Suppose, for example, that part A of the Body is so strengthened by the force of some external cause that it prevails over the rest (by Proposition 6 of this part). This part will not, therefore, try to lose its strength so that the remaining parts of the Body may perform their function; for it would have to have the force or power of losing its own strength, which (by Proposition 6 of Part 3) is absurd. Therefore, that part—and consequently (by Propositions 7 and 12 of Part 3) the Mind also—will strive to preserve that state; and thus the Desire which arises from such an emotion of Joy does not take into account the whole. If, on the contrary, it is supposed that part A is hindered so that the rest prevail, it is demonstrated in the same way that neither does the Desire arising from Sadness take into account the whole. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### S C H O L I U M.

Since, therefore, Joy is mostly (by the Scholium to Proposition 44 of this part) related to one...

<meta>Google Digitized watermark at bottom right.</meta>

<summary>Spinoza argues that Reason can direct us to the same actions as our passions, but more effectively. He also warns that desires focused on only one part of the body can be detrimental to the person's overall well-being.</summary>
