referred to a part of the Body; we therefore mostly desire to preserve our own existence without taking into account our total health; to which is added that Desires <note>original: "Cupiditates"</note>, by which we are mostly held (by the Corollary to Proposition 9 of this part), take account only of the present time, but not of the future.

### PROPOSITION LXI.
*Desire which arises from Reason cannot be excessive.*

->PROOF.<-

Desire (by the 1st Definition of the Affects), considered in itself, is the very essence of man, insofar as it is conceived as determined in any way to doing something; and therefore Desire which arises from Reason, that is (by Proposition 3, Part 3), which is generated in us insofar as we act, is the very essence or nature of man, insofar as it is conceived as determined to doing those things which are adequately conceived through the essence of man alone (by Definition 2, Part 3). If, therefore, this Desire could be excessive, then human nature, considered in itself alone, could exceed itself, or could do more than it can, which is a manifest contradiction; and consequently, this Desire cannot be excessive. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### PROPOSITION LXII.
*Insofar as the Mind conceives things from the dictate of Reason, it is affected in the same way, whether the idea be of a future or past thing, or of a present one.*

->PROOF.<-

Whatever the Mind conceives under the guidance of Reason, it conceives all of it under the same aspect of eternity or necessity <note>original: "sub eadem æternitatis seu necessitatis specie"</note> (by Corollary 2 to Proposition 44, Part 2), and it is affected by the same certainty (by Proposition 43, Part 2, and its Note). Therefore, whether the idea be of a future or past thing, or of a present one, the Mind conceives the thing with the same necessity, and is affected by the same certainty; and, whether the idea be of a future or past thing, or of a present one, it will nonetheless be equally true (by Proposition 41, Part 2); that is (by Definition 4, Part 2), it will nonetheless always have the same properties of an adequate idea; and thus, insofar as the Mind conceives things from the dictate of Reason, it is affected in the same way, whether the idea be of a future or past thing, or of a present one. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

->NOTE.<-

If we could have adequate knowledge of the duration of things, and determine their times of existence by Reason, we would contemplate future and present things with the same effect; and the good which the Mind conceived as future, it would seek just as if it were present. Consequently, it would necessarily neglect a lesser present good for a greater future one, and it would not at all seek that which was good in the present but the cause of some future evil, as we shall soon demonstrate. But we can have (by Proposition 31, Part 2) only a very inadequate knowledge of the duration of things, and we determine the times of existence of things (by the Note to Proposition 44, Part 2) only by the imagination, which is not affected in the same way by the image of a present thing as by a future one. From this it happens that the true knowledge of good and evil which we have is only abstract or universal, and the judgment which we make concerning the order of things and the connection of causes, so that we might determine—

<summary>Spinoza argues that desires based on Reason are inherently balanced because they stem from our true nature, and that Reason views all time (past, present, future) equally, unlike the imagination which favors the present.</summary>

And so it is not surprising if Desire <note>original: "Cupiditas"</note>, which arises from the knowledge of good and evil insofar as this knowledge looks toward the future, can be more easily restrained by the Desire for things that are pleasant in the present, concerning which see Proposition 16 <note>original footnote 1: The First Edition says 18; editors Schmidt and Auerbach suggest 15.</note> of this Part.

### PROPOSITION LXIII.

->*He who is led by Fear, and does good in order to avoid evil, is not led by Reason.*<-

### PROOF.

All emotions <note>original: "affectus"</note> which are related to the Mind insofar as it acts—that is (by Proposition 3, Part 3), which are referred to Reason—are nothing other than emotions of Joy <note>original: "Lætitiæ"</note> and Desire (by Proposition 59, Part 3); and therefore (by the 13th Definition of the Emotions), he who is led by Fear, and does good through the dread of evil, is not led by Reason. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### SCHOLIUM.

The superstitious <note>original: "Superstitiosi"</note>, who know more about reproaching vice than teaching virtue, and who strive to restrain men not by leading them through Reason, but through Fear—so that they flee evil rather than love virtue—intend nothing else than to make everyone else as miserable as they are themselves; and so it is not surprising if they are for the most part troublesome and hateful to men.

### COROLLARY.

Through the Desire which arises from Reason, we follow good directly and flee evil indirectly.

### PROOF.

For Desire which arises from Reason can only arise from an emotion of Joy, which is not a passion (by Proposition 59, Part 3)—that is, from a Joy which cannot be excessive (by Proposition 61 of this Part)—but not from Sadness <note>original: "Tristitia"</note>. Consequently, this Desire (by Proposition 8 of this Part) arises from the knowledge of good, not of evil; and so, under the guidance of Reason, we desire good directly, and only to that extent do we flee evil. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### SCHOLIUM.

This Corollary is explained by the example of a sick person and a healthy person. The sick man eats what he dislikes out of fear of death; but the healthy man enjoys his food, and thus enjoys life better than if he feared death and desired to avoid it directly. So also a judge, who condemns a criminal to death not out of Hatred or Anger, etc., but solely out of Love for the public welfare <note>original: "salutis publicæ"</note>, is led by Reason alone.

### PROPOSITION LXIV.

->*The knowledge of evil is inadequate knowledge.*<-

### PROOF.

The knowledge of evil (by Proposition 8 of this Part) is Sadness itself, insofar as we are conscious of it. Sadness, however, is a transition to a lesser perfection (by the 3rd Definition of the Emotions), which therefore through the very essence of man...

E. D." (Quod erat demonstrandum), meaning "Which was to be demonstrated."</note>

### COROLLARY.

From this it follows that if the human Mind had nothing but <term>adequate ideas</term><note>Clear and distinct thoughts that represent things as they truly are in relation to the whole of nature.</note>, it would form no notion of evil.

## PROPOSITION LXV.

->***Under the guidance of Reason, we shall follow the greater of two goods, and the lesser of two evils.***<-

### PROOF.

A good which prevents us from enjoying a greater good is in reality an evil; for "evil" and "good" (*as we showed in the Preface of this Part*) are said of things insofar as we compare them with one another; and (*by the same reasoning*) a lesser evil is in reality a good. Wherefore (*through the Corollary to Proposition 63 of this Part*) <note>1) The editor Schmidt notes that the First Edition reads: "by the Corollary of the preceding Proposition."</note> under the guidance of Reason, we shall desire or follow only the greater good and the lesser evil. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### COROLLARY.

Under the guidance of Reason, we shall follow a lesser evil as if it were a greater good, and we shall neglect a lesser good that is the cause of a greater evil. For the evil which is here called "lesser" is in reality a good, and the good, conversely, is an evil; wherefore (*through the Corollary to Proposition 63 of this Part*) <note>1)</note> we shall desire the former and neglect the latter. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

## PROPOSITION LXVI.

->***Under the guidance of Reason, we shall desire a greater future good over a lesser present one, and a lesser present evil over a greater future one <note>2) The editors Camerer and Schmidt note that the First Edition reads: "and a lesser present evil, which is the cause of some future evil." The Dutch version and the scholar Pollock (p. 268) suggest: "of some greater future good." Editors Gfrörer and Saisset suggest: "of some future good."</note>.***<-

### PROOF.

If the Mind could have an adequate knowledge of a future thing, it would be affected with the same <term>emotion</term><note>Spinoza uses the word "affectus," referring to changes in the body's power to act and the ideas of those changes.</note> toward the future thing as toward the present one (*through Proposition 62 of this Part*); wherefore, insofar as we attend to Reason itself—as we suppose ourselves to do in this Proposition—the matter is the same whether a greater future good or evil is supposed, or a present one; and accordingly (*through Proposition 65 of this Part*) we shall desire a greater future good over a lesser present one, etc. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### COROLLARY.

Under the guidance of Reason, we shall desire a lesser present evil which is the cause of a greater future good; and we shall neglect a lesser present good which is the cause of a greater future evil. This Corollary relates to the preceding Proposition in the same way that the Corollary to Proposition 65 relates to Proposition 65 itself.

<meta>This page continues the exploration of how Reason guides human behavior. Having previously established how a rational person weighs different goods and evils, Spinoza now introduces his famous definition of the "free man" and contrasts this state of freedom with the "bondage" of living by emotion alone. This leads into an allegorical interpretation of the story of Adam in Genesis.</meta>

<page-num>149</page-num>
<header>ON HUMAN BONDAGE.</header>

By what we showed in Proposition 18 regarding the power of the emotions, we will easily see the difference between a person who is led solely by emotion or opinion, and a person who is led by <term>Reason</term><note>For Spinoza, Reason is the power of the mind to understand things through logic and their true causes, rather than just reacting to external stimuli.</note>. The former, whether they wish it or not, performs actions they are most ignorant of; the latter, however, complies with no one but themselves, and performs only those actions they know to be of primary importance in life, and which they therefore desire most. For this reason, I call the former a "slave," but the latter a "free person," about whose character and way of living I should like to note a few things here.

## PROPOSITION LXVII.

*A free person thinks of nothing less than of death; and their wisdom is a meditation not on death, but on life.*

### PROOF.

A free person—that is, one who lives solely by the dictates of Reason—is not led by the <term>Fear of death</term><note>original: "mortis Metu"</note> (*by Prop. 63 of this Part*), but desires the good directly (*by the Corollary of the same Prop.*); that is (*by Prop. 24 of this Part*), they desire to act, to live, and to preserve their own being on the foundation of seeking what is truly useful to themselves. And therefore, they think of nothing less than of death; instead, their wisdom is a meditation on life. <note>original: "Q. E. D." (Quod erat demonstrandum), meaning "Which was to be demonstrated."</note>

## PROPOSITION LXVIII.

*If humans were born free, they would form no concept of good and evil as long as they remained free.*

### PROOF.

I have said that a person is free who is led by Reason alone. Whoever is born free, then, and remains free, has only <term>adequate ideas</term><note>Clear, complete thoughts that reflect the true nature of reality.</note>; and therefore has no concept of evil (*by the Corollary to Prop. 64 of this Part*), and consequently (since good and evil are correlates) has no concept of good either. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

## SCHOLIUM.

It is clear from the 4th Proposition of this Part that the hypothesis of this Proposition is false, and cannot be conceived except insofar as we look at human nature alone, or rather at God—not insofar as God is infinite, but only insofar as God is the cause of human existence. This, along with other things we have already demonstrated, seems to be signified by Moses in the history of the first man. 

In that story, no other power of God is conceived except that by which he created man—that is, the power by which he consulted only the utility of man. Thus, it is told that God prohibited a free man from eating from the <term>tree of the knowledge of good and evil</term><note>Spinoza interprets this biblical symbol as the transition from living by pure reason to living by comparative, emotional judgments of "good" and "bad."</note>, and that as soon as he ate from it, he immediately feared death rather than desiring to live. 

Next, it is told that after man found a wife who agreed entirely with his own nature, he realized there could be nothing in Nature more useful to him than she was; but after he believed the beasts to be like himself, he immediately began to imitate their emotions (see *Prop. 27, Part 3*) and to lose his freedom. This freedom was later recovered by the Patriarchs, led by the <term>Spirit of Christ</term><note>original: "Spiritu Christi." Spinoza uses this term to mean the "idea of God," or the universal wisdom that leads to freedom, rather than in a traditional theological sense.</note>—that is, by the idea of God, on which alone it depends that a person be free, and that they desire for other humans the good they desire for themselves, as we have demonstrated above (*by Prop. 37 of this Part*).

<summary>Spinoza defines the "free man" as one who meditates on life rather than death and interprets the Fall of Adam as an allegory for losing rational freedom to emotional imitation.</summary>
<keywords>free man, meditation on life, fear of death, good and evil, Moses, Adam and Eve, Spirit of Christ, human utility, bondage</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Spinoza’s portrait of the "free person"—someone guided by reason rather than emotion. Having established that such a person focuses on life rather than death, he now explores how they handle danger and social obligations.</meta>

<page-num>150</page-num>
<header>ETHICS PART IV.</header>

### PROPOSITION LXIX.

*The virtue of a free person is seen to be just as great in avoiding dangers as in overcoming them.*

#### DEMONSTRATION.

An emotion cannot be restrained or removed except by a contrary and stronger emotion (*by Prop. 7 of this Part*). However, blind <term>Audacity</term><note>original: "Audacia"; a reckless boldness driven by emotion rather than logic.</note> and <term>Fear</term><note>original: "Metus"</note> are emotions that can be conceived of as equally great (*by Prop. 5 and 3 of this Part*). Therefore, an equally great virtue of the mind, or <term>Fortitude</term><note>original: "fortitudo"; Spinoza defines this in Part 3 as the desire to act according to reason.</note> (*see the definition of this in the Scholium to Prop. 59, Part 3*), is required to restrain Audacity as is required to restrain Fear. That is to say (*by Definitions 40 and 41 of the Emotions*), a free person avoids dangers by the same virtue of the mind with which they attempt to overcome them. <note>Which was to be demonstrated.</note>

#### COROLLARARY.

Therefore, for a free person, a timely flight is considered to show as much <term>Courage</term><note>original: "Animositas"; a specific type of fortitude where one strives to preserve their own being according to reason.</note> as fighting; or, a free person chooses flight with the same courage or presence of mind as they choose a contest.

#### SCHOLIUM.

What <term>Courage</term> is, or what I understand by it, I explained in the Scholium to Prop. 59, Part 3. By "danger," however, I understand everything that can be a cause of any evil—namely, Sadness, Hate, Discord, and so forth.

### PROPOSITION LXX.

*A free person, who lives among the ignorant <margin>1) 2)</margin>, strives as much as possible to avoid their favors.*

#### DEMONSTRATION.

Everyone judges according to their own temperament what is good (*see Scholium to Prop. 39, Part 3*); therefore, an ignorant person who has bestowed a favor on someone will value it according to their own temperament, and if they see it is valued less by the one who received it, they will be saddened (*by Prop. 42, Part 3*). But a free person strives to join other people to themselves in friendship (*by Prop. 37 of this Part*), not to repay people with favors equal to their emotional state, but to lead themselves and others by the free judgment of <term>Reason</term>, and to do only those things that they themselves know to be of primary importance. Therefore, a free person, lest they be hated by the ignorant and lest they obey the cravings of others rather than Reason alone, will endeavor as much as possible to decline their favors. <note>Which was to be demonstrated.</note>

#### SCHOLIUM.

I say *as much as possible*. For even though people may be ignorant, they are still human beings, who in times of need can provide human assistance—than which nothing is more excellent. And so it often happens that it is necessary to accept a favor from them, and consequently to thank them in a way that suits their temperament. To this we must add that caution must be used even in declining favors, lest we seem to despise them, or through <note>The text ends mid-sentence here.</note>

2) <note>Dutch Version from the author's poorly read manuscript: *the lazy (ignavos)*; also in the Proof and Scholium.</note>

<summary>Spinoza argues that a rational person shows equal strength of character by avoiding unnecessary danger as by facing it, and prefers to avoid social debts to those who do not live by reason.</summary>

<header>ON HUMAN BONDAGE.</header>

### PROPOSITION LXXI.

*Only free people are most grateful toward one another.*

->DEMONSTRATION<-

Only free people are most useful to one another, and are joined by the greatest necessity of friendship (*by Prop. 35 of this Part, and its 1st Coroll.*), and they strive to benefit one another with an equal zeal of love (*by Prop. 37 of this Part*); and therefore (*by Definition 34 of the Emotions*) only free people are most grateful toward one another. <note>Which was to be demonstrated.</note>

->SCHOLIUM<-

<term>Gratitude</term><note>original: "Gratia"</note>, which people who are led by blind <term>Desire</term><note>original: "Cupiditas"</note> have for one another, is more often a kind of business or a trap than it is true gratitude. Furthermore, ingratitude is not itself an emotion. Nevertheless, ingratitude is shameful because it usually indicates that a person is affected by too much Hate, Anger, Pride, or Greed, etc. For a person who, out of sheer foolishness, does not know how to return a gift is not "ungrateful," and much less is the person who is not moved by the gifts of a prostitute to serve her lust, or by the gifts of a thief to hide his thefts, or other similar cases. On the contrary, this person shows that they possess a constant mind, specifically one that does not allow itself to be corrupted by any gifts to its own ruin or the common ruin.

### PROPOSITION LXXII.

*A free person never acts with evil deceit, but always with good faith.*

->DEMONSTRATIO<-

If a free person, insofar as they are free, did anything with evil deceit, they would do it by the dictate of <term>Reason</term><note>original: "Ratio"</note> (for only to that extent do we call them free): and therefore acting with evil deceit would be a virtue (*by Prop. 24 of this Part*), and consequently (*by the same Prop.*) it would be more advisable for everyone to act with evil deceit in order to preserve their own being; that is (*as is self-evident*), it would be more advisable for people to agree only in words, while being contrary to one another in fact, which (*by the Coroll. to Prop. 31 of this Part*) is absurd. Therefore, a free person, etc. <note>Which was to be demonstrated.</note>

->SCHOLIUM<-

If it is now asked: what if a person could free themselves from a present danger of death by treachery? Would not the principle of preserving one's own being fully persuade them to be treacherous? The answer will be the same: that if Reason should persuade this, it would persuade it for all people, and therefore Reason would fully persuade all people not to join forces and have common laws except through evil deceit—that is, not to have common laws at all, which is absurd.

### PROPOSITION LXXIII.

*A person who is led by Reason is more free in a <term>State</term><note>original: "Civitas"; meaning a political community or organized society.</note>, where they live according to a common decree, than in solitude, where they obey only themselves.*

<summary>This page argues that true gratitude and honesty are only possible among free, rational individuals, and concludes that living within a law-abiding society actually increases a person's freedom compared to living alone.</summary>
<keywords>gratitude, social contract, honesty, deceit, reason, the State, self-preservation, freedom</keywords>

--- Page 169 ---
<meta>This page continues the transition from the social interactions of the free person to their role within a political state, concluding the formal propositions of Part IV before beginning an organized summary (Appendix) of the right way of living.</meta>

<page-num>152</page-num>
<header>PART IV OF THE ETHICS.</header>

->DEMONSTRATION<-

A person who is guided by Reason is not led by Fear to obey (*by Prop. 63 of this Part*); but, in as far as they strive to preserve their being according to the dictate of Reason—that is (*by the Scholium to Prop. 66 of this Part*), in as far as they strive to live freely—they are bound to take into account the principles of common life and utility (*by Prop. 37 of this Part*), and consequently (*as we showed in the 2nd Scholium to Prop. 37 of this Part*) they desire to live according to the common decree of the State. Therefore, a person who is guided by Reason desires, in order to live more freely, to uphold the common laws of the State. <note>original: "Q. E. D." – Which was to be demonstrated.</note>

->SCHOLIUM<-

These and similar points that we have shown regarding true human freedom relate to <term>Strength of Character</term> <note>original: "Fortitudo"; Spinoza uses this to describe the virtue of a person acting solely from reason.</note>, that is (*by the Scholium to Prop. 59, Part 3*) to <term>Courage</term> <note>original: "Animositas"; the desire to preserve one's own being.</note> and <term>Nobility</term> <note>original: "Generositas"; the desire to join with and help others.</note>. Nor do I think it worth the effort to demonstrate all the properties of Strength of Character here separately, and much less to show that a strong person hates no one, is angry with no one, envies no one, is indignant at no one, despises no one, and is least of all proud. For these things, and everything that pertains to true life and <term>Religion</term> <note>Spinoza uses this term to mean any desire or action of which we are the cause insofar as we have the idea of God/Nature.</note>, are easily proven from Propositions 37 and 46 of this Part; namely, that Hatred must be overcome by Love, and that everyone who is guided by Reason desires for others also the good that they seek for themselves. 

To this is added what we noted in the Scholium of Proposition 50 of this Part, and in other places: namely, that a strong person considers this above all—that all things follow from the necessity of the divine nature <note>A core Spinozist idea: everything happens because it must, following the laws of nature.</note>, and therefore whatever they think is troublesome and evil, and whatever besides appears impious, horrific, unjust, and shameful, arises from the fact that they conceive things in a disturbed, mutilated, and confused way. For this reason, they strive above all to conceive things as they are in themselves, and to remove the obstacles to true knowledge, such as Hatred, Anger, Envy, Mockery, Pride, and other such things that we noted previously; and thus they strive, as we said, as much as they can to act well and to rejoice. To what extent human virtue extends to achieving these things, and what it can do, I shall demonstrate in the following Part.

# APPENDIX.

> *What I have handed down in this Part concerning the right way of living are not arranged so that they can be seen at a single glance, but have been demonstrated by me in a dispersed way, according as I was able to deduce one thing from another more easily. I have therefore proposed to collect these same things here and reduce them to main headings.*

## CHAPTER I.

All our efforts, or <term>Desires</term> <note>original: "Cupiditates"</note>, follow from the necessity of our nature in such a way that they can be understood either through that nature alone, as through their proximate cause, or insofar as we are a part of Nature, which cannot be adequately conceived through itself without other individuals.

## CHAPTER II.

Desires that follow from our nature in such a way that they can be understood through it alone are those which are referred to the Mind, insofar as the Mind is conceived to consist of adequate ideas; but other Desires are referred to

It then transitions into an Appendix summarizing the rules for a virtuous life, focusing on how our desires relate to our nature.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the Appendix to Part IV of the Ethics, specifically the chapters where Spinoza summarizes the right way of living. It transitions from the definition of actions versus passions to the pursuit of the rational life.</meta>

<page-num>153</page-num>
<header>ON HUMAN BONDAGE.</header>

...[attributed] to the Mind only insofar as it conceives things inadequately, and whose force and growth must be defined not by human power, but by the power of things outside of us. For this reason, the former are rightly called actions, while the latter are called passions; for the former always indicate our power, while the latter, on the contrary, indicate our lack of power and <term>mutilated knowledge</term> <note>Spinoza's term for incomplete or fragmented understanding that leads to error.</note>.

### CHAPTER III.

Our actions—that is, those <term>Desires</term> <note>original: "Cupiditates"</note> which are defined by human power or <term>Reason</term> <note>original: "Ratio"</note>—are always good; the others, however, can be both good and bad.

### CHAPTER IV.

Therefore, in life it is especially useful to perfect the understanding or Reason as much as we can, and in this one thing consists man’s highest happiness or <term>blessedness</term> <note>original: "beatitudo"</note>. Indeed, blessedness is nothing other than that very <term>peace of mind</term> <note>original: "animi acquiescentia"; a state of satisfaction or contentment with oneself and one's knowledge.</note> which arises from the intuitive knowledge of God. But to perfect the understanding is also nothing other than to understand God, and the attributes and actions of God which follow from the necessity of His nature. Therefore, the ultimate goal of a person who is led by Reason—that is, their highest Desire, by which they strive to moderate all others—is that by which they are led to adequately conceive themselves and all things that can fall under their understanding.

### CHAPTER V.

There is, therefore, no rational life without understanding; and things are good only insofar as they help a person to enjoy the life of the Mind, which is defined by understanding. Those things, however, which prevent a person from being able to perfect their Reason and enjoy a rational life, we call evil only.

### CHAPTER VI.

But because all those things of which a person is the <term>efficient cause</term> <note>original: "efficiens causa"; the direct source or producer of an effect.</note> are necessarily good, no evil can happen to a person except from external causes; namely, insofar as they are a part of the whole of <term>Nature</term> <note>original: "Natura"</note>, whose laws human nature is forced to obey and to which it is compelled to adapt itself in almost infinite ways.

### CHAPTER VII.

It is impossible for a person not to be a part of Nature and not to follow its common order; but if they live among individuals who agree with their own nature, their power of acting will be helped and fostered by that very fact. If, on the other hand, they are among those who do not agree with their nature at all, they will hardly be able to adapt to them without a great change in themselves.

### CHAPTER VIII.

Whatever exists in the nature of things that we judge to be evil, or which can prevent us from being able to exist and enjoy a rational life, we are permitted to remove from us by whatever way seems safer; and on the other hand, whatever exists that we judge to be good or useful for our being...

<summary>In this section of the Appendix, Spinoza defines the rational life as one focused on perfecting the intellect, which leads to a state of blessedness or peace of mind derived from knowing God. He explains that while human actions stemming from reason are always good, we remain subject to external nature, which can cause us harm or require us to adapt to our surroundings.</summary>
<keywords>blessedness, reason, intuitive knowledge, God, peace of mind, efficient cause, human nature, external causes, rational life</keywords>

--- Page 171 ---
<meta>This page continues the Appendix to Part IV of the Ethics, providing Spinoza’s practical rules for social life and the cultivation of human relationships. It follows the discussion of perfecting the individual reason as the foundation for a good life.</meta>

<page-num>154</page-num>
<header>THE ETHICS, PART IV.</header>

...preserving it, and enjoying a rational life, we are permitted to take it for our own use and use it in whatever way we like; and absolutely, everyone is permitted by the <term>highest right of Nature</term> <note>Spinoza's concept that "right" is coextensive with "power"; in a state of nature, everything a being has the power to do to survive, it has the right to do.</note> to do that which they judge to contribute to their own utility.

## CHAPTER IX.

Nothing can agree more with the nature of any thing than other individuals of the same species; and so (according to Chapter 7) nothing is more useful to a human being for preserving their existence and enjoying a rational life than another human being who is guided by <term>Reason</term> <note>original: "Ratio"</note>. Furthermore, because among individual things we know of nothing more excellent than a human being who is guided by Reason, one can show how much they excel in skill and talent in no better way than by educating people so that they eventually live by the command of their own Reason.

## CHAPTER X.

Insofar as humans are carried away against one another by Envy or some emotion of Hate, to that extent they are contrary to one another, and consequently are all the more to be feared, the more power they have over the other individuals of Nature.

## CHAPTER XI.

Yet minds are conquered not by arms, but by Love and <term>Generosity</term> <note>original: "Generositate"; for Spinoza, this is the desire to join with and help other people out of the dictates of reason alone.</note>.

## CHAPTER XII.

It is especially useful for humans to join together in customs and to bind themselves with those bonds by which they may best make one out of all of them, and absolutely to do those things which serve to strengthen friendships.

## CHAPTER XIII.

But for these things, skill and vigilance are required. For humans are varied (since those who live according to the prescription of Reason are rare), and yet they are for the most part envious, and more prone to vengeance than to <term>Compassion</term> <note>original: "Misericordia"</note>. It is therefore a work of singular mental power to bear with each person according to their own character and to restrain oneself from imitating their emotions. But those who know how to carp at humans, and to reproach vices rather than teach virtues, and not to strengthen human minds but to break them—these are troublesome both to themselves and to others. Hence many, out of an excessive impatience of mind and a false zeal for religion, have preferred to live among beasts rather than among humans; like children or adolescents who, unable to bear the scoldings of their parents with a calm mind, flee to military service, and choose the hardships of war and the command of a tyrant over domestic comforts and paternal admonitions, allowing any burden whatsoever to be imposed on them, provided they may take revenge on their parents.

## CHAPTER XIV.

Although, therefore, humans for the most part govern everything according to their own lust, yet from their common society many more advantages than disadvantages follow. Therefore, it is better to bear their injuries with a calm mind and to apply oneself to those things which serve to reconcile harmony and friendship.

<vocab>Ethics, Spinoza, Reason, Nature, Love, Generosity, Compassion, Harmony, Friendship</vocab>

<meta>This page continues the Appendix to Part IV of the Ethics. While the previous page discussed the utility of human cooperation and the use of reason to overcome hate, this page (starting from Chapter XV) details the specific social mechanisms—such as justice, marriage, and charity—that foster harmony among people.</meta>

<page-num>155</page-num>
<header>ON HUMAN BONDAGE.</header>

### CHAPTER XV.

Those things which produce harmony are those that relate to justice, equity, and <term>honor</term> <note>original: "honestatem"; in Spinoza’s context, this refers to behavior that is socially upright and earns the respect of others.</note>. For humans find it difficult to endure not only what is unjust and unfair, but also what is considered shameful, or when someone scorns the accepted customs of the state. Moreover, for the sake of winning love, those things that pertain to Religion and <term>Piety</term> <note>original: "Pietatem"; for Spinoza, piety is the desire to do good that is produced by living according to the guidance of reason.</note> are especially necessary. On these matters, see the first and second Scholiums to Proposition 37, the Scholium to Proposition 46, and the Scholium to Proposition 73 in Part 4.

### CHAPTER XVI.

Furthermore, harmony is also frequently produced by Fear, but such harmony is without trust. Add to this that Fear arises from a weakness of mind, and therefore does not belong to the use of <term>Reason</term> <note>original: "Ratio"</note>; nor does Pity, although it may appear to take on the outward appearance of Piety.

### CHAPTER XVII.

Moreover, people are also won over by generosity, especially those who do not have the means to acquire what is necessary for sustaining life. However, providing aid to every person in need far exceeds the strength and resources of a private individual. For the wealth of a private citizen is far too unequal to the task of supplying such needs. Furthermore, the capacity of a single man's intellect <note>Note in the First Edition: the capacity is more limited.</note> is too limited for him to be able to join everyone to himself in friendship; therefore, the care of the poor is a duty that falls upon society as a whole, and it regards only the common benefit.

### CHAPTER XVIII.

In accepting benefits and in returning gratitude, a completely different kind of care must be taken, concerning which see the Scholiums to Propositions 70 and 71 in Part 4.

### CHAPTER XIX.

Furthermore, lustful love <note>original: "Amor meretricius," literally "the love of a prostitute," but used by Spinoza to describe purely physical, non-rational lust.</note>—that is, the lust for procreation that arises from physical beauty—and absolutely every Love that acknowledges any cause other than freedom of mind, easily turns into Hate; unless, what is worse, it is a kind of madness, and then discord is fostered more than harmony <note>Note in the First Edition: used the terms "by discord, than by concord."</note>. See the Corollary to Proposition 31 in Part 3.

### CHAPTER XX.

As for what concerns marriage, it is certain that it agrees with Reason if the desire for physical union is generated not from physical beauty alone, but also from the Love of procreating children and educating them wisely; and furthermore, if the Love of both—namely, the man and the woman—has as its cause not just physical beauty, but especially freedom of mind.

### CHAPTER XXI.

Flattery also produces harmony; but it does so through the foul crime of servitude, or through treachery; for no one is more easily taken in by flattery than the proud, who wish to be first, but are not.

<summary>This page outlines the foundations of social stability, arguing that true harmony arises from justice and shared values, while warning that charity is a communal rather than individual duty and that lustful or prideful relationships are inherently unstable.</summary>

Here, Spinoza explores specific social emotions—such as shame and indignation—and defines our relationship with the non-human natural world.</meta>

<page-num>156</page-num>
<header>ETHICS PART IV.</header>

### CHAPTER XXII.

In <term>self-abasement</term> <note>original: "Abjectioni"; a state of despondency or thinking less of oneself than is fair.</note>, there is a false appearance of piety and religion. And although self-abasement is the opposite of pride, the self-abased man is nevertheless very close to the proud man. See the Scholium to Proposition 57, Part 4.

### CHAPTER XXIII.

Furthermore, <term>shame</term> <note>original: "Pudor"</note> contributes to social harmony only in those matters that cannot be hidden. Moreover, because shame itself is a species of <term>sadness</term> <note>original: "Tristitiæ"; for Spinoza, any emotion that decreases a person's power to act is categorized as sadness or "pain".</note>, it does not belong to the use of Reason.

### CHAPTER XXIV.

The other emotions of sadness directed toward people are directly opposed to justice, equity, honor, piety, and religion. And although <term>indignation</term> <note>original: "Indignatio"; anger caused by the perceived injustice done to another.</note> may seem to present an appearance of equity, a society lives without law wherever individuals are permitted to pass judgment on the deeds of others and to enforce their own rights or the rights of others.

### CHAPTER XXV.

<term>Courtesy</term> <note>original: "Modestia"; often translated as modesty, but here meaning the desire to please others.</note>—that is, the desire to please people which is determined by Reason—is related to <term>Piety</term> <note>original: "Pietatem"; acting well according to the guidance of reason.</note> (as we said in the first Scholium to Proposition 37, Part 4). But if it arises from an emotion, it is <term>ambition</term> <note>original: "Ambitio"</note>, or a desire through which people, under a false image of piety, generally stir up discord and rebellion. For he who desires to help others by counsel or deed, so that they may all enjoy the highest good together, will especially strive to win their love; he will not try to lead them into such admiration of him that his teaching is named after him, nor will he give any cause for envy. Furthermore, in common conversations, he will take care not to recount the vices of men, and will be careful to speak only sparingly of human weakness; but he will speak at length of human virtue or power, and the way by which it can be perfected. In this way, people—moved not by fear or aversion, but solely by the emotion of Joy—may try to live, as much as they are able, according to the command of Reason.

### CHAPTER XXVI.

Besides human beings, we know of no individual thing in Nature in whose mind we can find joy, or which we can join to ourselves in friendship or any kind of association. Therefore, whatever exists in the nature of things outside of human beings, the principle of our own <term>utility</term> <note>original: "utilitatis"; Spinoza argues that our moral obligations are centered on other rational beings (humans) rather than animals or inanimate objects.</note> does not require us to preserve it. Instead, reason teaches us to preserve it, destroy it, or adapt it to our use in whatever way is required by its various functions.

### CHAPTER XXVII.

The utility we derive from things outside ourselves—besides the experience and knowledge we acquire from observing them and changing them from one form to another—is primarily the preservation of the body. For this reason, those things are especially useful which can nourish and feed the body in such a way that all its parts can properly perform their functions. For the more the body is capable of being affected in many ways, and of affecting external bodies in many ways, the more the Mind is capable of thinking (see Propositions 38 and 39, Part 4). But very few things of this kind seem to exist in Nature; wherefore, to nourish the body as required,

...it is necessary to use many foods of various kinds. Indeed, the human **Body** is composed of a great many parts of different natures, which require continuous and varied nourishment, so that the whole **Body** may be equally capable of performing all the actions that can follow from its nature, and consequently, so that the **Mind** may also be equally capable of perceiving many things. <note>Spinoza argues that a diverse diet isn't just for health, but to make the mind more capable of complex thought by keeping the body versatile.</note>

### CHAPTER XXVIII.

To acquire these things, however, the strength of any single individual would hardly suffice unless people exchanged mutual services. But money has provided a shorthand for all things; hence it has come to pass that its image usually occupies the **Mind** of the common people most of all, because they can hardly imagine any kind of **Joy** <note>original: "Lætitiæ"; in Spinoza’s system, Joy is the emotion of moving toward a greater state of perfection.</note> without the accompanying idea of money as its cause.

### CHAPTER XXIX.

But this vice belongs only to those who seek money not out of poverty or for their necessities, but because they have learned the arts of profit, with which they boast magnificently. Otherwise, they feed their **Body** out of habit, but sparingly, because they believe they lose as much of their goods as they spend on the preservation of their **Body**. But those who know the true use of money, and regulate the measure of wealth by need alone, live contented with little.

### CHAPTER XXX.

Since, therefore, those things are good which help the parts of the **Body** to perform their function, and **Joy** consists in this: that the power of a human being—insofar as they consist of **Mind** and **Body**—is helped or increased; therefore, all those things that bring **Joy** are good. However, since things in nature do not act for the purpose of bringing us **Joy**, nor is their power of acting regulated by what is useful to us, and finally, since **Joy** is usually related to one part of the **Body** above all others; therefore, the affects of **Joy** (unless **Reason** and vigilance are present)—and consequently the **Desires** generated from them—usually tend toward excess. To this is added that, by our emotions, we prioritize what is pleasant in the present moment, and we are unable to estimate future things with an equal state of mind. See the <term>Scholium</term> <note>An explanatory note or commentary added to a text.</note> to Proposition 44 and the Scholium to Proposition 60, Part 4. 1)

### CHAPTER XXXI.

But superstition, on the contrary, seems to establish that what brings **Sadness** <note>original: "Tristitiam"; any emotion that decreases a person's power to act.</note> is good, and what brings **Joy** is bad. But, as we have already said (see the Scholium to Proposition 45, Part 4), no one, unless they are envious, delights in my lack of power or my misfortune. For the greater the **Joy** with which we are affected, the more we pass to a greater perfection, and consequently the more we participate in the divine nature; nor can **Joy** ever be evil when it is governed by a true account of our utility. But he who, on the contrary, is led by **Fear**, and does good only to avoid evil, is not led by **Reason**.

Here, Spinoza concludes by explaining how the individual relates to the infinite power of the universe and how intellectual understanding leads to peace of mind.</meta>

<page-num>158</page-num>
<header>ETHICS PART IV.</header>

### CHAPTER XXXII.

But human **power** <note>original: "potentia"; referring to our internal strength or ability to act.</note> is extremely limited, and it is infinitely surpassed by the power of external causes; and thus we do not have absolute power to adapt things outside of us for our own use. Nevertheless, we shall bear with a calm mind those things that happen to us contrary to what the consideration of our own advantage requires, if we are conscious that we have performed our duty, and that the power we possess could not have extended so far as to avoid those events; and further, that we are a part of the whole of **Nature**, whose order we follow. If we understand this clearly and distinctly, that part of us which is defined by intelligence—that is, the **better part of us** <note>Spinoza identifies our "better part" with our reason and intellect, which can grasp universal truths.</note>—will find complete **satisfaction** <note>original: "acquiescentia"; a state of mental peace or contentment arising from understanding.</note> in this, and will strive to persist in that satisfaction. For, insofar as we understand, we can desire nothing except what is necessary, nor can we find absolute satisfaction in anything except what is true; and therefore, insofar as we understand these things correctly, the **striving** <note>original: "conatus"; the fundamental drive of every being to persist in its own existence.</note> of the better part of us agrees with the order of the whole of **Nature**.

True contentment comes from the intellect's alignment with the necessary truths of the universe.</summary>

Now, Spinoza transitions from our passive endurance of external forces to the active power of the mind, setting the stage for his theory of human freedom and intellectual peace.</meta>

# ETHICS

## PART FIVE,

### ON THE POWER OF THE INTELLECT,

### OR ON HUMAN FREEDOM.

### PREFACE.

*I pass finally to the other part of Ethics, which concerns the method or way that leads to Freedom. In this part, therefore, I will deal with the power of Reason, showing what Reason itself can do against the emotions <note>original: "affectus"; often translated as "affects," referring to those feelings or passions that move us to action.</note>, and then, what Freedom of Mind or Blessedness <note>original: "Beatitudo"; a state of ultimate spiritual or mental well-being.</note> is; from which we shall see how much more powerful the wise person is than the ignorant. However, how and by what way the Intellect ought to be perfected, and by what art the Body should then be cared for so it can perform its function rightly, does not belong here; for the former pertains to Logic, the latter to Medicine. Here, therefore, as I said, I will deal only with the power of the Mind or Reason, and before all else I will show how much and what kind of command it has over the emotions for restraining and moderating them. For we have already demonstrated above that we do not have absolute command over them. The Stoics <note>A school of ancient Greek and Roman philosophers who taught that virtue is based on knowledge and that we should live in harmony with divine Reason, often advocating for total control over one's passions.</note>, however, thought that they depend absolutely on our will, and that we can command them absolutely. Yet, compelled by experience crying out against them, rather than by their own principles, they were forced to admit that no small use and effort is required to restrain and moderate them; which someone attempted to show by the example of two dogs (if I remember correctly), namely one domestic and the other a hunting dog; because by practice he was finally able to bring it about that the domestic dog became accustomed to hunting, while the hunting dog, conversely, learned to refrain from chasing hares. René Descartes <note>A famous French philosopher and mathematician; Spinoza critiques his theory that the mind and body interact through a physical point in the brain.</note> favors this opinion not a little. For he established that the Soul or Mind is united principally to a certain part of the brain, namely the so-called **pineal gland**, by whose help the Mind perceives all the motions that are excited in the Body and external objects, and which the Mind can move in various ways simply because it wills it. He established that this gland is suspended in the middle of the brain in such a way that it can be moved by the slightest motion of the animal spirits <term>Animal spirits: In 17th-century biology, these were thought to be extremely fine fluids or "vapors" that traveled through the nerves to transmit sensation and movement.</term>. Next, he established that this gland is suspended in the middle of the brain in as many different ways as the spirits...*

<summary>Spinoza begins the final section of his work by defining "Human Freedom" through the power of reason. He rejects the views of the Stoics and Descartes, who both argued—in different ways—that the mind could exercise absolute, voluntary control over the body's passions.</summary>

<keywords>reason, freedom, blessedness, Stoicism, Descartes, pineal gland, animal spirits, emotions, self-control</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Spinoza’s critique of René Descartes’ theory of the soul and body. # ETHICS PART V.

*animal spirits strike against the same [gland], and that furthermore, as many different traces are impressed upon it as there are different external objects that propel the animal spirits <note>In 17th-century science, "animal spirits" were thought to be very subtle fluids that flowed through the nerves to move muscles and carry sensory information.</note> toward it; whence it happens that if the gland is later suspended in this or that way by the will of the Soul, moving it in various ways—the way it was once suspended by the spirits agitated in this or that way—then the gland itself will propel and determine the animal spirits in the same way as they had previously been repelled by a similar suspension of the gland. Furthermore, he maintains that every act of the Mind’s will is by Nature united to a certain movement of the gland. For example, if someone has the will to look at a distant object, this will causes the pupil to dilate; but if he thinks only of dilating the pupil, it will do no good to have the will for that thing, because Nature has not joined the movement of the gland—which serves to impel the spirits toward the Optic nerve in a manner suitable for dilating or contracting the pupil—with the will to dilate or contract it, but only with the will to look at distant or near objects. Finally, he maintains that although each movement of this gland seems to be connected by Nature to each of our thoughts from the beginning of our life, they can nevertheless be joined to others through habit; which he tries to prove in Article 50, Part 1, of* The Passions of the Soul.
*From these things he concludes that there is no Soul so weak that it cannot, when well directed, acquire absolute power over its Passions <note>In this context, "Passions" refers to emotions or internal disturbances caused by external forces.</note>. For these, as defined by him, are perceptions, or sensations, or commotions of the soul, which are specifically referred to it, and which—NB <note>The Latin abbreviation "NB" stands for "nota bene," meaning "note well" or "take notice."</note>. <sup>1)</sup> are produced, preserved, and strengthened by some movement of the spirits (see Article 27, Part 1, of* The Passions of the Soul*). But since we can join any movement of the gland, and consequently of the spirits, to any will; and since the determination of the will depends on our power alone; if we therefore determine our will by certain and firm judgments according to which we wish to direct the actions of our life, and join the movements of the passions we wish to have to these judgments, we shall acquire absolute command over our Passions. This is the opinion of this most distinguished man (as far as I can gather from his own words), which I could hardly have believed had been put forward by so great a man, had it been less clever. Indeed, I cannot wonder enough that a Philosopher, who had firmly resolved to deduce nothing except from principles known through themselves, and to affirm nothing except what he clearly and distinctly perceived, and who had so often criticized the Scholastics <note>The Scholastics were medieval scholars who relied on traditional Church authority and Aristotelian logic, often using "occult qualities"—mysterious, unobservable properties—to explain natural phenomena.</note> for wanting to explain obscure things through occult qualities, should take up a Hypothesis more occult than any occult quality. What, I ask, does he understand by the union of Mind and Body? What clear and distinct concept does he have, I say, of thought most closely united to a certain tiny portion of quantity? I would certainly wish that he had explained this union through its proximate cause. But he had conceived the Mind as so distinct from the Body that he could not assign any particular cause for this union, nor for the Mind itself, but found it necessary to recur to the cause of the whole Universe, that is, to God. Next, I would very much like to know how many degrees of motion the Mind can attribute to that pineal gland, and with how much force it can hold it suspended. For I do not know whether this*

---

1) <note>This footnote discusses a textual variation in different editions.</note> Dutch Version: *and which (note) are produced by some movement of the spirits...* — First Edition: *which are produced.* Correction: the Author had written it thus: *NB. produced.*

<summary>Spinoza summarizes and then sharply criticizes Descartes' theory of the pineal gland as the bridge between mind and body, calling the idea of a soul-body union through a "tiny portion of quantity" an unscientific mystery.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Spinoza’s critique of René Descartes’ theory of the soul and body. On the preceding page, Spinoza began summarizing Descartes’ view that the "animal spirits" (the fine fluids then thought to transmit nerve signals) interact with the soul via the pineal gland. # ON HUMAN FREEDOM.

<note>original: "glans"</note> the gland is turned more slowly or more quickly by the *Mind* than by the *animal spirits*, and whether the movements of the *Passions*, which we have closely joined to firm judgments, cannot be separated from them again by bodily causes; from which it would follow that, although the *Mind* has firmly proposed to go against dangers, and has joined this decree to the movement of boldness, nonetheless, once the danger is seen, the gland might be suspended in such a way that the *Mind* can think of nothing but flight. And truly, since there is no proportion between the will and motion, there is also no comparison between the power or forces of the *Mind* and those of the *Body*; and consequently the forces of the latter can by no means be determined by the forces of the former. Add to this that this gland is not found to be so situated in the middle of the brain that it can be turned around so easily and in so many ways, and that not all nerves extend as far as the cavities of the brain. Finally, I omit everything he asserts concerning the will and its freedom, since I have already shown more than sufficiently that these things are false. Therefore, because the power of the *Mind*, as I have shown above, is defined by intelligence alone, we shall determine the remedies for the emotions—which I believe everyone experiences, but does not accurately observe nor clearly see—solely by the knowledge of the *Mind*, and from that same knowledge we shall deduce all those things that pertain to its <term>blessedness</term> <note>Latin: "beatitudo"; in Spinoza's philosophy, this is the state of peace achieved through the understanding of God or Nature.</note>.

## ->A X I O M S.<-

I. If two contrary actions are excited in the same subject, a change must necessarily occur in both, or in one of them, until they cease to be contrary.
II. The power of an effect is defined by the power of its cause, insofar as its essence is explained or defined by the essence of its cause.
This Axiom is evident from Proposition 7, Part 3.

## ->PROPOSITION I.<-

*Just as thoughts and ideas of things are ordered and linked in the Mind, so the affections of the Body, or the images of things, are exactly ordered and linked in the Body.*

### ->D E M O N S T R A T I O N.<-

The order and connection of ideas is the same <note>original: "per Prop. 7. p. 2."</note> (*through Proposition 7, part 2*) as the order and connection of things, and vice versa, the order and connection of things is the same (*through the Corollary to Propositions 6 and 7, part 2*) as the order and connection of ideas. Therefore, just as the order and connection of ideas in the Mind occurs according to the order and linking of the affections of the Body (*through Proposition 18, part 2*), so vice versa (*through Proposition 2, part 3*) the order and connection of the affections of the Body occurs just as thoughts and ideas of things are ordered and linked in the Mind. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

## ->PROPOSITION II.<-

*If we remove an agitation of the mind, or emotion, from the thought of an external cause, and join it to other thoughts, then the Love or Hate toward the external cause, as well as the fluctuations of the mind arising from these emotions, will be destroyed.*

### ->D E M O N S T R A T I O N.<-

For that which constitutes the form of Love or Hate is Joy or Sadness, accompanied by the idea of an external cause (*through Definitions 6 and 7 of the Emotions*);

<sig>11</sig>

<summary>Spinoza concludes his rejection of Descartes' pineal gland theory, arguing that the mind's power comes from understanding rather than physical control of the brain. He begins laying out the geometric foundations for how the mind can overcome emotions by reordering its thoughts.</summary>
<keywords>pineal gland, René Descartes, animal spirits, mind-body parallelism, axioms, joy, sadness, love, hate, Spinoza's Ethics</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Spinoza’s transition from criticizing Descartes' physiological theories of the soul to his own psychological and logical remedies for the emotions. On the previous page, he concluded that the Mind’s power is defined by intelligence alone. Here, he begins to explain how clear thinking can dissolve the power that "passions" hold over us.</meta>

ETHICS PART V.

this <note>the external cause</note> having been removed, the form of Love or Hate is simultaneously taken away; and thus these emotions, and those which arise from them, are destroyed. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

## PROPOSITION III.

*An emotion <term>affectus: a state of mind or body; an emotion</term>, which is a passion <term>passio: a "passive" emotion where the mind is acted upon from the outside</term>, ceases to be a passion as soon as we form a clear and distinct idea of it.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

*An emotion which is a passion is a confused idea (by the general Definition of the Emotions). If, therefore, we form a clear and distinct idea of the emotion itself, this idea is distinguished from the emotion itself—insofar as it is referred to the Mind alone—only by reason (by Proposition 21, Part 2, with its Scholium); and thus (by Proposition 3, Part 3) the emotion will cease to be a passion. Which was to be demonstrated.*

### COROLLARY.

*An emotion is therefore more in our power, and the Mind suffers less from it, the more it is known to us.*

## PROPOSITION IV.

*There is no affection <term>affectio: a specific state or change in the body</term> of the Body of which we cannot form some clear and distinct concept.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

*Those things which are common to all things can only be conceived adequately (by Proposition 38, Part 2); and thus (by Proposition 12 and Lemma 2, found after the Scholium of Proposition 13, Part 2) there is no affection of the Body of which we cannot form some clear and distinct concept. Which was to be demonstrated.*

### COROLLARY.

*From this it follows that there is no emotion of which we cannot form some clear and distinct concept. For an emotion is the idea of an affection of the Body (by the general Definition of the Emotions), which therefore (by the preceding Proposition) must involve some clear and distinct concept.*

### SCHOLIUM.

*Since nothing exists from which some effect does not follow (by Proposition 36, Part 1), and since we clearly and distinctly understand everything that follows from an idea which is adequate in us (by Proposition 40, Part 2); it follows from this that everyone has the power to understand themselves and their emotions—if not absolutely, then at least in part—clearly and distinctly, and consequently to bring it about that they suffer less from them. Therefore, we must take special care to know each emotion as clearly and distinctly as possible, so that the Mind may be determined by the emotion to think of those things which it perceives clearly and distinctly, and in which it fully acquiesces; and thus, that the emotion itself may be separated from the thought of an external cause and joined to true thoughts. The result will be not only that Love, Hate, etc., are destroyed (by Proposition 2 of this part), but also that the appetites or Desires <term>Cupiditates: the very essence of a human insofar as it is determined to some action</term> which usually arise from such an emotion,*

---

1) Schmidt, Bruder. — First Edition: 22. <note>This refers to variations in numbering between different historical editions of Spinoza's text.</note>

<summary>Spinoza argues that "passions"—emotions that control us—lose their power as soon as we understand them clearly. He asserts that because all bodily states can be understood through reason, we have the innate power to transform our reactive emotions into active, clear thoughts.</summary>

--- Page 180 ---
<meta>This page continues Spinoza’s exploration of the "remedies" for the emotions. Having established that clear thinking transforms a passive emotion into an active one, he now examines how our perception of whether a thing is "free," "necessary," or "contingent" changes the intensity of our feelings toward it.</meta>

-># ON HUMAN FREEDOM<-

cannot have excess <note>original: "excessum habere nequeant"</note> (by Proposition 61, Part 4). For it is especially to be noted that it is one and the same appetite <term>appetitus: the basic drive or striving of a human being</term> through which a person is said both to act and to suffer. For example, we have shown that human nature is so constituted that everyone desires <note>original: "appetat"</note> that others should live according to his own temperament <note>see Scholium 2 <margin>Schmidt, Auerbach: Corollary</margin> of Proposition 31, Part 3</note>; this appetite, in a person who is not led by Reason, is a passion <term>passio: an emotion where we are the "passive" victim of external causes</term> which is called Ambition <note>original: "Ambitio"</note>, and it does not differ much from Pride; but on the contrary, in a person who lives according to the dictates of Reason, it is an action or virtue, which is called Piety <term>pietas: here, a rational desire to do good and join in friendship with others</term> <note>see Scholium 1 of Proposition 37, Part 4, and the second Demonstration of the same Proposition</note>. In this way, all appetites or Desires <note>original: "Cupiditates"</note> are passions only insofar as they arise from inadequate ideas; and these same desires are accounted as virtues when they are excited or generated by adequate ideas. For all Desires by which we are determined to do something can arise both from adequate and from inadequate ideas (by Proposition 59, Part 4). And (to return to the point from which I digressed) no other remedy for the emotions that depends on our power can be devised which is more excellent than this, which consists in a true knowledge of them; since no other power of the Mind is given than that of thinking and forming adequate ideas, as we have shown above (by Proposition 3, Part 3).

### PROPOSITION V.
*The emotion <term>affectus: an emotion or state of being</term> toward a thing which we imagine simply, and not as necessary, nor as possible, nor as contingent, is, all other things being equal, the greatest of all.*

### DEMONSTRATION.
The emotion toward a thing which we imagine to be free is greater than toward a necessary one (by Proposition 49, Part 3), and consequently even greater than toward that which we imagine as possible or contingent (by Proposition 11, Part 4). But to imagine a thing as free can be nothing else than to imagine the thing simply, while we are ignorant of the causes by which it was determined to act (by what we showed in the Scholium to Proposition 35, Part 2); therefore, the emotion toward a thing which we imagine simply is, all other things being equal, greater than toward a necessary, possible, or contingent thing, and consequently is the greatest. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### PROPOSITION VI.
*Insofar as the Mind understands all things as necessary, it has a greater power over the emotions, or suffers less from them.*

### DEMONSTRATION.
The Mind understands all things to be necessary (by Proposition 29, Part 1), and to be determined to exist and operate by an infinite chain of causes (by Proposition 28, Part 1); and therefore (by the preceding Proposition) it brings it about that it suffers less from the emotions which arise from them, and (by Proposition 48, Part 3) is less affected toward them. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

<summary>Spinoza argues that our desires can be either destructive passions or rational virtues depending on our level of understanding, and that recognizing the necessity of all events lessens the emotional impact they have on us.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Part V of the Ethics, specifically the discussion on the power of the Mind (intellect) over the emotions. The previous page ended while explaining that there is no remedy for the emotions within our power more excellent than the true knowledge of them.</meta>

-># PART V OF THE ETHICS<-

### SCHOLIUM.

The more this knowledge—namely, the realization that things are necessary <note>original: "res necessariæ sint"</note>—is focused on singular things, which we imagine more distinctly and vividly, the greater is the Mind's power over the emotions; this is a fact that experience itself bears witness to. For we see that the Sadness <term>Tristitia: in Spinoza's system, a decrease in a person's power of acting</term> over a lost good is lessened as soon as the person who lost it considers that there was no way that good could have been preserved. Similarly, we see that no one pities an infant because it does not know how to speak, walk, or reason, or because it lives so many years as if unaware of itself. But if most people were born as adults, and only one or two were born as infants, then everyone would pity infants; for then one would consider infancy itself not as a natural and necessary thing, but as a defect or a sin of Nature <note>original: "Naturæ vitium seu peccatum"</note>. We could note many other examples of this kind.

## PROPOSITION VII.

*Emotions that arise from or are excited by Reason are, if we consider the factor of time, more powerful than those related to singular things which we regard as absent.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

We do not regard a thing as absent because of the emotion with which we imagine it, but because the Body is affected by another emotion that excludes the existence of that thing (by Proposition 17, Part 2). Therefore, an emotion related to a thing we regard as absent is not of such a nature as to overcome a person’s other actions and power (regarding which, see Proposition 6, Part 4). But an emotion that arises from Reason is necessarily related to the common properties of things <note>see the definition of Reason in Scholium 2, Proposition 40, Part 2</note>, which we always regard as present (for nothing can exist that excludes their present existence), and which we always imagine in the same way (by Proposition 38, Part 2). Therefore, such an emotion always remains the same; and consequently (by Axiom 1 of this Part), emotions that are contrary to it, and which are not fueled by their own external causes, will have to accommodate themselves to it more and more, until they are no longer contrary; to that extent, the emotion arising from Reason is more powerful. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

## PROPOSITION VIII.

*The more causes that work together simultaneously to excite an emotion, the greater it is.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

Many causes together can do more than if there were fewer (by Proposition 7, Part 3); and thus (by Proposition 5, Part 4), the more causes that simultaneously excite an emotion, the stronger it is. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### SCHOLIUM.

This Proposition is also evident from Axiom 2 of this Part.

<summary>Spinoza explains how understanding the necessity of events reduces emotional pain and argues that rational emotions are stronger than those directed at absent objects because reason deals with eternal, "present" truths.</summary>

## PROPOSITION IX.

*An emotion <term>Affectus: often translated as "affect," this refers to any state of mind that increases or decreases our power to act</term> that is referred to many and diverse causes, which the Mind contemplates together with the emotion itself, is less harmful; we suffer less through it, and are less affected toward each individual cause, than another equally great emotion that is referred to only one or a few causes.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

An emotion is only bad or harmful insofar as it prevents the Mind from being able to think (*by Propositions 26 and 27, Part 4*). Therefore, an emotion that determines the Mind to contemplate many objects at the same time is less harmful than another equally great emotion that holds the Mind fixed <gloss>1) The First Edition reads: "the Mind by force alone."</gloss> in the contemplation of only one or a few objects in such a way that it cannot think of others. This was the first point. Next, because the essence of the Mind—that is (*by Proposition 7, Part 3*), its power—consists in thinking alone (*by Proposition 11, Part 2* <gloss>2) Editors Schmidt and Auerbach note that the First Edition incorrectly cited Part 3 here.</gloss>), it follows that the Mind suffers <note>original: "patitur"—in Spinoza's philosophy, "suffering" or "passion" occurs when the Mind is not the adequate cause of its own actions</note> less through an emotion that determines it to contemplate many things at once than through an equally great emotion that keeps the Mind occupied in the contemplation of only one or a few objects. This was the second point. Finally, this emotion (*by Proposition 48, Part 3*), insofar as it is referred to many external causes, is also smaller toward each individual cause. ***Which was to be demonstrated.***

## PROPOSITION X.

*As long as we are not torn by emotions that are contrary to our nature, we have the power to order and connect the affections <term>Affectiones: the physical or mental modifications the body undergoes when interacting with the world</term> of the Body according to the order of the intellect.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

Emotions that are contrary to our nature—that is (*by Proposition 30, Part 4*), those that are bad—are bad only insofar as they prevent the Mind from understanding (*by Proposition 27, Part 4*). Therefore, as long as we are not torn by emotions contrary to our nature, the power of the Mind by which it strives to understand things (*by Proposition 26, Part 4*) is not hindered. Thus, for that time, the Mind has the power to form clear and distinct ideas and to deduce one from another (*see the second Scholium to Proposition 40 and the Scholium to Proposition 47, Part 2*). Consequently (*by Proposition 1 of this Part*), for that same time, we have the power to order and connect the affections of the Body according to the order of the intellect. ***Which was to be demonstrated.***

### SCHOLIUM.

By this power of correctly ordering and connecting the affections of the Body, we can ensure that we are not easily affected by bad emotions. For (*by Proposition 7 of this Part*), a greater force is required to restrain emotions that are ordered and connected according to the order of the intellect than to restrain those that are uncertain and wandering. Therefore, the best we can do—as long as we do not have perfect knowledge of our emotions—is to conceive a correct way of living, or certain rules of life <note>original: "vitæ dogmata"</note>, to commit them to memory, and to apply them continually to the particular situations that frequently occur in life. In this way, our imagination may be widely affected by them, and they will always be ready for us

---

<summary>Spinoza argues that spreading an emotion across multiple causes reduces its power over us and explains that in calm moments, we can use our intellect to train our minds and bodies through "rules of life."</summary>
<keywords>emotion, power of the mind, intellect, rules of life, causes, suffering, clear and distinct ideas</keywords>

--- Page 183 ---
<meta>This page continues the Scholium to Proposition 10 in Part V of the Ethics. Spinoza is explaining practical "rules for living" (dogmata) that help the mind maintain its power over the emotions by preparing rational responses in advance.</meta>

<page-num>166</page-num>
<header>PART V OF THE ETHICS.</header>

...should always be present. For example, among the rules of life we have placed (*see Proposition 46, Part 4, with its Scholium*), that **Hate** is to be conquered by **Love** or **Nobility** <term>Generositas: Spinoza defines this as the desire to join oneself to others in friendship and mutual aid</term>, and not compensated by reciprocal **Hate**. To have this prescript of Reason always ready at hand where it is needed, we must think and often meditate on the common injuries of men, and how and by what way they are best repelled by Nobility; for in this way, we shall join the image of an injury to the imagination of this rule, and it will (*by Proposition 18, Part 2*) always be ready for us whenever an injury is offered. 

If we also have ready a calculation of our true advantage, and also of the good that follows from mutual friendship and common society, and furthermore that from the right way of living arises the highest peace of mind (*by Proposition 52, Part 4*), and that men, like everything else, act from the necessity of nature: then the injury, or the **Hate** that usually arises from it, will occupy the smallest part of the imagination and be easily overcome. Or if **Anger**, which usually arises from the greatest injuries, is not so easily overcome, it will nevertheless be overcome—though not without mental struggle—in a much shorter space of time than if we had not premeditated these things, as is clear from Propositions 6, 7, and 8 of this Part. 

We must think in the same way about **Courage** <term>Animositas: the desire by which each individual strives to preserve their own being through the dictates of reason</term> to lay aside **Fear**; specifically, the common dangers of life must be enumerated and often imagined, and how they can best be avoided and overcome by presence of mind and fortitude. But it should be noted that in ordering our thoughts and images, we must always attend (*by the Corollary to Proposition 63, Part 4, and Proposition 59, Part 3*) to those things that are *good* in each thing, so that we are always determined to act from an affect of **Joy**. 

For example, if someone sees that they pursue **Glory** <note>original: "Gloriam"—referring here to the desire for reputation or public praise</note> too much, let them think of its right use, for what end it is to be pursued, and by what means it can be acquired; but not of its abuse, and vanity, and the inconstancy of men, or other things of this sort, which no one thinks of except out of a sickness of mind. For with such thoughts the most ambitious most afflict themselves when they despair of attaining the honor they seek; and while they vomit out their **Anger**, they want to appear wise. Therefore, it is certain that those are most greedy for glory who cry out most loudly about its abuse and the vanity of the world. 

Nor is this peculiar to the ambitious, but common to all for whom fortune is adverse and who are weak in spirit. For even a greedy poor man does not stop talking about the abuse of money and the vices of the rich; by which he achieves nothing else but to afflict himself and show others that he bears with an uneasy mind not only his own poverty but also the wealth of others. So too, those who have been poorly received by a lover think of nothing but the inconstancy of women, their deceitful hearts, and their other well-worn vices; all of which they immediately consign to oblivion as soon as they are received by their lover again. 

He, therefore, who strives to moderate his affects and appetites solely out of the love of **Freedom**, will strive as much as he can to know the virtues and their causes, and to fill his mind with the joy that arises from a true knowledge of them; but not at all to contemplate men's vices, to disparage men, or to rejoice in a false appearance of freedom. And he who observes these things diligently <note>1) Saisset. — The First Edition reads: *observed.*</note>

<summary>Spinoza provides practical psychological exercises for mastering emotions, advising that we should pre-meditate on rational responses to injury and focus on positive virtues rather than dwelling on the vices of others or the unfairness of life.</summary>
