(for these things are not difficult) and practices them, surely in a short space of time he will be able to direct his actions for the most part according to the command of Reason.

## PROPOSITION XI.

***The more an image is related to many things, the more frequent it is, or the more often it is active, and the more it occupies the Mind.***

### DEMONSTRATION.

For the more an image or emotion <term>Affectus: In Spinoza’s philosophy, an "affect" is a change in our power to act, often translated as "emotion" or "feeling"</term> is related to many things, the more causes there are by which it can be stirred up and fostered, all of which the Mind (by hypothesis) contemplates simultaneously with the emotion itself; and therefore the emotion is more frequent, or more often active, and (by Proposition 8 of this Part) occupies the Mind more. This is what was to be demonstrated.

## PROPOSITION XII.

***Images of things are more easily joined to images that relate to things we understand clearly and distinctly, than to other images.***

### DEMONSTRATION.

The things we understand clearly and distinctly <note>To Spinoza, a "clear and distinct" idea is one that is so sharp and well-defined that we can perceive its essential truth without confusion.</note> are either the common properties of things or what is deduced from them (see the definition of Reason in the 2nd Scholium to Proposition 40, Part 2), and consequently, they are more often stirred up in us (by the preceding Proposition); and so it can more easily happen that we contemplate other things together with these than with others, and consequently (by Proposition 18, Part 2), they are more easily joined with these than with others. This is what was to be demonstrated.

## PROPOSITION XIII.

***The more an image is joined to many other images, the more often it is active.***

### DEMONSTRATION.

For the more an image is joined to many other images, the more causes there are (by Proposition 18, Part 2) by which it can be stirred up. This is what was to be demonstrated.

## PROPOSITION XIV.

***The Mind can bring it about that all states of the Body, or images of things, are related to the idea of God.***

### DEMONSTRATION.

There is no state of the Body <term>Affectio: A state or modification of the body; how the body is affected by external things</term> of which the Mind cannot form some clear and distinct concept (by Proposition 4 of this Part); and therefore it can bring it about (by Proposition 15, Part 1) that all states of the body are related to the idea of God. This is what was to be demonstrated.

## PROPOSITION XV.

***He who understands himself and his emotions clearly and distinctly loves God, and the more so, the more he understands himself and his emotions.***

### DEMONSTRATION.

He who understands himself and his emotions clearly and distinctly rejoices (by Proposition 53, Part 3), and this is accompanied by the idea of God (by the preceding Proposition); and therefore (by the 6th Definition of the Emotions <note>Spinoza defines Love as "Joy accompanied by the idea of an external cause."</note>) he loves God, and (for the same reason) the more so, the more he understands himself and his emotions. This is what was to be demonstrated.

<summary>Spinoza explains how the mind can gain mastery over the emotions by linking them to clear, rational thoughts and ultimately to the idea of God, leading to a state of rational love.</summary>

## PROPOSITION XVI.
***This Love toward God ought to occupy the Mind most of all.***

### DEMONSTRATION.
For this Love is joined to all the affections <term>Affectus: Changes in the body's power to act, coupled with the ideas of those changes; often translated as "emotions" or "feelings"</term> of the Body (through Proposition 14 of this Part), all of which foster it (through Proposition 15 of this Part); and therefore (through Proposition 11 of this Part) it ought to occupy the Mind most of all. This is what was to be demonstrated.

## PROPOSITION XVII.
***God is without passions, and is not affected by any emotion of Joy or Sadness.***

### DEMONSTRATION.
All ideas, insofar as they are related to God, are true (through Proposition 32, Part 2), that is (through Definition 4, Part 2) they are adequate; and therefore (through the General Definition of the Emotions) God is without passions <note>In Spinoza’s system, a "passion" is an emotion where we are not the adequate cause of the change—it is something that happens *to* us. Since God is the cause of all things and is never passive, God cannot have passions.</note>. Furthermore, God can pass neither to a greater nor to a lesser perfection (through the 2nd Corollary to Proposition 20, Part 1); and so (through the 2nd and 3rd Definitions of the Emotions) He is affected by no emotion of Joy or Sadness. This is what was to be demonstrated.

### COROLLARY.
Strictly speaking, God loves no one and hates no one. For God (through the preceding Proposition) is affected by no emotion of Joy or Sadness, and consequently (through the 6th and 7th Definitions of the Emotions) He neither loves nor hates anyone.

## PROPOSITION XVIII.
***No one can hate God.***

### DEMONSTRATION.
The idea of God which is in us is adequate and perfect (through Propositions 46 and 47, Part 2); and therefore insofar as we contemplate God, to that extent we act (through Proposition 3, Part 3); and consequently (through Proposition 59, Part 3) no Sadness can exist accompanied by the idea of God, which is to say (through the 7th Definition of the Emotions), no one can hate God. This is what was to be demonstrated.

### COROLLARY.
***Love toward God cannot be turned into hate.***

### SCHOLIUM.
But it may be objected that, while we understand God to be the cause of all things, by 그 very fact we consider God to be the cause of Sadness. But to this I reply that, insofar as we understand the causes of Sadness, to that extent (through Proposition 3 of this Part) it ceases to be a passion, that is (through Proposition 59, Part 3), to that extent it ceases to be Sadness; and therefore, insofar as we understand God to be the cause of Sadness, we rejoice.

## PROPOSITION XIX.
***He who loves God cannot strive that God should love him in return.***

### DEMONSTRATION.
If a man were to strive for this, he would desire (through the Corollary to Proposition 17 of this Part) that God, whom he loves, should not be God, and consequently (through Proposition 19... <note>The text cuts off here as it continues to the next page.</note>

This establishes the "Intellectual Love of God" as a one-way devotion based on understanding rather than a reciprocal emotional relationship.</summary>

Part 3) ...would desire to be saddened, which (*through Proposition 28, Part 3*) is absurd. **Therefore, he who loves God, cannot strive that God should love him in return. Which was to be demonstrated.**

### PROPOSITION XX.

***This Love toward God cannot be stained by the emotion of Envy or Jealousy; but it is fostered the more, the more men we imagine to be joined to God by the same bond of Love.***

### DEMONSTRATION.

**This Love toward God is the highest good which we can desire according to the dictate of Reason (**_through Proposition 28, Part 4_**), and it is common to all men (**_through Proposition 36, Part 4_**), and we desire that all may enjoy the same (**_through Proposition 37, Part 4_**); and therefore (**_through the 23rd Definition of the Emotions_**) it cannot be stained by the emotion of Envy, nor even (**_through Proposition 18 of this Part, and the definition of Jealousy, for which see the Scholium to Proposition 35, Part 3_**) by the emotion of Jealousy; but on the contrary (**_through Proposition 31, Part 3_**) it must be fostered the more, the more men we imagine to enjoy the same. Which was to be demonstrated.**

### SCHOLIUM.
<note>A Scholium is an explanatory note or commentary where the author steps away from formal geometric proofs to offer further clarification or practical application.</note>

We can show in this same way that there is no emotion directly contrary to this Love by which this Love itself can be destroyed; and so we can conclude that this Love toward God is the most constant of all emotions, and, insofar as it is related to the Body, it cannot be destroyed except with the Body itself. Of what nature it is, however, insofar as it is related to the Mind alone, we shall see later.

And with these things, I have embraced all the remedies for the emotions—or everything that the Mind, considered in itself alone, can do against the emotions. From these it appears that the Mind's power over the emotions consists: 
I. In the knowledge itself of the emotions (*see the Scholium to Proposition 4 of this Part*); 
II. In the fact that it separates the emotions from the thought of an external cause, which we imagine confusedly (*see Proposition 2 with the same Scholium to Proposition<note>original: "Prop. 1)" with footnote 1: "First Edition: with the same Scholium and Proposition 4 of this part."</note> 4 of this Part*); 
III. In the time by which the affections<note>original: "2) Camerer [an editor]: emotions which... surpass those which... emotions which."</note> related to things we understand surpass those related to things we conceive confusedly or in a mutilated way (*see Proposition 7 of this Part*); 
IV. In the multitude of causes by which the affections related to common properties <term>Common Properties: Universal principles that are equally in the part and the whole, such as the laws of physics or logic, which Spinoza believes are always understood adequately.</term> or to God are fostered (*see Propositions 9 and 11 of this Part*); 
V. Finally, in the order in which the Mind can arrange its emotions and link them together (*see the Scholium to Proposition 10 and, in addition, Propositions 12, 13, and 14 of this Part*). 

But so that this power of the Mind over the emotions may be better understood, it is especially to be noted that emotions are called "great" by us when we compare one man's emotion with another's and see one man to be more conflicted by the same emotion than another; or when we compare the emotions of one and the same man with each other and find him to be more affected or moved by one emotion than another. For (*through Proposition 5, Part 4*) the force of any emotion is defined by the power of an external cause compared with our own power. But the Mind's power is defined by knowledge alone, while its lack of power...

<note>2) Camerer: emotions which... surpass those which... emotions which.</note>

<summary>This page argues that the intellectual love of God is immune to envy because it is a universal good, and begins a five-point summary of how the mind exerts power over its passive emotions through rational understanding.</summary>

--- Page 187 ---
<meta>This page continues the Scholium to Proposition 20 in Part V of the Ethics. Spinoza is concluding his "remedies for the affects" (emotions) that apply to our physical lives before transitioning to his famous and controversial discussion of the eternity of the mind.</meta>

<page-num>170</page-num>
<header>ETHICS, PART V.</header>

...or a passion <term>passio; a state where the mind is acted upon by external forces rather than being the cause of its own actions</term> is measured only by the privation of knowledge—that is, by that which causes ideas to be called <term>inadequate; ideas that are fragmented or confused because we do not see the whole chain of causes</term>. From this it follows that the Mind is most passive when inadequate ideas constitute its greatest part, so that it is characterized more by what it suffers than by what it does. Conversely, the Mind is most active when <term>adequate; clear, distinct, and complete</term> ideas constitute its greatest part, so that, even if it contains as many inadequate ideas as the former, it is nevertheless characterized more by those ideas which are attributed to human virtue <note>virtue here means "power" or "capacity"</note> than by those which prove human lack of power.

Next, it should be noted that sicknesses of the soul and misfortunes draw their origin chiefly from an excessive Love toward a thing subject to many changes, and which we can never truly possess. For no one is worried or anxious about anything unless he loves it; nor do injuries, suspicions, or enmities arise, except from Love toward things of which no one can truly be the master. From these points we easily conceive what power clear and distinct knowledge—and especially that third kind of knowledge <note>original: "tertium illud cognitionis genus." This is "Intuitive Science," the highest form of understanding in Spinoza's system; see Part 2, Prop. 47, Scholium</note>, the foundation of which is the very knowledge of God—has over the affects. Even if this knowledge does not absolutely remove the affects insofar as they are passions <note>see Prop. 3 and the Scholium to Prop. 4 of this Part</note>, it at least ensures that they constitute the smallest part of the Mind <note>see Prop. 14 of this Part</note>. 

Furthermore, it begets Love toward a thing immutable and eternal <note>see Prop. 15 of this Part</note>, which we truly can possess <note>see Prop. 45, Part 2</note>; and therefore this Love cannot be polluted by any of the vices found in common Love, but can always become greater and greater <note>by Prop. 15 of this Part</note>, and occupy the greatest part of the Mind <note>by Prop. 16 of this Part</note>, affecting it extensively. With this, I have completed everything that concerns this present life. For anyone who attends to what we have said in this Scholium, and at the same time to the definitions of the Mind and its affects, and finally to Propositions 1 and 3 of Part 3, will easily see that I have embraced all the remedies for the affects in these few words. It is time, therefore, to pass on to those things which pertain to the duration of the Mind without relation to the Body.

## PROPOSITION XXI.

->*The Mind can imagine nothing, nor can it remember past things, except while the Body lasts.*<-

### DEMONSTRATION.

The Mind does not express the actual existence of its Body, nor does it conceive the affections of the Body as actual, except while the Body lasts <note>by Corollary to Prop. 8, Part 2</note>; and consequently <note>by Prop. 26, Part 2</note> it conceives no body as actually existing except while its own Body lasts; and therefore it can imagine nothing <note>see the definition of Imagination in the Scholium to Prop. 17, Part 2</note> nor remember past things except while the Body lasts <note>see the definition of Memory in the Scholium to Prop. 18, Part 2</note>. Which was to be demonstrated.

## PROPOSITION XXII.

->*Nevertheless, there is necessarily in God an idea which expresses the essence of this or that human Body under the aspect of eternity.*<-

### DEMONSTRATION.

God is not only the cause of the existence of this or that human Body, but also of its essence...

<header>ON HUMAN LIBERTY.</header>

...of power, but also of essence <note>original: "sed etiam essentiæ"</note> (by Proposition 25, Part 1); which must therefore be conceived through the very essence of God by necessity (by Axiom 4, Part 1), and that by a certain eternal necessity (by Proposition 16, Part 1); which concept must necessarily exist in God (by Proposition 3, Part 2). *Which was to be demonstrated.*

### PROPOSITION XXIII.

->*The human Mind cannot be absolutely destroyed with the Body, but something of it remains which is eternal.*<-

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

In God, there is necessarily a concept or idea that expresses the essence of the human Body (by the preceding Proposition); this idea is therefore necessarily something that belongs to the essence of the human Mind (by Proposition 13, Part 2). However, we do not attribute any duration to the human Mind that can be defined by time, except insofar as it expresses the actual existence of the Body, which is explained through duration and can be defined by time; that is (by the Corollary to Proposition 8, Part 2), we do not attribute duration to the Mind except while the Body lasts. Since, nevertheless, there is something that is conceived by a certain eternal necessity through the very essence of God (by the preceding Proposition), this "something" which belongs to the essence of the Mind will necessarily be eternal. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

->SCHOLIUM.<-

This idea, as we have said, which expresses the essence of the Body <term>under the aspect of eternity; viewing things not as they appear in a specific moment of time, but as they follow necessarily from the eternal laws of nature</term>, is a certain mode of thinking that belongs to the essence of the Mind, and which is necessarily eternal. Yet, it is impossible that we should remember having existed before the Body, since no traces of such a state can exist in the Body, and because eternity cannot be defined by time, nor have any relation to time. But regardless, we feel and experience <note>original: "sentimus experimurque"</note> that we are eternal. For the Mind feels those things that it conceives by understanding no less than those it has in memory. Indeed, the eyes of the Mind, by which it sees and observes things, are the demonstrations themselves. Therefore, even if we do not remember existing before the Body, we nevertheless feel that our Mind—insofar as it involves the essence of the Body under the aspect of eternity—is eternal, and that this existence of the Mind cannot be defined by time or explained through duration. Our Mind, then, can only be said to endure, and its existence can only be defined by a certain time, insofar as it involves the actual existence of the Body; and only to that extent does it have the power to determine the existence of things in time and conceive of them under the category of duration.

### PROPOSITION XXIV.

->*The more we understand singular things, the more we understand God.*<-

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

This is evident from the Corollary to Proposition 25, Part 1.

### PROPOSITION XXV.

->*The Mind’s highest effort and its highest virtue is to understand things by the third kind of knowledge.*<-

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

The <term>third kind of knowledge; Intuitive Science, which proceeds from an adequate idea of the formal essence of certain attributes of God to the adequate knowledge of the essence of things</term> proceeds from an adequate idea of certain...

...of God’s attributes to an adequate knowledge of the essence of things (see *the second Scholium to Proposition 40, Part 2*); and the more we understand things in this way, the more (*by the preceding Proposition*) we understand God; and therefore (*by Proposition 28, Part 4*) the highest virtue of the Mind, that is (*by Definition 8, Part 4*) the power or nature of the Mind, or (*by Proposition 7, Part 3*) its highest striving <note>original: "summus conatus"</note>, is to understand things by the third kind of knowledge <term>Knowledge of the third kind; Spinoza’s term for "intuitive knowledge," which sees things as following directly from the eternal nature of God rather than just through logical rules or sensory experience.</term>. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

## PROPOSITION XXVI.

*The more the Mind is capable of understanding things by the third kind of knowledge, the more it desires to understand things by this same kind of knowledge.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

This is evident. For insofar as we conceive the Mind to be capable of understanding things by this kind of knowledge, we conceive it to be determined to understand things by this same kind of knowledge; and consequently (*by the first Definition of the Affects*), the more capable the Mind is of this, the more it desires it. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

## PROPOSITION XXVII.

*From this third kind of knowledge arises the highest contentment of the Mind <term>Acquiescentia; often translated as "self-contentment" or "peace of mind," it refers to the joy that arises when a person contemplates their own power of acting.</term> that can possibly exist.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

The highest virtue of the Mind is to know God (*by Proposition 28, Part 4*), or to understand things by the third kind of knowledge (*by Proposition 25 of this Part*); this virtue is greater the more the Mind knows things by this kind of knowledge (*by Proposition 24 of this Part*); and thus, he who knows things by this kind of knowledge passes to the highest human perfection, and consequently (*by the second Definition of the Affects*) is affected by the highest Joy, and this (*by Proposition 43, Part 2*) accompanied by the idea of himself and his virtue; and therefore (*by the 25th Definition of the Affects*) from this kind of knowledge arises the highest contentment that can possibly exist. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

## PROPOSITION XXVIII.

*The striving or Desire for knowing things by the third kind of knowledge cannot arise from the first kind of knowledge, but indeed from the second.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

This Proposition is self-evident. For whatever we understand clearly and distinctly, we understand either through itself or through that which is conceived through itself; that is, ideas which are clear and distinct in us, or which refer to the third kind of knowledge (see *the second Scholium to Proposition 40, Part 2*), cannot follow from mutilated and confused ideas, which (*by the same Scholium*) refer to the first kind of knowledge <note>The "first kind" refers to sensory experience and imagination, which Spinoza considers unreliable and fragmentary.</note>, but from adequate ideas, or (*by the same Scholium*) from the second and third kinds of knowledge; and therefore (*by the first Definition of the Affects*) the Desire for knowing things by the third kind of knowledge cannot arise from the first, but indeed from the second <note>The "second kind" of knowledge is reason, or "common notions," which provides the logical foundation for intuition.</note>. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

## PROPOSITION XXIX.

*Whatever the Mind understands under the aspect of eternity <term>Sub specie æternitatis; viewing things not as they exist in a specific moment of time, but as they follow necessarily from the eternal laws of nature/God.</term>, it does not understand from the fact that it conceives the Body’s present actual existence, but from the fact that it conceives the Body’s essence under the aspect of eternity.*

<meta>This page continues the argument from page 172 regarding the "third kind of knowledge" (intuition). Spinoza is moving from the Mind's ability to understand the world to its inherent connection to the eternal nature of God, specifically how the Mind can view itself and the Body outside of the flow of time.</meta>

<page-num>173</page-num>
<header>->ON HUMAN FREEDOM.<-</header>

### DEMONSTRATION.

Insofar as the Mind conceives the present existence of its Body, it conceives a duration that can be determined by time, and only to that extent does it have the power of conceiving things in relation to time <note>original: "cum relatione ad tempus"</note> (*by Proposition 21 of this Part and Proposition 26, Part 2*). But eternity cannot be explained through duration (*by Definition 8, Part 1, and its explanation*). Therefore, the Mind to that extent does not have the power of conceiving things under the form of eternity; but because it is of the nature of Reason to conceive things under the form of eternity <term>Sub specie aeternitatis; a central concept in Spinoza's philosophy meaning to see things as they are in their eternal, necessary relationship to God, rather than as they appear in a specific moment of time.</term> (*by the second Corollary to Proposition 44, Part 2*), and it also pertains to the nature of the Mind to conceive the essence of the Body under the form of eternity (*by Proposition 23 of this Part*), and besides these two nothing else pertains to the essence of the Mind (*by Proposition 13, Part 2*); therefore, this power of conceiving things under the form of eternity does not pertain to the Mind, except insofar as it conceives the essence of the Body under the form of eternity. <note>original: "Q. E. D." (Quod erat demonstrandum), meaning "Which was to be demonstrated."</note>

### SCHOLIUM.

Things are conceived by us as actual in two ways: either insofar as we conceive them to exist in relation to a certain time and place, or insofar as we conceive them to be contained in God and to follow from the necessity of the divine nature. Those things, however, which are conceived in this second way as true or real, we conceive under the form of eternity, and the ideas of them involve the eternal and infinite essence of God, as we showed in Proposition 45 of Part 2; see also the Scholium to that Proposition.

## PROPOSITION XXX.

*Our Mind, insofar as it knows itself and the Body under the form of eternity, necessarily has knowledge of God, and knows that it is in God and is conceived through God.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

Eternity is the very essence of God, insofar as this involves necessary existence (*by Definition 8, Part 1*). Therefore, to conceive things under the form of eternity is to conceive things insofar as they are conceived through the essence of God as real beings, or insofar as they involve existence through the essence of God; and therefore our Mind, insofar as it conceives itself and the Body under the form of eternity, necessarily has knowledge of God, and knows etc. <note>original: "Q. E. D."</note>

## PROPOSITION XXXI.

*The third kind of knowledge depends on the Mind as its formal cause, insofar as the Mind itself is eternal.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

The Mind conceives nothing under the form of eternity except insofar as it conceives the essence of its own Body under the form of eternity (*by Proposition 29 of this Part*), that is (*by Propositions 21 and 23 of this Part*), except insofar as it is eternal; and therefore (*by the preceding Proposition*), insofar as it is eternal, it has knowledge of God; which knowledge is necessarily adequate (*by Proposition 46, Part 2*); and consequently the Mind, insofar as it is eternal, is capable of knowing all those things which can follow from this given knowledge of God (*by Proposition 40, Part 2*), that is, of knowing things by the third kind of knowledge (see

<meta>This page continues the exploration of the "third kind of knowledge"—intuition—and its relationship to eternity. On the previous page, Spinoza established that the Mind can conceive the essence of the Body "under the form of eternity." Here, he transitions into how this high-level understanding produces a specific kind of spiritual joy and love for the Divine.</meta>

<page-num>174</page-num>
<header>ETHICS PART V.</header>

(see the definition of this in the second Scholium to Proposition 40, Part 2), for which reason the Mind (by Definition 1, Part 3), insofar as it is eternal, is its adequate or formal cause. <note>original: "Q. E. D." (Quod erat demonstrandum), meaning "Which was to be demonstrated."</note>

### SCHOLIUM.

Therefore, the more anyone excels in this kind of knowledge, the better they are conscious of themselves and of God; that is, the more perfect and blessed they are, as will appear even more clearly from what follows. But it should be noted here that, although we are now certain that the Mind is eternal insofar as it conceives things under the form of eternity, we shall—in order for the things we wish to show to be more easily explained and better understood—consider the Mind as if it were just now beginning to exist and just now beginning to understand things under the form of eternity, as we have done up to this point. We may do this without any danger of error, provided we are careful to conclude nothing except from clear premises.

## PROPOSITION XXXII.

*Whatever we understand by the third kind of knowledge, we take pleasure in, and this pleasure is accompanied by the idea of God as its cause.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

From this kind of knowledge arises the highest possible mental satisfaction <term>Acquiescentia; in Spinoza’s system, this is a state of deep contentment or peace of mind that comes from a correct understanding of oneself and one's place in the universe.</term>, that is (by Definition 25 of the Emotions) Joy, and this is accompanied by the idea of oneself (by Proposition 27 of this Part), and consequently (by Proposition 30 of this Part) it is also accompanied by the idea of God as its cause. <note>original: "Q. E. D."</note>

### COROLLARY.

From the third kind of knowledge, the intellectual Love of God necessarily arises. For from this kind of knowledge (by the preceding Proposition) arises Joy accompanied by the idea of God as its cause, which is (by Definition 6 of the Emotions) the Love of God—not insofar as we imagine Him as present (by Proposition 29 of this Part), but insofar as we understand God to be eternal; and this is what I call the intellectual Love of God.

## PROPOSITION XXXIII.

*The intellectual Love of God, which arises from the third kind of knowledge, is eternal.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

For the third kind of knowledge (by Proposition 31 of this Part, and Axiom 3, Part 1) is eternal; and therefore (by the same Axiom, Part 1) the Love which arises from it is also necessarily eternal. <note>original: "Q. E. D."</note>

### SCHOLIUM.

Although this Love toward God had no beginning (by the preceding Proposition), it nevertheless possesses all the perfections of Love, just as if it had come into being, as we imagined in the Corollary to the preceding Proposition. Nor is there any difference here, except that the Mind has eternally possessed those same perfections which we just now imagined were being added to it, and that this was accompanied by the idea of God as an eternal cause. If Joy consists in the transition to a greater perfection, then Blessedness <term>Beatitudo; for Spinoza, this is not a reward for virtue, but virtue itself; the state of being completely realized and unified with the divine order.</term> must surely consist in the fact that the Mind is endowed with perfection itself.

<summary>Spinoza introduces the "intellectual Love of God," arguing that the highest form of human knowledge (intuition) leads to an eternal state of mental satisfaction and joy rooted in the understanding of God. He clarifies that while we describe this state as a "becoming," it is actually an eternal reality of the Mind's existence.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the logical progression of Spinoza’s "Ethics." Having established that the mind has an eternal part, Spinoza now distinguishes between the temporary emotions tied to the physical body and the "Intellectual Love of God," which is eternal. He concludes with the profound claim that our love for the Divine is actually a part of the infinite love God has for Himself.</meta>

<page-num>175</page-num>
<header>OF HUMAN FREEDOM.</header>

## PROPOSITION XXXIV.

*The Mind is subject to emotions that are classified as passions only while the Body endures.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

Imagination is an idea by which the Mind regards a certain thing as present (see its definition in the second Scholium to Proposition 17, Part 2); yet this idea indicates the present constitution of the human Body more than it indicates the nature of the external thing (by the second Corollary to Proposition 16, Part 2). Therefore, an emotion is an imagination <note>original: "imaginatio"; in Spinoza’s system, imagination refers to the mind's way of representing things based on bodily sensations, which can be Vivid but often lack logical clarity.</note> insofar as it indicates the present constitution of the Body; and therefore (by Proposition 21 of this Part) the Mind is subject to emotions that are classified as passions <term>Passions; these are "passive" emotions like fear or hate where we are being acted upon by external forces, rather than acting from our own reason.</term> only while the Body lasts. <note>original: "Q. E. D." (Quod erat demonstrandum), meaning "Which was to be demonstrated."</note>

### COROLLARIUM.

From this it follows that no Love is eternal except for **Intellectual Love**.

### SCHOLIUM.

If we look at the common opinion of men, we shall see that they are indeed conscious of the eternity of their own Minds; but they confuse this eternity with duration <note>Spinoza distinguishes between "duration" (time that has a beginning and end) and "eternity" (a timeless, logical necessity).</note>, and they attribute it to the imagination or memory, which they believe remains after death.

## PROPOSITION XXXV.

*God loves himself with an infinite intellectual Love.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

God is absolutely infinite (by Definition 6, Part 1), which is to say (by Definition 6, Part 2) that the nature of God enjoys infinite perfection, and this is accompanied (by Proposition 3, Part 2) by the idea of himself—that is (by Proposition 11 and Definition 1, Part 1) the idea of his own cause; and this is what we said in the Corollary to Proposition 32 of this Part constitutes **Intellectual Love**.

## PROPOSITION XXXVI.

*The Mind's intellectual Love toward God is the very Love of God by which God loves himself—not insofar as he is infinite, but insofar as he can be explained through the essence of the human Mind, considered under the form of eternity. That is, the Mind's intellectual Love toward God is part of the infinite love by which God loves himself.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

This Love of the Mind must be related to the actions of the Mind (by the Corollary to Proposition 32 of this Part, and by Proposition 3, Part 3); this love is therefore an action by which the Mind contemplates itself, accompanied by the idea of God as its cause (by Proposition 32 of this Part, and its Corollary). That is (by the Corollary to Proposition 25, Part 1, and the Corollary to Proposition 11, Part 2), it is an action by which God, insofar as he can be explained through the human Mind, contemplates himself, accompanied by the idea of himself; and therefore (by the preceding Proposition) this Love of the Mind is part of the infinite love by which God loves himself. *Q. E. D.*

<summary>Spinoza argues that while passive emotions die with the body, the "Intellectual Love of God" is eternal. He concludes that when a human loves God through reason, it is actually God loving Himself through that person's mind.</summary>

--- Page 193 ---
<meta>This page follows Proposition 36, which established that the mind’s intellectual love for God is part of the infinite love with which God loves Himself. This section explores the practical and spiritual implications of that unity—namely, how it constitutes human salvation and freedom.</meta>

<page-num>176</page-num>
<header>ETHICS PART V.</header>

### COROLLARIUM.

From this it follows that God, insofar as He loves Himself, loves mankind, and consequently, that God's love toward mankind and the Mind's **Intellectual Love** toward God are one and the same thing.

### SCHOLIUM.

From these points, we clearly understand what our salvation, or **Blessedness**, or **Freedom** consists in: namely, in a constant and eternal Love toward God, or in God's Love toward mankind. This Love or Blessedness is called **Glory** in the Holy Scriptures, and not without reason. For whether this Love is referred to God or to the Mind, it can rightly be called "Contentment of the Soul" <note>original: "Acquiescentia animi"; a state of inner peace or satisfaction arising from the mind's own power.</note>, which is in fact not distinguished from Glory (by the 25th and 30th Definitions of the Emotions). 

For insofar as it is referred to God (by Proposition 35 of this Part), it is **Joy**—if I may still use this word—accompanied by the idea of Himself; and it is the same insofar as it is referred to the Mind (by Proposition 27 of this Part). <note>original: "Lætitia"; Spinoza uses "Joy" carefully here. Since God is perfect and unchanging, He does not feel "emotions" in the human sense, but Spinoza uses the term to describe the state of supreme perfection.</note> 

Furthermore, because the essence of our Mind consists solely in knowledge, whose principle and foundation is God (by Proposition 15, Part 1, and the Scholium to Proposition 47, Part 2), it becomes clear to us how and by what reasoning our Mind, according to its essence and existence, follows from the divine nature and continually depends on God. I thought it worthwhile to note this here, so that I might show by this example how powerful that knowledge of individual things is—which I called **intuitive** or "of the third kind" (see the 2nd Scholium to Proposition 40, Part 2)—and how it is superior to the "universal" knowledge I called "of the second kind." 

For although I showed generally in the first Part that all things (and consequently the human Mind also) depend on God according to their essence and existence; nevertheless, that demonstration, though legitimate and beyond the reach of doubt, does not affect our Mind as strongly as when this very thing is concluded from the essence of any singular thing which we say depends on God.

### PROPOSITION XXXVII.

*Nothing exists in Nature that is contrary to this Intellectual Love, or that can take it away.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

This Intellectual Love follows necessarily from the nature of the Mind, insofar as the Mind is considered an eternal truth through the nature of God (by Propositions 33 and 29 of this Part). If, therefore, anything existed that was contrary to this Love, it would be contrary to the truth; and consequently, whatever could take away this Love would make that which is true, false—which (as is self-evident) is absurd. Therefore, nothing exists in Nature, etc. <note>original: "Q. E. D." (Quod erat demonstrandum), meaning "Which was to be demonstrated."</note>

### SCHOLIUM.

The Axiom of Part Four refers to singular things insofar as they are considered in relation to a specific time and place; I believe no one doubts this.

### PROPOSITION XXXVIII.

*The more things the Mind understands by the second and third kinds of knowledge, the less it suffers from emotions that are evil, and the less it fears death.*

<vocab>God, Intellectual Love, Blessedness, Freedom, Glory, Mind, Joy, Intuitive Knowledge, Essence, Existence</vocab>

<summary>Spinoza explains that human salvation consists in the "intellectual love of God," identifying this state with the biblical concept of "Glory." He argues that this intuitive knowledge of our connection to God provides a peace of mind that protects us from harmful emotions and the fear of death.</summary>
<keywords>intellectual love of God, salvation, blessedness, glory, intuitive knowledge, peace of mind, fear of death, essence of the mind</keywords>

--- Page 194 ---
<meta>This page continues the demonstration of how the human mind achieves a state of eternity through knowledge. It builds on the previous page's discussion of the "Intellectual Love of God" by connecting the physical capabilities of the body to the spiritual endurance of the mind.</meta>

<page-num>177</page-num>
<header>ON HUMAN FREEDOM.</header>

### PROOF.
The essence of the Mind consists in knowledge <note>original: "cognitione"; Spinoza defines the mind's very being as its power to think and understand</note> (by Proposition 11, Part 2); therefore, the more the Mind knows things by the **second** and **third kinds of knowledge**, the greater the part of it that remains (by Propositions 29 and 23 of this Part), and consequently (by the preceding Proposition), the greater the part of it that is not touched by **affects** <note>original: "affectibus"; these are the emotions or passions triggered by external forces</note> which are contrary to our nature—that is (by Proposition 30, Part 4), which are evil. Therefore, the more things the Mind understands by the second and third kinds of knowledge, the greater the part of it that remains unharmed, and consequently the less it suffers from its emotions, etc. **Which was to be demonstrated.**

### SCHOLIUM.
From this we understand what I touched upon in the Scholium to Proposition 39, Part 4, and what I promised to explain in this Part; namely, that **death** is less harmful the greater the Mind’s clear and distinct knowledge is, and consequently, the more the Mind loves God. Furthermore, because (by Proposition 27 of this Part) the highest possible **contentment of spirit** <note>original: "acquiescentia"; a state of inner peace or satisfaction arising from the mind's own power</note> arises from the third kind of knowledge <note>The "third kind" refers to intuition, or the direct insight into the essence of things and God</note>, it follows that the human Mind can be of such a nature that the part of it which we showed perishes with the Body (see Proposition 21 of this Part) is of no importance in relation to the part of it that remains. But more on these matters shortly.

## PROPOSITION XXXIX.
*He who has a Body capable of doing many things has a Mind whose greatest part is eternal.*

### PROOF.
He who has a Body capable of doing many things is least troubled by emotions that are evil (by Proposition 38, Part 4)—that is (by Proposition 30, Part 4), by emotions that are contrary to our nature. And so (by Proposition 10 of this Part), he has the power of ordering and connecting the Body's affections <note>original: "affectiones"; the various states or changes the body undergoes</note> according to the order of the **intellect**, and consequently of ensuring (by Proposition 14 of this Part) that all the Body's affections are related to the **idea of God**. From this it will happen (by Proposition 15 of this Part) that he is affected with **Love toward God**, which (by Proposition 16 of this Part) must occupy or constitute the greatest part of the Mind; and therefore (by Proposition 33 of this Part), he has a Mind whose greatest part is eternal. **Which was to be demonstrated.**

### SCHOLIUM.
Because human Bodies are capable of many things, there is no doubt that they can be of such a nature as to be related to Minds which have a great knowledge of themselves and of God, and whose greatest or principal part is eternal, and thus they hardly fear death. But so that these things may be understood more clearly, we must notice here that we live in continuous variation, and according to whether we change for the better or the worse, we are called happy or unhappy. For he who has passed from being an infant or a child to being a corpse is called unhappy; and on the contrary, it is attributed to happiness if we have been able to run through the whole span of life with a **sound Mind in a sound Body**. And in truth, he who has a Body capable of very few things, like an infant or a child, and who depends most on external causes, has a Mind which, considered in itself alone, is conscious of almost nothing of itself, nor of God, nor of things; and on the contrary, he who has a Body capable of many things, has a Mind which, considered in itself alone...

<summary>Spinoza argues that the more a person understands through reason and intuition, the less they are affected by harmful emotions and the greater the eternal part of their mind becomes. He suggests that a physically capable body supports a mind capable of the "Love of God," which transcends the fear of death.</summary>
<keywords>human freedom, eternity of the mind, kinds of knowledge, love of God, contentment of spirit, sound mind in a sound body, affects, intellect</keywords>

--- Page 195 ---
<meta>This page continues Spinoza’s discussion on the transition from the physical limitations of the body to the intellectual freedom of the mind. It concludes the previous scholium regarding how a mind focused on reason becomes less vulnerable to the passing of the body before moving into the core logic of perfection and the ethical life.</meta>

<page-num>178</page-num>
<header>ETHICS PART V.</header>

...is highly conscious of itself, of God, and of things. In this life, therefore, we primarily strive that the body of infancy be changed into another which—as far as its nature allows and is beneficial to it—is capable of many things, and which is related to a Mind that is highly conscious of itself, of God, and of things. We do this so that everything related to its memory or imagination is of hardly any importance in respect to the intellect, as I have already said in the Scholium to the preceding Proposition.

### PROPOSITION XL.

*The more perfection each thing has, the more it acts and the less it is acted upon; conversely, the more it acts, the more perfect it is.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

The more perfect each thing is, the more reality it has (by Definition 6, Part 2), and consequently (by Proposition 3, Part 3, with its Scholium) the more it acts and the less it is acted upon. This proof proceeds in the same way in reverse order, from which it follows that a thing is conversely more perfect the more it acts. **Which was to be demonstrated.**

### COROLLARY.

From this it follows that the part of the Mind that remains, however large it may be, is more perfect than the rest. For the eternal part of the Mind (by Propositions 23 and 29 of this Part) is the intellect, through which alone we are said to act (by Proposition 3, Part 3); whereas that which we have shown to perish is the imagination itself (by Proposition 21 of this Part), through which alone we are said to be acted upon <note>original: "pati"; in Spinoza’s system, to "suffer" or be "passive" means to be influenced by external forces rather than one's own internal reason</note> (by Proposition 3, Part 3 and the General Definition of Affects); and therefore (by the preceding Proposition) that eternal part, however large it may be, is more perfect than this perishing part. **Which was to be demonstrated.**

### SCHOLIUM.

These are the things I decided to demonstrate regarding the Mind, insofar as it is considered without relation to the Body's existence. From these, and simultaneously from Proposition 21, Part 1, and others, it appears that our Mind, insofar as it understands, is an eternal mode of thinking, which is determined by another eternal mode of thinking, and this again by another, and so on to infinity; such that all of them together constitute the eternal and infinite intellect of God.

### PROPOSITION XLI.

*Even if we did not know that our Mind is eternal, we would still consider as primary Piety, Religion, and absolutely everything that we showed in the Fourth Part to relate to Tenacity and Nobility.*

<note>Spinoza uses "Tenacity" (Animositas) to mean the courage to preserve one's own being according to reason, and "Nobility" (Generositas) to mean the desire to help others and join them in friendship.</note>

### DEMONSTRATION.

The first and only foundation of virtue, or the rule for living rightly (by the Corollary to Proposition 22 and by Proposition 24, Part 4), is seeking what is useful to oneself. However, in order to determine those things that Reason dictates as useful, we had no regard for the eternity of the Mind, which we only came to know in this fifth Part. Therefore, even if we were ignorant at that time that the Mind is eternal, we would still consider those things that relate to Tenacity and Nobility...

---

1) Schmidt, Auerbach, Bruder. — First Edition, Kirchmann: *Axiom 6.*

<vocab>Mind, Body, Intellect, Perfection, Piety, Religion, Tenacity, Nobility, Spinoza, Ethics</vocab>

<summary>This page establishes that the mind's perfection is directly linked to its capacity for rational action and that the "eternal" part of the mind is the intellect. Spinoza argues that even without the promise of an eternal soul, the virtues of courage and nobility remain the only rational way to live.</summary>
<keywords>perfection, activity vs passivity, eternal mind, intellect vs imagination, piety, tenacity, nobility, foundations of virtue</keywords>

--- Page 196 ---
<meta>This page concludes the final propositions of the Ethics, focusing on the nature of "Blessedness" and the internal reward of virtue. It transitions from the technical proofs of the Mind's power to a final reflection on the common misconceptions of religious life.</meta>

<page-num>179</page-num>
<header>ON HUMAN FREEDOM.</header>

...we have shown to be related to the Mind, we regarded as primary; and therefore, even if we were still ignorant of this very fact, we would still regard those same dictates of Reason as primary. **Which was to be demonstrated.**

### SCHOLIUM.

The common belief of the masses seems to be otherwise. For most people seem to believe that they are free only insofar as they are allowed to obey their own lusts, and that they surrender their rights only insofar as they are bound to live according to the command of divine law. They believe, therefore, that Piety and Religion <note>Correction from the First Edition: The original text read "Piety therefore, and Religion."</note>, and absolutely everything related to Strength of Mind <term>Fortitudo: Spinoza’s term for the combined virtues of courage and nobility</term>, are burdens which they hope to lay down after death, and they hope to receive a reward for their servitude—namely, for their Piety and Religion. They are driven not by this Hope alone, but also and chiefly by Fear <note>Spinoza argues that living by fear and hope is a form of spiritual bondage</note>, namely the fear of being punished with terrible torments after death; it is this that induces them to live according to the command of divine law, as far as their weakness and powerless spirit allow.

And if this Hope and Fear were not present in men—but if, on the contrary, they believed that Minds perish with the Body, and that no longer life remained for the wretched who are worn out by the burden of Piety—they would return to their natural inclinations and wish to govern everything by lust, obeying fortune rather than themselves. This seems no less absurd to me than if someone, because he does not believe he can nourish his Body with healthy food forever, should prefer to saturate himself with poisons and deadly substances; or because he sees that the Mind is not eternal or immortal, he would rather be insane and live without Reason. These things are so absurd they hardly deserve mention.

## PROPOSITION XLII.

***Blessedness*** <term>Beatitudo: A state of supreme happiness or peace arising from the knowledge of God</term> *is not the reward of virtue, but virtue itself; nor do we enjoy it because we restrain our lusts, but on the contrary, because we enjoy it, we are able to restrain our lusts.*

### DEMONSTRATION.

Blessedness consists in the Love of God (*by Proposition 36 of this part and its Scholium*), which Love indeed arises from the third kind of knowledge (*by the Corollary to Proposition 32 of this part*); and therefore this Love (*by Propositions 59 and 3 of Part 3*) must be referred to the Mind insofar as it acts; and consequently (*by Definition 8 of Part 4*) it is virtue itself. **That was the first point.**

Next, the more the Mind enjoys this divine Love or blessedness, the more it understands (*by Proposition 32 of this part*), that is (*by the Corollary to Proposition 3 of this part*), the more power it has over its emotions <term>Affectus: In Spinoza's system, these are the "affects" or changes in the body’s power to act, along with the ideas of those changes</term>, and (*by Proposition 38 of this part*) the less it suffers from emotions that are bad; and thus, from the fact that the Mind enjoys this divine Love or Blessedness, it has the power to restrain lusts. And because human power to restrain emotions consists in the intellect alone, therefore no one enjoys blessedness because he has restrained his emotions; on the contrary, the power to restrain lusts arises from blessedness itself. **Which was to be demonstrated.**

### SCHOLIUM.

With this, I have completed everything I wanted to show regarding the Mind's power over the emotions, and the Mind's Freedom. From these things, it is clear how much...

<summary>This page concludes the Ethics by arguing that virtue is its own reward (Blessedness) and that true self-control over lusts comes from the joy of understanding God, rather than through fear of punishment or hope for rewards after death.</summary>
<keywords>Blessedness, Virtue, Love of God, Human Freedom, Emotions, Piety, Religion, Reason</keywords>

--- Page 197 ---
<meta>This page concludes Part V of the Ethics and the entire work. It provides the final contrast between the wise person, who achieves freedom through understanding, and the ignorant person, who is a slave to external causes. It ends with Spinoza’s famous maxim on the difficulty of reaching such a state.</meta>

<page-num>180</page-num>
<header>ETHICS PART V.</header>

The wise person excels, and is more powerful than the ignorant person, who is driven by mere impulse <note>original: *libidine*, referring to blind desire or lust</note> alone. For the ignorant person, besides being driven in many ways by external causes and never possessing true **peace of mind** <term>Acquiescentia: Spinoza’s technical term for the highest contentment or joy that arises when the Mind contemplates its own power of acting</term>, lives moreover as if unaware of himself, of God, and of things; and as soon as he ceases to be acted upon <note>original: *pati*, meaning to be passive or to be a victim of circumstances</note>, he also ceases to be. 

On the contrary, the wise person, insofar as he is considered as such, is scarcely troubled in spirit, but being conscious of himself, of God, and of things by a certain **eternal necessity**, he never ceases to be, but always possesses true peace of mind. 

If the way which I have shown to lead to these things now seems very difficult, it can nevertheless be found. And indeed, it must be difficult, since it is so rarely discovered. For how could it be, if salvation <note>original: *salus*; in Spinoza's context, this refers to mental health, freedom, and well-being rather than a religious afterlife</note> were ready at hand and could be found without great effort, that it is neglected by almost everyone? 

But all things excellent <note>original: *præclara*; also translated as "noble" or "beautiful"</note> are as difficult as they are rare.

->***T H E   E N D .***<-

---

<summary>This concluding passage of the Ethics contrasts the enlightened stability of the wise person with the reactive, unstable life of the ignorant. It famously ends by acknowledging that the path to such philosophical freedom is arduous and rare.</summary>
<keywords>wisdom, ignorance, peace of mind, eternal necessity, salvation, excellence, Spinoza's Ethics conclusion</keywords>
